  This volume is a self-contained introduction to interactive proof in higher-order logic (HOL), using the proof assistant Isabelle 2002. It provides a direct route into higher-order logic and presents techniques for modelling functional programs, reasoning about sets and relations, and verifying a security protocol.

=== PAGE 3 ===

   17  Preface

   18  This volume is a self-contained introduction to interactive proof in higher-

   19  order logic (HOL), using the proof assistant Isabelle. It is written for potential

   20  users rather than for our colleagues in the research world.

   21  The book has three parts.

   22  – The first part, Elementary Techniques, shows how to model functional

   23  programs in higher-order logic. Early examples involve lists and the natural

   24  numbers. Most proofs are two steps long, consisting of induction on a
   25  chosen variable followed by the auto tactic. But even this elementary part

   26  covers such advanced topics as nested and mutual recursion.

   27  – The second part, Logic and Sets, presents a collection of lower-level

   28  tactics that you can use to apply rules selectively. It also describes Isa-

   29  belle/HOL's treatment of sets, functions and relations and explains how to

   30  define sets inductively. One of the examples concerns the theory of model

   31  checking, and another is drawn from a classic textbook on formal languages.

   32  – The third part, Advanced Material, describes a variety of other topics.

   33  Among these are the real numbers, records and overloading. Advanced

   34  techniques for induction and recursion are described. A whole chapter is

   35  devoted to an extended example: the verification of a security protocol.

   36  The typesetting relies on Wenzel's theory presentation tools. An annotated source file is run, typesetting the theory in the form of a LATEX source

   37  file. This book is derived almost entirely from output generated in this way.

   38  The final chapter of Part I explains how users may produce their own formal

   39  documents in a similar fashion.
   40  Isabelle's web site1 contains links to the download area and to documenta-

   41  tion and other information. The classic Isabelle user interface is Proof Gen-

   42  eral / Emacs by David Aspinall's. This book says very little about Proof

   43  General, which has its own documentation.

   44  This tutorial owes a lot to the constant discussions with and the valuable

   45  feedback from the Isabelle group at Munich: Stefan Berghofer, Olaf M¨uller,

   46  Wolfgang Naraschewski, David von Oheimb, Leonor Prensa Nieto, Cornelia

   47  Pusch, Norbert Schirmer and Martin Strecker. Stephan Merz was also kind
   48  1 https://isabelle.in.tum.de/

  276  Part I

=== PAGE 13 ===

  278  1. The Basics

  279  1.1 Introduction

  280  This book is a tutorial on how to use the theorem prover Isabelle/HOL as a

  281  specification and verification system. Isabelle is a generic system for imple-

  282  menting logical formalisms, and Isabelle/HOL is the specialization of Isabelle

  283  for HOL, which abbreviates Higher-Order Logic. We introduce HOL step by

  284  step following the equation

  285  HOL = Functional Programming + Logic.

  286  We do not assume that you are familiar with mathematical logic. However, we

  287  do assume that you are used to logical and set theoretic notation, as covered

  288  in a good discrete mathematics course [34], and that you are familiar with

  289  the basic concepts of functional programming [5, 14, 29, 35]. Although this

  290  tutorial initially concentrates on functional programming, do not be misled:

  291  HOL can express most mathematical concepts, and functional programming

  292  is just one particularly simple and ubiquitous instance.

  293  Isabelle [28] is implemented in ML [19]. This has influenced some of Isa-

  294  belle/HOL's concrete syntax but is otherwise irrelevant for us: this tutorial

  295  is based on Isabelle/Isar [37], an extension of Isabelle which hides the im-

  296  plementation language almost completely. Thus the full name of the system

  297  should be Isabelle/Isar/HOL, but that is a bit of a mouthful.

  298  There are other implementations of HOL, in particular the one by Mike

  299  Gordon et al., which is usually referred to as "the HOL system" [10]. For

  300  us, HOL refers to the logical system, and sometimes its incarnation Isa-

  301  belle/HOL.

  302  A tutorial is by definition incomplete. Currently the tutorial only intro-

  303  duces the rudiments of Isar's proof language. To fully exploit the power of

  304  Isar, in particular the ability to write readable and structured proofs, you

  305  should start with Nipkow's overview [24] and consult the Isabelle/Isar Refer-

  306  ence Manual [37] and Wenzel's PhD thesis [38] (which discusses many proof

  307  patterns) for further details. If you want to use Isabelle's ML level directly

  308  (for example for writing your own proof procedures) see the Isabelle Reference

  309  Manual [26]; for details relating to HOL see the Isabelle/HOL manual [25].

  310  All manuals have a comprehensive index.
=== PAGE 14 ===

  311  4 1. The Basics

  312  1.2 Theories

  313  Working with Isabelle means creating theories. Roughly speaking, a theory

  314  is a named collection of types, functions, and theorems, much like a module

  315  in a programming language or a specification in a specification language. In

  316  fact, theories in HOL can be either. The general format of a theory T is
  317  theory T
  318  imports B1 . . . Bn
  319  begin
  320  declarations, definitions, and proofs
  321  end
  322  where B1 . . . Bn are the names of existing theories that T is based on and

  323  declarations, definitions, and proofs represents the newly introduced concepts
  324  (types, functions etc.) and proofs about them. The Bi are the direct parent

  325  theories of T. Everything defined in the parent theories (and their parents,

  326  recursively) is automatically visible. To avoid name clashes, identifiers can

  327  be qualified by theory names as in T.f and B.f. Each theory T must reside

  328  in a theory file named T.thy.

  329  This tutorial is concerned with introducing you to the different linguistic

  330  constructs that can fill the declarations, definitions, and proofs above. A com-

  331  plete grammar of the basic constructs is found in the Isabelle/Isar Reference

  332  Manual [37].
  333  !!HOL contains a theory Main , the union of all the basic predefined theories like
  334  arithmetic, lists, sets, etc. Unless you know what you are doing, always include
  335  Main as a direct or indirect parent of all your theories.

  336  HOL's theory collection is available online at
  337  https://isabelle.in.tum.de/library/HOL/

  338  and is recommended browsing. In subdirectory Library you find a growing
  339  library of useful theories that are not part of Main but can be included among

  340  the parents of a theory and will then be loaded automatically.

  341  For the more adventurous, there is the Archive of Formal Proofs, a journal-

  342  like collection of more advanced Isabelle theories:
  343  https://isa-afp.org/

  344  We hope that you will contribute to it yourself one day.

  345  1.3 Types, Terms and Formulae

  346  Embedded in a theory are the types, terms and formulae of HOL. HOL is

  347  a typed logic whose type system resembles that of functional programming

  348  languages like ML or Haskell. Thus there are
=== PAGE 15 ===

  349  1.3 Types, Terms and Formulae 5

  350  base types, in particular bool , the type of truth values, and nat , the type of

  351  natural numbers.
  352  type constructors, in particular list , the type of lists, and set , the type of
  353  sets. Type constructors are written postfix, e.g. (nat)list is the type

  354  of lists whose elements are natural numbers. Parentheses around single
  355  arguments can be dropped (as in nat list ), multiple arguments are separated by commas (as in (bool,nat)ty).

  356  function types, denoted by ⇒. In HOL ⇒ represents total functions only. As
  357  is customary, τ1 ⇒ τ2 ⇒ τ3 means τ1 ⇒ (τ2 ⇒ τ3 ). Isabelle also supports the notation [τ1, . . . , τn] ⇒ τ which abbreviates τ1 ⇒ · · · ⇒ τn
  358  ⇒ τ .
  359  type variables, denoted by 'a , 'b etc., just like in ML. They give rise to
  360  polymorphic types like 'a ⇒ 'a, the type of the identity function.

  361  !!Types are extremely important because they prevent us from writing nonsense.
  362  Isabelle insists that all terms and formulae must be well-typed and will print an
  363  error message if a type mismatch is encountered. To reduce the amount of explicit
  364  type information that needs to be provided by the user, Isabelle infers the type of
  365  all variables automatically (this is called type inference) and keeps quiet about
  366  it. Occasionally this may lead to misunderstandings between you and the system. If
  367  anything strange happens, we recommend that you ask Isabelle to display all type
  368  information via the Proof General menu item Isabelle > Settings > Show Types (see
  369  Sect. 1.5 for details).

  370  Terms are formed as in functional programming by applying functions
  371  to arguments. If f is a function of type τ1 ⇒ τ2 and t is a term of type
  372  τ1 then f t is a term of type τ2. HOL also supports infix functions like +

  373  and some basic constructs from functional programming, such as conditional

  374  expressions:
  375  if b then t1 else t2 Here b is of type bool and t1 and t2 are of the same

  376  type.
  377  let x = t in u is equivalent to u where all free occurrences of x have been
  378  replaced by t. For example, let x = 0 in x+x is equivalent to 0+0. Multiple bindings are separated by semicolons: let x1 = t1;...; xn = tn in
  379  u.
  380  case e of c1 ⇒ e1 | ... | cn ⇒ en evaluates to ei if e is of the form ci.
  381  Terms may also contain λ-abstractions. For example, λx. x+1 is the function that takes an argument x and returns x+1. Instead of λx. λy. λz. t we
  382  can write λx y z. t.
  383  Formulae are terms of type bool. There are the basic constants True
  384  and False and the usual logical connectives (in decreasing order of priority):

  385  ¬, ∧, ∨, and −→, all of which (except the unary ¬) associate to the right.
  386  In particular A −→ B −→ C means A −→ (B −→ C) and is thus logically
  387  equivalent to A ∧ B −→ C (which is (A ∧ B) −→ C ).
  388  Equality is available in the form of the infix function = of type 'a ⇒ 'a
  389  ⇒ bool. Thus t1 = t2 is a formula provided t1 and t2 are terms of the same
=== PAGE 16 ===

  390  6 1. The Basics

  391  type. If t1 and t2 are of type bool then = acts as if-and-only-if. The formula
  392  t1 6= t2 is merely an abbreviation for ¬(t1 = t2).
  393  Quantifiers are written as ∀ x. P and ∃ x. P. There is even ∃! x. P, which
  394  means that there exists exactly one x that satisfies P . Nested quantifications
  395  can be abbreviated: ∀ x y z. P means ∀ x. ∀ y.∀ z. P.

  396  Despite type inference, it is sometimes necessary to attach explicit type
  397  constraints to a term. The syntax is t::τ as in x < (y::nat). Note that
  398  :: binds weakly and should therefore be enclosed in parentheses. For instance, x < y::nat is ill-typed because it is interpreted as (x < y)::nat. Type

  399  constraints may be needed to disambiguate expressions involving overloaded
  400  functions such as +, * and <. Section 8.3.1 discusses overloading, while Ta-

  401  ble A.2 presents the most important overloaded function symbols.

  402  In general, HOL's concrete syntax tries to follow the conventions of func-

  403  tional programming and mathematics. Here are the main rules that you

  404  should be familiar with to avoid certain syntactic traps:
  405  – Remember that f t u means (f t) u and not f(t u) !
  406  – Isabelle allows infix functions like +. The prefix form of function application
  407  binds more strongly than anything else and hence f x + y means (f x) + y
  408  and not f(x+y).

  409  – Remember that in HOL if-and-only-if is expressed using equality. But

  410  equality has a high priority, as befitting a relation, while if-and-only-if
  411  typically has the lowest priority. Thus, ¬ ¬ P = P means ¬¬(P = P) and
  412  not (¬¬P) = P. When using = to mean logical equivalence, enclose both
  413  operands in parentheses, as in (A ∧ B) = (B ∧ A).

  414  – Constructs with an opening but without a closing delimiter bind very

  415  weakly and should therefore be enclosed in parentheses if they appear in
  416  subterms, as in (λx. x) = f. This includes if, let, case, λ, and quantifiers.
  417  – Never write λx.x or ∀ x.x=x because x.x is always taken as a single qualified
  418  identifier. Write λx. x and ∀ x. x=x instead.
  419  – Identifiers may contain the characters _ and ', except at the beginning.

  420  For the sake of readability, we use the usual mathematical symbols

  421  throughout the tutorial. Their ascii-equivalents are shown in table A.1 in

  422  the appendix.
  423  !!A particular problem for novices can be the priority of operators. If you are
  424  unsure, use additional parentheses. In those cases where Isabelle echoes your
  425  input, you can see which parentheses are dropped — they were superfluous. If you
  426  are unsure how to interpret Isabelle's output because you don't know where the
  427  (dropped) parentheses go, set the Proof General flag Isabelle > Settings > Show
  428  Brackets (see Sect. 1.5).
=== PAGE 17 ===

  429  1.4 Variables 7

  430  1.4 Variables

  431  Isabelle distinguishes free and bound variables, as is customary. Bound vari-

  432  ables are automatically renamed to avoid clashes with free variables. In ad-

  433  dition, Isabelle has a third kind of variable, called a schematic variable or
  434  unknown, which must have a ? as its first character. Logically, an unknown

  435  is a free variable. But it may be instantiated by another term during the proof

  436  process. For example, the mathematical theorem x = x is represented in Isabelle as ?x = ?x, which means that Isabelle can instantiate it arbitrarily. This

  437  is in contrast to ordinary variables, which remain fixed. The programming

  438  language Prolog calls unknowns logical variables.

  439  Most of the time you can and should ignore unknowns and work with

  440  ordinary variables. Just don't be surprised that after you have finished the

  441  proof of a theorem, Isabelle will turn your free variables into unknowns. It

  442  indicates that Isabelle will automatically instantiate those unknowns suitably

  443  when the theorem is used in some other proof. Note that for readability we
  444  often drop the ? s when displaying a theorem.
  445  !!For historical reasons, Isabelle accepts ? as an ASCII representation of the
  446  ∃ symbol. However, the ? character must then be followed by a space, as in
  447  ? x. f(x) = 0. Otherwise, ?x is interpreted as a schematic variable. The preferred
  448  ASCII representation of the ∃ symbol is EX .

  449  1.5 Interaction and Interfaces

  450  The recommended interface for Isabelle/Isar is the (X)Emacs-based Proof

  451  General [1, 2]. Interaction with Isabelle at the shell level, although possible,

  452  should be avoided. Most of the tutorial is independent of the interface and is

  453  phrased in a neutral language. For example, the phrase "to abandon a proof"

  454  corresponds to the obvious action of clicking on the Undo symbol in Proof

  455  General. Proof General specific information is often displayed in paragraphs

  456  identified by a miniature Proof General icon. Here are two examples:
  457  Proof General supports a special font with mathematical symbols known as "xsymbols". All symbols have ascii-equivalents: for example, you can enter either
  458  & or \<and> to obtain ∧. For a list of the most frequent symbols see table A.1 in
  459  the appendix.
  460  Note that by default x-symbols are not enabled. You have to switch them on
  461  via the menu item Proof-General > Options > X-Symbols (and save the option via
  462  the top-level Options menu).

  463  Proof General offers the Isabelle menu for displaying information and setting
  464  flags. A particularly useful flag is Isabelle > Settings > Show Types which causes
  465  Isabelle to output the type information that is usually suppressed. This is indispensible in case of errors of all kinds because often the types reveal the source of
  466  the problem. Once you have diagnosed the problem you may no longer want to see
  467  the types because they clutter all output. Simply reset the flag.
=== PAGE 18 ===

  468  8 1. The Basics

  469  1.6 Getting Started

  470  Assuming you have installed Isabelle and Proof General, you start it by typing

  471  Isabelle in a shell window. This launches a Proof General window. By
  472  default, you are in HOL1.
  473  You can choose a different logic via the Isabelle > Logics menu.

  474  1 This is controlled by the ISABELLE_LOGIC setting, see The Isabelle System Manual
  475  for more details.
=== PAGE 19 ===

  476  2. Functional Programming in HOL

  477  This chapter describes how to write functional programs in HOL and how

  478  to verify them. However, most of the constructs and proof procedures in-

  479  troduced are general and recur in any specification or verification task. We

  480  really should speak of functional modelling rather than functional program-

  481  ming: our primary aim is not to write programs but to design abstract models

  482  of systems. HOL is a specification language that goes well beyond what can

  483  be expressed as a program. However, for the time being we concentrate on

  484  the computable.

  485  If you are a purist functional programmer, please note that all functions in

  486  HOL must be total: they must terminate for all inputs. Lazy data structures

  487  are not directly available.

  488  2.1 An Introductory Theory

  489  Functional programming needs datatypes and functions. Both of them can be

  490  defined in a theory with a syntax reminiscent of languages like ML or Haskell.

  491  As an example consider the theory in figure 2.1. We will now examine it line

  492  by line.
  493  theory ToyList
  494  imports Main
  495  begin
  496  HOL already has a predefined theory of lists called List — ToyList is merely

  497  a small fragment of it chosen as an example. To avoid some ambiguities

  498  caused by defining lists twice, we manipulate the concrete syntax and name
  499  space of theory Main as follows.
  500  unbundle no list_syntax
  501  no notation append (infixr "@" 65)
  502  hide type list
  503  hide const rev
  504  datatype 'a list = Nil ("[]")
  505  | Cons 'a "'a list" (infixr "#" 65)
  506  The datatype list introduces two constructors Nil and Cons , the empty list

  507  and the operator that adds an element to the front of a list. For example,
=== PAGE 20 ===

  508  10 2. Functional Programming in HOL
  509  theory ToyList
  510  imports Main
  511  begin
  512  no_notation Nil ("[]") and Cons (infixr "#" 65) and append (infixr "@" 65)
  513  hide_type list
  514  hide_const rev
  515  datatype 'a list = Nil ("[]")
  516  | Cons 'a "'a list" (infixr "#" 65)
  517  (* This is the append function: *)
  518  primrec app :: "'a list => 'a list => 'a list" (infixr "@" 65)
  519  where
  520  "[] @ ys = ys" |
  521  "(x # xs) @ ys = x # (xs @ ys)"
  522  primrec rev :: "'a list => 'a list" where
  523  "rev [] = []" |
  524  "rev (x # xs) = (rev xs) @ (x # [])"
  525  Fig. 2.1. A Theory of Lists

  526  the term Cons True (Cons False Nil) is a value of type bool list, namely
  527  the list with the elements True and False. Because this notation quickly

  528  becomes unwieldy, the datatype declaration is annotated with an alternative
  529  syntax: instead of Nil and Cons x xs we can write [] and x # xs . In fact,
  530  this alternative syntax is the familiar one. Thus the list Cons True (Cons
  531  False Nil) becomes True # False # []. The annotation infixr means that #
  532  associates to the right: the term x # y # z is read as x # (y # z) and not as
  533  (x # y) # z. The 65 is the priority of the infix #.
  534  !!Syntax annotations can be powerful, but they are difficult to master and are
  535  never necessary. You could drop them from theory ToyList and go back to the
  536  identifiers Nil and Cons. Novices should avoid using syntax annotations in their
  537  own theories.
  538  Next, two functions app and rev are defined recursively, in this order,

  539  because Isabelle insists on definition before use:
  540  primrec app :: "'a list ⇒ 'a list ⇒ 'a list" (infixr "@" 65) where
  541  "[] @ ys = ys" |
  542  "(x # xs) @ ys = x # (xs @ ys)"
  543  primrec rev :: "'a list ⇒ 'a list" where
  544  "rev [] = []" |
  545  "rev (x # xs) = (rev xs) @ (x # [])"

  546  Each function definition is of the form
  547  primrec name :: type (optional syntax) where equations
=== PAGE 21 ===

  548  2.2 Evaluation 11

  549  The equations must be separated by |. Function app is annotated with concrete syntax. Instead of the prefix syntax app xs ys the infix xs @ ys becomes

  550  the preferred form.
  551  The equations for app and rev hardly need comments: app appends two
  552  lists and rev reverses a list. The keyword primrec indicates that the recur-

  553  sion is of a particularly primitive kind where each recursive call peels off a

  554  datatype constructor from one of the arguments. Thus the recursion always

  555  terminates, i.e. the function is total.

  556  The termination requirement is absolutely essential in HOL, a logic of

  557  total functions. If we were to drop it, inconsistencies would quickly arise: the

  558  "definition" f (n) = f(n) + 1 immediately leads to 0 = 1 by subtracting f(n)

  559  on both sides.
  560  !!As we have indicated, the requirement for total functions is an essential characteristic of HOL. It is only because of totality that reasoning in HOL is comparatively easy. More generally, the philosophy in HOL is to refrain from asserting
  561  arbitrary axioms (such as function definitions whose totality has not been proved)
  562  because they quickly lead to inconsistencies. Instead, fixed constructs for introducing types and functions are offered (such as datatype and primrec) which are
  563  guaranteed to preserve consistency.

  564  A remark about syntax. The textual definition of a theory follows a fixed

  565  syntax with keywords like datatype and end. Embedded in this syntax are

  566  the types and formulae of HOL, whose syntax is extensible (see Sect. 4.1), e.g.

  567  by new user-defined infix operators. To distinguish the two levels, everything

  568  HOL-specific (terms and types) should be enclosed in ". . . ". To lessen this

  569  burden, quotation marks around a single identifier can be dropped, unless the
  570  identifier happens to be a keyword, for example "end". When Isabelle prints

  571  a syntax error message, it refers to the HOL syntax as the inner syntax and

  572  the enclosing theory language as the outer syntax.

  573  Comments must be in enclosed in (*and*).

  574  2.2 Evaluation

  575  Assuming you have processed the declarations and definitions of ToyList

  576  presented so far, you may want to test your functions by running them. For
  577  example, what is the value of rev (True # False # [])? Command
  578  value "rev (True # False # [])"
  579  yields the correct result False # True # []. But we can go beyond mere func-

  580  tional programming and evaluate terms with variables in them, executing

  581  functions symbolically:
  582  value "rev (a # b # c # [])"
  583  yields c # b # a # [].
=== PAGE 22 ===

  584  12 2. Functional Programming in HOL

  585  2.3 An Introductory Proof

  586  Having convinced ourselves (as well as one can by testing) that our definitions

  587  capture our intentions, we are ready to prove a few simple theorems. This

  588  will illustrate not just the basic proof commands but also the typical proof

  589  process.

  590  Main Goal. Our goal is to show that reversing a list twice produces the

  591  original list.
  592  theorem rev_rev [simp]: "rev(rev xs) = xs"

  593  This theorem command does several things:
  594  – It establishes a new theorem to be proved, namely rev (rev xs) = xs.
  595  – It gives that theorem the name rev_rev, for later reference.
  596  – It tells Isabelle (via the bracketed attribute simp ) to take the eventual

  597  theorem as a simplification rule: future proofs involving simplification will
  598  replace occurrences of rev (rev xs) by xs.

  599  The name and the simplification attribute are optional. Isabelle's response

  600  is to print the initial proof state consisting of some header information (like

  601  how many subgoals there are) followed by
  602  1. rev (rev xs) = xs

  603  For compactness reasons we omit the header in this tutorial. Until we have

  604  finished a proof, the proof state proper always looks like this:
  605  1. G1
  606  ..
  607  .
  608  n. Gn
  609  The numbered lines contain the subgoals G1, . . . , Gn that we need to prove to

  610  establish the main goal. Initially there is only one subgoal, which is identical

  611  with the main goal. (If you always want to see the main goal as well, set the
  612  flag Proof.show_main_goal — this flag used to be set by default.)
  613  Let us now get back to rev (rev xs) = xs. Properties of recursively de-

  614  fined functions are best established by induction. In this case there is nothing
  615  obvious except induction on xs :
  616  apply(induct_tac xs)
  617  This tells Isabelle to perform induction on variable xs. The suffix tac stands

  618  for tactic, a synonym for "theorem proving function". By default, induction

  619  acts on the first subgoal. The new proof state contains two subgoals, namely
  620  the base case (Nil ) and the induction step (Cons ):
  621  1. rev (rev []) = []
  622  2. Vx1 x2. rev (rev x2) = x2 =⇒ rev (rev (x1 # x2)) = x1 # x2

  623  The induction step is an example of the general format of a subgoal:
=== PAGE 23 ===

  624  2.3 An Introductory Proof 13

  625  i. Vx1 ...xn . assumptions =⇒ conclusion

  626  The prefix of bound variables Vx . . . x can be ignored most of the time, or
  627  1 n

  628  simply treated as a list of variables local to this subgoal. Their deeper signif-

  629  icance is explained in Chapter 5. The assumptions are the local assumptions

  630  for this subgoal and conclusion is the actual proposition to be proved. Typical

  631  proof steps that add new assumptions are induction and case distinction. In
  632  our example the only assumption is the induction hypothesis rev (rev list)
  633  = list, where list is a variable name chosen by Isabelle. If there are multiple

  634  assumptions, they are enclosed in the bracket pair [[ and ]] and separated by

  635  semicolons.

  636  Let us try to solve both goals automatically:
  637  apply(auto)
  638  This command tells Isabelle to apply a proof strategy called auto to all subgoals. Essentially, auto tries to simplify the subgoals. In our case, subgoal 1
  639  is solved completely (thanks to the equation rev [] = []) and disappears;

  640  the simplified version of subgoal 2 becomes the new subgoal 1:
  641  1. Vx1 x2. rev (rev x2) = x2 =⇒ rev (rev x2 @ x1 # []) = x1 # x2

  642  In order to simplify this subgoal further, a lemma suggests itself.

  643  First Lemma. After abandoning the above proof attempt (at the shell level

  644  type oops) we start a new proof:
  645  lemma rev_app [simp]: "rev(xs @ ys) = (rev ys) @ (rev xs)"

  646  The keywords theorem and lemma are interchangeable and merely indi-

  647  cate the importance we attach to a proposition. Therefore we use the words

  648  theorem and lemma pretty much interchangeably, too.
  649  There are two variables that we could induct on: xs and ys. Because @ is
  650  defined by recursion on the first argument, xs is the correct one:
  651  apply(induct_tac xs)

  652  This time not even the base case is solved automatically:
  653  apply(auto)

  654  1. rev ys = rev ys @ []
  655  A total of 2 subgoals...

  656  Again, we need to abandon this proof attempt and prove another simple

  657  lemma first. In the future the step of abandoning an incomplete proof before

  658  embarking on the proof of a lemma usually remains implicit.
=== PAGE 24 ===

  659  14 2. Functional Programming in HOL

  660  Second Lemma. We again try the canonical proof procedure:
  661  lemma app_Nil2 [simp]: "xs @ [] = xs"
  662  apply(induct_tac xs)
  663  apply(auto)
  664  It works, yielding the desired message No subgoals!:
  665  xs @ [] = xs
  666  No subgoals!

  667  We still need to confirm that the proof is now finished:
  668  done

  669  As a result of that final done, Isabelle associates the lemma just proved with

  670  its name. In this tutorial, we sometimes omit to show that final done if it is

  671  obvious from the context that the proof is finished.
  672  Notice that in lemma app_Nil2, as printed out after the final done, the
  673  free variable xs has been replaced by the unknown ?xs, just as explained in

  674  Sect. 1.4.

  675  Going back to the proof of the first lemma
  676  lemma rev_app [simp]: "rev(xs @ ys) = (rev ys) @ (rev xs)"
  677  apply(induct_tac xs)
  678  apply(auto)
  679  we find that this time auto solves the base case, but the induction step merely

  680  simplifies to
  681  1. Vx1 x2.
  682  rev (x2 @ ys) = rev ys @ rev x2 =⇒
  683  (rev ys @ rev x2) @ x1 # [] = rev ys @ rev x2 @ x1 # []
  684  Now we need to remember that @ associates to the right, and that # and @
  685  have the same priority (namely the 65 in their infixr annotation). Thus the

  686  conclusion really is
  687  (rev ys @ rev list) @ (a # []) = rev ys @ (rev list @ (a # []))
  688  and the missing lemma is associativity of @.

  689  Third Lemma. Abandoning the previous attempt, the canonical proof pro-

  690  cedure succeeds without further ado.
  691  lemma app_assoc [simp]: "(xs @ ys) @ zs = xs @ (ys @ zs)"
  692  apply(induct_tac xs)
  693  apply(auto)
  694  done

  695  Now we can prove the first lemma:
  696  lemma rev_app [simp]: "rev(xs @ ys) = (rev ys) @ (rev xs)"
  697  apply(induct_tac xs)
  698  apply(auto)
  699  done
=== PAGE 25 ===

  700  2.3 An Introductory Proof 15

  701  Finally, we prove our main theorem:
  702  theorem rev_rev [simp]: "rev(rev xs) = xs"
  703  apply(induct_tac xs)
  704  apply(auto)
  705  done

  706  The final end tells Isabelle to close the current theory because we are finished

  707  with its development:
  708  end

  709  The complete proof script is shown in Fig. 2.2. The concatenation of

  710  Figs. 2.1 and 2.2 constitutes the complete theory ToyList and should reside

  711  in file ToyList.thy.

  712  lemma app_Nil2 [simp]: "xs @ [] = xs"
  713  apply(induct_tac xs)
  714  apply(auto)
  715  done
  716  lemma app_assoc [simp]: "(xs @ ys) @ zs = xs @ (ys @ zs)"
  717  apply(induct_tac xs)
  718  apply(auto)
  719  done
  720  lemma rev_app [simp]: "rev(xs @ ys) = (rev ys) @ (rev xs)"
  721  apply(induct_tac xs)
  722  apply(auto)
  723  done
  724  theorem rev_rev [simp]: "rev(rev xs) = xs"
  725  apply(induct_tac xs)
  726  apply(auto)
  727  done
  728  end
  729  Fig. 2.2. Proofs about Lists

  730  Review This is the end of our toy proof. It should have familiarized you

  731  with

  732  – the standard theorem proving procedure: state a goal (lemma or theorem);

  733  proceed with proof until a separate lemma is required; prove that lemma;

  734  come back to the original goal.

  735  – a specific procedure that works well for functional programs: induction
  736  followed by all-out simplification via auto.

  737  – a basic repertoire of proof commands.
=== PAGE 26 ===

  738  16 2. Functional Programming in HOL
  739  !!It is tempting to think that all lemmas should have the simp attribute just
  740  because this was the case in the example above. However, in that example all
  741  lemmas were equations, and the right-hand side was simpler than the left-hand
  742  side — an ideal situation for simplification purposes. Unless this is clearly the case,
  743  novices should refrain from awarding a lemma the simp attribute, which has a
  744  global effect. Instead, lemmas can be applied locally where they are needed, which
  745  is discussed in the following chapter.

  746  2.4 Some Helpful Commands

  747  This section discusses a few basic commands for manipulating the proof state

  748  and can be skipped by casual readers.

  749  There are two kinds of commands used during a proof: the actual proof

  750  commands and auxiliary commands for examining the proof state and con-

  751  trolling the display. Simple proof commands are of the form apply(method),
  752  where method is typically induct_tac or auto. All such theorem proving oper-

  753  ations are referred to as methods, and further ones are introduced through-

  754  out the tutorial. Unless stated otherwise, you may assume that a method
  755  attacks merely the first subgoal. An exception is auto, which tries to solve all

  756  subgoals.

  757  The most useful auxiliary commands are as follows:

  758  Modifying the order of subgoals: defer moves the first subgoal to the end

  759  and prefer n moves subgoal n to the front.
  760  Printing theorems: thm name1 . . . namen prints the named theorems.

  761  Reading terms and types: term string reads, type-checks and prints the

  762  given string as a term in the current context; the inferred type is output

  763  as well. typ string reads and prints the given string as a type in the

  764  current context.

  765  Further commands are found in the Isabelle/Isar Reference Manual [37].

  766  Clicking on the State button redisplays the current proof state. This is helpful
  767  in case commands like thm have overwritten it.

  768  We now examine Isabelle's functional programming constructs systemat-

  769  ically, starting with inductive datatypes.

  770  2.5 Datatypes

  771  Inductive datatypes are part of almost every non-trivial application of HOL.

  772  First we take another look at an important example, the datatype of lists,

  773  before we turn to datatypes in general. The section closes with a case study.
=== PAGE 27 ===

  774  2.5 Datatypes 17

  775  2.5.1 Lists

  776  Lists are one of the essential datatypes in computing. We expect that you are
  777  already familiar with their basic operations. Theory ToyList is only a small
  778  fragment of HOL's predefined theory List 1. The latter contains many further
  779  operations. For example, the functions hd ("head") and tl ("tail") return

  780  the first element and the remainder of a list. (However, pattern matching is
  781  usually preferable to hd and tl.) Also available are higher-order functions like
  782  map and filter. Theory List also contains more syntactic sugar: [x1, . . ., xn]
  783  abbreviates x1# . . . #xn#[]. In the rest of the tutorial we always use HOL's
  784  predefined lists by building on theory Main.
  785  !!Looking ahead to sets and quanifiers in Part II: The best way to express that
  786  some element x is in a list xs is x ∈ set xs, where set is a function that turns
  787  a list into the set of its elements. By the same device you can also write bounded
  788  quantifiers like ∀x ∈ set xs or embed lists in other set expressions.

  789  2.5.2 The General Format

  790  The general HOL datatype definition is of the form

  791  datatype (α1, . . . , αn)t = C1 τ11 . . . τ1k | . . . | Cm τm1 . . . τmk
  792  1 m
  793  where αi are distinct type variables (the parameters), Ci are distinct constructor names and τij are types; it is customary to capitalize the first letter

  794  in constructor names. There are a number of restrictions (such as that the

  795  type should not be empty) detailed elsewhere [25]. Isabelle notifies you if you

  796  violate them.
  797  Laws about datatypes, such as [] 6= x#xs and (x#xs = y#ys) = (x=y
  798  ∧ xs=ys), are used automatically during proofs by simplification. The same

  799  is true for the equations in primitive recursive function definitions.
  800  Every2 datatype t comes equipped with a size function from t into the
  801  natural numbers (see Sect. 2.6.1 below). For lists, size is just the length, i.e.
  802  size [] = 0 and size(x # xs) = size xs + 1. In general, size returns

  803  – zero for all constructors that do not have an argument of type t,

  804  – one plus the sum of the sizes of all arguments of type t, for all other

  805  constructors.
  806  Note that because size is defined on every datatype, it is overloaded; on lists
  807  size is also called length , which is not overloaded. Isabelle will always show
  808  size on lists as length.
  809  1 https://isabelle.in.tum.de/library/HOL/List.html
  810  2 Except for advanced datatypes where the recursion involves "⇒" as in Sect. 3.4.3.
=== PAGE 28 ===

  811  18 2. Functional Programming in HOL

  812  2.5.3 Primitive Recursion

  813  Functions on datatypes are usually defined by recursion. In fact, most of

  814  the time they are defined by what is called primitive recursion over some

  815  datatype t. This means that the recursion equations must be of the form

  816  f x1 . . . (C y1 . . . yk) . . . xn = r

  817  such that C is a constructor of t and all recursive calls of f in r are of the
  818  form f . . . yi . . . for some i. Thus Isabelle immediately sees that f terminates

  819  because one (fixed!) argument becomes smaller with every recursive call.

  820  There must be at most one equation for each constructor. Their order is

  821  immaterial. A more general method for defining total recursive functions is

  822  introduced in Sect. 3.5.

  823  Exercise 2.5.1 Define the datatype of binary trees:
  824  datatype 'a tree = Tip | Node "'a tree" 'a "'a tree"
  825  Define a function mirror that mirrors a binary tree by swapping subtrees

  826  recursively. Prove
  827  lemma mirror_mirror: "mirror(mirror t) = t"
  828  Define a function flatten that flattens a tree into a list by traversing it in

  829  infix order. Prove
  830  lemma "flatten(mirror t) = rev(flatten t)"

  831  2.5.4 Case Expressions

  832  HOL also features case -expressions for analyzing elements of a datatype. For

  833  example,
  834  case xs of [] ⇒ [] | y # ys ⇒ y
  835  evaluates to [] if xs is [] and to y if xs is y # ys. (Since the result in both
  836  branches must be of the same type, it follows that y is of type 'a list and
  837  hence that xs is of type 'a list list.)

  838  In general, case expressions are of the form

  839  case e of pattern1 ⇒ e1 | . . . | patternm ⇒ em

  840  Like in functional programming, patterns are expressions consisting of datatype constructors (e.g. [] and #) and variables, including the wildcard "_".

  841  Not all cases need to be covered and the order of cases matters. However,

  842  one is well-advised not to wallow in complex patterns because complex case

  843  distinctions tend to induce complex proofs.
=== PAGE 29 ===

  844  2.5 Datatypes 19
  845  !!Internally Isabelle only knows about exhaustive case expressions with nonnested patterns: patterni must be of the form Ci xi1 . . . xik and C1, . . . , Cm
  846  i
  847  must be exactly the constructors of the type of e. More complex case expressions are
  848  automatically translated into the simpler form upon parsing but are not translated
  849  back for printing. This may lead to surprising output.

  850  !!Like if, case -expressions may need to be enclosed in parentheses to indicate
  851  their scope.

  852  2.5.5 Structural Induction and Case Distinction

  853  Induction is invoked by induct_tac, as we have seen above; it works for any

  854  datatype. In some cases, induction is overkill and a case distinction over all
  855  constructors of the datatype suffices. This is performed by case_tac . Here is

  856  a trivial example:
  857  lemma "(case xs of [] ⇒ [] | y#ys ⇒ xs) = xs"
  858  apply(case_tac xs)

  859  results in the proof state
  860  1. xs = [] =⇒ (case xs of [] ⇒ [] | y # ys ⇒ xs) = xs
  861  2. Va list.
  862  xs = a # list =⇒ (case xs of [] ⇒ [] | y # ys ⇒ xs) = xs

  863  which is solved automatically:
  864  apply(auto)

  865  Note that we do not need to give a lemma a name if we do not intend

  866  to refer to it explicitly in the future. Other basic laws about a datatype

  867  are applied automatically during simplification, so no special methods are

  868  provided for them.
  869  !!Induction is only allowed on free (or V-bound) variables that should not occur
  870  among the assumptions of the subgoal; see Sect. 9.2.1 for details. Case distinction (case_tac ) works for arbitrary terms, which need to be quoted if they are
  871  non-atomic. However, apart from V-bound variables, the terms must not contain
  872  variables that are bound outside. For example, given the goal ∀ xs. xs = [] ∨
  873  (∃ y ys. xs = y # ys), case_tac xs will not work as expected because Isabelle
  874  interprets the xs as a new free variable distinct from the bound xs in the goal.

  875  2.5.6 Case Study: Boolean Expressions

  876  The aim of this case study is twofold: it shows how to model boolean expres-

  877  sions and some algorithms for manipulating them, and it demonstrates the

  878  constructs introduced above.
=== PAGE 30 ===

  879  20 2. Functional Programming in HOL

  880  Modelling Boolean Expressions. We want to represent boolean expres-

  881  sions built up from variables and constants by negation and conjunction. The

  882  following datatype serves exactly that purpose:
  883  datatype boolex = Const bool | Var nat | Neg boolex
  884  | And boolex boolex
  885  The two constants are represented by Const True and Const False. Variables
  886  are represented by terms of the form Var n, where n is a natural number
  887  (type nat ). For example, the formula P0 ∧ ¬P1 is represented by the term
  888  And (Var 0) (Neg (Var 1)).

  889  The Value of a Boolean Expression. The value of a boolean expression
  890  depends on the value of its variables. Hence the function value takes an additional parameter, an environment of type nat ⇒ bool, which maps variables

  891  to their values:
  892  primrec "value" :: "boolex ⇒ (nat ⇒ bool) ⇒ bool" where
  893  "value (Const b) env = b" |
  894  "value (Var x) env = env x" |
  895  "value (Neg b) env = (¬ value b env)" |
  896  "value (And b c) env = (value b env ∧ value c env)"

  897  If-Expressions. An alternative and often more efficient (because in a cer-

  898  tain sense canonical) representation are so-called If-expressions built up from
  899  constants (CIF), variables (VIF ) and conditionals (IF):
  900  datatype ifex = CIF bool | VIF nat | IF ifex ifex ifex
  901  The evaluation of If-expressions proceeds as for boolex:
  902  primrec valif :: "ifex ⇒ (nat ⇒ bool) ⇒ bool" where
  903  "valif (CIF b) env = b" |
  904  "valif (VIF x) env = env x" |
  905  "valif (IF b t e) env = (if valif b env then valif t env
  906  else valif e env)"

  907  Converting Boolean and If-Expressions. The type boolex is close to
  908  the customary representation of logical formulae, whereas ifex is designed
  909  for efficiency. It is easy to translate from boolex into ifex :
  910  primrec bool2if :: "boolex ⇒ ifex" where
  911  "bool2if (Const b) = CIF b" |
  912  "bool2if (Var x) = VIF x" |
  913  "bool2if (Neg b) = IF (bool2if b) (CIF False) (CIF True)" |
  914  "bool2if (And b c) = IF (bool2if b) (bool2if c) (CIF False)"
  915  At last, we have something we can verify: that bool2if preserves the value

  916  of its argument:
  917  lemma "valif (bool2if b) env = value b env"

  918  The proof is canonical:
  919  apply(induct_tac b)
=== PAGE 31 ===

  920  2.5 Datatypes 21

  921  apply(auto)
  922  done

  923  In fact, all proofs in this case study look exactly like this. Hence we do not

  924  show them below.

  925  More interesting is the transformation of If-expressions into a normal
  926  form where the first argument of IF cannot be another IF but must be a

  927  constant or variable. Such a normal form can be computed by repeatedly
  928  replacing a subterm of the form IF (IF b x y) z u by IF b (IF x z u) (IF
  929  y z u), which has the same value. The following primitive recursive functions

  930  perform this task:
  931  primrec normif :: "ifex ⇒ ifex ⇒ ifex ⇒ ifex" where
  932  "normif (CIF b) t e = IF (CIF b) t e" |
  933  "normif (VIF x) t e = IF (VIF x) t e" |
  934  "normif (IF b t e) u f = normif b (normif t u f) (normif e u f)"
  935  primrec norm :: "ifex ⇒ ifex" where
  936  "norm (CIF b) = CIF b" |
  937  "norm (VIF x) = VIF x" |
  938  "norm (IF b t e) = normif b (norm t) (norm e)"

  939  Their interplay is tricky; we leave it to you to develop an intuitive under-

  940  standing. Fortunately, Isabelle can help us to verify that the transformation

  941  preserves the value of the expression:
  942  theorem "valif (norm b) env = valif b env"

  943  The proof is canonical, provided we first show the following simplification
  944  lemma, which also helps to understand what normif does:
  945  lemma [simp]:
  946  " ∀t e. valif (normif b t e) env = valif (IF b t e) env"

  947  Note that the lemma does not have a name, but is implicitly used in the
  948  proof of the theorem shown above because of the [simp] attribute.
  949  But how can we be sure that norm really produces a normal form in the

  950  above sense? We define a function that tests If-expressions for normality:
  951  primrec normal :: "ifex ⇒ bool" where
  952  "normal(CIF b) = True" |
  953  "normal(VIF x) = True" |
  954  "normal(IF b t e) = (normal t ∧ normal e ∧
  955  (case b of CIF b ⇒ True | VIF x ⇒ True | IF x y z ⇒ False))"
  956  Now we prove normal (norm b). Of course, this requires a lemma about normality of normif :
  957  lemma [simp]: "∀ t e. normal(normif b t e) = (normal t ∧ normal e)"

  958  How do we come up with the required lemmas? Try to prove the main
  959  theorems without them and study carefully what auto leaves unproved. This
  960  can provide the clue. The necessity of universal quantification (∀ t e ) in the

  961  two lemmas is explained in Sect. 3.2
=== PAGE 32 ===

  962  22 2. Functional Programming in HOL

  963  Exercise 2.5.2 We strengthen the definition of a normal If-expression as
  964  follows: the first argument of all IF s must be a variable. Adapt the above

  965  development to this changed requirement. (Hint: you may need to formulate
  966  some of the goals as implications (−→) rather than equalities (=).)

  967  2.6 Some Basic Types

  968  This section introduces the types of natural numbers and ordered pairs. Also
  969  described is type option, which is useful for modelling exceptional cases.

  970  2.6.1 Natural Numbers

  971  The type nat of natural numbers is predefined to have the constructors 0
  972  and Suc . It behaves approximately as if it were declared like this:
  973  datatype nat = zero ("0") | Suc nat
  974  In particular, there are case -expressions, for example
  975  case n of 0 ⇒ 0 | Suc m ⇒ m

  976  primitive recursion, for example
  977  primrec sum :: "nat ⇒ nat" where
  978  "sum 0 = 0" |
  979  "sum (Suc n) = Suc n + sum n"

  980  and induction, for example
  981  lemma "sum n + sum n = n*(Suc n)"
  982  apply(induct_tac n)
  983  apply(auto)
  984  done
  985  The arithmetic operations + , - , * , div , mod , min and max are predefined,
  986  as are the relations ≤ and < . As usual, m - n = 0 if m < n. There is even a
  987  least number operation LEAST . For example, (LEAST n. 0 < n) = Suc 0.
  988  !! The constants 0 and 1 and the operations +, - , *, min , max, ≤ and < are
  989  overloaded: they are available not just for natural numbers but for other types
  990  as well. For example, given the goal x + 0 = x, there is nothing to indicate that
  991  you are talking about natural numbers. Hence Isabelle can only infer that x is
  992  of some arbitrary type where 0 and + are declared. As a consequence, you will
  993  be unable to prove the goal. To alert you to such pitfalls, Isabelle flags numerals
  994  without a fixed type in its output: x + 0 = x. (In the absence of a numeral, it may
  995  take you some time to realize what has happened if Show Types is not set). In this
  996  particular example, you need to include an explicit type constraint, for example x+0
  997  = (x::nat). If there is enough contextual information this may not be necessary:
  998  Suc x = x automatically implies x::nat because Suc is not overloaded.
  999  For details on overloading see Sect. 8.3.1. Table A.2 in the appendix shows the
 1000  most important overloaded operations.
=== PAGE 33 ===

 1001  2.6 Some Basic Types 23
 1002  !!The symbols > and ≥ are merely syntax: x > y stands for y < x and similary
 1003  for ≥ and ≤.

 1004  !!Constant 1::nat is defined to equal Suc 0. This definition (see Sect. 2.7.2) is
 1005  unfolded automatically by some tactics (like auto, simp and arith ) but not by
 1006  others (especially the single step tactics in Chapter 5). If you need the full set of
 1007  numerals, see Sect. 8.4.1. Novices are advised to stick to 0 and Suc.
 1008  Both auto and simp (a method introduced below, Sect. 3.1) prove simple

 1009  arithmetic goals automatically:
 1010  lemma "[[ ¬ m < n; m < n + (1::nat) ]] =⇒ m = n"

 1011  For efficiency's sake, this built-in prover ignores quantified formulae, many

 1012  logical connectives, and all arithmetic operations apart from addition. In
 1013  consequence, auto and simp cannot prove this slightly more complex goal:
 1014  lemma "m 6= (n::nat) =⇒ m < n ∨ n < m"
 1015  The method arith is more general. It attempts to prove the first subgoal

 1016  provided it is a linear arithmetic formula. Such formulas may involve the
 1017  usual logical connectives (¬, ∧, ∨, −→, =, ∀ , ∃ ), the relations =, ≤ and <, and
 1018  the operations +, -, min and max. For example,
 1019  lemma "min i (max j (k*k)) = max (min (k*k) i) (min i (j::nat))"
 1020  apply(arith)
 1021  succeeds because k * k can be treated as atomic. In contrast,
 1022  lemma "n*n = n+1 =⇒ n=0"
 1023  is not proved by arith because the proof relies on properties of multiplication.

 1024  Only multiplication by numerals (which is the same as iterated addition) is

 1025  taken into account.
 1026  !!The running time of arith is exponential in the number of occurrences of -,
 1027  min and max because they are first eliminated by case distinctions.
 1028  If k is a numeral, div k, mod k and k dvd are also supported, where the former
 1029  two are eliminated by case distinctions, again blowing up the running time.
 1030  If the formula involves quantifiers, arith may take super-exponential time and
 1031  space.

 1032  2.6.2 Pairs

 1033  HOL also has ordered pairs: (a1 ,a2) is of type τ1 × τ2 provided each ai
 1034  is of type τi. The functions fst and snd extract the components of a pair:
 1035  fst(x,y) = x and snd(x,y) = y. Tuples are simulated by pairs nested to the
 1036  right: (a ,a ,a ) stands for (a ,(a ,a )) and τ1 × τ2 × τ3 for τ1 × (τ2 × τ3).
 1037  1 2 3 1 2 3
 1038  Therefore we have fst(snd(a1,a2,a3)) = a2.

 1039  Remarks:
=== PAGE 34 ===

 1040  24 2. Functional Programming in HOL

 1041  – There is also the type unit , which contains exactly one element denoted
 1042  by () . This type can be viewed as a degenerate product with 0 components.
 1043  – Products, like type nat, are datatypes, which means in particular that
 1044  induct_tac and case_tac are applicable to terms of product type. Both

 1045  split the term into a number of variables corresponding to the tuple struc-

 1046  ture (up to 7 components).

 1047  – Tuples with more than two or three components become unwieldy; records

 1048  are preferable.

 1049  For more information on pairs and records see Chapter 8.

 1050  2.6.3 Datatype option

 1051  Our final datatype is very simple but still eminently useful:
 1052  datatype 'a option = None | Some 'a

 1053  Frequently one needs to add a distinguished element to some existing type.
 1054  For example, type t option can model the result of a computation that may
 1055  either terminate with an error (represented by None) or return some value
 1056  v (represented by Some v ). Similarly, nat extended with ∞ can be modeled
 1057  by type nat option. In both cases one could define a new datatype with
 1058  customized constructors like Error and Infinity, but it is often simpler to
 1059  use option. For an application see Sect. 3.4.4.

 1060  2.7 Definitions

 1061  A definition is simply an abbreviation, i.e. a new name for an existing con-

 1062  struction. In particular, definitions cannot be recursive. Isabelle offers defini-

 1063  tions on the level of types and terms. Those on the type level are called type

 1064  synonyms; those on the term level are simply called definitions.

 1065  2.7.1 Type Synonyms

 1066  Type synonyms are similar to those found in ML. They are created by a

 1067  type synonym command:
 1068  type synonym number = nat
 1069  type synonym gate = "bool ⇒ bool ⇒ bool"
 1070  type synonym ('a, 'b) alist = "('a × 'b) list"

 1071  Internally all synonyms are fully expanded. As a consequence Isabelle's out-

 1072  put never contains synonyms. Their main purpose is to improve the readabil-

 1073  ity of theories. Synonyms can be used just like any other type.
=== PAGE 35 ===

 1074  2.8 The Definitional Approach 25

 1075  2.7.2 Constant Definitions

 1076  Nonrecursive definitions can be made with the definition command, for
 1077  example nand and xor gates (based on type gate above):
 1078  definition nand :: gate where "nand A B ≡ ¬(A ∧ B)"
 1079  definition xor :: gate where "xor A B ≡ A ∧ ¬B ∨ ¬A ∧ B"

 1080  The symbol ≡ is a special form of equality that must be used in constant

 1081  definitions. Pattern-matching is not allowed: each definition must be of the
 1082  form f x1 . . . xn ≡ t. Section 3.1.6 explains how definitions are used in proofs.
 1083  The default name of each definition is f_def, where f is the name of the

 1084  defined constant.

 1085  !!A common mistake when writing definitions is to introduce extra free variables
 1086  on the right-hand side. Consider the following, flawed definition (where dvd
 1087  means "divides"):
 1088  "prime p ≡ 1 < p ∧ (m dvd p −→ m = 1 ∨ m = p)"
 1089  Isabelle rejects this "definition" because of the extra m on the right-hand side, which
 1090  would introduce an inconsistency (why?). The correct version is
 1091  "prime p ≡ 1 < p ∧ (∀ m. m dvd p −→ m = 1 ∨ m = p)"

 1092  2.8 The Definitional Approach

 1093  As we pointed out at the beginning of the chapter, asserting arbitrary ax-

 1094  ioms such as f (n) = f(n) + 1 can easily lead to contradictions. In order to

 1095  avoid this danger, we advocate the definitional rather than the axiomatic ap-

 1096  proach: introduce new concepts by definitions. However, Isabelle/HOL seems

 1097  to support many richer definitional constructs, such as primrec. The point

 1098  is that Isabelle reduces such constructs to first principles. For example, each

 1099  primrec function definition is turned into a proper (nonrecursive!) definition

 1100  from which the user-supplied recursion equations are automatically proved.

 1101  This process is hidden from the user, who does not have to understand the

 1102  details. Other commands described later, like fun and inductive, work sim-

 1103  ilarly. This strict adherence to the definitional approach reduces the risk of

 1104  soundness errors.
=== PAGE 37 ===

 1105  3. More Functional Programming

 1106  The purpose of this chapter is to deepen your understanding of the con-

 1107  cepts encountered so far and to introduce advanced forms of datatypes and

 1108  recursive functions. The first two sections give a structured presentation of

 1109  theorem proving by simplification (Sect. 3.1) and discuss important heuris-

 1110  tics for induction (Sect. 3.2). You can skip them if you are not planning to

 1111  perform proofs yourself. We then present a case study: a compiler for ex-

 1112  pressions (Sect. 3.3). Advanced datatypes, including those involving function

 1113  spaces, are covered in Sect. 3.4; it closes with another case study, search trees

 1114  ("tries"). Finally we introduce fun, a general form of recursive function def-

 1115  inition that goes well beyond primrec (Sect. 3.5).

 1116  3.1 Simplification

 1117  So far we have proved our theorems by auto, which simplifies all subgoals.
 1118  In fact, auto can do much more than that. To go beyond toy examples, you
 1119  need to understand the ingredients of auto. This section covers the method
 1120  that auto always applies first, simplification.

 1121  Simplification is one of the central theorem proving tools in Isabelle and

 1122  many other systems. The tool itself is called the simplifier. This section

 1123  introduces the many features of the simplifier and is required reading if you

 1124  intend to perform proofs. Later on, Sect. 9.1 explains some more advanced

 1125  features and a little bit of how the simplifier works. The serious student should

 1126  read that section as well, in particular to understand why the simplifier did

 1127  something unexpected.

 1128  3.1.1 What is Simplification?

 1129  In its most basic form, simplification means repeated application of equations
 1130  from left to right. For example, taking the rules for @ and applying them to
 1131  the term [0,1] @ [] results in a sequence of simplification steps:
 1132  (0#1#[]) @ [] ; 0#((1#[]) @ []) ; 0#(1#([] @ [])) ; 0#1#[]

 1133  This is also known as term rewriting and the equations are referred to as

 1134  rewrite rules. "Rewriting" is more honest than "simplification" because the

 1135  terms do not necessarily become simpler in the process.
=== PAGE 38 ===

 1136  28 3. More Functional Programming

 1137  The simplifier proves arithmetic goals as described in Sect. 2.6.1 above.

 1138  Arithmetic expressions are simplified using built-in procedures that go be-

 1139  yond mere rewrite rules. New simplification procedures can be coded and

 1140  installed, but they are definitely not a matter for this tutorial.

 1141  3.1.2 Simplification Rules

 1142  To facilitate simplification, the attribute [simp] declares theorems to be sim-

 1143  plification rules, which the simplifier will use automatically. In addition,

 1144  datatype and primrec declarations (and a few others) implicitly declare

 1145  some simplification rules. Explicit definitions are not declared as simplifica-

 1146  tion rules automatically!

 1147  Nearly any theorem can become a simplification rule. The simplifier will
 1148  try to transform it into an equation. For example, the theorem ¬ P is turned
 1149  into P = False. The details are explained in Sect. 9.1.2.

 1150  The simplification attribute of theorems can be turned on and off:
 1151  declare theorem-name[simp]
 1152  declare theorem-name[simp del]
 1153  Only equations that really simplify, like rev (rev xs) = xs and xs @ [] = xs,

 1154  should be declared as default simplification rules. More specific ones should

 1155  only be used selectively and should not be made default. Distributivity laws,

 1156  for example, alter the structure of terms and can produce an exponential

 1157  blow-up instead of simplification. A default simplification rule may need to

 1158  be disabled in certain proofs. Frequent changes in the simplification status of

 1159  a theorem may indicate an unwise use of defaults.
 1160  !!Simplification can run forever, for example if both f (x ) = g(x) and g(x ) = f (x )
 1161  are simplification rules. It is the user's responsibility not to include simplification
 1162  rules that can lead to nontermination, either on their own or in combination with
 1163  other simplification rules.

 1164  !!It is inadvisable to toggle the simplification attribute of a theorem from a parent
 1165  theory A in a child theory B for good. The reason is that if some theory C
 1166  is based both on B and (via a different path) on A, it is not defined what the
 1167  simplification attribute of that theorem will be in C : it could be either.

 1168  3.1.3 The simp Method

 1169  The general format of the simplification method is
 1170  simp list of modifiers

 1171  where the list of modifiers fine tunes the behaviour and may be empty. Specific

 1172  modifiers are discussed below. Most if not all of the proofs seen so far could
=== PAGE 39 ===

 1173  3.1 Simplification 29
 1174  have been performed with simp instead of auto, except that simp attacks
 1175  only the first subgoal and may thus need to be repeated — use simp_all to
 1176  simplify all subgoals. If nothing changes, simp fails.

 1177  3.1.4 Adding and Deleting Simplification Rules

 1178  If a certain theorem is merely needed in a few proofs by simplification, we do

 1179  not need to make it a global simplification rule. Instead we can modify the

 1180  set of simplification rules used in a simplification step by adding rules to it

 1181  and/or deleting rules from it. The two modifiers for this are
 1182  add: list of theorem names
 1183  del: list of theorem names

 1184  Or you can use a specific list of theorems and omit all others:
 1185  only: list of theorem names

 1186  In this example, we invoke the simplifier, adding two distributive laws:
 1187  apply(simp add: mod_mult_distrib add_mult_distrib)

 1188  3.1.5 Assumptions

 1189  By default, assumptions are part of the simplification process: they are used

 1190  as simplification rules and are simplified themselves. For example:
 1191  lemma "[[ xs @ zs = ys @ xs; [] @ xs = [] @ [] ]] =⇒ ys = zs"
 1192  apply simp
 1193  done
 1194  The second assumption simplifies to xs = [], which in turn simplifies the first
 1195  assumption to zs = ys, thus reducing the conclusion to ys = ys and hence
 1196  to True.

 1197  In some cases, using the assumptions can lead to nontermination:
 1198  lemma "∀ x. f x = g (f (g x)) =⇒ f [] = f [] @ []"
 1199  An unmodified application of simp loops. The culprit is the simplification rule
 1200  f x = g (f (g x)), which is extracted from the assumption. (Isabelle notices

 1201  certain simple forms of nontermination but not this one.) The problem can

 1202  be circumvented by telling the simplifier to ignore the assumptions:
 1203  apply(simp (no_asm))
 1204  done

 1205  Three modifiers influence the treatment of assumptions:
 1206  (no_asm) means that assumptions are completely ignored.
 1207  (no_asm_simp) means that the assumptions are not simplified but are used

 1208  in the simplification of the conclusion.
=== PAGE 40 ===

 1209  30 3. More Functional Programming
 1210  (no_asm_use) means that the assumptions are simplified but are not used in

 1211  the simplification of each other or the conclusion.

 1212  Only one of the modifiers is allowed, and it must precede all other modifiers.

 1213  3.1.6 Rewriting with Definitions

 1214  Constant definitions (Sect. 2.7.2) can be used as simplification rules, but by

 1215  default they are not: the simplifier does not expand them automatically.

 1216  Definitions are intended for introducing abstract concepts and not merely as

 1217  abbreviations. Of course, we need to expand the definition initially, but once

 1218  we have proved enough abstract properties of the new constant, we can forget

 1219  its original definition. This style makes proofs more robust: if the definition

 1220  has to be changed, only the proofs of the abstract properties will be affected.

 1221  For example, given
 1222  definition xor :: "bool ⇒ bool ⇒ bool" where
 1223  "xor A B ≡ (A ∧ ¬B) ∨ (¬A ∧ B)"

 1224  we may want to prove
 1225  lemma "xor A (¬A)"

 1226  Typically, we begin by unfolding some definitions:
 1227  apply(simp only: xor_def)

 1228  In this particular case, the resulting goal
 1229  1. A ∧ ¬ ¬ A ∨ ¬ A ∧ ¬ A

 1230  can be proved by simplification. Thus we could have proved the lemma out-

 1231  right by
 1232  apply(simp add: xor_def)

 1233  Of course we can also unfold definitions in the middle of a proof.
 1234  !!If you have defined f x y ≡ t then you can only unfold occurrences of f with at
 1235  least two arguments. This may be helpful for unfolding f selectively, but it may
 1236  also get in the way. Defining f ≡ λx y. t allows to unfold all occurrences of f .
 1237  There is also the special method unfold which merely unfolds one or
 1238  several definitions, as in apply(unfold xor_def). This is can be useful in
 1239  situations where simp does too much. Warning: unfold acts on all subgoals!

 1240  3.1.7 Simplifying let-Expressions
 1241  Proving a goal containing let-expressions almost invariably requires the letconstructs to be expanded at some point. Since let . . . =. . . in . . . is just syntactic sugar for the predefined constant Let, expanding let -constructs means
 1242  rewriting with Let_def :
=== PAGE 41 ===

 1243  3.1 Simplification 31
 1244  lemma "(let xs = [] in xs@ys@xs) = ys"
 1245  apply(simp add: Let_def)
 1246  done

 1247  If, in a particular context, there is no danger of a combinatorial explosion
 1248  of nested let s, you could even simplify with Let_def by default:
 1249  declare Let_def [simp]

 1250  3.1.8 Conditional Simplification Rules

 1251  So far all examples of rewrite rules were equations. The simplifier also accepts

 1252  conditional equations, for example
 1253  lemma hd_Cons_tl[simp]: "xs 6= [] =⇒ hd xs # tl xs = xs"
 1254  apply(case_tac xs, simp, simp)
 1255  done
 1256  Note the use of ", " to string together a sequence of methods. Assuming
 1257  that the simplification rule (rev xs = []) = (xs = []) is present as well, the

 1258  lemma below is proved by plain simplification:
 1259  lemma "xs 6= [] =⇒ hd(rev xs) # tl(rev xs) = rev xs"
 1260  The conditional equation hd_Cons_tl above can simplify hd (rev xs) # tl
 1261  (rev xs) to rev xs because the corresponding precondition rev xs 6= [] simplifies to xs 6= [], which is exactly the local assumption of the subgoal.

 1262  3.1.9 Automatic Case Splits
 1263  Goals containing if-expressions are usually proved by case distinction on the

 1264  boolean condition. Here is an example:
 1265  lemma "∀ xs. if xs = [] then rev xs = [] else rev xs 6= []"
 1266  The goal can be split by a special method, split :
 1267  apply(split if_split)
 1268  1. ∀ xs. (xs = [] −→ rev xs = []) ∧ (xs 6= [] −→ rev xs 6= [])
 1269  where if_split is a theorem that expresses splitting of if s. Because splitting
 1270  the if s is usually the right proof strategy, the simplifier does it automatically.
 1271  Try apply(simp) on the initial goal above.
 1272  This splitting idea generalizes from if to case . Let us simplify a case

 1273  analysis over lists:
 1274  lemma "(case xs of [] ⇒ zs | y#ys ⇒ y#(ys@zs)) = xs@zs"
 1275  apply(split list.split)
 1276  1. (xs = [] −→ zs = xs @ zs) ∧
 1277  (∀ x21 x22. xs = x21 # x22 −→ x21 # x22 @ zs = xs @ zs)
=== PAGE 42 ===

 1278  32 3. More Functional Programming

 1279  The simplifier does not split case-expressions, as it does if -expressions, be-

 1280  cause with recursive datatypes it could lead to nontermination. Instead, the
 1281  simplifier has a modifier split for adding splitting rules explicitly. The lemma

 1282  above can be proved in one step by
 1283  apply(simp split: list.split)
 1284  whereas apply(simp) alone will not succeed.
 1285  Every datatype t comes with a theorem t.split which can be declared
 1286  to be a split rule either locally as above, or by giving it the split attribute

 1287  globally:
 1288  declare list.split [split]
 1289  The split attribute can be removed with the del modifier, either locally
 1290  apply(simp split del: if_split)

 1291  or globally:
 1292  declare list.split [split del]
 1293  Polished proofs typically perform splitting within simp rather than invoking the split method. However, if a goal contains several if and case
 1294  expressions, the split method can be helpful in selectively exploring the

 1295  effects of splitting.

 1296  The split rules shown above are intended to affect only the subgoal's
 1297  conclusion. If you want to split an if or case -expression in the assumptions,
 1298  you have to apply if_split_asm or t.split_asm :
 1299  lemma "if xs = [] then ys 6= [] else ys = [] =⇒ xs @ ys 6= []"
 1300  apply(split if_split_asm)

 1301  Unlike splitting the conclusion, this step creates two separate subgoals, which
 1302  here can be solved by simp_all :
 1303  1. [[xs = []; ys 6= []]] =⇒ xs @ ys 6= []
 1304  2. [[xs 6= []; ys = []]] =⇒ xs @ ys 6= []
 1305  If you need to split both in the assumptions and the conclusion, use t.splits
 1306  which subsumes t.split and t.split_asm. Analogously, there is if_splits.
 1307  !!The simplifier merely simplifies the condition of an if but not the then or
 1308  else parts. The latter are simplified only after the condition reduces to True
 1309  or False, or after splitting. The same is true for case -expressions: only the selector
 1310  is simplified at first, until either the expression reduces to one of the cases or it is
 1311  split.

 1312  3.1.10 Tracing

 1313  Using the simplifier effectively may take a bit of experimentation. Set the

 1314  Proof General flag Isabelle > Settings > Trace Simplifier to get a better idea

 1315  of what is going on:
=== PAGE 43 ===

 1316  3.1 Simplification 33

 1317  lemma "rev [a] = []"
 1318  apply(simp)

 1319  produces the following trace in Proof General's Trace buffer:
 1320  [1]Applying instance of rewrite rule "List.rev.simps_2":
 1321  rev (?x1 # ?xs1) ≡ rev ?xs1 @ [?x1]
 1322  [1]Rewriting:
 1323  rev [a] ≡ rev [] @ [a]
 1324  [1]Applying instance of rewrite rule "List.rev.simps_1":
 1325  rev [] ≡ []
 1326  [1]Rewriting:
 1327  rev [] ≡ []
 1328  [1]Applying instance of rewrite rule "List.append.append_Nil":
 1329  [] @ ?y ≡ ?y
 1330  [1]Rewriting:
 1331  [] @ [a] ≡ [a]
 1332  [1]Applying instance of rewrite rule
 1333  ?x2 # ?t1 = ?t1 ≡ False
 1334  [1]Rewriting:
 1335  [a] = [] ≡ False

 1336  The trace lists each rule being applied, both in its general form and the

 1337  instance being used. The [i] in front (where above i is always 1) indicates

 1338  that we are inside the ith invocation of the simplifier. Each attempt to apply

 1339  a conditional rule shows the rule followed by the trace of the (recursive!)

 1340  simplification of the conditions, the latter prefixed by [i + 1] instead of

 1341  [i]. Another source of recursive invocations of the simplifier are proofs of

 1342  arithmetic formulae. By default, recursive invocations are not shown, you

 1343  must increase the trace depth via Isabelle > Settings > Trace Simplifier Depth.

 1344  Many other hints about the simplifier's actions may appear.

 1345  In more complicated cases, the trace can be very lengthy. Thus it is ad-

 1346  visable to reset the Trace Simplifier flag after having obtained the desired

 1347  trace. Since this is easily forgotten (and may have the unpleasant effect of

 1348  swamping the interface with trace information), here is how you can switch

 1349  the trace on locally in a proof:
 1350  using [[simp_trace=true]]
 1351  apply simp

 1352  Within the current proof, all simplifications in subsequent proof steps will be
 1353  traced, but the text reminds you to remove the using clause after it has done

 1354  its job.
=== PAGE 44 ===

 1355  34 3. More Functional Programming

 1356  3.1.11 Finding Theorems

 1357  Isabelle's large database of proved theorems offers a powerful search engine.

 1358  Its chief limitation is its restriction to the theories currently loaded.

 1359  The search engine is started by clicking on Proof General's Find icon. You specify
 1360  your search textually in the input buffer at the bottom of the window.

 1361  The simplest form of search finds theorems containing specified patterns.

 1362  A pattern can be any term (even a single identifier). It may contain " ", a

 1363  wildcard standing for any term. Here are some examples:
 1364  length
 1365  "_ # _ = _ # _"
 1366  "_ + _"
 1367  "_ * (_ - (_::nat))"

 1368  Specifying types, as shown in the last example, constrains searches involving

 1369  overloaded operators.
 1370  !!Always use " " rather than variable names: searching for "x + y" will usually
 1371  not find any matching theorems because they would need to contain x and y
 1372  literally. When searching for infix operators, do not just type in the symbol, such
 1373  as +, but a proper term such as "_ + _". This remark applies to more complicated
 1374  syntaxes, too.

 1375  If you are looking for rewrite rules (possibly conditional) that could sim-

 1376  plify some term, prefix the pattern with simp:.
 1377  simp: "_ * (_ + _)"
 1378  This finds all equations—not just those with a simp attribute—whose con-

 1379  clusion has the form
 1380  _ * (_ + _) = . . .

 1381  It only finds equations that can simplify the given pattern at the root, not
 1382  somewhere inside: for example, equations of the form _ + _ = . . . do not

 1383  match.

 1384  You may also search for theorems by name—you merely need to specify

 1385  a substring. For example, you could search for all commutativity theorems

 1386  like this:
 1387  name: comm

 1388  This retrieves all theorems whose name contains comm.

 1389  Search criteria can also be negated by prefixing them with "-". For exam-

 1390  ple,
 1391  -name: List

 1392  finds theorems whose name does not contain List. You can use this to exclude

 1393  particular theories from the search: the long name of a theorem contains the

 1394  name of the theory it comes from.
=== PAGE 45 ===

 1395  3.2 Induction Heuristics 35

 1396  Finallly, different search criteria can be combined arbitrarily. The effect

 1397  is conjuctive: Find returns the theorems that satisfy all of the criteria. For

 1398  example,
 1399  "_ + _" -"_ - _" -simp: "_ * (_ + _)" name: assoc

 1400  looks for theorems containing plus but not minus, and which do not simplify
 1401  _ * (_ + _) at the root, and whose name contains assoc.

 1402  Further search criteria are explained in Sect. 5.14.

 1403  Proof General keeps a history of all your search expressions. If you click on Find,
 1404  you can use the arrow keys to scroll through previous searches and just modify
 1405  them. This saves you having to type in lengthy expressions again and again.

 1406  3.2 Induction Heuristics

 1407  The purpose of this section is to illustrate some simple heuristics for inductive

 1408  proofs. The first one we have already mentioned in our initial example:

 1409  Theorems about recursive functions are proved by induction.

 1410  In case the function has more than one argument

 1411  Do induction on argument number i if the function is defined by

 1412  recursion in argument number i.
 1413  When we look at the proof of (xs@ys) @ zs = xs @ (ys@zs) in Sect. 2.3 we

 1414  find
 1415  – @ is recursive in the first argument
 1416  – xs occurs only as the first argument of @
 1417  – both ys and zs occur at least once as the second argument of @
 1418  Hence it is natural to perform induction on xs.

 1419  The key heuristic, and the main point of this section, is to generalize the

 1420  goal before induction. The reason is simple: if the goal is too specific, the

 1421  induction hypothesis is too weak to allow the induction step to go through.

 1422  Let us illustrate the idea with an example.
 1423  Function rev has quadratic worst-case running time because it calls function @ for each element of the list and @ is linear in its first argument. A linear
 1424  time version of rev reqires an extra argument where the result is accumulated
 1425  gradually, using only #:
 1426  primrec itrev :: "'a list ⇒ 'a list ⇒ 'a list" where
 1427  "itrev [] ys = ys" |
 1428  "itrev (x#xs) ys = itrev xs (x#ys)"
 1429  The behaviour of itrev is simple: it reverses its first argument by stacking

 1430  its elements onto the second argument, and returning that second argument
=== PAGE 46 ===

 1431  36 3. More Functional Programming

 1432  when the first one becomes empty. Note that itrev is tail-recursive: it can

 1433  be compiled into a loop.
 1434  Naturally, we would like to show that itrev does indeed reverse its first

 1435  argument provided the second one is empty:
 1436  lemma "itrev xs [] = rev xs"

 1437  There is no choice as to the induction variable, and we immediately simplify:
 1438  apply(induct_tac xs, simp_all)

 1439  Unfortunately, this attempt does not prove the induction step:
 1440  1. Va list.
 1441  itrev list [] = rev list =⇒ itrev list [a] = rev list @ [a]
 1442  The induction hypothesis is too weak. The fixed argument, [], prevents it

 1443  from rewriting the conclusion. This example suggests a heuristic:

 1444  Generalize goals for induction by replacing constants by variables.
 1445  Of course one cannot do this na¨ıvely: itrev xs ys = rev xs is just not true.

 1446  The correct generalization is
 1447  lemma "itrev xs ys = rev xs @ ys"
 1448  If ys is replaced by [], the right-hand side simplifies to rev xs, as required.

 1449  In this instance it was easy to guess the right generalization. Other situ-

 1450  ations can require a good deal of creativity.
 1451  Although we now have two variables, only xs is suitable for induction,

 1452  and we repeat our proof attempt. Unfortunately, we are still not there:
 1453  1. Va list.
 1454  itrev list ys = rev list @ ys =⇒
 1455  itrev list (a # ys) = rev list @ a # ys

 1456  The induction hypothesis is still too weak, but this time it takes no intuition
 1457  to generalize: the problem is that ys is fixed throughout the subgoal, but the
 1458  induction hypothesis needs to be applied with a # ys instead of ys. Hence
 1459  we prove the theorem for all ys instead of a fixed one:
 1460  lemma "∀ ys. itrev xs ys = rev xs @ ys"
 1461  This time induction on xs followed by simplification succeeds. This leads to

 1462  another heuristic for generalization:

 1463  Generalize goals for induction by universally quantifying all free vari-

 1464  ables (except the induction variable itself!).

 1465  This prevents trivial failures like the one above and does not affect the validity

 1466  of the goal. However, this heuristic should not be applied blindly. It is not

 1467  always required, and the additional quantifiers can complicate matters in

 1468  some cases. The variables that should be quantified are typically those that

 1469  change in recursive calls.
=== PAGE 47 ===

 1470  3.3 Case Study: Compiling Expressions 37

 1471  A final point worth mentioning is the orientation of the equation we just
 1472  proved: the more complex notion (itrev ) is on the left-hand side, the simpler one (rev) on the right-hand side. This constitutes another, albeit weak
