 1473  heuristic that is not restricted to induction:

 1474  The right-hand side of an equation should (in some sense) be simpler

 1475  than the left-hand side.
 1476  This heuristic is tricky to apply because it is not obvious that rev xs @ ys is
 1477  simpler than itrev xs ys. But see what happens if you try to prove rev xs
 1478  @ ys = itrev xs ys!

 1479  If you have tried these heuristics and still find your induction does not go

 1480  through, and no obvious lemma suggests itself, you may need to generalize

 1481  your proposition even further. This requires insight into the problem at hand

 1482  and is beyond simple rules of thumb. Additionally, you can read Sect. 9.2 to

 1483  learn about some advanced techniques for inductive proofs.

 1484  Exercise 3.2.1 Define the following addition function
 1485  primrec add :: "nat ⇒ nat ⇒ nat" where
 1486  "add m 0 = m" |
 1487  "add m (Suc n) = add (Suc m) n"

 1488  and prove
 1489  lemma "add m n = m+n"
 1490  Exercise 3.2.2 In Exercise 2.5.1 we defined a function flatten from trees
 1491  to lists. The straightforward version of flatten is based on @ and is thus,
 1492  like rev, quadratic. A linear time version of flatten again reqires an extra

 1493  argument, the accumulator. Define
 1494  flatten2 :: "'a tree ⇒ 'a list ⇒ 'a list"

 1495  and prove
 1496  lemma "flatten2 t [] = flatten t"

 1497  3.3 Case Study: Compiling Expressions

 1498  The task is to develop a compiler from a generic type of expressions (built

 1499  from variables, constants and binary operations) to a stack machine. This

 1500  generic type of expressions is a generalization of the boolean expressions

 1501  in Sect. 2.5.6. This time we do not commit ourselves to a particular type of

 1502  variables or values but make them type parameters. Neither is there a fixed set

 1503  of binary operations: instead the expression contains the appropriate function

 1504  itself.
 1505  type synonym 'v binop = "'v ⇒ 'v ⇒ 'v"
 1506  datatype (dead 'a, 'v) expr = Cex 'v
=== PAGE 48 ===

 1507  38 3. More Functional Programming

 1508  | Vex 'a
 1509  | Bex "'v binop" "('a,'v)expr" "('a,'v)expr"

 1510  The three constructors represent constants, variables and the application of

 1511  a binary operation to two subexpressions.

 1512  The value of an expression with respect to an environment that maps

 1513  variables to values is easily defined:
 1514  primrec "value" :: "('a,'v)expr ⇒ ('a ⇒ 'v) ⇒ 'v" where
 1515  "value (Cex v) env = v" |
 1516  "value (Vex a) env = env a" |
 1517  "value (Bex f e1 e2) env = f (value e1 env) (value e2 env)"

 1518  The stack machine has three instructions: load a constant value onto the

 1519  stack, load the contents of an address onto the stack, and apply a binary

 1520  operation to the two topmost elements of the stack, replacing them by the
 1521  result. As for expr, addresses and values are type parameters:
 1522  datatype (dead 'a, 'v) instr = Const 'v
 1523  | Load 'a
 1524  | Apply "'v binop"
 1525  The execution of the stack machine is modelled by a function exec that

 1526  takes a list of instructions, a store (modelled as a function from addresses

 1527  to values, just like the environment for evaluating expressions), and a stack

 1528  (modelled as a list) of values, and returns the stack at the end of the execution

 1529  — the store remains unchanged:
 1530  primrec exec :: "('a,'v)instr list ⇒ ('a ⇒'v) ⇒ 'v list ⇒ 'v list"
 1531  where
 1532  "exec [] s vs = vs" |
 1533  "exec (i#is) s vs = (case i of
 1534  Const v ⇒ exec is s (v#vs)
 1535  | Load a ⇒ exec is s ((s a)#vs)
 1536  | Apply f ⇒ exec is s ((f (hd vs) (hd(tl vs)))#(tl(tl vs))))"
 1537  Recall that hd and tl return the first element and the remainder of a list.
 1538  Because all functions are total, hd is defined even for the empty list, although

 1539  we do not know what the result is. Thus our model of the machine always

 1540  terminates properly, although the definition above does not tell us much
 1541  about the result in situations where Apply was executed with fewer than two

 1542  elements on the stack.

 1543  The compiler is a function from expressions to a list of instructions. Its

 1544  definition is obvious:
 1545  primrec compile :: "('a,'v)expr ⇒ ('a,'v)instr list" where
 1546  "compile (Cex v) = [Const v]" |
 1547  "compile (Vex a) = [Load a]" |
 1548  "compile (Bex f e1 e2) = (compile e2) @ (compile e1) @ [Apply f]"

 1549  Now we have to prove the correctness of the compiler, i.e. that the exe-

 1550  cution of a compiled expression results in the value of the expression:
 1551  theorem "exec (compile e) s [] = [value e s]"
=== PAGE 49 ===

 1552  3.4 Advanced Datatypes 39

 1553  This theorem needs to be generalized:
 1554  theorem " ∀ vs. exec (compile e) s vs = (value e s) # vs"
 1555  It will be proved by induction on e followed by simplification. First, we

 1556  must prove a lemma about executing the concatenation of two instruction

 1557  sequences:
 1558  lemma exec_app[simp]:
 1559  " ∀vs. exec (xs@ys) s vs = exec ys s (exec xs s vs)"
 1560  This requires induction on xs and ordinary simplification for the base cases.

 1561  In the induction step, simplification leaves us with a formula that contains
 1562  two case -expressions over instructions. Thus we add automatic case splitting,

 1563  which finishes the proof:
 1564  apply(induct_tac xs, simp, simp split: instr.split)
 1565  Note that because both simp_all and auto perform simplification, they can
 1566  be modified in the same way as simp. Thus the proof can be rewritten as
 1567  apply(induct_tac xs, simp_all split: instr.split)

 1568  Although this is more compact, it is less clear for the reader of the proof.
 1569  We could now go back and prove exec (compile e) s [] = [value e s]

 1570  merely by simplification with the generalized version we just proved. How-

 1571  ever, this is unnecessary because the generalized version fully subsumes its

 1572  instance.

 1573  3.4 Advanced Datatypes

 1574  This section presents advanced forms of datatypes: mutual and nested re-

 1575  cursion. A series of examples will culminate in a treatment of the trie data

 1576  structure.

 1577  3.4.1 Mutual Recursion

 1578  Sometimes it is necessary to define two datatypes that depend on each other.

 1579  This is called mutual recursion. As an example consider a language of

 1580  arithmetic and boolean expressions where

 1581  – arithmetic expressions contain boolean expressions because there are con-

 1582  ditional expressions like "if m < n then n − m else m − n", and

 1583  – boolean expressions contain arithmetic expressions because of comparisons

 1584  like "m < n".

 1585  In Isabelle this becomes
 1586  datatype 'a aexp = IF "'a bexp" "'a aexp" "'a aexp"
 1587  | Sum "'a aexp" "'a aexp"
=== PAGE 50 ===

 1588  40 3. More Functional Programming

 1589  | Diff "'a aexp" "'a aexp"
 1590  | Var 'a
 1591  | Num nat
 1592  and 'a bexp = Less "'a aexp" "'a aexp"
 1593  | And "'a bexp" "'a bexp"
 1594  | Neg "'a bexp"
 1595  Type aexp is similar to expr in Sect. 3.3, except that we have added an IF
 1596  constructor, fixed the values to be of type nat and declared the two binary
 1597  operations Sum and Diff. Boolean expressions can be arithmetic comparisons,

 1598  conjunctions and negations. The semantics is given by two evaluation func-

 1599  tions:
 1600  primrec evala :: "'a aexp ⇒ ('a ⇒ nat) ⇒ nat" and
 1601  evalb :: "'a bexp ⇒ ('a ⇒ nat) ⇒ bool" where
 1602  "evala (IF b a1 a2) env =
 1603  (if evalb b env then evala a1 env else evala a2 env)" |
 1604  "evala (Sum a1 a2) env = evala a1 env + evala a2 env" |
 1605  "evala (Diff a1 a2) env = evala a1 env - evala a2 env" |
 1606  "evala (Var v) env = env v" |
 1607  "evala (Num n) env = n" |

 1608  "evalb (Less a1 a2) env = (evala a1 env < evala a2 env)" |
 1609  "evalb (And b1 b2) env = (evalb b1 env ∧ evalb b2 env)" |
 1610  "evalb (Neg b) env = (¬ evalb b env)"
 1611  Both take an expression and an environment (a mapping from variables 'a
 1612  to values nat ) and return its arithmetic/boolean value. Since the datatypes

 1613  are mutually recursive, so are functions that operate on them. Hence they

 1614  need to be defined in a single primrec section. Notice the and separating
 1615  the declarations of evala and evalb. Their defining equations need not be

 1616  split into two groups; the empty line is purely for readability.

 1617  In the same fashion we also define two functions that perform substitution:
 1618  primrec substa :: "('a ⇒ 'b aexp) ⇒ 'a aexp ⇒ 'b aexp" and
 1619  substb :: "('a ⇒ 'b aexp) ⇒ 'a bexp ⇒ 'b bexp" where
 1620  "substa s (IF b a1 a2) =
 1621  IF (substb s b) (substa s a1) (substa s a2)" |
 1622  "substa s (Sum a1 a2) = Sum (substa s a1) (substa s a2)" |
 1623  "substa s (Diff a1 a2) = Diff (substa s a1) (substa s a2)" |
 1624  "substa s (Var v) = s v" |
 1625  "substa s (Num n) = Num n" |

 1626  "substb s (Less a1 a2) = Less (substa s a1) (substa s a2)" |
 1627  "substb s (And b1 b2) = And (substb s b1) (substb s b2)" |
 1628  "substb s (Neg b) = Neg (substb s b)"

 1629  Their first argument is a function mapping variables to expressions, the sub-

 1630  stitution. It is applied to all variables in the second argument. As a result,
 1631  the type of variables in the expression may change from 'a to 'b. Note that

 1632  there are only arithmetic and no boolean variables.

 1633  Now we can prove a fundamental theorem about the interaction between

 1634  evaluation and substitution: applying a substitution s to an expression a
=== PAGE 51 ===

 1635  3.4 Advanced Datatypes 41

 1636  and evaluating the result in an environment env yields the same result as

 1637  evaluation a in the environment that maps every variable x to the value of

 1638  s(x) under env. If you try to prove this separately for arithmetic or boolean

 1639  expressions (by induction), you find that you always need the other theorem

 1640  in the induction step. Therefore you need to state and prove both theorems

 1641  simultaneously:
 1642  lemma "evala (substa s a) env = evala a (λx. evala (s x) env) ∧
 1643  evalb (substb s b) env = evalb b (λx. evala (s x) env)"
 1644  apply(induct_tac a and b)

 1645  The resulting 8 goals (one for each constructor) are proved in one fell swoop:
 1646  apply simp_all
 1647  In general, given n mutually recursive datatypes τ1, . . . , τn, an inductive

 1648  proof expects a goal of the form
 1649  P1(x1) ∧ · · · ∧ Pn(xn)
 1650  where each variable xi is of type τi. Induction is started by
 1651  apply(induct_tac x1 and ... and xn)
 1652  Exercise 3.4.1 Define a function norma of type 'a aexp ⇒ 'a aexp that replaces IFs with complex boolean conditions by nested IF s; it should eliminate
 1653  the constructors And and Neg, leaving only Less. Prove that norma preserves
 1654  the value of an expression and that the result of norma is really normal, i.e.
 1655  no more And s and Negs occur in it. (Hint: proceed as in Sect. 2.5.6 and read
 1656  the discussion of type annotations following lemma subst_id below).

 1657  3.4.2 Nested Recursion

 1658  So far, all datatypes had the property that on the right-hand side of their

 1659  definition they occurred only at the top-level: directly below a constructor.

 1660  Now we consider nested recursion, where the recursive datatype occurs nested

 1661  in some other datatype (but not inside itself!). Consider the following model

 1662  of terms where function symbols can be applied to a list of arguments:
 1663  datatype ('v,'f)"term" = Var 'v | App 'f "('v,'f)term list"
 1664  Note that we need to quote term on the left to avoid confusion with the
 1665  Isabelle command term. Parameter 'v is the type of variables and 'f the
 1666  type of function symbols. A mathematical term like f(x, g(y)) becomes App
 1667  f [Var x, App g [Var y]], where f, g, x, y are suitable values, e.g. numbers

 1668  or strings.
 1669  What complicates the definition of term is the nested occurrence of term
 1670  inside list on the right-hand side. In principle, nested recursion can be elim-

 1671  inated in favour of mutual recursion by unfolding the offending datatypes,
 1672  here list. The result for term would be something like
=== PAGE 52 ===

 1673  42 3. More Functional Programming

 1674  datatype ('v,'f)"term" = Var 'v | App 'f "('v,'f)term_list"
 1675  and ('v,'f)term_list = Nil | Cons "('v,'f)term" "('v,'f)term_list"

 1676  Although we do not recommend this unfolding to the user, it shows how to

 1677  simulate nested recursion by mutual recursion. Now we return to the initial
 1678  definition of term using nested recursion.

 1679  Let us define a substitution function on terms. Because terms involve term

 1680  lists, we need to define two substitution functions simultaneously:
 1681  primrec
 1682  subst :: "('v⇒('v,'f)term) ⇒ ('v,'f)term ⇒ ('v,'f)term" and
 1683  substs:: "('v⇒('v,'f)term) ⇒ ('v,'f)term list ⇒ ('v,'f)term list"
 1684  where
 1685  "subst s (Var x) = s x" |
 1686  subst_App:
 1687  "subst s (App f ts) = App f (substs s ts)" |
 1688  "substs s [] = []" |
 1689  "substs s (t # ts) = subst s t # substs s ts"

 1690  Individual equations in a primrec definition may be named as shown for
 1691  subst_App. The significance of this device will become apparent below.

 1692  Similarly, when proving a statement about terms inductively, we need

 1693  to prove a related statement about term lists simultaneously. For example,

 1694  the fact that the identity substitution does not change a term needs to be

 1695  strengthened and proved as follows:
 1696  lemma subst_id: "subst Var t = (t ::('v,'f)term) ∧
 1697  substs Var ts = (ts::('v,'f)term list)"
 1698  apply(induct_tac t and ts rule: subst.induct substs.induct, simp_all)
 1699  done
 1700  Note that Var is the identity substitution because by definition it leaves variables unchanged: subst Var (Var x) = Var x. Note also that the type anno-

 1701  tations are necessary because otherwise there is nothing in the goal to enforce
 1702  that both halves of the goal talk about the same type parameters ('v,'f).

 1703  As a result, induction would fail because the two halves of the goal would be

 1704  unrelated.

 1705  Exercise 3.4.2 The fact that substitution distributes over composition can

 1706  be expressed roughly as follows:
 1707  subst (f ◦ g) t = subst f (subst g t)

 1708  Correct this statement (you will find that it does not type-check), strengthen
 1709  it, and prove it. (Note: ◦ is function composition; its definition is found in
 1710  theorem o_def ).
 1711  Exercise 3.4.3 Define a function trev of type ('v, 'f) Nested.term ⇒
 1712  ('v, 'f) Nested.term that recursively reverses the order of arguments of all
 1713  function symbols in a term. Prove that trev (trev t) = t.
=== PAGE 53 ===

 1714  3.4 Advanced Datatypes 43

 1715  The experienced functional programmer may feel that our definition of
 1716  subst is too complicated in that substs is unnecessary. The App -case can be

 1717  defined directly as
 1718  subst s (App f ts) = App f (map (subst s) ts)
 1719  where map is the standard list function such that map f [x1,...,xn] = [f
 1720  x1,...,f xn]. This is true, but Isabelle insists on the conjunctive format.

 1721  Fortunately, we can easily prove that the suggested equation holds:

 1722  lemma [simp]: "subst s (App f ts) = App f (map (subst s) ts)"
 1723  apply(induct_tac ts, simp_all)
 1724  done

 1725  What is more, we can now disable the old defining equation as a simplification

 1726  rule:
 1727  declare subst_App [simp del]
 1728  The advantage is that now we have replaced substs by map, we can profit from
 1729  the large number of pre-proved lemmas about map. Unfortunately, inductive
 1730  proofs about type term are still awkward because they expect a conjunc-

 1731  tion. One could derive a new induction principle as well (see Sect. 9.2.3), but

 1732  simpler is to stop using primrec and to define functions with fun instead.

 1733  Simple uses of fun are described in Sect. 3.5 below. Advanced applications,
 1734  including functions over nested datatypes like term, are discussed in a sepa-

 1735  rate tutorial [17].

 1736  Of course, you may also combine mutual and nested recursion of datatypes. For example, constructor Sum in Sect. 3.4.1 could take a list of expressions as its argument: Sum "'a aexp list".

 1737  3.4.3 The Limits of Nested Recursion

 1738  How far can we push nested recursion? By the unfolding argument above,

 1739  we can reduce nested to mutual recursion provided the nested recursion only

 1740  involves previously defined datatypes. This does not include functions:
 1741  datatype t = C "t ⇒ bool"

 1742  This declaration is a real can of worms. In HOL it must be ruled out because
 1743  it requires a type t such that t and its power set t ⇒ bool have the same

 1744  cardinality — an impossibility. For the same reason it is not possible to allow
 1745  recursion involving the type t set, which is isomorphic to t ⇒ bool.

 1746  Fortunately, a limited form of recursion involving function spaces is per-

 1747  mitted: the recursive type may occur on the right of a function arrow, but

 1748  never on the left. Hence the above can of worms is ruled out but the following

 1749  example of a potentially infinitely branching tree is accepted:
 1750  datatype (dead 'a,'i) bigtree = Tip | Br 'a "'i ⇒ ('a,'i)bigtree"
=== PAGE 54 ===

 1751  44 3. More Functional Programming

 1752  Parameter 'a is the type of values stored in the Branches of the tree, whereas
 1753  'i is the index type over which the tree branches. If 'i is instantiated to bool,
 1754  the result is a binary tree; if it is instantiated to nat, we have an infinitely

 1755  branching tree because each node has as many subtrees as there are natural

 1756  numbers. How can we possibly write down such a tree? Using functional

 1757  notation! For example, the term
 1758  Br 0 (λi. Br i (λn. Tip))
 1759  of type (nat, nat) bigtree is the tree whose root is labeled with 0 and whose
 1760  ith subtree is labeled with i and has merely Tip s as further subtrees.
 1761  Function map_bt applies a function to all labels in a bigtree :
 1762  primrec map_bt :: "('a ⇒ 'b) ⇒ ('a,'i)bigtree ⇒ ('b,'i)bigtree"
 1763  where
 1764  "map_bt f Tip = Tip" |
 1765  "map_bt f (Br a F) = Br (f a) (λi. map_bt f (F i))"
 1766  This is a valid primrec definition because the recursive calls of map_bt involve
 1767  only subtrees of F, which is itself a subterm of the left-hand side. Thus termi-

 1768  nation is assured. The seasoned functional programmer might try expressing
 1769  λi. map_bt f (F i) as map_bt f ◦ F, which Isabelle however will reject. Applying map_bt to only one of its arguments makes the termination proof less

 1770  obvious.

 1771  The following lemma has a simple proof by induction:
 1772  lemma "map_bt (g o f) T = map_bt g (map_bt f T)"
 1773  apply(induct_tac T, simp_all)
 1774  done

 1775  Because of the function type, the proof state after induction looks unusual.

 1776  Notice the quantified induction hypothesis:
 1777  1. map_bt (g ◦ f) Tip = map_bt g (map_bt f Tip)
 1778  2. Vx1 F.
 1779  (Vx2a. x2a ∈ range F =⇒
 1780  map_bt (g ◦ f) x2a = map_bt g (map_bt f x2a)) =⇒
 1781  map_bt (g ◦ f) (Br x1 F) = map_bt g (map_bt f (Br x1 F))

 1782  If you need nested recursion on the left of a function arrow, there are

 1783  alternatives to pure HOL. In the Logic for Computable Functions (LCF),

 1784  types like
 1785  datatype lam = C "lam → lam"
 1786  do indeed make sense [27]. Note the different arrow, → instead of ⇒, express-

 1787  ing the type of continuous functions. There is even a version of LCF on top

 1788  of HOL, called HOLCF [20].

 1789  3.4.4 Case Study: Tries

 1790  Tries are a classic search tree data structure [16] for fast indexing with strings.

 1791  Figure 3.1 gives a graphical example of a trie containing the words "all", "an",
=== PAGE 55 ===

 1792  3.4 Advanced Datatypes 45

 1793  "ape", "can", "car" and "cat". When searching a string in a trie, the letters of

 1794  the string are examined sequentially. Each letter determines which subtrie to

 1795  search next. In this case study we model tries as a datatype, define a lookup

 1796  and an update function, and prove that they behave as expected.

 1797  Q

 1798  Q
 1799  a Q c

 1800  Q

 1801  Q

 1802  QQ
 1803  l n p a

 1804  Q

 1805  QQ
 1806  l e n r t

 1807  Fig. 3.1. A Sample Trie

 1808  Proper tries associate some value with each string. Since the information

 1809  is stored only in the final node associated with the string, many nodes do not

 1810  carry any value. This distinction is modeled with the help of the predefined
 1811  datatype option (see Sect. 2.6.3).

 1812  To minimize running time, each node of a trie should contain an array that

 1813  maps letters to subtries. We have chosen a representation where the subtries

 1814  are held in an association list, i.e. a list of (letter,trie) pairs. Abstracting over
 1815  the alphabet 'a and the values 'v we define a trie as follows:
 1816  datatype ('a,'v)trie = Trie "'v option" "('a * ('a,'v)trie)list"

 1817  The first component is the optional value, the second component the associa-

 1818  tion list of subtries. This is an example of nested recursion involving products,

 1819  which is fine because products are datatypes as well. We define two selector

 1820  functions:
 1821  primrec "value" :: "('a,'v)trie ⇒ 'v option" where
 1822  "value(Trie ov al) = ov"
 1823  primrec alist :: "('a,'v)trie ⇒ ('a * ('a,'v)trie)list" where
 1824  "alist(Trie ov al) = al"

 1825  Association lists come with a generic lookup function. Its result involves type
 1826  option because a lookup can fail:
 1827  primrec assoc :: "('key * 'val)list ⇒ 'key ⇒ 'val option" where
 1828  "assoc [] x = None" |
 1829  "assoc (p#ps) x =
 1830  (let (a,b) = p in if a=x then Some b else assoc ps x)"

 1831  Now we can define the lookup function for tries. It descends into the trie

 1832  examining the letters of the search string one by one. As recursion on lists is

 1833  simpler than on tries, let us express this as primitive recursion on the search

 1834  string argument:
=== PAGE 56 ===

 1835  46 3. More Functional Programming

 1836  primrec lookup :: "('a,'v)trie ⇒ 'a list ⇒ 'v option" where
 1837  "lookup t [] = value t" |
 1838  "lookup t (a#as) = (case assoc (alist t) a of
 1839  None ⇒ None
 1840  | Some at ⇒ lookup at as)"

 1841  As a first simple property we prove that looking up a string in the empty
 1842  trie Trie None [] always returns None. The proof merely distinguishes the

 1843  two cases whether the search string is empty or not:
 1844  lemma [simp]: "lookup (Trie None []) as = None"
 1845  apply(case_tac as, simp_all)
 1846  done

 1847  Things begin to get interesting with the definition of an update func-

 1848  tion that adds a new (string, value) pair to a trie, overwriting the old value

 1849  associated with that string:
 1850  primrec update:: "('a,'v)trie ⇒ 'a list ⇒ 'v ⇒ ('a,'v)trie" where
 1851  "update t [] v = Trie (Some v) (alist t)" |
 1852  "update t (a#as) v =
 1853  (let tt = (case assoc (alist t) a of
 1854  None ⇒ Trie None [] | Some at ⇒ at)
 1855  in Trie (value t) ((a,update tt as v) # alist t))"
 1856  The base case is obvious. In the recursive case the subtrie tt associated
 1857  with the first letter a is extracted, recursively updated, and then placed in
 1858  front of the association list. The old subtrie associated with a is still in the
 1859  association list but no longer accessible via assoc. Clearly, there is room here

 1860  for optimizations!
 1861  Before we start on any proofs about update we tell the simplifier to expand
 1862  all lets and to split all case-constructs over options:
 1863  declare Let_def[simp] option.split[split]

 1864  The reason becomes clear when looking (probably after a failed proof attempt) at the body of update : it contains both let and a case distinction
 1865  over type option.
 1866  Our main goal is to prove the correct interaction of update and lookup :
 1867  theorem " ∀ t v bs. lookup (update t as v) bs =
 1868  (if as=bs then Some v else lookup t bs)"
 1869  Our plan is to induct on as ; hence the remaining variables are quantified.
 1870  From the definitions it is clear that induction on either as or bs is required.
 1871  The choice of as is guided by the intuition that simplification of lookup might
 1872  be easier if update has already been simplified, which can only happen if as

 1873  is instantiated. The start of the proof is conventional:
 1874  apply(induct_tac as, auto)

 1875  Unfortunately, this time we are left with three intimidating looking subgoals:
=== PAGE 57 ===

 1876  3.5 Total Recursive Functions: fun 47

 1877  1. ... =⇒ lookup ... bs = lookup t bs
 1878  2. ... =⇒ lookup ... bs = lookup t bs
 1879  3. ... =⇒ lookup ... bs = lookup t bs
 1880  Clearly, if we want to make headway we have to instantiate bs as well now.

 1881  It turns out that instead of induction, case distinction suffices:
 1882  apply(case_tac[!] bs, auto)
 1883  done
 1884  All methods ending in tac take an optional first argument that specifies the
 1885  range of subgoals they are applied to, where [!] means all subgoals, i.e. [1-3]
 1886  in our case. Individual subgoal numbers, e.g. [2] are also allowed.

 1887  This proof may look surprisingly straightforward. However, note that this

 1888  comes at a cost: the proof script is unreadable because the intermediate
 1889  proof states are invisible, and we rely on the (possibly brittle) magic of auto
 1890  (simp_all will not do — try it) to split the subgoals of the induction up in
 1891  such a way that case distinction on bs makes sense and solves the proof.
 1892  Exercise 3.4.4 Modify update (and its type) such that it allows both in-

 1893  sertion and deletion of entries with a single function. Prove the correspond-

 1894  ing version of the main theorem above. Optimize your function such that it

 1895  shrinks tries after deletion if possible.
 1896  Exercise 3.4.5 Write an improved version of update that does not suffer

 1897  from the space leak (pointed out above) caused by not deleting overwritten

 1898  entries from the association list. Prove the main theorem for your improved
 1899  update.

 1900  Exercise 3.4.6 Conceptually, each node contains a mapping from letters to

 1901  optional subtries. Above we have implemented this by means of an association
 1902  list. Replay the development replacing ('a × ('a, 'v) trie) list with 'a
 1903  ⇒ ('a, 'v) trie option.

 1904  3.5 Total Recursive Functions: fun

 1905  Although many total functions have a natural primitive recursive definition,

 1906  this is not always the case. Arbitrary total recursive functions can be defined

 1907  by means of fun: you can use full pattern matching, recursion need not involve

 1908  datatypes, and termination is proved by showing that the arguments of all

 1909  recursive calls are smaller in a suitable sense. In this section we restrict our-

 1910  selves to functions where Isabelle can prove termination automatically. More

 1911  advanced function definitions, including user supplied termination proofs,

 1912  nested recursion and partiality, are discussed in a separate tutorial [17].
=== PAGE 58 ===

 1913  48 3. More Functional Programming

 1914  3.5.1 Definition

 1915  Here is a simple example, the Fibonacci function:
 1916  fun fib :: "nat ⇒ nat" where
 1917  "fib 0 = 0" |
 1918  "fib (Suc 0) = 1" |
 1919  "fib (Suc(Suc x)) = fib x + fib (Suc x)"

 1920  This resembles ordinary functional programming languages. Note the obligatory where and |. Command fun declares and defines the function in one
 1921  go. Isabelle establishes termination automatically because fib 's argument

 1922  decreases in every recursive call.

 1923  Slightly more interesting is the insertion of a fixed element between any

 1924  two elements of a list:
 1925  fun sep :: "'a ⇒ 'a list ⇒ 'a list" where
 1926  "sep a [] = []" |
 1927  "sep a [x] = [x]" |
 1928  "sep a (x#y#zs) = x # a # sep a (y#zs)"

 1929  This time the length of the list decreases with the recursive call; the first

 1930  argument is irrelevant for termination.

 1931  Pattern matching need not be exhaustive and may employ wildcards:
 1932  fun last :: "'a list ⇒ 'a" where
 1933  "last [x] = x" |
 1934  "last (_#y#zs) = last (y#zs)"

 1935  Overlapping patterns are disambiguated by taking the order of equations

 1936  into account, just as in functional programming:
 1937  fun sep1 :: "'a ⇒ 'a list ⇒ 'a list" where
 1938  "sep1 a (x#y#zs) = x # a # sep1 a (y#zs)" |
 1939  "sep1 _ xs = xs"

 1940  To guarantee that the second equation can only be applied if the first one

 1941  does not match, Isabelle internally replaces the second equation by the two
 1942  possibilities that are left: sep1 a [] = [] and sep1 a [x] = [x]. Thus the
 1943  functions sep and sep1 are identical.

 1944  Because of its pattern matching syntax, fun is also useful for the definition

 1945  of non-recursive functions:
 1946  fun swap12 :: "'a list ⇒ 'a list" where
 1947  "swap12 (x#y#zs) = y#x#zs" |
 1948  "swap12 zs = zs"

 1949  After a function f has been defined via fun, its defining equations (or variants derived from them) are available under the name f.simps as theorems.
 1950  For example, look (via thm) at sep.simps and sep1.simps to see that they

 1951  define the same function. What is more, those equations are automatically

 1952  declared as simplification rules.
=== PAGE 59 ===

 1953  3.5 Total Recursive Functions: fun 49

 1954  3.5.2 Termination

 1955  Isabelle's automatic termination prover for fun has a fixed notion of the size
 1956  (of type nat ) of an argument. The size of a natural number is the number

 1957  itself. The size of a list is its length. For the general case see Sect. 2.5.2.

 1958  A recursive function is accepted if fun can show that the size of one fixed

 1959  argument becomes smaller with each recursive call.

 1960  More generally, fun allows any lexicographic combination of size measures

 1961  in case there are multiple arguments. For example, the following version of

 1962  Ackermann's function is accepted:
 1963  fun ack2 :: "nat ⇒ nat ⇒ nat" where
 1964  "ack2 n 0 = Suc n" |
 1965  "ack2 0 (Suc m) = ack2 (Suc 0) m" |
 1966  "ack2 (Suc n) (Suc m) = ack2 (ack2 n (Suc m)) m"

 1967  The order of arguments has no influence on whether fun can prove ter-

 1968  mination of a function. For more details see elsewhere [6].

 1969  3.5.3 Simplification

 1970  Upon a successful termination proof, the recursion equations become simpli-

 1971  fication rules, just as with primrec. In most cases this works fine, but there

 1972  is a subtle problem that must be mentioned: simplification may not terminate
 1973  because of automatic splitting of if. Let us look at an example:
 1974  fun gcd :: "nat ⇒ nat ⇒ nat" where
 1975  "gcd m n = (if n=0 then m else gcd n (m mod n))"

 1976  The second argument decreases with each recursive call. The termination

 1977  condition
 1978  n 6= 0 =⇒ m mod n < n

 1979  is proved automatically because it is already present as a lemma in HOL. Thus

 1980  the recursion equation becomes a simplification rule. Of course the equation

 1981  is nonterminating if we are allowed to unfold the recursive call inside the
 1982  else branch, which is why programming languages and our simplifier don't

 1983  do that. Unfortunately the simplifier does something else that leads to the
 1984  same problem: it splits each if-expression unless its condition simplifies to
 1985  True or False. For example, simplification reduces
 1986  fun0.gcd m n = k

 1987  in one step to
 1988  (if n = 0 then m else fun0.gcd n (m mod n)) = k

 1989  where the condition cannot be reduced further, and splitting leads to
 1990  (n = 0 −→ m = k) ∧ (n 6= 0 −→ fun0.gcd n (m mod n) = k)
=== PAGE 60 ===

 1991  50 3. More Functional Programming

 1992  Since the recursive call fun0.gcd n (m mod n) is no longer protected by an if,

 1993  it is unfolded again, which leads to an infinite chain of simplification steps.

 1994  Fortunately, this problem can be avoided in many different ways.
 1995  The most radical solution is to disable the offending theorem if_split,

 1996  as shown in Sect. 3.1.9. However, we do not recommend this approach: you
 1997  will often have to invoke the rule explicitly when if is involved.

 1998  If possible, the definition should be given by pattern matching on the left
 1999  rather than if on the right. In the case of fun0.gcd the following alternative

 2000  definition suggests itself:
 2001  fun gcd1 :: "nat ⇒ nat ⇒ nat" where
 2002  "gcd1 m 0 = m" |
 2003  "gcd1 m n = gcd1 n (m mod n)"
 2004  The order of equations is important: it hides the side condition n 6= 0. Un-

 2005  fortunately, not all conditionals can be expressed by pattern matching.
 2006  A simple alternative is to replace if by case, which is also available for
 2007  bool and is not split automatically:
 2008  fun gcd2 :: "nat ⇒ nat ⇒ nat" where
 2009  "gcd2 m n = (case n=0 of True ⇒ m | False ⇒ gcd2 n (m mod n))"

 2010  This is probably the neatest solution next to pattern matching, and it is

 2011  always available.

 2012  A final alternative is to replace the offending simplification rules by derived conditional ones. For fun0.gcd it means we have to prove these lemmas:
 2013  lemma [simp]: "gcd m 0 = m"
 2014  apply(simp)
 2015  done
 2016  lemma [simp]: "n 6= 0 =⇒ gcd m n = gcd n (m mod n)"
 2017  apply(simp)
 2018  done
 2019  Simplification terminates for these proofs because the condition of the if
 2020  simplifies to True or False. Now we can disable the original simplification

 2021  rule:
 2022  declare gcd.simps [simp del]

 2023  3.5.4 Induction

 2024  Having defined a function we might like to prove something about it. Since the

 2025  function is recursive, the natural proof principle is again induction. But this

 2026  time the structural form of induction that comes with datatypes is unlikely

 2027  to work well — otherwise we could have defined the function by primrec.
 2028  Therefore fun automatically proves a suitable induction rule f.induct that

 2029  follows the recursion pattern of the particular function f. We call this re-

 2030  cursion induction. Roughly speaking, it requires you to prove for each fun
=== PAGE 61 ===

 2031  3.5 Total Recursive Functions: fun 51

 2032  equation that the property you are trying to establish holds for the left-hand

 2033  side provided it holds for all recursive calls on the right-hand side. Here is a
 2034  simple example involving the predefined map functional on lists:
 2035  lemma "map f (sep x xs) = sep (f x) (map f xs)"
 2036  Note that map f xs is the result of applying f to all elements of xs. We prove
 2037  this lemma by recursion induction over sep :
 2038  apply(induct_tac x xs rule: sep.induct)

 2039  The resulting proof state has three subgoals corresponding to the three
 2040  clauses for sep:
 2041  1. Va. map f (sep a []) = sep (f a) (map f [])
 2042  2. Va x. map f (sep a [x]) = sep (f a) (map f [x])
 2043  3. Va x y zs.
 2044  map f (sep a (y # zs)) = sep (f a) (map f (y # zs)) =⇒
 2045  map f (sep a (x # y # zs)) = sep (f a) (map f (x # y # zs))

 2046  The rest is pure simplification:
 2047  apply simp_all
 2048  done

 2049  The proof goes smoothly because the induction rule follows the recursion of
 2050  sep. Try proving the above lemma by structural induction, and you find that

 2051  you need an additional case distinction.

 2052  In general, the format of invoking recursion induction is
 2053  apply(induct_tac x1 . . . xn rule: f.induct)
 2054  where x1 . . . xn is a list of free variables in the subgoal and f the name of a

 2055  function that takes n arguments. Usually the subgoal will contain the term
 2056  fx1 . . . xn but this need not be the case. The induction rules do not mention
 2057  f at all. Here is sep.induct :
 2058  [[ Va. P a [];
 2059  Va x. P a [x];
 2060  Va x y zs. P a (y # zs) =⇒ P a (x # y # zs)]]
 2061  =⇒ P u v
 2062  It merely says that in order to prove a property P of u and v you need to
 2063  prove it for the three cases where v is the empty list, the singleton list, and

 2064  the list with at least two elements. The final case has an induction hypothesis:
 2065  you may assume that P holds for the tail of that list.
=== PAGE 63 ===

 2066  4. Presenting Theories

 2067  By now the reader should have become sufficiently acquainted with elemen-

 2068  tary theory development in Isabelle/HOL. The following interlude describes

 2069  how to present theories in a typographically pleasing manner. Isabelle pro-

 2070  vides a rich infrastructure for concrete syntax of the underlying λ-calculus

 2071  language (see Sect. 4.1), as well as document preparation of theory texts based
 2072  on existing PDF-LATEX technology (see Sect. 4.2).

 2073  As pointed out by Leibniz more than 300 years ago, notions are in princi-

 2074  ple more important than notations, but suggestive textual representation of

 2075  ideas is vital to reduce the mental effort to comprehend and apply them.

 2076  4.1 Concrete Syntax

 2077  The core concept of Isabelle's framework for concrete syntax is that of mixfix

 2078  annotations. Associated with any kind of constant declaration, mixfixes

 2079  affect both the grammar productions for the parser and output templates for

 2080  the pretty printer.

 2081  In full generality, parser and pretty printer configuration is a subtle af-

 2082  fair [37]. Your syntax specifications need to interact properly with the existing

 2083  setup of Isabelle/Pure and Isabelle/HOL. To avoid creating ambiguities with

 2084  existing elements, it is particularly important to give new syntactic constructs

 2085  the right precedence.

 2086  Below we introduce a few simple syntax declaration forms that already

 2087  cover many common situations fairly well.

 2088  4.1.1 Infix Annotations

 2089  Syntax annotations may be included wherever constants are declared, such as

 2090  definition and primrec — and also datatype, which declares constructor

 2091  operations. Type-constructors may be annotated as well, although this is less
 2092  frequently encountered in practice (the infix type × comes to mind).

 2093  Infix declarations provide a useful special case of mixfixes. The following

 2094  example of the exclusive-or operation on boolean values illustrates typical

 2095  infix declarations.
=== PAGE 64 ===

 2096  54 4. Presenting Theories

 2097  definition xor :: "bool ⇒ bool ⇒ bool" (infixl "[+]" 60)
 2098  where "A [+] B ≡ (A ∧ ¬ B) ∨ (¬ A ∧ B)"
 2099  Now xor A B and A [+] B refer to the same expression internally. Any cur-

 2100  ried function with at least two arguments may be given infix syntax. For

 2101  partial applications with fewer than two operands, the operator is enclosed
 2102  in parentheses. For instance, xor without arguments is represented as ([+]);
 2103  together with ordinary function application, this turns xor A into ([+]) A.

 2104  The keyword infixl seen above specifies an infix operator that is nested

 2105  to the left: in iterated applications the more complex expression appears on
 2106  the left-hand side, and A [+] B [+] C stands for (A [+] B) [+] C. Similarly,
 2107  infixr means nesting to the right, reading A [+] B [+] C as A [+] (B [+] C).
 2108  A non-oriented declaration via infix would render A [+] B [+] C illegal, but

 2109  demand explicit parentheses to indicate the intended grouping.
 2110  The string [+] in our annotation refers to the concrete syntax to represent the operator (a literal token), while the number 60 determines the prece-

 2111  dence of the construct: the syntactic priorities of the arguments and result.

 2112  Isabelle/HOL already uses up many popular combinations of ASCII symbols
 2113  for its own use, including both + and ++. Longer character combinations are
 2114  more likely to be still available for user extensions, such as our [+].

 2115  Operator precedences have a range of 0–1000. Very low or high priorities

 2116  are reserved for the meta-logic. HOL syntax mainly uses the range of 10–100:
 2117  the equality infix = is centered at 50; logical connectives (like ∨ and ∧) are
 2118  below 50; algebraic ones (like + and *) are above 50. User syntax should strive

 2119  to coexist with common HOL forms, or use the mostly unused range 100–900.

 2120  4.1.2 Mathematical Symbols

 2121  Concrete syntax based on ASCII characters has inherent limitations. Math-

 2122  ematical notation demands a larger repertoire of glyphs. Several standards

 2123  of extended character sets have been proposed over decades, but none has

 2124  become universally available so far. Isabelle has its own notion of symbols

 2125  as the smallest entities of source text, without referring to internal encodings.

 2126  There are three kinds of such "generalized characters":

 2127  1. 7-bit ASCII characters

 2128  2. named symbols: \<ident>

 2129  3. named control symbols: \<ˆident>

 2130  Here ident is any sequence of letters. This results in an infinite store of

 2131  symbols, whose interpretation is left to further front-end tools. For example,

 2132  the Isabelle document processor (see Sect. 4.2) display the \<forall> symbol
 2133  as ∀ .

 2134  A list of standard Isabelle symbols is given in [37]. You may introduce

 2135  your own interpretation of further symbols by configuring the appropriate
 2136  front-end tool accordingly, e.g. by defining certain LATEX macros (see also
=== PAGE 65 ===

 2137  4.1 Concrete Syntax 55

 2138  Sect. 4.2.4). There are also a few predefined control symbols, such as \<ˆsub>

 2139  and \<ˆsup> for sub- and superscript of the subsequent printable symbol,
 2140  respectively. For example, A\<ˆsup>\<star>, is output as A ?.

 2141  A number of symbols are considered letters by the Isabelle lexer and can
 2142  be used as part of identifiers. These are the greek letters α (\<alpha>), β
 2143  (\<beta>), etc. (excluding λ), special letters like A (\<A>) and A (\<AA>).

 2144  Moreover the control symbol \<ˆsub> may be used to subscript a single letter

 2145  or digit in the trailing part of an identifier. This means that the input
 2146  \<forall>\<alpha>\<ˆsub>1. \<alpha>\<ˆsub>1 = \<Pi>\<ˆsub>\<A>
 2147  is recognized as the term ∀ α1. α1 = ΠA by Isabelle.
 2148  Replacing our previous definition of xor by the following specifies an Isa-

 2149  belle symbol for the new operator:
 2150  definition xor :: "bool ⇒ bool ⇒ bool" (infixl " ⊕" 60)
 2151  where "A ⊕ B ≡ (A ∧ ¬ B) ∨ (¬ A ∧ B)"

 2152  It is possible to provide alternative syntax forms through the print mode

 2153  concept [37]. By convention, the mode of "xsymbols" is enabled whenever
 2154  Proof General's X-Symbol mode or LATEX output is active. Now consider the
 2155  following hybrid declaration of xor :
 2156  definition xor :: "bool ⇒ bool ⇒ bool" (infixl "[+]" 60)
 2157  where "A [+] B ≡ (A ∧ ¬ B) ∨ (¬ A ∧ B)"
 2158  notation (xsymbols) xor (infixl " ⊕" 60)

 2159  The notation command associates a mixfix annotation with a known constant. The print mode specification, here (xsymbols), is optional.
 2160  We may now write A [+] B or A ⊕ B in input, while output uses the nicer

 2161  syntax of xsymbols whenever that print mode is active. Such an arrange-

 2162  ment is particularly useful for interactive development, where users may type

 2163  ASCII text and see mathematical symbols displayed during proofs.

 2164  4.1.3 Prefix Annotations

 2165  Prefix syntax annotations are another form of mixfixes [37], without any

 2166  template arguments or priorities — just some literal syntax. The following

 2167  example associates common symbols with the constructors of a datatype.
 2168  datatype currency =
 2169  Euro nat ("e")
 2170  | Pounds nat ("£")
 2171  | Yen nat ("U")
 2172  | Dollar nat ("$")

 2173  Here the mixfix annotations on the rightmost column happen to consist of a

 2174  single Isabelle symbol each: \<euro>, \<pounds>, \<yen>, and $. Recall that
 2175  a constructor like Euro actually is a function nat ⇒ currency. The expression
=== PAGE 66 ===

 2176  56 4. Presenting Theories

 2177  Euro 10 will be printed as e 10; only the head of the application is subject to

 2178  our concrete syntax. This rather simple form already achieves conformance

 2179  with notational standards of the European Commission.

 2180  Prefix syntax works the same way for other commands that introduce new

 2181  constants, e.g. primrec.

 2182  4.1.4 Abbreviations

 2183  Mixfix syntax annotations merely decorate particular constant application
 2184  forms with concrete syntax, for instance replacing xor A B by A ⊕ B. Occa-

 2185  sionally, the relationship between some piece of notation and its internal form

 2186  is more complicated. Here we need abbreviations.

 2187  Command abbreviation introduces an uninterpreted notational constant

 2188  as an abbreviation for a complex term. Abbreviations are unfolded upon

 2189  parsing and re-introduced upon printing. This provides a simple mechanism

 2190  for syntactic macros.

 2191  A typical use of abbreviations is to introduce relational notation for membership in a set of pairs, replacing (x, y) ∈ sim by x ≈ y. We assume that
 2192  a constant sim of type ('a × 'a) set has been introduced at this point.
 2193  abbreviation sim2 :: "'a ⇒ 'a ⇒ bool" (infix " ≈" 50)
 2194  where "x ≈ y ≡ (x, y) ∈ sim"

 2195  The given meta-equality is used as a rewrite rule after parsing (replacing
 2196  x ≈ y by (x,y) ∈ sim ) and before printing (turning (x,y) ∈ sim back into
 2197  x ≈ y ). The name of the dummy constant sim2 does not matter, as long as

 2198  it is unique.

 2199  Another common application of abbreviations is to provide variant versions of fundamental relational expressions, such as 6= for negated equalities.

 2200  The following declaration stems from Isabelle/HOL itself:
 2201  abbreviation not_equal :: "'a ⇒ 'a ⇒ bool" (infixl "˜=" 50)
 2202  where "x ˜= y ≡ ¬ (x = y)"
 2203  notation (xsymbols) not_equal (infix "6=" 50)
 2204  The notation 6= is introduced separately to restrict it to the xsymbols mode.

 2205  Abbreviations are appropriate when the defined concept is a simple vari-

 2206  ation on an existing one. But because of the automatic folding and unfolding

 2207  of abbreviations, they do not scale up well to large hierarchies of concepts.

 2208  Abbreviations do not replace definitions.

 2209  Abbreviations are a simplified form of the general concept of syntax trans-

 2210  lations; even heavier transformations may be written in ML [37].
=== PAGE 67 ===

 2211  4.2 Document Preparation 57

 2212  4.2 Document Preparation

 2213  Isabelle/Isar is centered around the concept of formal proof documents.

 2214  The outcome of a formal development effort is meant to be a human-readable

 2215  record, presented as browsable PDF file or printed on paper. The overall

 2216  document structure follows traditional mathematical articles, with sections,

 2217  intermediate explanations, definitions, theorems and proofs.

 2218  The Isabelle document preparation system essentially acts as a frontend to LATEX. After checking specifications and proofs formally, the theory

 2219  sources are turned into typesetting instructions in a schematic manner. This

 2220  lets you write authentic reports on theory developments with little effort:

 2221  many technical consistency checks are handled by the system.

 2222  Here is an example to illustrate the idea of Isabelle document preparation.

 2223  The following datatype definition of 'a bintree models binary
 2224  trees with nodes being decorated by elements of type 'a.
 2225  datatype 'a bintree =
 2226  Leaf | Branch 'a "'a bintree" "'a bintree"

 2227  The datatype induction rule generated here is of the form
 2228  [[P Leaf; Vx1 x2 x3. [[P x2; P x3]] =⇒ P (Branch x1 x2 x3)]] =⇒ P
 2229  bintree

 2230  The above document output has been produced as follows:
 2231  text {*
 2232  The following datatype definition of @{text "'a bintree"}
 2233  models binary trees with nodes being decorated by elements
 2234  of type @{typ 'a}.
 2235  *}
 2236  datatype 'a bintree =
 2237  Leaf | Branch 'a "'a bintree" "'a bintree"

 2238  text {*
 2239  \noindent The datatype induction rule generated here is
 2240  of the form @{thm [display] bintree.induct [no_vars]}
 2241  *}

 2242  Here we have augmented the theory by formal comments (using text blocks),

 2243  the informal parts may again refer to formal entities by means of "antiquota-

 2244  tions" (such as @{text "'a bintree"} or @{typ 'a}), see also Sect. 4.2.3.
=== PAGE 68 ===

 2245  58 4. Presenting Theories

 2246  4.2.1 Isabelle Sessions

 2247  In contrast to the highly interactive mode of Isabelle/Isar theory develop-

 2248  ment, the document preparation stage essentially works in batch-mode. An

 2249  Isabelle session consists of a collection of source files that may contribute to

 2250  an output document. Each session is derived from a single parent, usually an

 2251  object-logic image like HOL. This results in an overall tree structure, which

 2252  is reflected by the output location in the file system (the root directory is

 2253  determined by the Isabelle settings variable ISABELLE_BROWSER_INFO).

 2254  The easiest way to manage Isabelle sessions is via isabelle mkroot (to

 2255  generate an initial session source setup) and isabelle build (to run sessions

 2256  as specified in the corresponding ROOT file). These Isabelle tools are described

 2257  in further detail in the Isabelle System Manual [36].

 2258  For example, a new session MySession (with document preparation) may

 2259  be produced as follows:

 2260  isabelle mkroot MySession

 2261  isabelle build -D MySession

 2262  The isabelle build job also informs about the file-system location of

 2263  the ultimate results. The above dry run should be able to produce some

 2264  document.pdf (with dummy title, empty table of contents etc.). Any failure
 2265  at this stage usually indicates technical problems of the LATEX installation.

 2266  The detailed arrangement of the session sources is as follows.
 2267  – Directory MySession holds the required theory files T1.thy, . . . , Tn.thy.

 2268  – File MySession/ROOT specifies the session options and content, with decla-

 2269  rations for all wanted theories; it is sufficient to specify the terminal nodes

 2270  of the theory dependency graph.
 2271  – Directory MySession/document contains everything required for the LATEX

 2272  stage; only root.tex needs to be provided initially.
 2273  The latter file holds appropriate LATEX code to commence a document
 2274  (\documentclass etc.), and to include the generated files Ti.tex for each
 2275  theory. Isabelle will generate a file session.tex holding LATEX commands

 2276  to include all generated theory output files in topologically sorted order, so

 2277  \input{session} in the body of root.tex does the job in most situations.

 2278  One may now start to populate the directory MySession and its ROOT file

 2279  accordingly. The file MySession/document/root.tex should also be adapted

 2280  at some point; the default version is mostly self-explanatory. Note that

 2281  \isabellestyle enables fine-tuning of the general appearance of characters

 2282  and mathematical symbols (see also Sect. 4.2.4).
 2283  Especially observe the included LATEX packages isabelle (mandatory),

 2284  isabellesym (required for mathematical symbols), and the final pdfsetup

 2285  (provides sane defaults for hyperref, including URL markup). All three are
=== PAGE 69 ===

 2286  4.2 Document Preparation 59

 2287  distributed with Isabelle. Further packages may be required in particular

 2288  applications, say for unusual mathematical symbols.
 2289  Any additional files for the LATEX stage go into the MySession/document

 2290  directory as well. In particular, adding a file named root.bib causes an au-

 2291  tomatic run of bibtex to process a bibliographic database; see also isabelle

 2292  document [36].

 2293  Any failure of the document preparation phase in an Isabelle batch ses-

 2294  sion leaves the generated sources in their target location, identified by the
 2295  accompanying error message. This lets you trace LATEX problems with the

 2296  generated files at hand.

 2297  4.2.2 Structure Markup

 2298  The large-scale structure of Isabelle documents follows existing LATEX conven-

 2299  tions, with chapters, sections, subsubsections etc. The Isar language includes

 2300  separate markup commands, which do not affect the formal meaning of a
 2301  theory (or proof), but result in corresponding LATEX elements.

 2302  From the Isabelle perspective, each markup command takes a single text
 2303  argument (delimited by " . . . " or {* . . . *}). After stripping any surrounding
 2304  white space, the argument is passed to a LATEX macro \isamarkupXYZ for

 2305  command XYZ. These macros are defined in isabelle.sty according to

 2306  the meaning given in the rightmost column above.

 2307  The following source fragment illustrates structure markup of a theory.
 2308  Note that LATEX labels may be included inside of section headings as well.
=== PAGE 70 ===

 2309  60 4. Presenting Theories

 2310  section {* Some properties of Foo Bar elements *}
 2311  theory Foo_Bar
 2312  imports Main
 2313  begin
 2314  subsection {* Basic definitions *}
 2315  definition foo :: ...
 2316  definition bar :: ...
 2317  subsection {* Derived rules *}
 2318  lemma fooI: ...
 2319  lemma fooE: ...
 2320  subsection {* Main theorem \label{sec:main-theorem} *}
 2321  theorem main: ...
 2322  end

 2323  4.2.3 Formal Comments and Antiquotations

 2324  Isabelle source comments, which are of the form (* . . . *), essentially act

 2325  like white space and do not really contribute to the content. They mainly

 2326  serve technical purposes to mark certain oddities in the raw input text. In

 2327  contrast, formal comments are portions of text that are associated with

 2328  formal Isabelle/Isar commands (marginal comments), or as standalone

 2329  paragraphs within a theory or proof context (text blocks).

 2330  Marginal comments are part of each command's concrete syntax [37]; the
 2331  common form is "-- text" where text is delimited by ". . . " or {* . . . *} as

 2332  before. Multiple marginal comments may be given at the same time. Here is

 2333  a simple example:
 2334  lemma "A --> A"
 2335  — a triviality of propositional logic
 2336  — (should not really bother)
 2337  by (rule impI) — implicit assumption step involved here

 2338  The above output has been produced as follows:

 2339  lemma "A --> A"

 2340  -- "a triviality of propositional logic"

 2341  -- "(should not really bother)"

 2342  by (rule impI) -- "implicit assumption step involved here"
=== PAGE 71 ===

 2343  4.2 Document Preparation 61

 2344  From the LATEX viewpoint, "--" acts like a markup command, associated

 2345  with the macro \isamarkupcmt (taking a single argument).

 2346  Text blocks are introduced by the commands text and txt. Each takes

 2347  again a single text argument, which is interpreted as a free-form paragraph
 2348  in LATEX (surrounded by some additional vertical space). The typesetting
 2349  may be changed by redefining the LATEX environments of isamarkuptext or

 2350  isamarkuptxt, respectively (via \renewenvironment).

 2351  The text part of Isabelle markup commands essentially inserts quoted ma-

 2352  terial into a formal text, mainly for instruction of the reader. An antiquo-

 2353  tation is again a formal object embedded into such an informal portion. The

 2354  interpretation of antiquotations is limited to some well-formedness checks,

 2355  with the result being pretty printed to the resulting document. Quoted text

 2356  blocks together with antiquotations provide an attractive means of referring

 2357  to formal entities, with good confidence in getting the technical details right

 2358  (especially syntax and types).

 2359  The general syntax of antiquotations is as follows: @{name arguments},

 2360  or @{name [options] arguments} for a comma-separated list of options con-

 2361  sisting of a name or name=value each. The syntax of arguments depends on

 2362  the kind of antiquotation, it generally follows the same conventions for types,

 2363  terms, or theorems as in the formal part of a theory.
 2364  This sentence demonstrates quotations and antiquotations: λx y. x is a

 2365  well-typed term.

 2366  The output above was produced as follows:
 2367  text {*
 2368  This sentence demonstrates quotations and antiquotations:
 2369  @{term "%x y. x"} is a well-typed term.
 2370  *}

 2371  The notational change from the ASCII character % to the symbol λ reveals

 2372  that Isabelle printed this term, after parsing and type-checking. Document

 2373  preparation enables symbolic output by default.

 2374  The next example includes an option to show the type of all variables.

 2375  The antiquotation @{term [show_types] "%x y. x"} produces the output
 2376  λ(x::'a) y::'b. x. Type inference has figured out the most general typings

 2377  in the present theory context. Terms may acquire different typings due to con-

 2378  straints imposed by their environment; within a proof, for example, variables

 2379  are given the same types as they have in the main goal statement.

 2380  Several further kinds of antiquotations and options are available [37]. Here

 2381  are a few commonly used combinations:
=== PAGE 72 ===

 2382  62 4. Presenting Theories

 2383  @{typ τ} print type τ

 2384  @{const c} check existence of c and print it

 2385  @{term t} print term t

 2386  @{prop φ} print proposition φ

 2387  @{prop [display] φ} print large proposition φ (with linebreaks)

 2388  @{prop [source] φ} check proposition φ, print its input

 2389  @{thm a} print fact a

 2390  @{thm a [no_vars]} print fact a, fixing schematic variables

 2391  @{thm [source] a} check availability of fact a, print its name

 2392  @{text s} print uninterpreted text s
 2393  Note that no_vars given above is not an antiquotation option, but an

 2394  attribute of the theorem argument given here. This might be useful with a

 2395  diagnostic command like thm, too.

 2396  The @{text s} antiquotation is particularly interesting. Embedding un-

 2397  interpreted text within an informal body might appear useless at first sight.

 2398  Here the key virtue is that the string s is processed as Isabelle output, inter-

 2399  preting Isabelle symbols appropriately.
 2400  For example, @{text "\<forall>\<exists>"} produces ∀ ∃ , according

 2401  to the standard interpretation of these symbol (cf. Sect. 4.2.4). Thus we

 2402  achieve consistent mathematical notation in both the formal and informal

 2403  parts of the document very easily, independently of the term language of Isabelle. Manual LATEX code would leave more control over the typesetting, but

 2404  is also slightly more tedious.

 2405  4.2.4 Interpretation of Symbols

 2406  As has been pointed out before (Sect. 4.1.2), Isabelle symbols are the smallest

 2407  syntactic entities — a straightforward generalization of ASCII characters.

 2408  While Isabelle does not impose any interpretation of the infinite collection of
 2409  named symbols, LATEX documents use canonical glyphs for certain standard

 2410  symbols [37].
 2411  The LATEX code produced from Isabelle text follows a simple scheme. You

 2412  can tune the final appearance by redefining certain macros, say in root.tex

 2413  of the document.

 2414  1. 7-bit ASCII characters: letters A...Z and a...z are output directly, dig-

 2415  its are passed as an argument to the \isadigit macro, other characters

 2416  are replaced by specifically named macros of the form \isacharXYZ.

 2417  2. Named symbols: \<XYZ> is turned into {\isasymXYZ}; note the additional

 2418  braces.

 2419  3. Named control symbols: \<ˆXYZ> is turned into \isactrlXYZ; subsequent

 2420  symbols may act as arguments if the control macro is defined accordingly.
 2421  You may occasionally wish to give new LATEX interpretations of named

 2422  symbols. This merely requires an appropriate definition of \isasymXYZ,
=== PAGE 73 ===

 2423  4.2 Document Preparation 63

 2424  for \<XYZ> (see isabelle.sty for working examples). Control symbols are

 2425  slightly more difficult to get right, though.

 2426  The \isabellestyle macro provides a high-level interface to tune the

 2427  general appearance of individual symbols. For example, \isabellestyle{it}
 2428  uses the italics text style to mimic the general appearance of the LATEX math

 2429  mode; double quotes are not printed at all. The resulting quality of type-

 2430  setting is quite good, so this should be the default style for work that gets

 2431  distributed to a broader audience.

 2432  4.2.5 Suppressing Output

 2433  By default, Isabelle's document system generates a LATEX file for each theory

 2434  that gets loaded while running the session. The generated session.tex will

 2435  include all of these in order of appearance, which in turn gets included by

 2436  the standard root.tex. Certainly one may change the order or suppress

 2437  unwanted theories by ignoring session.tex and load individual files directly

 2438  in root.tex. On the other hand, such an arrangement requires additional

 2439  maintenance whenever the collection of theories changes.

 2440  Alternatively, one may tune the theory loading process in ROOT itself:

 2441  some sequential order of theories sections may enforce a certain traversal

 2442  of the dependency graph, although this could degrade parallel processing.

 2443  The nodes of each sub-graph that is specified here are presented in some

 2444  topological order of their formal dependencies.

 2445  Moreover, the system build option document=false allows to disable doc-

 2446  ument generation for some theories. Its usage in the session ROOT is like this:

 2447  theories [document = false] T

 2448  Theory output may be suppressed more selectively, either via tagged

 2449  command regions or ignored material.

 2450  Tagged command regions works by annotating commands with named
 2451  tags, which correspond to certain LATEX markup that tells how to treat par-

 2452  ticular parts of a document when doing the actual type-setting. By default,

 2453  certain Isabelle/Isar commands are implicitly marked up using the predefined

 2454  tags "theory" (for theory begin and end), "proof " (for proof commands), and

 2455  "ML" (for commands involving ML code). Users may add their own tags us-

 2456  ing the %tag notation right after a command name. In the subsequent example

 2457  we hide a particularly irrelevant proof:
 2458  lemma "x = x" by (simp)

 2459  The original source has been "lemma "x = x" by %invisible (simp)".

 2460  Tags observe the structure of proofs; adjacent commands with the same tag

 2461  are joined into a single region. The Isabelle document preparation system

 2462  allows the user to specify how to interpret a tagged region, in order to keep,
=== PAGE 74 ===

 2463  64 4. Presenting Theories

 2464  drop, or fold the corresponding parts of the document. See the Isabelle System

 2465  Manual [36] for further details, especially on isabelle build and isabelle

 2466  document.

 2467  Ignored material is specified by delimiting the original formal source with

 2468  special source comments (*<*) and (*>*). These parts are stripped before

 2469  the type-setting phase, without affecting the formal checking of the theory, of

 2470  course. For example, we may hide parts of a proof that seem unfit for general

 2471  public inspection. The following "fully automatic" proof is actually a fake:
 2472  lemma "x 6= (0::int) =⇒ 0 < x * x"
 2473  by (auto)

 2474  The real source of the proof has been as follows:

 2475  by (auto(*<*)simp add: zero_less_mult_iff(*>*))

 2476  Suppressing portions of printed text demands care. You should not mis-

 2477  represent the underlying theory development. It is easy to invalidate the

 2478  visible text by hiding references to questionable axioms, for example.
=== PAGE 75 ===

 2479  Part II

 2480  Logic and Sets
=== PAGE 77 ===

 2481  5. The Rules of the Game

 2482  This chapter outlines the concepts and techniques that underlie reasoning

 2483  in Isabelle. Until now, we have proved everything using only induction and

 2484  simplification, but any serious verification project requires more elaborate

 2485  forms of inference. The chapter also introduces the fundamentals of predicate

 2486  logic. The first examples in this chapter will consist of detailed, low-level proof

 2487  steps. Later, we shall see how to automate such reasoning using the methods
 2488  blast, auto and others. Backward or goal-directed proof is our usual style,

 2489  but the chapter also introduces forward reasoning, where one theorem is

 2490  transformed to yield another.

 2491  5.1 Natural Deduction

 2492  In Isabelle, proofs are constructed using inference rules. The most familiar

 2493  inference rule is probably modus ponens:

 2494  P → Q P

 2495  Q

 2496  This rule says that from P → Q and P we may infer Q.

 2497  Natural deduction is an attempt to formalize logic in a way that mirrors

 2498  human reasoning patterns. For each logical symbol (say, ∧), there are two

 2499  kinds of rules: introduction and elimination rules. The introduction rules

 2500  allow us to infer this symbol (say, to infer conjunctions). The elimination

 2501  rules allow us to deduce consequences from this symbol. Ideally each rule

 2502  should mention one symbol only. For predicate logic this can be done, but

 2503  when users define their own concepts they typically have to refer to other

 2504  symbols as well. It is best not to be dogmatic. Our system is not based on

 2505  pure natural deduction, but includes elements from the sequent calculus and

 2506  free-variable tableaux.

 2507  Natural deduction generally deserves its name. It is easy to use. Each proof

 2508  step consists of identifying the outermost symbol of a formula and applying

 2509  the corresponding rule. It creates new subgoals in an obvious way from parts

 2510  of the chosen formula. Expanding the definitions of constants can blow up

 2511  the goal enormously. Deriving natural deduction rules for such constants
=== PAGE 78 ===

 2512  68 5. The Rules of the Game

 2513  lets us reason in terms of their key properties, which might otherwise be

 2514  obscured by the technicalities of its definition. Natural deduction rules also

 2515  lend themselves to automation. Isabelle's classical reasoner accepts any

 2516  suitable collection of natural deduction rules and uses them to search for

 2517  proofs automatically. Isabelle is designed around natural deduction and many

 2518  of its tools use the terminology of introduction and elimination rules.

 2519  5.2 Introduction Rules

 2520  An introduction rule tells us when we can infer a formula containing a specific

 2521  logical symbol. For example, the conjunction introduction rule says that if

 2522  we have P and if we have Q then we have P ∧ Q. In a mathematics text, it

 2523  is typically shown like this:

 2524  P Q

 2525  P ∧ Q

 2526  The rule introduces the conjunction symbol (∧) in its conclusion. In Isabelle

 2527  proofs we mainly reason backwards. When we apply this rule, the subgoal

 2528  already has the form of a conjunction; the proof step makes this conjunction

 2529  symbol disappear.

 2530  In Isabelle notation, the rule looks like this:
 2531  [[?P; ?Q ]] =⇒ ?P ∧ ?Q (conjI)

 2532  Carefully examine the syntax. The premises appear to the left of the arrow

 2533  and the conclusion to the right. The premises (if more than one) are grouped

 2534  using the fat brackets. The question marks indicate schematic variables

 2535  (also called unknowns): they may be replaced by arbitrary formulas. If we

 2536  use the rule backwards, Isabelle tries to unify the current subgoal with the
 2537  conclusion of the rule, which has the form ?P ∧ ?Q. (Unification is discussed

 2538  below, Sect. 5.8.) If successful, it yields new subgoals given by the formulas
 2539  assigned to ?P and ?Q.

 2540  The following trivial proof illustrates how rules work. It also introduces

 2541  a style of indentation. If a command adds a new subgoal, then the next

 2542  command's indentation is increased by one space; if it proves a subgoal, then

 2543  the indentation is reduced. This provides the reader with hints about the

 2544  subgoal structure.
 2545  lemma conj_rule: "[[P; Q ]] =⇒ P ∧ (Q ∧ P)"
 2546  apply (rule conjI)
 2547  apply assumption
 2548  apply (rule conjI)
 2549  apply assumption
 2550  apply assumption
 2551  At the start, Isabelle presents us with the assumptions (P and Q ) and with
 2552  the goal to be proved, P ∧ (Q ∧ P). We are working backwards, so when we
=== PAGE 79 ===

 2553  5.3 Elimination Rules 69

 2554  apply conjunction introduction, the rule removes the outermost occurrence
 2555  of the ∧ symbol. To apply a rule to a subgoal, we apply the proof method
 2556  rule — here with conjI, the conjunction introduction rule.
 2557  1. [[P; Q ]] =⇒ P
 2558  2. [[P; Q ]] =⇒ Q ∧ P

 2559  Isabelle leaves two new subgoals: the two halves of the original conjunction.
 2560  The first is simply P, which is trivial, since P is among the assumptions.
 2561  We can apply the assumption method, which proves a subgoal by finding a

 2562  matching assumption.
 2563  1. [[P; Q ]] =⇒ Q ∧ P
 2564  We are left with the subgoal of proving Q ∧ P from the assumptions P and Q.
 2565  We apply rule conjI again.
 2566  1. [[P; Q ]] =⇒ Q
 2567  2. [[P; Q ]] =⇒ P
 2568  We are left with two new subgoals, Q and P, each of which can be proved
 2569  using the assumption method.

 2570  5.3 Elimination Rules

 2571  Elimination rules work in the opposite direction from introduction rules. In

 2572  the case of conjunction, there are two such rules. From P ∧ Q we infer P.

 2573  also, from P ∧ Q we infer Q:

 2574  P ∧ Q P ∧ Q

 2575  P Q

 2576  Now consider disjunction. There are two introduction rules, which resem-

 2577  ble inverted forms of the conjunction elimination rules:

 2578  P Q

 2579  P ∨ Q P ∨ Q

 2580  What is the disjunction elimination rule? The situation is rather different

 2581  from conjunction. From P ∨ Q we cannot conclude that P is true and we

 2582  cannot conclude that Q is true; there are no direct elimination rules of the sort

 2583  that we have seen for conjunction. Instead, there is an elimination rule that

 2584  works indirectly. If we are trying to prove something else, say R, and we know

 2585  that P ∨ Q holds, then we have to consider two cases. We can assume that

 2586  P is true and prove R and then assume that Q is true and prove R a second

 2587  time. Here we see a fundamental concept used in natural deduction: that of

 2588  the assumptions. We have to prove R twice, under different assumptions.

 2589  The assumptions are local to these subproofs and are visible nowhere else.
=== PAGE 80 ===

 2590  70 5. The Rules of the Game

 2591  In a logic text, the disjunction elimination rule might be shown like this:

 2592  [P] [Q]

 2593  .. ..

 2594  .. ..

 2595  P ∨ Q R R

 2596  R

 2597  The assumptions [P] and [Q] are bracketed to emphasize that they are local
 2598  to their subproofs. In Isabelle notation, the already-familiar =⇒ syntax serves

 2599  the same purpose:
 2600  [[?P ∨ ?Q; ?P =⇒ ?R; ?Q =⇒ ?R]] =⇒ ?R (disjE)

 2601  When we use this sort of elimination rule backwards, it produces a case

 2602  split. (We have seen this before, in proofs by induction.) The following proof

 2603  illustrates the use of disjunction elimination.
 2604  lemma disj_swap: "P ∨ Q =⇒ Q ∨ P"
 2605  apply (erule disjE)
 2606  apply (rule disjI2)
 2607  apply assumption
 2608  apply (rule disjI1)
 2609  apply assumption
 2610  We assume P ∨ Q and must prove Q ∨ P . Our first step uses the disjunction
 2611  elimination rule, disjE . We invoke it using erule , a method designed to work

 2612  with elimination rules. It looks for an assumption that matches the rule's

 2613  first premise. It deletes the matching assumption, regards the first premise as

 2614  proved and returns subgoals corresponding to the remaining premises. When
 2615  we apply erule to disjE, only two subgoals result. This is better than applying
 2616  it using rule to get three subgoals, then proving the first by assumption:
 2617  the other subgoals would have the redundant assumption P ∨ Q . Most of
 2618  the time, erule is the best way to use elimination rules, since it replaces

 2619  an assumption by its subformulas; only rarely does the original assumption

 2620  remain useful.
 2621  1. P =⇒ Q ∨ P
 2622  2. Q =⇒ Q ∨ P
 2623  These are the two subgoals returned by erule. The first assumes P and the
 2624  second assumes Q. Tackling the first subgoal, we need to show Q ∨ P. The
 2625  second introduction rule (disjI2) can reduce this to P, which matches the
 2626  assumption. So, we apply the rule method with disjI2 . . .
 2627  1. P =⇒ P
 2628  2. Q =⇒ Q ∨ P
 2629  . . . and finish off with the assumption method. We are left with the other
 2630  subgoal, which assumes Q.
 2631  1. Q =⇒ Q ∨ P
=== PAGE 81 ===

 2632  5.4 Destruction Rules: Some Examples 71

 2633  Its proof is similar, using the introduction rule disjI1.
 2634  The result of this proof is a new inference rule disj_swap, which is neither

 2635  an introduction nor an elimination rule, but which might be useful. We can

 2636  use it to replace any goal of the form Q ∨ P by one of the form P ∨ Q.

 2637  5.4 Destruction Rules: Some Examples

 2638  Now let us examine the analogous proof for conjunction.
 2639  lemma conj_swap: "P ∧ Q =⇒ Q ∧ P"
 2640  apply (rule conjI)
 2641  apply (drule conjunct2)
 2642  apply assumption
 2643  apply (drule conjunct1)
 2644  apply assumption

 2645  Recall that the conjunction elimination rules — whose Isabelle names are
 2646  conjunct1 and conjunct2 — simply return the first or second half of a con-

 2647  junction. Rules of this sort (where the conclusion is a subformula of a premise)
 2648  are called destruction rules because they take apart and destroy a premise.1

 2649  The first proof step applies conjunction introduction, leaving two sub-

 2650  goals:
 2651  1. P ∧ Q =⇒ Q
 2652  2. P ∧ Q =⇒ P
 2653  To invoke the elimination rule, we apply a new method, drule. Think of
 2654  the d as standing for destruction (or direct, if you prefer). Applying the
 2655  second conjunction rule using drule replaces the assumption P ∧ Q by Q.
 2656  1. Q =⇒ Q
 2657  2. P ∧ Q =⇒ P
 2658  The resulting subgoal can be proved by applying assumption. The other subgoal is similarly proved, using the conjunct1 rule and the assumption method.
 2659  Choosing among the methods rule, erule and drule is up to you. Isabelle

 2660  does not attempt to work out whether a rule is an introduction rule or an

 2661  elimination rule. The method determines how the rule will be interpreted.
 2662  Many rules can be used in more than one way. For example, disj_swap can

 2663  be applied to assumptions as well as to goals; it replaces any assumption of

 2664  the form P ∨ Q by a one of the form Q ∨ P.
 2665  Destruction rules are simpler in form than indirect rules such as disjE,

 2666  but they can be inconvenient. Each of the conjunction rules discards half of
 2667  1 This Isabelle terminology is not used in standard logic texts, although the distinction between the two forms of elimination rule is well known. Girard [9, page
 2668  74], for example, writes "The elimination rules [for ∨ and ∃] are very bad. What
 2669  is catastrophic about them is the parasitic presence of a formula [R] which has
 2670  no structural link with the formula which is eliminated." These Isabelle rules are
 2671  inspired by the sequent calculus.
=== PAGE 82 ===

 2672  72 5. The Rules of the Game

 2673  the formula, when usually we want to take both parts of the conjunction

 2674  as new assumptions. The easiest way to do so is by using an alternative
 2675  conjunction elimination rule that resembles disjE. It is seldom, if ever, seen

 2676  in logic books. In Isabelle syntax it looks like this:
 2677  [[?P ∧ ?Q; [[?P; ?Q ]] =⇒ ?R ]] =⇒ ?R (conjE)

 2678  Exercise 5.4.1 Use the rule conjE to shorten the proof above.

 2679  5.5 Implication

 2680  At the start of this chapter, we saw the rule modus ponens. It is, in fact, a

 2681  destruction rule. The matching introduction rule looks like this in Isabelle:
 2682  (?P =⇒ ?Q) =⇒ ?P −→ ?Q (impI)

 2683  And this is modus ponens:
 2684  [[?P −→ ?Q; ?P ]] =⇒ ?Q (mp)

 2685  Here is a proof using the implication rules. This lemma performs a sort

 2686  of uncurrying, replacing the two antecedents of a nested implication by a

 2687  conjunction. The proof illustrates how assumptions work. At each proof step,

 2688  the subgoals inherit the previous assumptions, perhaps with additions or
 2689  deletions. Rules such as impI and disjE add assumptions, while applying
 2690  erule or drule deletes the matching assumption.
 2691  lemma imp_uncurry: "P −→ (Q −→ R) =⇒ P ∧ Q −→ R"
 2692  apply (rule impI)
 2693  apply (erule conjE)
 2694  apply (drule mp)
 2695  apply assumption
 2696  apply (drule mp)
 2697  apply assumption
 2698  apply assumption
 2699  First, we state the lemma and apply implication introduction (rule impI),

 2700  which moves the conjunction to the assumptions.
 2701  1. [[P −→ Q −→ R; P ∧ Q ]] =⇒ R
 2702  Next, we apply conjunction elimination (erule conjE), which splits this con-

 2703  junction into two parts.
 2704  1. [[P −→ Q −→ R; P; Q ]] =⇒ R
 2705  Now, we work on the assumption P −→ (Q −→ R), where the parentheses

 2706  have been inserted for clarity. The nested implication requires two applications of modus ponens: drule mp. The first use yields the implication Q −→
 2707  R, but first we must prove the extra subgoal P, which we do by assumption.
=== PAGE 83 ===

 2708  5.6 Negation 73

 2709  1. [[P; Q ]] =⇒ P
 2710  2. [[P; Q; Q −→ R ]] =⇒ R
 2711  Repeating these steps for Q −→ R yields the conclusion we seek, namely R.
 2712  1. [[P; Q; Q −→ R ]] =⇒ R
 2713  The symbols =⇒ and −→ both stand for implication, but they differ in
 2714  many respects. Isabelle uses =⇒ to express inference rules; the symbol is

 2715  built-in and Isabelle's inference mechanisms treat it specially. On the other
 2716  hand, −→ is just one of the many connectives available in higher-order logic.
 2717  We reason about it using inference rules such as impI and mp, just as we
 2718  reason about the other connectives. You will have to use −→ in any context
 2719  that requires a formula of higher-order logic. Use =⇒ to separate a theorem's

 2720  preconditions from its conclusion.
