 2721  The by command is useful for proofs like these that use assumption heav-

 2722  ily. It executes an apply command, then tries to prove all remaining subgoals
 2723  using assumption. Since (if successful) it ends the proof, it also replaces the

 2724  done symbol. For example, the proof above can be shortened:
 2725  lemma imp_uncurry: "P −→ (Q −→ R) =⇒ P ∧ Q −→ R"
 2726  apply (rule impI)
 2727  apply (erule conjE)
 2728  apply (drule mp)
 2729  apply assumption
 2730  by (drule mp)

 2731  We could use by to replace the final apply and done in any proof, but
 2732  typically we use it to eliminate calls to assumption. It is also a nice way of

 2733  expressing a one-line proof.

 2734  5.6 Negation

 2735  Negation causes surprising complexity in proofs. Its natural deduction rules

 2736  are straightforward, but additional rules seem necessary in order to handle negated assumptions gracefully. This section also illustrates the intro

 2737  method: a convenient way of applying introduction rules.

 2738  Negation introduction deduces ¬P if assuming P leads to a contradiction.

 2739  Negation elimination deduces any formula in the presence of ¬P together

 2740  with P:
 2741  (?P =⇒ False) =⇒ ¬ ?P (notI)
 2742  [[¬ ?P; ?P ]] =⇒ ?R (notE)

 2743  Classical logic allows us to assume ¬P when attempting to prove P:
 2744  (¬ ?P =⇒ ?P) =⇒ ?P (classical)
=== PAGE 84 ===

 2745  74 5. The Rules of the Game

 2746  The implications P → Q and ¬Q → ¬P are logically equivalent, and

 2747  each is called the contrapositive of the other. Four further rules support

 2748  reasoning about contrapositives. They differ in the placement of the negation

 2749  symbols:
 2750  [[?Q; ¬ ?P =⇒ ¬ ?Q]] =⇒ ?P (contrapos_pp)
 2751  [[?Q; ?P =⇒ ¬ ?Q]] =⇒ ¬ ?P (contrapos_pn)
 2752  [[¬ ?Q; ¬ ?P =⇒ ?Q]] =⇒ ?P (contrapos_np)
 2753  [[¬ ?Q; ?P =⇒ ?Q]] =⇒ ¬ ?P (contrapos_nn)
 2754  These rules are typically applied using the erule method, where their effect

 2755  is to form a contrapositive from an assumption and the goal's conclusion.
 2756  The most important of these is contrapos_np. It is useful for applying

 2757  introduction rules to negated assumptions. For instance, the assumption

 2758  ¬(P → Q) is equivalent to the conclusion P → Q and we might want to

 2759  use conjunction introduction on it. Before we can do so, we must move that

 2760  assumption so that it becomes the conclusion. The following proof demon-

 2761  strates this technique:
 2762  lemma "[[¬(P −→Q); ¬(R −→Q) ]] =⇒ R"
 2763  apply (erule_tac Q = "R −→Q" in contrapos_np)
 2764  apply (intro impI)
 2765  by (erule notE)

 2766  There are two negated assumptions and we need to exchange the conclusion
 2767  with the second one. The method erule contrapos_np would select the first

 2768  assumption, which we do not want. So we specify the desired assumption
 2769  explicitly using a new method, erule_tac. This is the resulting subgoal:
 2770  1. [[¬ (P −→ Q); ¬ R ]] =⇒ R −→ Q
 2771  The former conclusion, namely R, now appears negated among the assumptions, while the negated formula R −→ Q becomes the new conclusion.
 2772  We can now apply introduction rules. We use the intro method, which

 2773  repeatedly applies the given introduction rules. Here its effect is equivalent
 2774  to rule impI.
 2775  1. [[¬ (P −→ Q); ¬ R; R ]] =⇒ Q
 2776  We can see a contradiction in the form of assumptions ¬ R and R, which
 2777  suggests using negation elimination. If applied on its own, notE will select

 2778  the first negated assumption, which is useless. Instead, we invoke the rule
 2779  using the by command. Now when Isabelle selects the first assumption, it
 2780  tries to prove P −→ Q and fails; it then backtracks, finds the assumption ¬ R
 2781  and finally proves R by assumption. That concludes the proof.

 2782  The following example may be skipped on a first reading. It involves a

 2783  peculiar but important rule, a form of disjunction introduction:
 2784  (¬ ?Q =⇒ ?P) =⇒ ?P ∨ ?Q (disjCI)
=== PAGE 85 ===

 2785  5.7 Interlude: the Basic Methods for Rules 75

 2786  This rule combines the effects of disjI1 and disjI2. Its great advantage is that

 2787  we can remove the disjunction symbol without deciding which disjunction

 2788  to prove. This treatment of disjunction is standard in sequent and tableau

 2789  calculi.
 2790  lemma "(P ∨ Q) ∧ R =⇒ P ∨ (Q ∧ R)"
 2791  apply (rule disjCI)
 2792  apply (elim conjE disjE)
 2793  apply assumption
 2794  by (erule contrapos_np, rule conjI)
 2795  The first proof step to applies the introduction rules disjCI. The resulting
 2796  subgoal has the negative assumption ¬(Q ∧ R) .
 2797  1. [[(P ∨ Q) ∧ R; ¬ (Q ∧ R) ]] =⇒ P
 2798  Next we apply the elim method, which repeatedly applies elimination rules;

 2799  here, the elimination rules given in the command. One of the subgoals is
 2800  trivial (apply assumption ), leaving us with one other:
 2801  1. [[¬ (Q ∧ R); R; Q ]] =⇒ P
 2802  Now we must move the formula Q ∧ R to be the conclusion. The combination
 2803  (erule contrapos_np, rule conjI)
 2804  is robust: the conjI forces the erule to select a conjunction. The two subgoals

 2805  are the ones we would expect from applying conjunction introduction to
 2806  Q ∧ R:
 2807  1. [[R; Q; ¬ P ]] =⇒ Q
 2808  2. [[R; Q; ¬ P ]] =⇒ R

 2809  They are proved by assumption, which is implicit in the by command.

 2810  5.7 Interlude: the Basic Methods for Rules

 2811  We have seen examples of many tactics that operate on individual rules. It

 2812  may be helpful to review how they work given an arbitrary rule such as this:
 2813  P1 . . . Pn

 2814  Q
 2815  Below, we refer to P1 as the major premise. This concept applies only to

 2816  elimination and destruction rules. These rules act upon an instance of their

 2817  major premise, typically to replace it by subformulas of itself.
 2818  Suppose that the rule above is called R. Here are the basic rule methods,

 2819  most of which we have already seen:
 2820  – Method rule R unifies Q with the current subgoal, replacing it by n new
 2821  subgoals: instances of P1, . . . , Pn. This is backward reasoning and is ap-

 2822  propriate for introduction rules.
=== PAGE 86 ===

 2823  76 5. The Rules of the Game

 2824  – Method erule R unifies Q with the current subgoal and simultaneously
 2825  unifies P1 with some assumption. The subgoal is replaced by the n −1 new
 2826  subgoals of proving instances of P2, . . . , Pn, with the matching assumption deleted. It is appropriate for elimination rules. The method (rule R,
 2827  assumption) is similar, but it does not delete an assumption.
 2828  – Method drule R unifies P1 with some assumption, which it then deletes.
 2829  The subgoal is replaced by the n −1 new subgoals of proving P2, . . . , Pn;

 2830  an nth subgoal is like the original one but has an additional assumption:

 2831  an instance of Q. It is appropriate for destruction rules.
 2832  – Method frule R is like drule R except that the matching assumption is

 2833  not deleted. (See Sect. 5.9.5 below.)

 2834  Other methods apply a rule while constraining some of its variables. The

 2835  typical form is
 2836  rule_tac v1 = t1 and ...and vk = tk in R
 2837  This method behaves like rule R, while instantiating the variables v1, . . . , vk
 2838  as specified. We similarly have erule_tac, drule_tac and frule_tac. These

 2839  methods also let us specify which subgoal to operate on. By default it is
 2840  the first subgoal, as with nearly all methods, but we can specify that rule R

 2841  should be applied to subgoal number i:
 2842  rule_tac [i] R

 2843  5.8 Unification and Substitution

 2844  As we have seen, Isabelle rules involve schematic variables, which begin with a

 2845  question mark and act as placeholders for terms. Unification — well known

 2846  to Prolog programmers — is the act of making two terms identical, possi-

 2847  bly replacing their schematic variables by terms. The simplest case is when

 2848  the two terms are already the same. Next simplest is pattern-matching,
 2849  which replaces variables in only one of the terms. The rule method typically
 2850  matches the rule's conclusion against the current subgoal. The assumption

 2851  method matches the current subgoal's conclusion against each of its assumptions. Unification can instantiate variables in both terms; the rule method

 2852  can do this if the goal itself contains schematic variables. Other occurrences

 2853  of the variables in the rule or proof state are updated at the same time.

 2854  Schematic variables in goals represent unknown terms. Given a goal such

 2855  as ∃x.P, they let us proceed with a proof. They can be filled in later, some-

 2856  times in stages and often automatically.

 2857  If unification fails when you think it should succeed, try setting the Proof General flag Isabelle > Settings > Trace Unification, which makes Isabelle show the
 2858  cause of unification failures (in Proof General's Trace buffer).
=== PAGE 87 ===

 2859  5.8 Unification and Substitution 77

 2860  For example, suppose we are trying to prove this subgoal by assumption:
 2861  1. P (a, f (b, g (e, a), b), a) =⇒ P (a, f (b, g (c, a), b), a)
 2862  The assumption method having failed, we try again with the flag set:
 2863  apply assumption
 2864  In this trivial case, the output clearly shows that e clashes with c :
 2865  Clash: e =/= c

 2866  Isabelle uses higher-order unification, which works in the typed λ-

 2867  calculus. The procedure requires search and is potentially undecidable. For

 2868  our purposes, however, the differences from ordinary unification are straight-

 2869  forward. It handles bound variables correctly, avoiding capture. The two
 2870  terms λx. f(x,z) and λy. f(y,z) are trivially unifiable because they differ only by a bound variable renaming. The two terms λx. ?P and λx. t x
 2871  are not unifiable; replacing ?P by t x is forbidden because the free occurrence of x would become bound. Unfortunately, even if trace_unify_fail is

 2872  set, Isabelle displays no information about this type of failure.
 2873  !!Higher-order unification sometimes must invent λ-terms to replace function
 2874  variables, which can lead to a combinatorial explosion. However, Isabelle proofs
 2875  tend to involve easy cases where there are few possibilities for the λ-term being
 2876  constructed. In the easiest case, the function variable is applied only to bound
 2877  variables, as when we try to unify λx y. f(?h x y) and λx y. f(x+y+a). The
 2878  only solution is to replace ?h by λx y. x+y+a. Such cases admit at most one unifier,
 2879  like ordinary unification. A harder case is unifying ?h a with a+b ; it admits two
 2880  solutions for ?h, namely λx. a+b and λx. x+b. Unifying ?h a with a+a+b admits
 2881  four solutions; their number is exponential in the number of occurrences of a in the
 2882  second term.

 2883  5.8.1 Substitution and the subst Method

 2884  Isabelle also uses function variables to express substitution. A typical sub-

 2885  stitution rule allows us to replace one term by another if we know that two

 2886  terms are equal.

 2887  s = t P[s/x]

 2888  P[t/x]

 2889  The rule uses a notation for substitution: P[t/x] is the result of replacing x

 2890  by t in P. The rule only substitutes in the positions designated by x. For

 2891  example, it can derive symmetry of equality from reflexivity. Using x = s

 2892  for P replaces just the first s in s = s by t:

 2893  s = t s = s

 2894  t = s

 2895  The Isabelle version of the substitution rule looks like this:
=== PAGE 88 ===

 2896  78 5. The Rules of the Game

 2897  [[?t = ?s; ?P ?s ]] =⇒ ?P ?t (ssubst)
 2898  Crucially, ?P is a function variable. It can be replaced by a λ-term with

 2899  one bound variable, whose occurrences identify the places in which s will be
 2900  replaced by t. The proof above requires ?P to be replaced by λx. x=s ; the
 2901  second premise will then be s=s and the conclusion will be t=s.
 2902  The simp method also replaces equals by equals, but the substitution rule

 2903  gives us more control. Consider this proof:
 2904  lemma "[[x = f x; odd(f x)]] =⇒ odd x"
 2905  by (erule ssubst)
 2906  The assumption x = f x, if used for rewriting, would loop, replacing x by f x
 2907  and then by f(f x) and so forth. (Here simp would see the danger and would

 2908  re-orient the equality, but in more complicated cases it can be fooled.) When
 2909  we apply the substitution rule, Isabelle replaces every x in the subgoal by f
 2910  x just once. It cannot loop. The resulting subgoal is trivial by assumption,

 2911  so the by command proves it implicitly.
 2912  We are using the erule method in a novel way. Hitherto, the conclusion of
 2913  the rule was just a variable such as ?R, but it may be any term. The conclusion
 2914  is unified with the subgoal just as it would be with the rule method. At
 2915  the same time erule looks for an assumption that matches the rule's first
 2916  premise, as usual. With ssubst the effect is to find, use and delete an equality

 2917  assumption.
 2918  The subst method performs individual substitutions. In simple cases, it

 2919  closely resembles a use of the substitution rule. Suppose a proof has reached

 2920  this point:
 2921  1. [[P x y z; Suc x < y ]] =⇒ f z = x * y

 2922  Now we wish to apply a commutative law:
 2923  ?m * ?n = ?n * ?m (mult.commute)

 2924  Isabelle rejects our first attempt:
 2925  apply (simp add: mult.commute)
 2926  The simplifier notices the danger of looping and refuses to apply the rule.2
 2927  The subst method applies mult.commute exactly once.
 2928  apply (subst mult.commute)
 2929  1. [[P x y z; Suc x < y ]] =⇒ f z = y * x
 2930  As we wanted, x * y has become y * x.
 2931  This use of the subst method has the same effect as the command
 2932  apply (rule mult.commute [THEN ssubst])
 2933  2 More precisely, it only applies such a rule if the new term is smaller under a
 2934  specified ordering; here, x * y is already smaller than y * x.
=== PAGE 89 ===

 2935  5.8 Unification and Substitution 79

 2936  The attribute THEN, which combines two rules, is described in Sect. 5.15.1 below. The subst method is more powerful than applying the substitution rule.

 2937  It can perform substitutions in a subgoal's assumptions. Moreover, if the sub-

 2938  goal contains more than one occurrence of the left-hand side of the equality,
 2939  the subst method lets us specify which occurrence should be replaced.

 2940  5.8.2 Unification and Its Pitfalls

 2941  Higher-order unification can be tricky. Here is an example, which you may

 2942  want to skip on your first reading:
 2943  lemma "[[x = f x; triple (f x) (f x) x]] =⇒ triple x x x"
 2944  apply (erule ssubst)
 2945  back
 2946  back
 2947  back
 2948  back
 2949  apply assumption
 2950  done
 2951  By default, Isabelle tries to substitute for all the occurrences. Applying erule
 2952  ssubst yields this subgoal:
 2953  1. triple (f x) (f x) x =⇒ triple (f x) (f x) (f x)
 2954  The substitution should have been done in the first two occurrences of x

 2955  only. Isabelle has gone too far. The back command allows us to reject this

 2956  possibility and demand a new one:
 2957  1. triple (f x) (f x) x =⇒ triple x (f x) (f x)
 2958  Now Isabelle has left the first occurrence of x alone. That is promising but

 2959  it is not the desired combination. So we use back again:
 2960  1. triple (f x) (f x) x =⇒ triple (f x) x (f x)

 2961  This also is wrong, so we use back again:
 2962  1. triple (f x) (f x) x =⇒ triple x x (f x)

 2963  And this one is wrong too. Looking carefully at the series of alternatives, we

 2964  see a binary countdown with reversed bits: 111, 011, 101, 001. Invoke back

 2965  again:
 2966  1. triple (f x) (f x) x =⇒ triple (f x) (f x) x

 2967  At last, we have the right combination! This goal follows by assumption.

 2968  This example shows that unification can do strange things with function

 2969  variables. We were forced to select the right unifier using the back command.

 2970  That is all right during exploration, but back should never appear in the final

 2971  version of a proof. You can eliminate the need for back by giving Isabelle

 2972  less freedom when you apply a rule.
=== PAGE 90 ===

 2973  80 5. The Rules of the Game

 2974  One way to constrain the inference is by joining two methods in a apply

 2975  command. Isabelle applies the first method and then the second. If the second

 2976  method fails then Isabelle automatically backtracks. This process continues

 2977  until the first method produces an output that the second method can use.

 2978  We get a one-line proof of our example:
 2979  lemma "[[x = f x; triple (f x) (f x) x]] =⇒ triple x x x"
 2980  apply (erule ssubst, assumption)
 2981  done

 2982  The by command works too, since it backtracks when proving subgoals by

 2983  assumption:
 2984  lemma "[[x = f x; triple (f x) (f x) x]] =⇒ triple x x x"
 2985  by (erule ssubst)

 2986  The most general way to constrain unification is by instantiating variables
 2987  in the rule. The method rule_tac is similar to rule, but it makes some of
 2988  the rule's variables denote specified terms. Also available are drule_tac and
 2989  erule_tac. Here we need erule_tac since above we used erule.
 2990  lemma "[[x = f x; triple (f x) (f x) x]] =⇒ triple x x x"
 2991  by (erule_tac P = "λu. triple u u x" in ssubst)
 2992  To specify a desired substitution requires instantiating the variable ?P with
 2993  a λ-expression. The bound variable occurrences in λu. P u u x indicate that

 2994  the first two arguments have to be substituted, leaving the third unchanged.

 2995  With this instantiation, backtracking is neither necessary nor possible.
 2996  An alternative to rule_tac is to use rule with a theorem modified using of, described in Sect. 5.15 below. But rule_tac, unlike of, can express

 2997  instantiations that refer to V-bound variables in the current subgoal.

 2998  5.9 Quantifiers

 2999  Quantifiers require formalizing syntactic substitution and the notion of arbi-

 3000  trary value. Consider the universal quantifier. In a logic book, its introduction

 3001  rule looks like this:

 3002  P

 3003  ∀x.P

 3004  Typically, a proviso written in English says that x must not occur in the as-

 3005  sumptions. This proviso guarantees that x can be regarded as arbitrary, since

 3006  it has not been assumed to satisfy any special conditions. Isabelle's under-

 3007  lying formalism, called the meta-logic, eliminates the need for English. It

 3008  provides its own universal quantifier (V) to express the notion of an arbitrary
 3009  value. We have already seen another operator of the meta-logic, namely =⇒,

 3010  which expresses inference rules and the treatment of assumptions. The only
 3011  other operator in the meta-logic is ≡, which can be used to define constants.
=== PAGE 91 ===

 3012  5.9 Quantifiers 81

 3013  5.9.1 The Universal Introduction Rule

 3014  Returning to the universal quantifier, we find that having a similar quantifier

 3015  as part of the meta-logic makes the introduction rule trivial to express:
 3016  (Vx. ?P x) =⇒ ∀ x. ?P x (allI)

 3017  The following trivial proof demonstrates how the universal introduction

 3018  rule works.
 3019  lemma "∀ x. P x −→ P x"
 3020  apply (rule allI)
 3021  by (rule impI)
 3022  The first step invokes the rule by applying the method rule allI.
 3023  1. Vx. P x −→ P x
 3024  Note that the resulting proof state has a bound variable, namely x. The rule

 3025  has replaced the universal quantifier of higher-order logic by Isabelle's metalevel quantifier. Our goal is to prove P x −→ P x for arbitrary x ; it is an
 3026  implication, so we apply the corresponding introduction rule (impI ).
 3027  1. Vx. P x =⇒ P x

 3028  This last subgoal is implicitly proved by assumption.

 3029  5.9.2 The Universal Elimination Rule

 3030  Now consider universal elimination. In a logic text, the rule looks like this:

 3031  ∀x.P

 3032  P[t/x]

 3033  The conclusion is P with t substituted for the variable x. Isabelle expresses

 3034  substitution using a function variable:
 3035  ∀ x. ?P x =⇒ ?P ?x (spec)

 3036  This destruction rule takes a universally quantified formula and removes the
 3037  quantifier, replacing the bound variable x by the schematic variable ?x. Re-

 3038  call that a schematic variable starts with a question mark and acts as a

 3039  placeholder: it can be replaced by any term.

 3040  The universal elimination rule is also available in the standard elimination
 3041  format. Like conjE, it never appears in logic books:
 3042  [[∀ x. ?P x; ?P ?x =⇒ ?R ]] =⇒ ?R (allE)
 3043  The methods drule spec and erule allE do precisely the same inference.

 3044  To see how ∀-elimination works, let us derive a rule about reducing the

 3045  scope of a universal quantifier. In mathematical notation we write

 3046  ∀x. P → Q

 3047  P → ∀x.Q
=== PAGE 92 ===

 3048  82 5. The Rules of the Game

 3049  with the proviso "x not free in P." Isabelle's treatment of substitution makes
 3050  the proviso unnecessary. The conclusion is expressed as P −→ ( ∀ x. Q x).
 3051  No substitution for the variable P can introduce a dependence upon x: that

 3052  would be a bound variable capture. Let us walk through the proof.
 3053  lemma "(∀ x. P −→ Q x) =⇒ P −→ (∀ x. Q x)"
 3054  First we apply implies introduction (impI ), which moves the P from the conclusion to the assumptions. Then we apply universal introduction (allI ).
 3055  apply (rule impI, rule allI)
 3056  1. Vx. [[∀ x. P −→ Q x; P ]] =⇒ Q x

 3057  As before, it replaces the HOL quantifier by a meta-level quantifier, producing
 3058  a subgoal that binds the variable x. The leading bound variables (here x)
 3059  and the assumptions (here ∀ x. P −→ Q x and P) form the context for the
 3060  conclusion, here Q x. Subgoals inherit the context, although assumptions can
 3061  be added or deleted (as we saw earlier), while rules such as allI add bound

 3062  variables.

 3063  Now, to reason from the universally quantified assumption, we apply the
 3064  elimination rule using the drule method. This rule is called spec because it

 3065  specializes a universal formula to a particular term.
 3066  apply (drule spec)
 3067  1. Vx. [[P; P −→ Q (?x2 x)]] =⇒ Q x

 3068  Observe how the context has changed. The quantified formula is gone, re-

 3069  placed by a new assumption derived from its body. We have removed the
 3070  quantifier and replaced the bound variable by the curious term ?x2 x. This
 3071  term is a placeholder: it may become any term that can be built from x.
 3072  (Formally, ?x2 is an unknown of function type, applied to the argument x.)

 3073  This new assumption is an implication, so we can use modus ponens on it,

 3074  which concludes the proof.
 3075  by (drule mp)

 3076  Let us take a closer look at this last step. Modus ponens yields two subgoals:
 3077  one where we prove the antecedent (in this case P) and one where we may
 3078  assume the consequent. Both of these subgoals are proved by the assumption

 3079  method, which is implicit in the by command. Replacing the by command
 3080  by apply (drule mp, assumption) would have left one last subgoal:
 3081  1. Vx. [[P; Q (?x2 x)]] =⇒ Q x
 3082  The consequent is Q applied to that placeholder. It may be replaced by any
 3083  term built from x, and here it should simply be x. The assumption need not

 3084  be identical to the conclusion, provided the two formulas are unifiable.

 3085  5.9.3 The Existential Quantifier

 3086  The concepts just presented also apply to the existential quantifier, whose

 3087  introduction rule looks like this in Isabelle:
=== PAGE 93 ===

 3088  5.9 Quantifiers 83

 3089  ?P ?x =⇒ ∃ x. ?P x (exI)

 3090  If we can exhibit some x such that P(x) is true, then ∃x.P(x) is also true.

 3091  It is a dual of the universal elimination rule, and logic texts present it using

 3092  the same notation for substitution.

 3093  The existential elimination rule looks like this in a logic text:

 3094  [P]

 3095  ..

 3096  ..

 3097  ∃x. P Q

 3098  Q

 3099  It looks like this in Isabelle:
 3100  [[∃ x. ?P x; Vx. ?P x =⇒ ?Q ]] =⇒ ?Q (exE)

 3101  Given an existentially quantified theorem and some formula Q to prove, it

 3102  creates a new assumption by removing the quantifier. As with the universal

 3103  introduction rule, the textbook version imposes a proviso on the quantified

 3104  variable, which Isabelle expresses using its meta-logic. It is enough to have a

 3105  universal quantifier in the meta-logic; we do not need an existential quantifier

 3106  to be built in as well.

 3107  Exercise 5.9.1 Prove the lemma

 3108  ∃x. P ∧ Q(x) =⇒ P ∧ (∃x. Q(x)).

 3109  Hint: the proof is similar to the one just above for the universal quantifier.

 3110  5.9.4 Renaming a Bound Variable: rename_tac
 3111  When you apply a rule such as allI, the quantified variable becomes a new

 3112  bound variable of the new subgoal. Isabelle tries to avoid changing its name,

 3113  but sometimes it has to choose a new name in order to avoid a clash. The

 3114  result may not be ideal:
 3115  lemma "x < y =⇒ ∀ x y. P x (f y)"
 3116  apply (intro allI)
 3117  1. Vxa ya. x < y =⇒ P xa (f ya)
 3118  The names x and y were already in use, so the new bound variables are called
 3119  xa and ya. You can rename them by invoking rename_tac:
 3120  apply (rename_tac v w)
 3121  1. Vv w. x < y =⇒ P v (f w)
 3122  Recall that rule_tac instantiates a theorem with specified terms. These terms

 3123  may involve the goal's bound variables, but beware of referring to variables
 3124  like xa. A future change to your theories could change the set of names produced at top level, so that xa changes to xb or reverts to x. It is safer to

 3125  rename automatically-generated variables before mentioning them.

 3126  If the subgoal has more bound variables than there are names given to
 3127  rename_tac, the rightmost ones are renamed.
=== PAGE 94 ===

 3128  84 5. The Rules of the Game

 3129  5.9.5 Reusing an Assumption: frule

 3130  Note that drule spec removes the universal quantifier and — as usual with

 3131  elimination rules — discards the original formula. Sometimes, a universal

 3132  formula has to be kept so that it can be used again. Then we use a new
 3133  method: frule. It acts like drule but copies rather than replaces the selected
 3134  assumption. The f is for forward.
 3135  In this example, going from P a to P(h(h a)) requires two uses of the
 3136  quantified assumption, one for each h in h(h a).
 3137  lemma "[[∀ x. P x −→ P (h x); P a]] =⇒ P(h (h a))"
 3138  Examine the subgoal left by frule:
 3139  apply (frule spec)
 3140  1. [[∀ x. P x −→ P (h x); P a; P ?x −→ P (h ?x) ]] =⇒ P (h (h a))
 3141  It is what drule would have left except that the quantified assumption is still
 3142  present. Next we apply mp to the implication and the assumption P a :
 3143  apply (drule mp, assumption)
 3144  1. [[∀ x. P x −→ P (h x); P a; P (h a) ]] =⇒ P (h (h a))
 3145  We have created the assumption P(h a), which is progress. To continue the
 3146  proof, we apply spec again. We shall not need it again, so we can use drule.
 3147  apply (drule spec)
 3148  1. [[P a; P (h a); P ?x2 −→ P (h ?x2) ]] =⇒ P (h (h a))
 3149  The new assumption bridges the gap between P(h a) and P(h(h a)).
 3150  by (drule mp)

 3151  A final remark. Replacing this by command with
 3152  apply (drule mp, assumption)
 3153  would not work: it would add a second copy of P(h a) instead of the desired
 3154  assumption, P(h(h a)). The by command forces Isabelle to backtrack until it

 3155  finds the correct one. Alternatively, we could have used the apply command
 3156  and bundled the drule mp with two calls of assumption. Or, of course, we
 3157  could have given the entire proof to auto.

 3158  5.9.6 Instantiating a Quantifier Explicitly

 3159  We can prove a theorem of the form ∃x. P x by exhibiting a suitable term t

 3160  such that P t is true. Dually, we can use an assumption of the form ∀x. P x to

 3161  generate a new assumption P t for a suitable term t. In many cases, Isabelle

 3162  makes the correct choice automatically, constructing the term by unification.

 3163  In other cases, the required term is not obvious and we must specify it ourselves. Suitable methods are rule_tac, drule_tac and erule_tac.

 3164  We have seen (just above, Sect. 5.9.5) a proof of this lemma:
=== PAGE 95 ===

 3165  5.9 Quantifiers 85
 3166  lemma "[[∀ x. P x −→ P (h x); P a]] =⇒ P(h (h a))"

 3167  We had reached this subgoal:
 3168  1. [[∀ x. P x −→ P (h x); P a; P (h a) ]] =⇒ P (h (h a))
 3169  The proof requires instantiating the quantified assumption with the term h a.
 3170  apply (drule_tac x = "h a" in spec)
 3171  1. [[P a; P (h a); P (h a) −→ P (h (h a))]] =⇒ P (h (h a))

 3172  We have forced the desired instantiation.

 3173  Existential formulas can be instantiated too. The next example uses the

 3174  divides relation of number theory:
 3175  ?m dvd ?n ≡ ∃ k. ?n = ?m * k (dvd_def)

 3176  Let us prove that multiplication of natural numbers is monotone with

 3177  respect to the divides relation:
 3178  lemma mult_dvd_mono: "[[i dvd m; j dvd n]] =⇒ i*j dvd (m*n :: nat)"
 3179  apply (simp add: dvd_def)

 3180  Unfolding the definition of divides has left this subgoal:
 3181  1. [[∃ k. m = i * k; ∃ k. n = j * k ]] =⇒ ∃ k. m * n = i * j * k

 3182  Next, we eliminate the two existential quantifiers in the assumptions:
 3183  apply (erule exE)
 3184  1. Vk. [[∃ k. n = j * k; m = i * k ]] =⇒ ∃ k. m * n = i * j * k
 3185  apply (erule exE)
 3186  1. Vk ka. [[m = i * k; n = j * ka ]] =⇒ ∃ k. m * n = i * j * k
 3187  The term needed to instantiate the remaining quantifier is k*ka. But ka is an

 3188  automatically-generated name. As noted above, references to such variable

 3189  names makes a proof less resilient to future changes. So, first we rename the
 3190  most recent variable to l :
 3191  apply (rename_tac l)
 3192  1. Vk l. [[m = i * k; n = j * l ]] =⇒ ∃ k. m * n = i * j * k
 3193  We instantiate the quantifier with k*l :
 3194  apply (rule_tac x="k*l" in exI)
 3195  1. Vk ka. [[m = i * k; n = j * ka ]] =⇒ m * n = i * j * (k * ka)

 3196  The rest is automatic, by arithmetic.
 3197  apply simp
 3198  done
=== PAGE 96 ===

 3199  86 5. The Rules of the Game

 3200  5.10 Description Operators

 3201  HOL provides two description operators. A definite description formalizes

 3202  the word "the," as in "the greatest divisior of n." It returns an arbitrary

 3203  value unless the formula has a unique solution. An indefinite description

 3204  formalizes the word "some," as in "some member of S." It differs from a

 3205  definite description in not requiring the solution to be unique: it uses the

 3206  axiom of choice to pick any solution.
 3207  !!Description operators can be hard to reason about. Novices should try to avoid
 3208  them. Fortunately, descriptions are seldom required.

 3209  5.10.1 Definite Descriptions

 3210  A definite description is traditionally written ιx.P(x). It denotes the x such

 3211  that P(x) is true, provided there exists a unique such x; otherwise, it returns an arbitrary value of the expected type. Isabelle uses THE for the Greek

 3212  letter ι.
 3213  We reason using this rule, where a is the unique solution:
 3214  [[P a; Vx. P x =⇒ x = a ]] =⇒ (THE x. P x) = a (the_equality)

 3215  For instance, we can define the cardinality of a finite set A to be that n

 3216  such that A is in one-to-one correspondence with {1, . . . ,n}. We can then

 3217  prove that the cardinality of the empty set is zero (since n = 0 satisfies the

 3218  description) and proceed to prove other facts.

 3219  A more challenging example illustrates how Isabelle/HOL defines the least
 3220  number operator, which denotes the least x satisfying P :
 3221  (LEAST x. P x) = (THE x. P x ∧ ( ∀ y. P y −→ x ≤ y))
 3222  Let us prove the analogue of the_equality for LEAST .
 3223  theorem Least_equality:
 3224  " [[P (k::nat); ∀ x. P x −→ k ≤ x]] =⇒ (LEAST x. P x) = k"
 3225  apply (simp add: Least_def)

 3226  1. [[P k; ∀ x. P x −→ k ≤ x]]
 3227  =⇒ (THE x. P x ∧ ( ∀ y. P y −→ x ≤ y)) = k

 3228  The first step has merely unfolded the definition.
 3229  apply (rule the_equality)

 3230  1. [[P k; ∀ x. P x −→ k ≤ x]] =⇒ P k ∧ ( ∀ y. P y −→ k ≤ y)
 3231  2. Vx. [[P k; ∀ x. P x −→ k ≤ x; P x ∧ ( ∀ y. P y −→ x ≤ y)]]
 3232  =⇒ x = k
 3233  As always with the_equality, we must show existence and uniqueness of the
 3234  claimed solution, k. Existence, the first subgoal, is trivial. Uniqueness, the

 3235  second subgoal, follows by antisymmetry:
=== PAGE 97 ===

 3236  5.10 Description Operators 87
 3237  [[x ≤ y; y ≤ x ]] =⇒ x = y (order_antisym)
 3238  The assumptions imply both k ≤ x and x ≤ k. One call to auto does it all:
 3239  by (auto intro: order_antisym)

 3240  5.10.2 Indefinite Descriptions

 3241  An indefinite description is traditionally written εx.P(x) and is known as

 3242  Hilbert's ε-operator. It denotes some x such that P(x) is true, provided one
 3243  exists. Isabelle uses SOME for the Greek letter ε.
 3244  Here is the definition of inv,3 which expresses inverses of functions:
 3245  inv f ≡ λy. SOME x. f x = y (inv_def)
 3246  Using SOME rather than THE makes inv f behave well even if f is not injective.
 3247  As it happens, most useful theorems about inv do assume the function to be

 3248  injective.
 3249  The inverse of f, when applied to y, returns some x such that f x = y.
 3250  For example, we can prove inv Suc really is the inverse of the Suc function
 3251  lemma "inv Suc (Suc n) = n"
 3252  by (simp add: inv_def)

 3253  The proof is a one-liner: the subgoal simplifies to a degenerate application of
 3254  SOME, which is then erased. In detail, the left-hand side simplifies to SOME x.
 3255  Suc x = Suc n, then to SOME x. x = n and finally to n.
 3256  We know nothing about what inv Suc returns when applied to zero. The
 3257  proof above still treats SOME as a definite description, since it only reasons
 3258  about situations in which the value is described uniquely. Indeed, SOME satis-

 3259  fies this rule:
 3260  [[P a; Vx. P x =⇒ x = a ]] =⇒ (SOME x. P x) = a (some_equality)

 3261  To go further is tricky and requires rules such as these:
 3262  P x =⇒ P (SOME x. P x) (someI)
 3263  [[P a; Vx. P x =⇒ Q x ]] =⇒ Q (SOME x. P x) (someI2)
 3264  Rule someI is basic: if anything satisfies P then so does SOME x. P x. The
 3265  repetition of P in the conclusion makes it difficult to apply in a backward
 3266  proof, so the derived rule someI2 is also provided.

 3267  For example, let us prove the axiom of choice:
 3268  theorem axiom_of_choice: "( ∀ x. ∃ y. P x y) =⇒ ∃ f. ∀ x. P x (f x)"
 3269  apply (rule exI, rule allI)
 3270  1. Vx. ∀ x. ∃ y. P x y =⇒ P x (?f x)

 3271  We have applied the introduction rules; now it is time to apply the elimination

 3272  rules.
 3273  3 In fact, inv is defined via a second constant inv_into, which we ignore here.
=== PAGE 98 ===

 3274  88 5. The Rules of the Game

 3275  apply (drule spec, erule exE)

 3276  1. Vx y. P (?x2 x) y =⇒ P x (?f x)
 3277  The rule someI automatically instantiates f to λx. SOME y. P x y, which is
 3278  the choice function. It also instantiates ?x2 x to x.
 3279  by (rule someI)

 3280  Historical Note. The original purpose of Hilbert's ε-operator was to ex-

 3281  press an existential destruction rule:

 3282  ∃x.P

 3283  P[(εx.P)/ x]

 3284  This rule is seldom used for that purpose — it can cause exponential blow-up

 3285  — but it is occasionally used as an introduction rule for the ε-operator. Its
 3286  name in HOL is someI_ex .

 3287  5.11 Some Proofs That Fail

 3288  Most of the examples in this tutorial involve proving theorems. But not every

 3289  conjecture is true, and it can be instructive to see how proofs fail. Here we

 3290  attempt to prove a distributive law involving the existential quantifier and

 3291  conjunction.
 3292  lemma "(∃ x. P x) ∧ (∃ x. Q x) =⇒ ∃ x. P x ∧ Q x"

 3293  The first steps are routine. We apply conjunction elimination to break the as-

 3294  sumption into two existentially quantified assumptions. Applying existential

 3295  elimination removes one of the quantifiers.
 3296  apply (erule conjE)
 3297  apply (erule exE)
 3298  1. Vx. [[∃ x. Q x; P x]] =⇒ ∃ x. P x ∧ Q x

 3299  When we remove the other quantifier, we get a different bound variable in
 3300  the subgoal. (The name xa is generated automatically.)
 3301  apply (erule exE)
 3302  1. Vx xa. [[P x; Q xa]] =⇒ ∃ x. P x ∧ Q x

 3303  The proviso of the existential elimination rule has forced the variables to

 3304  differ: we can hardly expect two arbitrary values to be equal! There is no

 3305  way to prove this subgoal. Removing the conclusion's existential quantifier

 3306  yields two identical placeholders, which can become any term involving the
 3307  variables x and xa. We need one to become x and the other to become xa,

 3308  but Isabelle requires all instances of a placeholder to be identical.
=== PAGE 99 ===

 3309  5.11 Some Proofs That Fail 89

 3310  apply (rule exI)
 3311  apply (rule conjI)
 3312  1. Vx xa. [[P x; Q xa]] =⇒ P (?x3 x xa)
 3313  2. Vx xa. [[P x; Q xa]] =⇒ Q (?x3 x xa)
 3314  We can prove either subgoal using the assumption method. If we prove the
 3315  first one, the placeholder changes into x.
 3316  apply assumption
 3317  1. Vx xa. [[P x; Q xa]] =⇒ Q x
 3318  We are left with a subgoal that cannot be proved. Applying the assumption

 3319  method results in an error message:
 3320  *** empty result sequence -- proof command failed

 3321  When interacting with Isabelle via the shell interface, you can abandon a

 3322  proof using the oops command.

 3323  Here is another abortive proof, illustrating the interaction between bound

 3324  variables and unknowns. If R is a reflexive relation, is there an x such that

 3325  R x y holds for all y? Let us see what happens when we attempt to prove it.
 3326  lemma "∀ y. R y y =⇒ ∃ x. ∀ y. R x y"

 3327  First, we remove the existential quantifier. The new proof state has an unknown, namely ?x.
 3328  apply (rule exI)
 3329  1. ∀ y. R y y =⇒ ∀ y. R ?x y
 3330  It looks like we can just apply assumption, but it fails. Isabelle refuses to
 3331  substitute y, a bound variable, for ?x; that would be a bound variable capture.

 3332  We can still try to finish the proof in some other way. We remove the universal
 3333  quantifier from the conclusion, moving the bound variable y into the subgoal.

 3334  But note that it is still bound!
 3335  apply (rule allI)
 3336  1. Vy. ∀ y. R y y =⇒ R ?x y

 3337  Finally, we try to apply our reflexivity assumption. We obtain a new assumption whose identical placeholders may be replaced by any term involving y.
 3338  apply (drule spec)
 3339  1. Vy. R (?z2 y) (?z2 y) =⇒ R ?x y
 3340  This subgoal can only be proved by putting y for all the placeholders, making
 3341  the assumption and conclusion become R y y. Isabelle can replace ?z2 y by
 3342  y; this involves instantiating ?z2 to the identity function. But, just as two
 3343  steps earlier, Isabelle refuses to substitute y for ?x. This example is typical

 3344  of how Isabelle enforces sound quantifier reasoning.
=== PAGE 100 ===

 3345  90 5. The Rules of the Game

 3346  5.12 Proving Theorems Using the blast Method

 3347  It is hard to prove many theorems using the methods described above. A

 3348  proof may be hundreds of steps long. You may need to search among different

 3349  ways of proving certain subgoals. Often a choice that proves one subgoal

 3350  renders another impossible to prove. There are further complications that we

 3351  have not discussed, concerning negation and disjunction. Isabelle's classical

 3352  reasoner is a family of tools that perform such proofs automatically. The
 3353  most important of these is the blast method.

 3354  In this section, we shall first see how to use the classical reasoner in its

 3355  default mode and then how to insert additional rules, enabling it to work in

 3356  new problem domains.

 3357  We begin with examples from pure predicate logic. The following exam-

 3358  ple is known as Andrew's challenge. Peter Andrews designed it to be hard

 3359  to prove by automatic means. It is particularly hard for a resolution prover,

 3360  where converting the nested biconditionals to clause form produces a combinatorial explosion [33]. However, the blast method proves it in a fraction of

 3361  a second.
 3362  lemma "(( ∃ x. ∀ y. p(x)=p(y)) = (( ∃ x. q(x))=(∀ y. p(y)))) =
 3363  (( ∃ x. ∀ y. q(x)=q(y)) = ((∃ x. p(x))=(∀ y. q(y))))"
 3364  by blast
 3365  The next example is a logic problem composed by Lewis Carroll. The blast

 3366  method finds it trivial. Moreover, it turns out that not all of the assumptions

 3367  are necessary. We can experiment with variations of this formula and see

 3368  which ones can be proved.
 3369  lemma "(∀ x. honest(x) ∧ industrious(x) −→ healthy(x)) ∧
 3370  ¬ (∃ x. grocer(x) ∧ healthy(x)) ∧
 3371  ( ∀ x. industrious(x) ∧ grocer(x) −→ honest(x)) ∧
 3372  ( ∀ x. cyclist(x) −→ industrious(x)) ∧
 3373  ( ∀ x. ¬healthy(x) ∧ cyclist(x) −→ ¬honest(x))
 3374  −→ (∀ x. grocer(x) −→ ¬cyclist(x))"
 3375  by blast
 3376  The blast method is also effective for set theory, which is described in the
 3377  next chapter. The formula below may look horrible, but the blast method

 3378  proves it in milliseconds.
 3379  lemma "(S i ∈I. A(i)) ∩ (S j ∈J. B(j)) =
 3380  ( S i∈I. S j ∈J. A(i) ∩ B(j))"
 3381  by blast

 3382  Few subgoals are couched purely in predicate logic and set theory. We can

 3383  extend the scope of the classical reasoner by giving it new rules. Extending

 3384  it effectively requires understanding the notions of introduction, elimination

 3385  and destruction rules. Moreover, there is a distinction between safe and un-

 3386  safe rules. A safe rule is one that can be applied backwards without losing
=== PAGE 101 ===

 3387  5.13 Other Classical Reasoning Methods 91

 3388  information; an unsafe rule loses information, perhaps transforming the sub-

 3389  goal into one that cannot be proved. The safe/unsafe distinction affects the

 3390  proof search: if a proof attempt fails, the classical reasoner backtracks to the

 3391  most recent unsafe rule application and makes another choice.

 3392  An important special case avoids all these complications. A logical equiv-

 3393  alence, which in higher-order logic is an equality between formulas, can be
 3394  given to the classical reasoner and simplifier by using the attribute iff . You

 3395  should do so if the right hand side of the equivalence is simpler than the

 3396  left-hand side.

 3397  For example, here is a simple fact about list concatenation. The result of

 3398  appending two lists is empty if and only if both of the lists are themselves

 3399  empty. Obviously, applying this equivalence will result in a simpler goal.
 3400  When stating this lemma, we include the iff attribute. Once we have proved

 3401  the lemma, Isabelle will make it known to the classical reasoner (and to the

 3402  simplifier).
 3403  lemma [iff]: "(xs@ys = []) = (xs=[] ∧ ys=[])"
 3404  apply (induct_tac xs)
 3405  apply (simp_all)
 3406  done
 3407  This fact about multiplication is also appropriate for the iff attribute:
 3408  (?m * ?n = 0) = (?m = 0 ∨ ?n = 0)

 3409  A product is zero if and only if one of the factors is zero. The reasoning

 3410  involves a disjunction. Proving new rules for disjunctive reasoning is hard,

 3411  but translating to an actual disjunction works: the classical reasoner handles

 3412  disjunction properly.
 3413  In more detail, this is how the iff attribute works. It converts the equiv-

 3414  alence P = Q to a pair of rules: the introduction rule Q =⇒ P and the

 3415  destruction rule P =⇒ Q. It gives both to the classical reasoner as safe rules,

 3416  ensuring that all occurrences of P in a subgoal are replaced by Q. The simplifier performs the same replacement, since iff gives P = Q to the simplifier.

 3417  Classical reasoning is different from simplification. Simplification is deter-

 3418  ministic. It applies rewrite rules repeatedly, as long as possible, transforming

 3419  a goal into another goal. Classical reasoning uses search and backtracking in

 3420  order to prove a goal outright.

 3421  5.13 Other Classical Reasoning Methods

 3422  The blast method is our main workhorse for proving theorems automatically.

 3423  Other components of the classical reasoner interact with the simplifier. Still

 3424  others perform classical reasoning to a limited extent, giving the user fine

 3425  control over the proof.
 3426  Of the latter methods, the most useful is clarify . It performs all obvious

 3427  reasoning steps without splitting the goal into multiple parts. It does not
=== PAGE 102 ===

 3428  92 5. The Rules of the Game

 3429  apply unsafe rules that could render the goal unprovable. By performing the
 3430  obvious steps, clarify lays bare the difficult parts of the problem, where

 3431  human intervention is necessary.

 3432  For example, the following conjecture is false:
 3433  lemma "(∀ x. P x) ∧ (∃ x. Q x) −→ (∀ x. P x ∧ Q x)"
 3434  apply clarify
 3435  The blast method would simply fail, but clarify presents a subgoal that

 3436  helps us see why we cannot continue the proof.
 3437  1. Vx xa. [[∀ x. P x; Q xa ]] =⇒ P x ∧ Q x
 3438  The proof must fail because the assumption Q xa and conclusion Q x refer to
 3439  distinct bound variables. To reach this state, clarify applied the introduction
 3440  rules for −→ and ∀ and the elimination rule for ∧. It did not apply the
 3441  introduction rule for ∧ because of its policy never to split goals.
 3442  Also available is clarsimp , a method that interleaves clarify and simp.
 3443  Also there is safe , which like clarify performs obvious steps but even applies

 3444  those that split goals.
 3445  The force method applies the classical reasoner and simplifier to one goal.
 3446  Unless it can prove the goal, it fails. Contrast that with the auto method,

 3447  which also combines classical reasoning with simplification. The latter's pur-

 3448  pose is to prove all the easy subgoals and parts of subgoals. Unfortunately,

 3449  it can produce large numbers of new subgoals; also, since it proves some

 3450  subgoals and splits others, it obscures the structure of the proof tree. The
 3451  force method does not have these drawbacks. Another difference: force tries
 3452  harder than auto to prove its goal, so it can take much longer to terminate.

 3453  Older components of the classical reasoner have largely been superseded
 3454  by blast, but they still have niche applications. Most important among these
 3455  are fast and best. While blast searches for proofs using a built-in first-

 3456  order reasoner, these earlier methods search for proofs using standard Isabelle

 3457  inference. That makes them slower but enables them to work in the presence

 3458  of the more unusual features of Isabelle rules, such as type classes and function

 3459  unknowns. For example, recall the introduction rule for Hilbert's ε-operator:
 3460  ?P ?x =⇒ ?P (SOME x. ?P x) (someI)
 3461  The repeated occurrence of the variable ?P makes this rule tricky to apply.

 3462  Consider this contrived example:
 3463  lemma "[[Q a; P a ]]
 3464  =⇒ P (SOME x. P x ∧ Q x) ∧ Q (SOME x. P x ∧ Q x)"
 3465  apply (rule someI)
 3466  We can apply rule someI explicitly. It yields the following subgoal:
 3467  1. [[Q a; P a ]] =⇒ P ?x ∧ Q ?x

 3468  The proof from this point is trivial. Could we have proved the theorem with
 3469  a single command? Not using blast : it cannot perform the higher-order unification needed here. The fast method succeeds:
=== PAGE 103 ===

 3470  5.14 Finding More Theorems 93

 3471  apply (fast intro!: someI)
 3472  The best method is similar to fast but it uses a best-first search instead

 3473  of depth-first search. Accordingly, it is slower but is less susceptible to divergence. Transitivity rules usually cause fast to loop where best can often

 3474  manage.

 3475  Here is a summary of the classical reasoning methods:
 3476  – blast works automatically and is the fastest
 3477  – clarify and clarsimp perform obvious steps without splitting the goal;
 3478  safe even splits goals
 3479  – force uses classical reasoning and simplification to prove a goal; auto is

 3480  similar but leaves what it cannot prove
 3481  – fast and best are legacy methods that work well with rules involving

 3482  unusual features

 3483  A table illustrates the relationships among four of these methods.

 3484  no split split
 3485  no simp clarify safe
 3486  simp clarsimp auto

 3487  5.14 Finding More Theorems

 3488  In Sect. 3.1.11, we introduced Proof General's Find button for finding theo-

 3489  rems in the database via pattern matching. If we are inside a proof, we can

 3490  be more specific; we can search for introduction, elimination and destruction

 3491  rules with respect to the current goal. For this purpose, Find provides three

 3492  aditional search criteria: intro, elim and dest.

 3493  For example, given the goal
 3494  1. A ∧ B

 3495  you can click on Find and type in the search expression intro. You will
 3496  be shown a few rules ending in =⇒ ?P ∧ ?Q, among them conjI . You may

 3497  even discover that the very theorem you are trying to prove is already in the

 3498  database. Given the goal
 3499  1. A −→ A
 3500  the search for intro finds not just impI but also imp_refl : ?P −→ ?P.

 3501  As before, search criteria can be combined freely: for example,
 3502  "_ @ _" intro

 3503  searches for all introduction rules that match the current goal and mention
 3504  the @ function.

 3505  Searching for elimination and destruction rules via elim and dest is anal-

 3506  ogous to intro but takes the assumptions into account, too.
=== PAGE 104 ===

 3507  94 5. The Rules of the Game

 3508  5.15 Forward Proof: Transforming Theorems

 3509  Forward proof means deriving new facts from old ones. It is the most funda-

 3510  mental type of proof. Backward proof, by working from goals to subgoals, can

 3511  help us find a difficult proof. But it is not always the best way of presenting

 3512  the proof thus found. Forward proof is particularly good for reasoning from

 3513  the general to the specific. For example, consider this distributive law for the

 3514  greatest common divisor:

 3515  k × gcd(m,n) = gcd(k × m,k × n)

 3516  Putting m = 1 we get (since gcd(1,n) = 1 and k × 1 = k)

 3517  k = gcd(k,k × n)

 3518  We have derived a new fact; if re-oriented, it might be useful for simplification.

 3519  After re-orienting it and putting n = 1, we derive another useful law:

 3520  gcd(k, k) = k

 3521  Substituting values for variables — instantiation — is a forward step. Re-

 3522  orientation works by applying the symmetry of equality to an equation, so it

 3523  too is a forward step.

 3524  5.15.1 Modifying a Theorem using of, where and THEN

 3525  Let us reproduce our examples in Isabelle. Recall that in Sect. 3.5.3 we declared the recursive function gcd:
 3526  fun gcd :: "nat ⇒ nat ⇒ nat" where
 3527  "gcd m n = (if n=0 then m else gcd n (m mod n))"

 3528  From this definition, it is possible to prove the distributive law. That takes

 3529  us to the starting point for our example.
 3530  ?k * gcd ?m ?n = gcd (?k * ?m) (?k * ?n) (gcd_mult_distrib2)
 3531  The first step in our derivation is to replace ?m by 1. We instantiate the
 3532  theorem using of , which identifies variables in order of their appearance from
 3533  left to right. In this case, the variables are ?k, ?m and ?n. So, the expression
 3534  [of k 1] replaces ?k by k and ?m by 1.
 3535  lemmas gcd_mult_0 = gcd_mult_distrib2 [of k 1]

 3536  The keyword lemmas declares a new theorem, which can be derived from an
 3537  existing one using attributes such as [of k 1]. The command thm gcd_mult_0

 3538  displays the result:
 3539  k * gcd 1 ?n = gcd (k * 1) (k * ?n)
=== PAGE 105 ===

 3540  5.15 Forward Proof: Transforming Theorems 95
 3541  Something is odd: k is an ordinary variable, while ?n is schematic. We did
 3542  not specify an instantiation for ?n. In its present form, the theorem does not
 3543  allow substitution for k. One solution is to avoid giving an instantiation for
 3544  ?k: instead of a term we can put an underscore (_). For example,
 3545  gcd_mult_distrib2 [of _ 1]
 3546  replaces ?m by 1 but leaves ?k unchanged.
 3547  An equivalent solution is to use the attribute where.
 3548  gcd mult distrib2 [where m=1]
 3549  While of refers to variables by their position, where refers to variables by
 3550  name. Multiple instantiations are separated by and, as in this example:
 3551  gcd mult distrib2 [where m=1 and k=1]
 3552  We now continue the present example with the version of gcd_mult_0
 3553  shown above, which has k instead of ?k. Once we have replaced ?m by 1, we
 3554  must next simplify the theorem gcd_mult_0, performing the steps gcd(1,n) =
 3555  1 and k × 1 = k. The simplified attribute takes a theorem and returns the

 3556  result of simplifying it, with respect to the default simplification rules:
 3557  lemmas gcd_mult_1 = gcd_mult_0 [simplified]

 3558  Again, we display the resulting theorem:
 3559  k = gcd k (k * ?n)

 3560  To re-orient the equation requires the symmetry rule:
 3561  ?s = ?t =⇒ ?t = ?s (sym)
 3562  The following declaration gives our equation to sym :
 3563  lemmas gcd_mult = gcd_mult_1 [THEN sym]

 3564  Here is the result:
 3565  gcd k (k * ?n) = k
 3566  THEN sym gives the current theorem to the rule sym and returns the result-

 3567  ing conclusion. The effect is to exchange the two operands of the equality.
 3568  Typically THEN is used with destruction rules. Also useful is THEN spec, which
 3569  removes the quantifier from a theorem of the form ∀x. P, and THEN mp, which

 3570  P
 3571  converts the implication P → Q into the rule . Similar to mp are the follow-

 3572  Q

 3573  ing two rules, which extract the two directions of reasoning about a boolean

 3574  equivalence:
 3575  [[?Q = ?P; ?Q]] =⇒ ?P (iffD1)
 3576  [[?P = ?Q; ?Q]] =⇒ ?P (iffD2)
 3577  Normally we would never name the intermediate theorems such as gcd_mult_0
 3578  and gcd_mult_1 but would combine the three forward steps:
 3579  lemmas gcd_mult = gcd_mult_distrib2 [of k 1, simplified, THEN sym]
=== PAGE 106 ===

 3580  96 5. The Rules of the Game

 3581  The directives, or attributes, are processed from left to right. This declaration
 3582  of gcd_mult is equivalent to the previous one.

 3583  Such declarations can make the proof script hard to read. Better is to
 3584  state the new lemma explicitly and to prove it using a single rule method

 3585  whose operand is expressed using forward reasoning:
 3586  lemma gcd mult [simp]: "gcd k (k*n) = k"
 3587  by (rule gcd_mult_distrib2 [of k 1, simplified, THEN sym])
 3588  Compared with the previous proof of gcd_mult, this version shows the reader

 3589  what has been proved. Also, the result will be processed in the normal way.

 3590  In particular, Isabelle generalizes over all variables: the resulting theorem will
 3591  have ?k instead of k .

 3592  At the start of this section, we also saw a proof of gcd(k, k) = k. Here is

 3593  the Isabelle version:
 3594  lemma gcd self [simp]: "gcd k k = k"
 3595  by (rule gcd_mult [of k 1, simplified])

 3596  !!To give of a nonatomic term, enclose it in quotation marks, as in [of "k*m"].
 3597  The term must not contain unknowns: an attribute such as [of "?k*m"] will
 3598  be rejected.

 3599  5.15.2 Modifying a Theorem using OF

 3600  Recall that of generates an instance of a rule by specifying values for its
 3601  variables. Analogous is OF, which generates an instance of a rule by specifying

 3602  facts for its premises.

 3603  We again need the divides relation of number theory, which as we recall

 3604  is defined by
 3605  ?m dvd ?n ≡ ∃ k. ?n = ?m * k (dvd_def)

 3606  Suppose, for example, that we have proved the following rule. It states that

 3607  if k and n are relatively prime and if k divides m × n then k divides m.
 3608  [[gcd ?k ?n = 1; ?k dvd ?m * ?n]] =⇒ ?k dvd ?m (relprime_dvd_mult)
 3609  We can use OF to create an instance of this rule. First, we prove an instance

 3610  of its first premise:
 3611  lemma relprime 20 81: "gcd 20 81 = 1"
 3612  by (simp add: gcd.simps)
 3613  We have evaluated an application of the gcd function by simplification. Ex-

 3614  pression evaluation involving recursive functions is not guaranteed to termi-

 3615  nate, and it can be slow; Isabelle performs arithmetic by rewriting symbolic

 3616  bit strings. Here, however, the simplification takes less than one second. We
 3617  can give this new lemma to OF. The expression
 3618  relprime_dvd_mult [OF relprime_20_81]
=== PAGE 107 ===

 3619  5.16 Forward Reasoning in a Backward Proof 97

 3620  yields the theorem
 3621  20 dvd (?m * 81) =⇒ 20 dvd ?m
 3622  OF takes any number of operands. Consider the following facts about the

 3623  divides relation:
 3624  [[?k dvd ?m; ?k dvd ?n ]] =⇒ ?k dvd ?m + ?n (dvd_add)
 3625  ?m dvd ?m (dvd_refl)
 3626  Let us supply dvd_refl for each of the premises of dvd_add :
 3627  dvd_add [OF dvd_refl dvd_refl]

 3628  Here is the theorem that we have expressed:
 3629  ?k dvd (?k + ?k)
 3630  As with of, we can use the _ symbol to leave some positions unspecified:
 3631  dvd_add [OF _ dvd_refl]

 3632  The result is
 3633  ?k dvd ?m =⇒ ?k dvd ?m + ?k
 3634  You may have noticed that THEN and OF are based on the same idea,

 3635  namely to combine two rules. They differ in the order of the combination
 3636  and thus in their effect. We use THEN typically with a destruction rule to
 3637  extract a subformula of the current theorem. We use OF with a list of facts

 3638  to generate an instance of the current theorem.

 3639  Here is a summary of some primitives for forward reasoning:
 3640  – of instantiates the variables of a rule to a list of terms
 3641  – OF applies a rule to a list of theorems
 3642  – THEN gives a theorem to a named rule and returns the conclusion
 3643  – simplified applies the simplifier to a theorem

 3644  – lemmas assigns a name to the theorem produced by the attributes above

 3645  5.16 Forward Reasoning in a Backward Proof

 3646  We have seen that the forward proof directives work well within a backward

 3647  proof. There are many ways to achieve a forward style using our existing

 3648  proof methods. We shall also meet some new methods that perform forward

 3649  reasoning.
 3650  The methods drule, frule, drule_tac, etc., reason forward from a subgoal.
 3651  We have seen them already, using rules such as mp and spec to operate on

 3652  formulae. They can also operate on terms, using rules such as these:
 3653  x = y =⇒ f x = f y (arg_cong)
 3654  i ≤ j =⇒ i * k ≤ j * k (mult_le_mono1)

 3655  For example, let us prove a fact about divisibility in the natural numbers:
=== PAGE 108 ===

 3656  98 5. The Rules of the Game

 3657  lemma "2 ≤ u =⇒ u*m 6= Suc(u*n)"
 3658  apply (intro notI)
 3659  1. [[2 ≤ u; u * m = Suc (u * n) ]] =⇒ False
 3660  The key step is to apply the function . . .mod u to both sides of the equation
 3661  u*m = Suc(u*n):
 3662  apply (drule_tac f="λx. x mod u" in arg_cong)
 3663  1. [[2 ≤ u; u * m mod u = Suc (u * n) mod u]] =⇒ False

 3664  Simplification reduces the left side to 0 and the right side to 1, yielding the

 3665  required contradiction.
 3666  apply (simp add: mod_Suc)
 3667  done

 3668  Our proof has used a fact about remainder:
 3669  Suc m mod n =
 3670  (if Suc (m mod n) = n then 0 else Suc (m mod n)) (mod_Suc)

 3671  5.16.1 The Method insert

 3672  The insert method inserts a given theorem as a new assumption of all sub-

 3673  goals. This already is a forward step; moreover, we may (as always when
 3674  using a theorem) apply of, THEN and other directives. The new assumption

 3675  can then be used to help prove the subgoals.

 3676  For example, consider this theorem about the divides relation. The first
 3677  proof step inserts the distributive law for gcd. We specify its variables as

 3678  shown.
 3679  lemma relprime dvd mult:
 3680  " [[ gcd k n = 1; k dvd m*n ]] =⇒ k dvd m"
 3681  apply (insert gcd_mult_distrib2 [of m k n])

 3682  In the resulting subgoal, note how the equation has been inserted:
 3683  1. [[gcd k n = 1; k dvd m * n; m * gcd k n = gcd (m * k) (m * n)]]
 3684  =⇒ k dvd m
 3685  The next proof step utilizes the assumption gcd k n = 1 (note that Suc 0 is

 3686  another expression for 1):
 3687  apply(simp)
 3688  1. [[gcd k n = Suc 0; k dvd m * n; m = gcd (m * k) (m * n) ]]
 3689  =⇒ k dvd m
 3690  Simplification has yielded an equation for m. The rest of the proof is omitted.
 3691  Here is another demonstration of insert. Division and remainder obey a

 3692  well-known law:
 3693  (?m div ?n) * ?n + ?m mod ?n = ?m (div_mult_mod_eq)

 3694  We refer to this law explicitly in the following proof:
=== PAGE 109 ===

 3695  5.16 Forward Reasoning in a Backward Proof 99

 3696  lemma div_mult_self_is_m:
 3697  "0<n =⇒ (m*n) div n = (m::nat)"
 3698  apply (insert div_mult_mod_eq [of "m*n" n])
 3699  apply (simp)
 3700  done
 3701  The first step inserts the law, specifying m*n and n for its variables. Notice

 3702  that non-trivial expressions must be enclosed in quotation marks. Here is the

 3703  resulting subgoal, with its new assumption:
 3704  1. [[0 < n; (m * n) div n * n + (m * n) mod n = m * n]]
 3705  =⇒ (m * n) div n = m
 3706  Simplification reduces (m * n) mod n to zero. Then it cancels the factor n on
 3707  both sides of the equation (m * n) div n * n = m * n, proving the theorem.
 3708  !!Any unknowns in the theorem given to insert will be universally quantified in
 3709  the new assumption.

 3710  5.16.2 The Method subgoal_tac
 3711  A related method is subgoal_tac, but instead of inserting a theorem as an

 3712  assumption, it inserts an arbitrary formula. This formula must be proved

 3713  later as a separate subgoal. The idea is to claim that the formula holds on

 3714  the basis of the current assumptions, to use this claim to complete the proof,

 3715  and finally to justify the claim. It gives the proof some structure. If you find

 3716  yourself generating a complex assumption by a long series of forward steps,
 3717  consider using subgoal_tac instead: you can state the formula you are aiming

 3718  for, and perhaps prove it automatically.

 3719  Look at the following example.
 3720  lemma "[[(z::int) < 37; 66 < 2*z; z*z 6= 1225; Q(34); Q(36) ]]
 3721  =⇒ Q(z)"
 3722  apply (subgoal_tac "z = 34 ∨ z = 36")
 3723  apply blast
 3724  apply (subgoal_tac "z 6= 35")
 3725  apply arith
 3726  apply force
 3727  done
 3728  The first assumption tells us that z is no greater than 36. The second tells
 3729  us that z is at least 34. The third assumption tells us that z cannot be 35,
 3730  since 35 × 35 = 1225. So z is either 34 or 36, and since Q holds for both of

 3731  those values, we have the conclusion.

 3732  The Isabelle proof closely follows this reasoning. The first step is to claim
 3733  that z is either 34 or 36. The resulting proof state gives us two subgoals:
 3734  1. [[z < 37; 66 < 2 * z; z * z 6= 1225; Q 34; Q 36;
 3735  z = 34 ∨ z = 36]]
 3736  =⇒ Q z
 3737  2. [[z < 37; 66 < 2 * z; z * z 6= 1225; Q 34; Q 36]]
 3738  =⇒ z = 34 ∨ z = 36
=== PAGE 110 ===

 3739  100 5. The Rules of the Game

 3740  The first subgoal is trivial (blast ), but for the second Isabelle needs help to
 3741  eliminate the case z=35. The second invocation of subgoal_tac leaves two

 3742  subgoals:
 3743  1. [[z < 37; 66 < 2 * z; z * z 6= 1225; Q 34; Q 36;
 3744  z 6= 35]]
 3745  =⇒ z = 34 ∨ z = 36
 3746  2. [[z < 37; 66 < 2 * z; z * z 6= 1225; Q 34; Q 36]]
 3747  =⇒ z 6= 35
 3748  Assuming that z is not 35, the first subgoal follows by linear arithmetic
 3749  (arith). For the second subgoal we apply the method force, which proceeds
 3750  by assuming that z=35 and arriving at a contradiction.

 3751  Summary of these methods:
 3752  – insert adds a theorem as a new assumption
 3753  – subgoal_tac adds a formula as a new assumption and leaves the subgoal

 3754  of proving that formula

 3755  5.17 Managing Large Proofs

 3756  Naturally you should try to divide proofs into manageable parts. Look for

 3757  lemmas that can be proved separately. Sometimes you will observe that they

 3758  are instances of much simpler facts. On other occasions, no lemmas suggest

 3759  themselves and you are forced to cope with a long proof involving many

 3760  subgoals.

 3761  5.17.1 Tacticals, or Control Structures

 3762  If the proof is long, perhaps it at least has some regularity. Then you can

 3763  express it more concisely using tacticals, which provide control structures.
 3764  Here is a proof (it would be a one-liner using blast, but forget that) that

 3765  contains a series of repeated commands:
 3766  lemma "[[P−→Q; Q−→R; R −→S; P ]] =⇒ S"
 3767  apply (drule mp, assumption)
 3768  apply (drule mp, assumption)
 3769  apply (drule mp, assumption)
 3770  apply (assumption)
 3771  done

 3772  Each of the three identical commands finds an implication and proves its
 3773  antecedent by assumption. The first one finds P −→Q and P, concluding Q ; the
 3774  second one concludes R and the third one concludes S. The final step matches
 3775  the assumption S with the goal to be proved.
 3776  Suffixing a method with a plus sign (+ ) expresses one or more repetitions:
=== PAGE 111 ===

 3777  5.17 Managing Large Proofs 101

 3778  lemma "[[P−→Q; Q−→R; R −→S; P ]] =⇒ S"
 3779  by (drule mp, assumption)+
 3780  Using by takes care of the final use of assumption. The new proof is more

 3781  concise. It is also more general: the repetitive method works for a chain of

 3782  implications having any length, not just three.

 3783  Choice is another control structure. Separating two methods by a vertical
 3784  bar (| ) gives the effect of applying the first method, and if that fails, trying

 3785  the second. It can be combined with repetition, when the choice must be

 3786  made over and over again. Here is a chain of implications in which most of

 3787  the antecedents are proved by assumption, but one is proved by arithmetic:
 3788  lemma "[[Q−→R; P−→Q; x<5−→P; Suc x < 5]] =⇒ R"
 3789  by (drule mp, (assumption|arith))+
 3790  The arith method can prove x < 5 from x+1 < 5, but it cannot duplicate the
 3791  effect of assumption. Therefore, we combine these methods using the choice

 3792  operator.
 3793  A postfixed question mark (? ) expresses zero or one repetitions of a

 3794  method. It can also be viewed as the choice between executing a method

 3795  and doing nothing. It is useless at top level but can be valuable within other
 3796  control structures; for example, (m+)? performs zero or more repetitions of

 3797  method m.

 3798  5.17.2 Subgoal Numbering

 3799  Another problem in large proofs is contending with huge subgoals or many

 3800  subgoals. Induction can produce a proof state that looks like this:
 3801  1. bigsubgoal1
 3802  2. bigsubgoal2
 3803  3. bigsubgoal3
 3804  4. bigsubgoal4
 3805  5. bigsubgoal5
 3806  6. bigsubgoal6
 3807  If each bigsubgoal is 15 lines or so, the proof state will be too big to scroll

 3808  through. By default, Isabelle displays at most 10 subgoals. The pr command

 3809  lets you change this limit:
 3810  pr 2
 3811  1. bigsubgoal1
 3812  2. bigsubgoal2
 3813  A total of 6 subgoals...

 3814  All methods apply to the first subgoal. Sometimes, not only in a large

 3815  proof, you may want to focus on some other subgoal. Then you should try

 3816  the commands defer or prefer.

 3817  In the following example, the first subgoal looks hard, while the others
 3818  look as if blast alone could prove them:
=== PAGE 112 ===

 3819  102 5. The Rules of the Game

 3820  1. hard
 3821  2. ¬ ¬ P =⇒ P
 3822  3. Q =⇒ Q

 3823  The defer command moves the first subgoal into the last position.
 3824  defer 1
 3825  1. ¬ ¬ P =⇒ P
 3826  2. Q =⇒ Q
 3827  3. hard
 3828  Now we apply blast repeatedly to the easy subgoals:
 3829  apply blast+
 3830  1. hard

 3831  Using defer, we have cleared away the trivial parts of the proof so that we

 3832  can devote attention to the difficult part.

 3833  The prefer command moves the specified subgoal into the first position.

 3834  For example, if you suspect that one of your subgoals is invalid (not a theo-

 3835  rem), then you should investigate that subgoal first. If it cannot be proved,

 3836  then there is no point in proving the other subgoals.
 3837  1. ok1
 3838  2. ok2
 3839  3. doubtful

 3840  We decide to work on the third subgoal.
 3841  prefer 3
 3842  1. doubtful
 3843  2. ok1
 3844  3. ok2
 3845  If we manage to prove doubtful, then we can work on the other subgoals,

 3846  confident that we are not wasting our time. Finally we revise the proof script

 3847  to remove the prefer command, since we needed it only to focus our ex-

 3848  ploration. The previous example is different: its use of defer stops trivial

 3849  subgoals from cluttering the rest of the proof. Even there, we should consider
 3850  proving hard as a preliminary lemma. Always seek ways to streamline your

 3851  proofs.

 3852  Summary:
 3853  – the control structures +, ? and | help express complicated proofs

 3854  – the pr command can limit the number of subgoals to display

 3855  – the defer and prefer commands move a subgoal to the last or first position
 3856  Exercise 5.17.1 Explain the use of ? and + in this proof.
 3857  lemma "[[P∧Q−→R; P−→Q; P]] =⇒ R"
 3858  by (drule mp, (intro conjI)?, assumption+)+
=== PAGE 113 ===

 3859  5.18 Proving the Correctness of Euclid's Algorithm 103

 3860  5.18 Proving the Correctness of Euclid's Algorithm

 3861  A brief development will demonstrate the techniques of this chapter, including blast applied with additional rules. We shall also see case_tac used to

 3862  perform a Boolean case split.
 3863  Let us prove that gcd computes the greatest common divisor of its two arguments. We use induction: gcd.induct is the induction rule returned by fun.

 3864  We simplify using rules proved in Sect. 3.5.3, since rewriting by the definition
 3865  of gcd can loop.
 3866  lemma gcd dvd both: "(gcd m n dvd m) ∧ (gcd m n dvd n)"

 3867  The induction formula must be a conjunction. In the inductive step, each

 3868  conjunct establishes the other.
 3869  1. Vm n. (n 6= 0 =⇒
 3870  gcd n (m mod n) dvd n ∧
 3871  gcd n (m mod n) dvd m mod n) =⇒
 3872  gcd m n dvd m ∧ gcd m n dvd n

 3873  The conditional induction hypothesis suggests doing a case analysis on
 3874  n=0. We apply case_tac with type bool — and not with a datatype, as we have
 3875  done until now. Since nat is a datatype, we could have written case_tac n
 3876  instead of case_tac "n=0". However, the definition of gcd makes a Boolean

 3877  decision:
 3878  "gcd m n = (if n=0 then m else gcd n (m mod n))"

 3879  Proofs about a function frequently follow the function's definition, so we

 3880  perform case analysis over the same formula.
 3881  apply (case_tac "n=0")
 3882  1. Vm n. [[n 6= 0 =⇒
 3883  gcd n (m mod n) dvd n ∧ gcd n (m mod n) dvd m mod n;
 3884  n = 0 ]]
 3885  =⇒ gcd m n dvd m ∧ gcd m n dvd n
 3886  2. Vm n. [[n 6= 0 =⇒
 3887  gcd n (m mod n) dvd n ∧ gcd n (m mod n) dvd m mod n;
 3888  n 6= 0]]
 3889  =⇒ gcd m n dvd m ∧ gcd m n dvd n

 3890  Simplification leaves one subgoal:
 3891  apply (simp_all)
 3892  1. Vm n. [[gcd n (m mod n) dvd n ∧ gcd n (m mod n) dvd m mod n;
 3893  0 < n ]]
 3894  =⇒ gcd n (m mod n) dvd m
 3895  Here, we can use blast. One of the assumptions, the induction hypothesis, is

 3896  a conjunction. The two divides relationships it asserts are enough to prove

 3897  the conclusion, for we have the following theorem at our disposal:
 3898  [[?k dvd (?m mod ?n); ?k dvd ?n]] =⇒ ?k dvd ?m (dvd_mod_imp_dvd)
=== PAGE 114 ===

 3899  104 5. The Rules of the Game

 3900  This theorem can be applied in various ways. As an introduction rule, it
 3901  would cause backward chaining from the conclusion (namely ?k dvd ?m) to

 3902  the two premises, which also involve the divides relation. This process does

 3903  not look promising and could easily loop. More sensible is to apply the rule
 3904  in the forward direction; each step would eliminate an occurrence of the mod

 3905  symbol, so the process must terminate.
 3906  apply (blast dest: dvd_mod_imp_dvd)
 3907  done
 3908  Attaching the dest attribute to dvd_mod_imp_dvd tells blast to use it as de-

 3909  struction rule; that is, in the forward direction.

 3910  We have proved a conjunction. Now, let us give names to each of the two

 3911  halves:
 3912  lemmas gcd_dvd1 [iff] = gcd_dvd_both [THEN conjunct1]
 3913  lemmas gcd_dvd2 [iff] = gcd_dvd_both [THEN conjunct2]
 3914  Here we see lemmas used with the iff attribute, which supplies the new
 3915  theorems to the classical reasoner and the simplifier. Recall that THEN is
 3916  frequently used with destruction rules; THEN conjunct1 extracts the first half
 3917  of a conjunctive theorem. Given gcd_dvd_both it yields
 3918  gcd ?m1 ?n1 dvd ?m1
 3919  The variable names ?m1 and ?n1 arise because Isabelle renames schematic

 3920  variables to prevent clashes. The second lemmas declaration yields
 3921  gcd ?m1 ?n1 dvd ?n1

 3922  To complete the verification of the gcd function, we must prove that it

 3923  returns the greatest of all the common divisors of its arguments. The proof

 3924  is by induction, case analysis and simplification.
 3925  lemma gcd greatest [rule format]:
 3926  "k dvd m −→ k dvd n −→ k dvd gcd m n"
 3927  The goal is expressed using HOL implication, −→, because the induction
 3928  affects the two preconditions. The directive rule_format tells Isabelle to replace each −→ by =⇒ before storing the eventual theorem. This directive can

 3929  also remove outer universal quantifiers, converting the theorem into the usual
 3930  format for inference rules. It can replace any series of applications of THEN to
 3931  the rules mp and spec. We did not have to write this:
 3932  lemma gcd_greatest [THEN mp, THEN mp]:
 3933  "k dvd m −→ k dvd n −→ k dvd gcd m n"
 3934  Because we are again reasoning about gcd, we perform the same induction

 3935  and case analysis as in the previous proof:
 3936  1. Vm n. [[n 6= 0 =⇒
 3937  k dvd n −→ k dvd m mod n −→ k dvd gcd n (m mod n);
 3938  n = 0 ]]
=== PAGE 115 ===

 3939  5.18 Proving the Correctness of Euclid's Algorithm 105

 3940  =⇒ k dvd m −→ k dvd n −→ k dvd gcd m n
 3941  2. Vm n. [[n 6= 0 =⇒
 3942  k dvd n −→ k dvd m mod n −→ k dvd gcd n (m mod n);
 3943  n 6= 0]]
 3944  =⇒ k dvd m −→ k dvd n −→ k dvd gcd m n

 3945  Simplification proves both subgoals.
 3946  apply (simp_all add: dvd_mod)
 3947  done
 3948  In the first, where n=0, the implication becomes trivial: k dvd gcd m n goes
 3949  to k dvd m. The second subgoal is proved by an unfolding of gcd, using this

 3950  rule about divides:
 3951  [[?f dvd ?m; ?f dvd ?n ]] =⇒ ?f dvd ?m mod ?n (dvd_mod)

 3952  The facts proved above can be summarized as a single logical equivalence.
 3953  This step gives us a chance to see another application of blast.
 3954  theorem gcd greatest iff [iff]:
 3955  "(k dvd gcd m n) = (k dvd m ∧ k dvd n)"
 3956  by (blast intro!: gcd_greatest intro: dvd_trans)
 3957  This theorem concisely expresses the correctness of the gcd function. We state
 3958  it with the iff attribute so that Isabelle can use it to remove some occurrences of gcd. The theorem has a one-line proof using blast supplied with
 3959  two additional introduction rules. The exclamation mark (intro ! ) signifies

 3960  safe rules, which are applied aggressively. Rules given without the exclama-

 3961  tion mark are applied reluctantly and their uses can be undone if the search

 3962  backtracks. Here the unsafe rule expresses transitivity of the divides relation:
 3963  [[?m dvd ?n; ?n dvd ?p ]] =⇒ ?m dvd ?p (dvd_trans)
 3964  Applying dvd_trans as an introduction rule entails a risk of looping, for it

 3965  multiplies occurrences of the divides symbol. However, this proof relies on
 3966  transitivity reasoning. The rule gcd greatest is safe to apply aggressively
 3967  because it yields simpler subgoals. The proof implicitly uses gcd_dvd1 and
 3968  gcd_dvd2 as safe rules, because they were declared using iff.
=== PAGE 117 ===

 3969  6. Sets, Functions and Relations

 3970  This chapter describes the formalization of typed set theory, which is the

 3971  basis of much else in HOL. For example, an inductive definition yields a set,

 3972  and the abstract theories of relations regard a relation as a set of pairs. The

 3973  chapter introduces the well-known constants such as union and intersection,

 3974  as well as the main operations on relations, such as converse, composition

 3975  and transitive closure. Functions are also covered. They are not sets in HOL,

 3976  but many of their properties concern sets: the range of a function is a set,

 3977  and the inverse image of a function maps sets to sets.

 3978  This chapter will be useful to anybody who plans to develop a substantial

 3979  proof. Sets are convenient for formalizing computer science concepts such

 3980  as grammars, logical calculi and state transition systems. Isabelle can prove

 3981  many statements involving sets automatically.

 3982  This chapter ends with a case study concerning model checking for the

 3983  temporal logic CTL. Most of the other examples are simple. The chapter

 3984  presents a small selection of built-in theorems in order to point out some key

 3985  properties of the various constants and to introduce you to the notation.

 3986  Natural deduction rules are provided for the set theory constants, but

 3987  they are seldom used directly, so only a few are presented here.

 3988  6.1 Sets

 3989  HOL's set theory should not be confused with traditional, untyped set theory,

 3990  in which everything is a set. Our sets are typed. In a given set, all elements
 3991  have the same type, say τ, and the set itself has type τ set .

 3992  We begin with intersection, union and complement. In addition to

 3993  the membership relation, there is a symbol for its negation. These points

 3994  can be seen below.

 3995  Here are the natural deduction rules for intersection. Note the resemblance

 3996  to those for conjunction.
 3997  [[c ∈ A; c ∈ B ]] =⇒ c ∈ A ∩ B (IntI)
 3998  c ∈ A ∩ B =⇒ c ∈ A (IntD1)
 3999  c ∈ A ∩ B =⇒ c ∈ B (IntD2)

 4000  Here are two of the many installed theorems concerning set complement.

 4001  Note that it is denoted by a minus sign.
=== PAGE 118 ===

 4002  108 6. Sets, Functions and Relations
 4003  (c ∈ - A) = (c ∈/ A) (Compl_iff)
 4004  - (A ∪ B) = - A ∩ - B (Compl_Un)

 4005  Set difference is the intersection of a set with the complement of another

 4006  set. Here we also see the syntax for the empty set and for the universal set.
