 4007  A ∩ (B - A) = {} (Diff_disjoint)
 4008  A ∪ - A = UNIV (Compl_partition)

 4009  The subset relation holds between two sets just if every element of one

 4010  is also an element of the other. This relation is reflexive. These are its natural

 4011  deduction rules:
 4012  (Vx. x ∈ A =⇒ x ∈ B) =⇒ A ⊆ B (subsetI)
 4013  [[A ⊆ B; c ∈ A]] =⇒ c ∈ B (subsetD)
 4014  In harder proofs, you may need to apply subsetD giving a specific term for c.
 4015  However, blast can instantly prove facts such as this one:
 4016  (A ∪ B ⊆ C) = (A ⊆ C ∧ B ⊆ C) (Un_subset_iff)

 4017  Here is another example, also proved automatically:
 4018  lemma "(A ⊆ -B) = (B ⊆ -A)"
 4019  by blast

 4020  This is the same example using ascii syntax, illustrating a pitfall:
 4021  lemma "(A <= -B) = (B <= -A)"

 4022  The proof fails. It is not a statement about sets, due to overloading; the
 4023  relation symbol <= can be any relation, not just subset. In this general form,

 4024  the statement is not valid. Putting in a type constraint forces the variables

 4025  to denote sets, allowing the proof to succeed:
 4026  lemma "((A:: 'a set) <= -B) = (B <= -A)"
 4027  Section 8.3 below describes overloading. Incidentally, A ⊆ -B asserts that the
 4028  sets A and B are disjoint.

 4029  Two sets are equal if they contain the same elements. This is the principle

 4030  of extensionality for sets.
 4031  (Vx. (x ∈ A) = (x ∈ B)) =⇒ A = B (set_ext)

 4032  Extensionality can be expressed as A = B ⇐⇒ (A ⊆ B) ∧ (B ⊆ A). The

 4033  following rules express both directions of this equivalence. Proving a set equation using equalityI allows the two inclusions to be proved independently.
 4034  [[A ⊆ B; B ⊆ A ]] =⇒ A = B (equalityI)
 4035  [[A = B; [[A ⊆ B; B ⊆ A]] =⇒ P ]] =⇒ P (equalityE)
=== PAGE 119 ===

 4036  6.1 Sets 109

 4037  6.1.1 Finite Set Notation
 4038  Finite sets are expressed using the constant insert , which is a form of union:
 4039  insert a A = {a} ∪ A (insert_is_Un)
 4040  The finite set expression {a,b} abbreviates insert a (insert b {}). Many

 4041  facts about finite sets can be proved automatically:
 4042  lemma "{a,b} ∪ {c,d} = {a,b,c,d}"
 4043  by blast

 4044  Not everything that we would like to prove is valid. Consider this attempt:
 4045  lemma "{a,b} ∩ {b,c} = {b}"
 4046  apply auto
 4047  The proof fails, leaving the subgoal b=c. To see why it fails, consider a correct

 4048  version:
 4049  lemma "{a,b} ∩ {b,c} = (if a=c then {a,b} else {b})"
 4050  apply simp
 4051  by blast

 4052  Our mistake was to suppose that the various items were distinct. Another
 4053  remark: this proof uses two methods, namely simp and blast . Calling simp
 4054  eliminates the if -then -else expression, which blast cannot break down. The
 4055  combined methods (namely force and auto ) can prove this fact in one step.

 4056  6.1.2 Set Comprehension
 4057  The set comprehension {x. P} expresses the set of all elements that satisfy the
 4058  predicate P. Two laws describe the relationship between set comprehension

 4059  and the membership relation:
 4060  (a ∈ {x. P x}) = P a (mem_Collect_eq)
 4061  {x. x ∈ A} = A (Collect_mem_eq)

 4062  Facts such as these have trivial proofs:
 4063  lemma "{x. P x ∨ x ∈ A} = {x. P x} ∪ A"
 4064  lemma "{x. P x −→ Q x} = -{x. P x} ∪ {x. Q x}"

 4065  Isabelle has a general syntax for comprehension, which is best described

 4066  through an example:
 4067  lemma "{p*q | p q. p∈prime ∧ q∈prime} =
 4068  {z. ∃ p q. z = p*q ∧ p∈prime ∧ q∈prime}"

 4069  The left and right hand sides of this equation are identical. The syntax used

 4070  in the left-hand side abbreviates the right-hand side: in this case, all numbers

 4071  that are the product of two primes. The syntax provides a neat way of ex-

 4072  pressing any set given by an expression built up from variables under specific
=== PAGE 120 ===

 4073  110 6. Sets, Functions and Relations

 4074  constraints. The drawback is that it hides the true form of the expression,

 4075  with its existential quantifiers.

 4076  Remark. We do not need sets at all. They are essentially equivalent to

 4077  predicate variables, which are allowed in higher-order logic. The main benefit
 4078  of sets is their notation; we can write x ∈A and {z. P} where predicates would
 4079  require writing A(x) and λz. P.

 4080  6.1.3 Binding Operators

 4081  Universal and existential quantifications may range over sets, with the obvi-

 4082  ous meaning. Here are the natural deduction rules for the bounded universal
 4083  quantifier. Occasionally you will need to apply bspec with an explicit instantiation of the variable x:
 4084  (Vx. x ∈ A =⇒ P x) =⇒ ∀ x ∈A. P x (ballI)
 4085  [[∀ x ∈A. P x; x ∈ A ]] =⇒ P x (bspec)

 4086  Dually, here are the natural deduction rules for the bounded existential quantifier. You may need to apply bexI with an explicit instantiation:
 4087  [[P x; x ∈ A ]] =⇒ ∃ x∈A. P x (bexI)
 4088  [[∃ x ∈A. P x; Vx. [[x ∈ A; P x ]] =⇒ Q ]] =⇒ Q (bexE)

 4089  Unions can be formed over the values of a given set. The syntax is
 4090  S x∈A. B or UN x:A. B in ascii. Indexed union satisfies this basic law:
 4091  (b ∈ (S x ∈A. B x)) = (∃ x ∈A. b ∈ B x) (UN_iff)

 4092  It has two natural deduction rules similar to those for the existential quantifier. Sometimes UN_I must be applied explicitly:
 4093  [[a ∈ A; b ∈ B a ]] =⇒ b ∈ (S x ∈A. B x) (UN_I)
 4094  [[b ∈ ( S x∈A. B x); Vx. [[x ∈ A; b ∈ B x ]] =⇒ R]] =⇒ R (UN_E)

 4095  The following built-in abbreviation (see Sect. 4.1.4) lets us express the union

 4096  over a type:
 4097  ( S x. B x) ≡ ( S x∈UNIV. B x)
 4098  We may also express the union of a set of sets, written Union C in ascii:
 4099  (A ∈ S C) = ( ∃ X∈C. A ∈ X) (Union_iff)

 4100  Intersections are treated dually, although they seem to be used less often
 4101  than unions. The syntax below would be INT x: A. B and Inter C in ascii.

 4102  Among others, these theorems are available:
 4103  (b ∈ (T x ∈A. B x)) = (∀ x ∈A. b ∈ B x) (INT_iff)
 4104  (A ∈ T C) = ( ∀ X∈C. A ∈ X) (Inter_iff)

 4105  Isabelle uses logical equivalences such as those above in automatic proof.

 4106  Unions, intersections and so forth are not simply replaced by their definitions.

 4107  Instead, membership tests are simplified. For example, x ∈ A ∪B is replaced

 4108  by x ∈ A ∨ x ∈ B.
=== PAGE 121 ===

 4109  6.2 Functions 111

 4110  The internal form of a comprehension involves the constant Collect ,

 4111  which occasionally appears when a goal or theorem is displayed. For example,
 4112  Collect P is the same term as {x. P x}. The same thing can happen with
 4113  quantifiers: All P is ∀ x. P x and Ex P is ∃ x. P x ; also Ball A P is ∀ x ∈A. P
 4114  x and Bex A P is ∃ x ∈A. P x. For indexed unions and intersections, you may
 4115  see the constants UNION and INTER . The internal constant for εx.P(x) is Eps .

 4116  We have only scratched the surface of Isabelle/HOL's set theory, which

 4117  provides hundreds of theorems for your use.

 4118  6.1.4 Finiteness and Cardinality

 4119  The predicate finite holds of all finite sets. Isabelle/HOL includes many
 4120  familiar theorems about finiteness and cardinality (card ). For example, we

 4121  have theorems concerning the cardinalities of unions, intersections and the

 4122  powerset:
 4123  [[finite A; finite B ]]
 4124  =⇒ card A + card B = card (A ∪ B) + card (A ∩ B) (card_Un_Int)

 4125  finite A =⇒ card (Pow A) = 2 ˆ card A (card_Pow)
 4126  finite A =⇒
 4127  card {B. B ⊆ A ∧ card B = k} = card A choose k (n_subsets)

 4128  Writing |A| as n, the last of these theorems says that the number of k-element
 4129  subsets of A is n .
 4130  k

 4131  6.2 Functions

 4132  This section describes a few concepts that involve functions. Some of the more

 4133  important theorems are given along with the names. A few sample proofs

 4134  appear. Unlike with set theory, however, we cannot simply state lemmas and
 4135  expect them to be proved using blast.

 4136  6.2.1 Function Basics

 4137  Two functions are equal if they yield equal results given equal arguments.

 4138  This is the principle of extensionality for functions:
 4139  (Vx. f x = g x) =⇒ f = g (ext)

 4140  Function update is useful for modelling machine states. It has the obvi-

 4141  ous definition and many useful facts are proved about it. In particular, the

 4142  following equation is installed as a simplification rule:
 4143  (f(x:=y)) z = (if z = x then y else f z) (fun_upd_apply)
=== PAGE 122 ===

 4144  112 6. Sets, Functions and Relations

 4145  Two syntactic points must be noted. In (f(x:=y)) z we are applying an

 4146  updated function to an argument; the outer parentheses are essential. A series

 4147  of two or more updates can be abbreviated as shown on the left-hand side of

 4148  this theorem:
 4149  f(x:=y, x:=z) = f(x:=z) (fun_upd_upd)
 4150  Note also that we can write f(x:=z) with only one pair of parentheses when

 4151  it is not being applied to an argument.

 4152  The identity function and function composition are defined:
 4153  id ≡ λx. x (id_def)
 4154  f ◦ g ≡ λx. f (g x) (o_def)

 4155  Many familiar theorems concerning the identity and composition are proved.

 4156  For example, we have the associativity of composition:
 4157  f ◦ (g ◦ h) = f ◦ g ◦ h (o_assoc)

 4158  6.2.2 Injections, Surjections, Bijections

 4159  A function may be injective, surjective or bijective:
 4160  inj_on f A ≡ ∀ x∈A. ∀ y ∈A. f x = f y −→ x = y (inj_on_def)
 4161  surj f ≡ ∀ y. ∃ x. y = f x (surj_def)
 4162  bij f ≡ inj f ∧ surj f (bij_def)
 4163  The second argument of inj_on lets us express that a function is injective over

 4164  a given set. This refinement is useful in higher-order logic, where functions are

 4165  total; in some cases, a function's natural domain is a subset of its domain type.
 4166  Writing inj f abbreviates inj_on f UNIV, for when f is injective everywhere.
 4167  The operator inv expresses the inverse of a function. In general the

 4168  inverse may not be well behaved. We have the usual laws, such as these:
 4169  inj f =⇒ inv f (f x) = x (inv_f_f)
 4170  surj f =⇒ f (inv f y) = y (surj_f_inv_f)
 4171  bij f =⇒ inv (inv f) = f (inv_inv_eq)

 4172  Theorems involving these concepts can be hard to prove. The following

 4173  example is easy, but it cannot be proved automatically. To begin with, we

 4174  need a law that relates the equality of functions to equality over all arguments:
 4175  (f = g) = (∀ x. f x = g x) (fun_eq_iff)

 4176  This is just a restatement of extensionality. Our lemma states that an injec-

 4177  tion can be cancelled from the left side of function composition:
 4178  lemma "inj f =⇒ (f o g = f o h) = (g = h)"
 4179  apply (simp add: fun_eq_iff inj_on_def)
 4180  apply auto
 4181  done

 4182  The first step of the proof invokes extensionality and the definitions of

 4183  injectiveness and composition. It leaves one subgoal:
=== PAGE 123 ===

 4184  6.3 Relations 113
 4185  1. ∀ x y. f x = f y −→ x = y =⇒
 4186  ( ∀ x. f (g x) = f (h x)) = (∀ x. g x = h x)
 4187  This can be proved using the auto method.

 4188  6.2.3 Function Image

 4189  The image of a set under a function is a most useful notion. It has the

 4190  obvious definition:
 4191  f ' A ≡ {y. ∃ x ∈A. y = f x} (image_def)

 4192  Here are some of the many facts proved about image:
 4193  (f ◦ g) ' r = f ' g ' r (image_compose)
 4194  f'(A ∪ B) = f'A ∪ f'B (image_Un)
 4195  inj f =⇒ f'(A ∩ B) = f'A ∩ f'B (image_Int)

 4196  Laws involving image can often be proved automatically. Here are two

 4197  examples, illustrating connections with indexed union and with the general

 4198  syntax for comprehension:
 4199  lemma "f'A ∪ g'A = ( S x∈A. {f x, g x})"
 4200  lemma "f ' {(x,y). P x y} = {f(x,y) | x y. P x y}"

 4201  A function's range is the set of values that the function can take on. It

 4202  is, in fact, the image of the universal set under that function. There is no
 4203  constant range. Instead, range abbreviates an application of image to UNIV :
 4204  range f f'UNIV
 4205  Few theorems are proved specifically for range ; in most cases, you should

 4206  look for a more general theorem concerning images.

 4207  Inverse image is also useful. It is defined as follows:
 4208  f -' B ≡ {x. f x ∈ B} (vimage_def)

 4209  This is one of the facts proved about it:
 4210  f -' (- A) = - f -' A (vimage_Compl)

 4211  6.3 Relations

 4212  A relation is a set of pairs. As such, the set operations apply to them. For

 4213  instance, we may form the union of two relations. Other primitives are defined

 4214  specifically for relations.
=== PAGE 124 ===

 4215  114 6. Sets, Functions and Relations

 4216  6.3.1 Relation Basics

 4217  The identity relation, also known as equality, has the obvious definition:
 4218  Id ≡ {p. ∃ x. p = (x,x)} (Id_def)
 4219  Composition of relations (the infix O ) is also available:
 4220  r O s = {(x,z). ∃ y. (x,y) ∈ s ∧ (y,z) ∈ r} (relcomp_unfold)

 4221  This is one of the many lemmas proved about these concepts:
 4222  R O Id = R (R_O_Id)

 4223  Composition is monotonic, as are most of the primitives appearing in this

 4224  chapter. We have many theorems similar to the following one:
 4225  [[r' ⊆ r; s' ⊆ s ]] =⇒ r' O s' ⊆ r O s (relcomp_mono)

 4226  The converse or inverse of a relation exchanges the roles of the two
 4227  operands. We use the postfix notation r−1 or rˆ-1 in ASCII.
 4228  ((a,b) ∈ r −1 ) = ((b,a) ∈ r) (converse_iff)

 4229  Here is a typical law proved about converse and composition:
 4230  (r O s) −1 = s−1 O r −1 (converse_relcomp)

 4231  The image of a set under a relation is defined analogously to image under

 4232  a function:
 4233  (b ∈ r '' A) = (∃ x ∈A. (x,b) ∈ r) (Image_iff)

 4234  It satisfies many similar laws.

 4235  The domain and range of a relation are defined in the standard way:
 4236  (a ∈ Domain r) = (∃ y. (a,y) ∈ r) (Domain_iff)
 4237  (a ∈ Range r) = ( ∃ y. (y,a) ∈ r) (Range_iff)

 4238  Iterated composition of a relation is available. The notation overloads that

 4239  of exponentiation. Two simplification rules are installed:
 4240  R ˆ 0 = Id
 4241  R ˆ Suc n = R O Rˆn

 4242  6.3.2 The Reflexive and Transitive Closure
 4243  The reflexive and transitive closure of the relation r is written with a
 4244  postfix syntax. In ASCII we write rˆ* and in symbol notation r ∗. It is the

 4245  least solution of the equation
 4246  r∗ = Id ∪ (r O r ∗) (rtrancl_unfold)

 4247  Among its basic properties are three that serve as introduction rules:
 4248  (a, a) ∈ r ∗ (rtrancl_refl)
 4249  p ∈ r =⇒ p ∈ r∗ (r_into_rtrancl)
 4250  [[(a,b) ∈ r ∗; (b,c) ∈ r∗]] =⇒ (a,c) ∈ r∗ (rtrancl_trans)
=== PAGE 125 ===

 4251  6.3 Relations 115

 4252  Induction over the reflexive transitive closure is available:
 4253  [[(a, b) ∈ r ∗; P a; Vy z. [[(a, y) ∈ r ∗; (y, z) ∈ r; P y ]] =⇒ P z]]
 4254  =⇒ P b (rtrancl_induct)

 4255  Idempotence is one of the laws proved about the reflexive transitive closure:
 4256  (r∗) ∗ = r ∗ (rtrancl_idemp)
 4257  The transitive closure is similar. The ASCII syntax is rˆ+. It has two

 4258  introduction rules:
 4259  p ∈ r =⇒ p ∈ r+ (r_into_trancl)
 4260  [[(a, b) ∈ r +; (b, c) ∈ r+]] =⇒ (a, c) ∈ r + (trancl_trans)

 4261  The induction rule resembles the one shown above. A typical lemma states

 4262  that transitive closure commutes with the converse operator:
 4263  (r−1) + = (r +) −1 (trancl_converse)

 4264  6.3.3 A Sample Proof

 4265  The reflexive transitive closure also commutes with the converse operator. Let

 4266  us examine the proof. Each direction of the equivalence is proved separately.

 4267  The two proofs are almost identical. Here is the first one:
 4268  lemma rtrancl_converseD: "(x,y) ∈ (r −1)∗ =⇒ (y,x) ∈ r ∗"
 4269  apply (erule rtrancl_induct)
 4270  apply (rule rtrancl_refl)
 4271  apply (blast intro: rtrancl_trans)
 4272  done

 4273  The first step of the proof applies induction, leaving these subgoals:
 4274  1. (x, x) ∈ r ∗
 4275  2. Vy z. [[(x,y) ∈ (r−1) ∗; (y,z) ∈ r −1; (y,x) ∈ r ∗]]
 4276  =⇒ (z,x) ∈ r ∗

 4277  The first subgoal is trivial by reflexivity. The second follows by first eliminating the converse operator, yielding the assumption (z,y) ∈ r, and then

 4278  applying the introduction rules shown above. The same proof script handles

 4279  the other direction:
 4280  lemma rtrancl_converseI: "(y,x) ∈ r ∗ =⇒ (x,y) ∈ (r −1) ∗"
 4281  apply (erule rtrancl_induct)
 4282  apply (rule rtrancl_refl)
 4283  apply (blast intro: rtrancl_trans)
 4284  done

 4285  Finally, we combine the two lemmas to prove the desired equation:
 4286  lemma rtrancl_converse: "(r −1 )∗ = (r∗) −1"
 4287  by (auto intro: rtrancl_converseI dest: rtrancl_converseD)
=== PAGE 126 ===

 4288  116 6. Sets, Functions and Relations
 4289  !!This trivial proof requires auto rather than blast because of a subtle issue
 4290  involving ordered pairs. Here is a subgoal that arises internally after the rules
 4291  equalityI and subsetI have been applied:
 4292  1. Vx. x ∈ (r −1)∗ =⇒ x ∈ (r ∗) −1
 4293  We cannot apply rtrancl_converseD . It refers to ordered pairs, while x is a variable of product type. The simp and blast methods can do nothing, so let us try
 4294  clarify :
 4295  1. Va b. (a,b) ∈ (r−1) ∗ =⇒ (b,a) ∈ r∗
 4296  Now that x has been replaced by the pair (a,b), we can proceed. Other methods
 4297  that split variables in this way are force, auto, fast and best. Section 8.1 will
 4298  discuss proof techniques for ordered pairs in more detail.

 4299  6.4 Well-Founded Relations and Induction

 4300  A well-founded relation captures the notion of a terminating process. Com-

 4301  plex recursive functions definitions must specify a well-founded relation that

 4302  justifies their termination [17]. Most of the forms of induction found in math-

 4303  ematics are merely special cases of induction over a well-founded relation.

 4304  Intuitively, the relation ≺ is well-founded if it admits no infinite de-

 4305  scending chains
 4306  · · · ≺ a2 ≺ a1 ≺ a0.

 4307  Well-foundedness can be hard to show. The various formulations are all com-

 4308  plicated. However, often a relation is well-founded by construction. HOL pro-

 4309  vides theorems concerning ways of constructing a well-founded relation. The
 4310  most familiar way is to specify a measure function f into the natural numbers, when x ≺ y ⇐⇒ f x < f y; we write this particular relation as
 4311  measure f.
 4312  !!You may want to skip the rest of this section until you need to perform a
 4313  complex recursive function definition or induction. The induction rule returned
 4314  by fun is good enough for most purposes. We use an explicit well-founded induction
 4315  only in Sect. 9.2.4.
 4316  Isabelle/HOL declares less_than as a relation object, that is, a set of

 4317  pairs of natural numbers. Two theorems tell us that this relation behaves as

 4318  expected and that it is well-founded:
 4319  ((x,y) ∈ less_than) = (x < y) (less_than_iff)
 4320  wf less_than (wf_less_than)

 4321  The notion of measure generalizes to the inverse image of a relation.
 4322  Given a relation r and a function f, we express a new relation using f as a

 4323  measure. An infinite descending chain on this new relation would give rise
 4324  to an infinite descending chain on r. Isabelle/HOL defines this concept and

 4325  proves a theorem stating that it preserves well-foundedness:
=== PAGE 127 ===

 4326  6.5 Fixed Point Operators 117

 4327  inv_image r f ≡ {(x,y). (f x, f y) ∈ r} (inv_image_def)
 4328  wf r =⇒ wf (inv_image r f) (wf_inv_image)
 4329  A measure function involves the natural numbers. The relation measure
 4330  size justifies primitive recursion and structural induction over a datatype.
 4331  Isabelle/HOL defines measure as shown:
 4332  measure ≡ inv_image less_than (measure_def)
 4333  wf (measure f) (wf_measure)

 4334  Of the other constructions, the most important is the lexicographic

 4335  product of two relations. It expresses the standard dictionary ordering over
 4336  pairs. We write ra <*lex*> rb, where ra and rb are the two operands. The

 4337  lexicographic product satisfies the usual definition and it preserves well-

 4338  foundedness:
 4339  ra <*lex*> rb ≡
 4340  {((a,b),(a',b')). (a,a') ∈ ra ∨
 4341  a=a' ∧ (b,b') ∈ rb} (lex_prod_def)
 4342  [[wf ra; wf rb]] =⇒ wf (ra <*lex*> rb) (wf_lex_prod)

 4343  The multiset ordering, useful for hard termination proofs, is available

 4344  in the Library [4]. Baader and Nipkow [3, Sect. 2.5] discuss it.

 4345  Induction comes in many forms, including traditional mathematical in-

 4346  duction, structural induction on lists and induction on size. All are instances

 4347  of the following rule, for a suitable well-founded relation ≺:

 4348  [∀y. y ≺ x → P(y)]

 4349  ..

 4350  ..

 4351  P(x)

 4352  P(a)

 4353  To show P(a) for a particular term a, it suffices to show P(x) for arbi-

 4354  trary x under the assumption that P(y) holds for y ≺ x. Intuitively, the

 4355  well-foundedness of ≺ ensures that the chains of reasoning are finite.

 4356  In Isabelle, the induction rule is expressed like this:
 4357  [[wf r; Vx. ∀ y. (y,x) ∈ r −→ P y =⇒ P x ]] =⇒ P a (wf_induct)
 4358  Here wf r expresses that the relation r is well-founded.

 4359  Many familiar induction principles are instances of this rule. For exam-

 4360  ple, the predecessor relation on the natural numbers is well-founded; induc-

 4361  tion over it is mathematical induction. The "tail of" relation on lists is well-

 4362  founded; induction over it is structural induction.

 4363  6.5 Fixed Point Operators

 4364  Fixed point operators define sets recursively. They are invoked implicitly

 4365  when making an inductive definition, as discussed in Chap. 7 below. However,
=== PAGE 128 ===

 4366  118 6. Sets, Functions and Relations

 4367  they can be used directly, too. The least or strongest fixed point yields an

 4368  inductive definition; the greatest or weakest fixed point yields a coinductive

 4369  definition. Mathematicians may wish to note that the existence of these fixed

 4370  points is guaranteed by the Knaster-Tarski theorem.
 4371  !!Casual readers should skip the rest of this section. We use fixed point operators
 4372  only in Sect. ??.

 4373  The theory applies only to monotonic functions. Isabelle's definition of

 4374  monotone is overloaded over all orderings:
 4375  mono f ≡ ∀ A B. A ≤ B −→ f A ≤ f B (mono_def)

 4376  For fixed point operators, the ordering will be the subset relation: if A ⊆ B

 4377  then we expect f (A) ⊆ f(B). In addition to its definition, monotonicity has

 4378  the obvious introduction and destruction rules:
 4379  (VA B. A ≤ B =⇒ f A ≤ f B) =⇒ mono f (monoI)
 4380  [[mono f; A ≤ B ]] =⇒ f A ≤ f B (monoD)

 4381  The most important properties of the least fixed point are that it is a

 4382  fixed point and that it enjoys an induction rule:
 4383  mono f =⇒ lfp f = f (lfp f) (lfp_unfold)
 4384  [[a ∈ lfp f; mono f;
 4385  Vx. x ∈ f (lfp f ∩ {x. P x}) =⇒ P x ]] =⇒ P a (lfp_induct)

 4386  The induction rule shown above is more convenient than the basic one derived
 4387  from the minimality of lfp . Observe that both theorems demand mono f as

 4388  a premise.

 4389  The greatest fixed point is similar, but it has a coinduction rule:
 4390  mono f =⇒ gfp f = f (gfp f) (gfp_unfold)
 4391  [[mono f; a ∈ X; X ⊆ f (X ∪ gfp f) ]] =⇒ a ∈ gfp f (coinduct)

 4392  A bisimulation is perhaps the best-known concept defined as a greatest

 4393  fixed point. Exhibiting a bisimulation to prove the equality of two agents in

 4394  a process algebra is an example of coinduction. The coinduction rule can be

 4395  strengthened in various ways.

 4396  6.5.1 Propositional Dynamic Logic — PDL

 4397  The formulae of PDL are built up from atomic propositions via negation and
 4398  conjunction and the two temporal connectives AX and EF . Since formulae are
 4399  essentially syntax trees, they are naturally modelled as a datatype:1
 4400  datatype formula = Atom "atom"
 4401  | Neg formula
 4402  | And formula formula
 4403  1 The customary definition of PDL [12] looks quite different from ours, but the
 4404  two are easily shown to be equivalent.
=== PAGE 129 ===

 4405  6.5 Fixed Point Operators 119

 4406  | AX formula
 4407  | EF formula

 4408  This resembles the boolean expression case study in Sect. 2.5.6. A validity

 4409  relation between states and formulae specifies the semantics. The syntax annotation allows us to write s |= f instead of valid s f. The definition is by

 4410  recursion over the syntax:
 4411  primrec valid :: "state ⇒ formula ⇒ bool" ("(_ |= _)" [80,80] 80)
 4412  where
 4413  "s |= Atom a = (a ∈ L s)" |
 4414  "s |= Neg f = (¬(s |= f))" |
 4415  "s |= And f g = (s |= f ∧ s |= g)" |
 4416  "s |= AX f = (∀ t. (s,t) ∈ M −→ t |= f)" |
 4417  "s |= EF f = (∃ t. (s,t) ∈ M ∗ ∧ t |= f)"

 4418  The first three equations should be self-explanatory. The temporal formula
 4419  AX f means that f is true in All neXt states whereas EF f means that there
 4420  Exists some Future state in which f is true. The future is expressed via ∗,

 4421  the reflexive transitive closure. Because of reflexivity, the future includes the

 4422  present.

 4423  Now we come to the model checker itself. It maps a formula into the set

 4424  of states where the formula is true. It too is defined by recursion over the

 4425  syntax:
 4426  primrec mc :: "formula ⇒ state set" where
 4427  "mc(Atom a) = {s. a ∈ L s}" |
 4428  "mc(Neg f) = -mc f" |
 4429  "mc(And f g) = mc f ∩ mc g" |
 4430  "mc(AX f) = {s. ∀ t. (s,t) ∈ M −→ t ∈ mc f}" |
 4431  "mc(EF f) = lfp(λT. mc f ∪ (M −1 '' T))"
 4432  Only the equation for EF deserves some comments. Remember that the postfix
 4433  −1 and the infix '' are predefined and denote the converse of a relation and
 4434  the image of a set under a relation. Thus M −1 '' T is the set of all predecessors
 4435  of T and the least fixed point (lfp) of λT. mc f ∪ M −1 '' T is the least set
 4436  T containing mc f and all predecessors of T. If you find it hard to see that
 4437  mc (EF f) contains exactly those states from which there is a path to a state
 4438  where f is true, do not worry — this will be proved in a moment.
 4439  First we prove monotonicity of the function inside lfp in order to make

 4440  sure it really has a least fixed point.
 4441  lemma mono_ef: "mono(λT. A ∪ (M −1 '' T))"
 4442  apply(rule monoI)
 4443  apply blast
 4444  done
 4445  Now we can relate model checking and semantics. For the EF case we need a

 4446  separate lemma:
 4447  lemma EF_lemma:
 4448  "lfp(λT. A ∪ (M −1 '' T)) = {s. ∃ t. (s,t) ∈ M∗ ∧ t ∈ A}"
=== PAGE 130 ===

 4449  120 6. Sets, Functions and Relations

 4450  The equality is proved in the canonical fashion by proving that each set

 4451  includes the other; the inclusion is shown pointwise:
 4452  apply(rule equalityI)
 4453  apply(rule subsetI)
 4454  apply(simp)

 4455  Simplification leaves us with the following first subgoal
 4456  1. Vs. s ∈ lfp (λT. A ∪ M −1 '' T) =⇒ ∃t. (s, t) ∈ M ∗ ∧ t ∈ A
 4457  A total of 2 subgoals...
 4458  which is proved by lfp -induction:
 4459  apply(erule lfp_induct_set)
 4460  apply(rule mono_ef)
 4461  apply(simp)

 4462  Having disposed of the monotonicity subgoal, simplification leaves us with

 4463  the following goal:
 4464  1. Vx. x ∈ A ∨
 4465  x ∈ M −1 '' (lfp (...) ∩ {x. ∃ t. (x, t) ∈ M∗ ∧ t ∈ A})
 4466  =⇒ ∃ t. (x, t) ∈ M ∗ ∧ t ∈ A
 4467  It is proved by blast, using the transitivity of M ∗.
 4468  apply(blast intro: rtrancl_trans)

 4469  We now return to the second set inclusion subgoal, which is again proved

 4470  pointwise:
 4471  apply(rule subsetI)
 4472  apply(simp, clarify)

 4473  After simplification and clarification we are left with
 4474  1. Vx t. [[(x, t) ∈ M ∗; t ∈ A ]] =⇒ x ∈ lfp (λT. A ∪ M −1 '' T)
 4475  This goal is proved by induction on (s, t) ∈ M ∗. But since the model
 4476  checker works backwards (from t to s ), we cannot use the induction theorem
 4477  rtrancl_induct: it works in the forward direction. Fortunately the converse
 4478  induction theorem converse_rtrancl_induct already exists:
 4479  [[(a, b) ∈ r ∗; P b;
 4480  Vy z. [[(y, z) ∈ r; (z, b) ∈ r ∗; P z ]] =⇒ P y]]
 4481  =⇒ P a
 4482  It says that if (a, b) ∈ r∗ and we know P b then we can infer P a provided
 4483  each step backwards from a predecessor z of b preserves P.
 4484  apply(erule converse_rtrancl_induct)

 4485  The base case
 4486  1. Vx t. t ∈ A =⇒ t ∈ lfp (λT. A ∪ M −1 '' T)
 4487  A total of 2 subgoals...
=== PAGE 131 ===

 4488  6.5 Fixed Point Operators 121
 4489  is solved by unrolling lfp once
 4490  apply(subst lfp_unfold[OF mono_ef])
 4491  1. Vx t. t ∈ A =⇒ t ∈ A ∪ M−1 '' lfp (λT. A ∪ M −1 '' T)
 4492  A total of 2 subgoals...

 4493  and disposing of the resulting trivial subgoal automatically:
 4494  apply(blast)

 4495  The proof of the induction step is identical to the one for the base case:
 4496  apply(subst lfp_unfold[OF mono_ef])
 4497  apply(blast)
 4498  done

 4499  The main theorem is proved in the familiar manner: induction followed
 4500  by auto augmented with the lemma as a simplification rule.
 4501  theorem "mc f = {s. s |= f}"
 4502  apply(induct_tac f)
 4503  apply(auto simp add: EF_lemma)
 4504  done
 4505  Exercise 6.5.1 AX has a dual operator EN ("there exists a next state such
 4506  that")2 with the intended semantics
 4507  s |= EN f = (∃ t. (s, t) ∈ M ∧ t |= f)
 4508  Fortunately, EN f can already be expressed as a PDL formula. How?
 4509  Show that the semantics for EF satisfies the following recursion equation:
 4510  s |= EF f = (s |= f ∨ s |= EN (EF f))

 4511  6.5.2 Computation Tree Logic — CTL

 4512  The semantics of PDL only needs reflexive transitive closure. Let us be ad-

 4513  venturous and introduce a more expressive temporal operator. We extend the
 4514  datatype formula by a new constructor
 4515  | AF formula

 4516  which stands for "Always in the Future": on all infinite paths, at some point

 4517  the formula holds. Formalizing the notion of an infinite path is easy in HOL:
 4518  it is simply a function from nat to state.
 4519  definition Paths :: "state ⇒ (nat ⇒ state)set" where
 4520  "Paths s ≡ {p. s = p 0 ∧ (∀ i. (p i, p(i+1)) ∈ M)}"
 4521  This definition allows a succinct statement of the semantics of AF: 3
 4522  2 We cannot use the customary EX : it is reserved as the ascii-equivalent of ∃ .
 4523  3 Do not be misled: neither datatypes nor recursive functions can be extended
 4524  by new constructors or equations. This is just a trick of the presentation (see
 4525  Sect. 4.2.5). In reality one has to define a new datatype and a new function.
=== PAGE 132 ===

 4526  122 6. Sets, Functions and Relations

 4527  "s |= AF f = (∀ p ∈ Paths s. ∃ i. p i |= f)"
 4528  Model checking AF involves a function which is just complicated enough to

 4529  warrant a separate definition:
 4530  definition af :: "state set ⇒ state set ⇒ state set" where
 4531  "af A T ≡ A ∪ {s. ∀ t. (s, t) ∈ M −→ t ∈ T}"
 4532  Now we define mc (AF f) as the least set T that includes mc f and all states
 4533  all of whose direct successors are in T:
 4534  "mc(AF f) = lfp(af(mc f))"
 4535  Because af is monotone in its second argument (and also its first, but that
 4536  is irrelevant), af A has a least fixed point:
 4537  lemma mono_af: "mono(af A)"
 4538  apply(simp add: mono_def af_def)
 4539  apply blast
 4540  done
 4541  All we need to prove now is mc (AF f) = {s. s |= AF f}, which states
 4542  that mc and |= agree for AF. This time we prove the two inclusions separately,

 4543  starting with the easy one:
 4544  theorem AF_lemma1: "lfp(af A) ⊆ {s. ∀ p ∈ Paths s. ∃ i. p i ∈ A}"
 4545  In contrast to the analogous proof for EF, and just for a change, we do not

 4546  use fixed point induction. Park-induction, named after David Park, is weaker

 4547  but sufficient for this proof:
 4548  f S ≤ S =⇒ lfp f ≤ S (lfp_lowerbound)
 4549  The instance of the premise f S ⊆ S is proved pointwise, a decision that auto

 4550  takes for us:
 4551  apply(rule lfp_lowerbound)
 4552  apply(auto simp add: af_def Paths_def)

 4553  1. Vp. [[∀ t. (p 0, t) ∈ M −→
 4554  (∀ p. t = p 0 ∧ (∀ i. (p i, p (Suc i)) ∈ M) −→
 4555  (∃ i. p i ∈ A));
 4556  ∀ i. (p i, p (Suc i)) ∈ M ]]
 4557  =⇒ ∃ i. p i ∈ A
 4558  In this remaining case, we set t to p 1. The rest is automatic, which is
 4559  surprising because it involves finding the instantiation λi. p (i + 1) for ∀ p.
 4560  apply(erule_tac x = "p 1" in allE)
 4561  apply(auto)
 4562  done
 4563  The opposite inclusion is proved by contradiction: if some state s is not in
 4564  lfp (af A), then we can construct an infinite A-avoiding path starting from s.
 4565  The reason is that by unfolding lfp we find that if s is not in lfp (af A),
 4566  then s is not in A and there is a direct successor of s that is again not in
=== PAGE 133 ===

 4567  6.5 Fixed Point Operators 123

 4568  lfp (af A). Iterating this argument yields the promised infinite A -avoiding

 4569  path. Let us formalize this sketch.

 4570  The one-step argument in the sketch above is proved by a variant of

 4571  contraposition:
 4572  lemma not_in_lfp_afD:
 4573  "s ∈/ lfp(af A) =⇒ s ∈/ A ∧ (∃ t. (s,t) ∈ M ∧ t ∈/ lfp(af A))"
 4574  apply(erule contrapos_np)
 4575  apply(subst lfp_unfold[OF mono_af])
 4576  apply(simp add: af_def)
 4577  done
 4578  We assume the negation of the conclusion and prove s ∈ lfp (af A). Unfolding lfp once and simplifying with the definition of af finishes the proof.

 4579  Now we iterate this process. The following construction of the desired
 4580  path is parameterized by a predicate Q that should hold along the path:
 4581  primrec path :: "state ⇒ (state ⇒ bool) ⇒ (nat ⇒ state)" where
 4582  "path s Q 0 = s" |
 4583  "path s Q (Suc n) = (SOME t. (path s Q n,t) ∈ M ∧ Q t)"
 4584  Element n + 1 on this path is some arbitrary successor t of element n such
 4585  that Q t holds. Remember that SOME t. R t is some arbitrary but fixed t
 4586  such that R t holds (see Sect. 5.10). Of course, such a t need not exist, but
 4587  that is of no concern to us since we will only use path when a suitable t does

 4588  exist.
 4589  Let us show that if each state s that satisfies Q has a successor that again
 4590  satisfies Q, then there exists an infinite Q -path:
 4591  lemma infinity_lemma:
 4592  " [[ Q s; ∀ s. Q s −→ (∃ t. (s,t) ∈ M ∧ Q t) ]] =⇒
 4593  ∃ p ∈Paths s. ∀ i. Q(p i)"

 4594  First we rephrase the conclusion slightly because we need to prove simultaneously both the path property and the fact that Q holds:
 4595  apply(subgoal_tac
 4596  " ∃p. s = p 0 ∧ (∀ i::nat. (p i, p(i+1)) ∈ M ∧ Q(p i))")

 4597  From this proposition the original goal follows easily:
 4598  apply(simp add: Paths_def, blast)
 4599  The new subgoal is proved by providing the witness path s Q for p :
 4600  apply(rule_tac x = "path s Q" in exI)
 4601  apply(clarsimp)

 4602  After simplification and clarification, the subgoal has the following form:
 4603  1. Vi. [[Q s; ∀ s. Q s −→ (∃ t. (s, t) ∈ M ∧ Q t)]]
 4604  =⇒ (path s Q i, SOME t. (path s Q i, t) ∈ M ∧ Q t) ∈ M ∧
 4605  Q (path s Q i)
 4606  It invites a proof by induction on i:
=== PAGE 134 ===

 4607  124 6. Sets, Functions and Relations

 4608  apply(induct_tac i)
 4609  apply(simp)

 4610  After simplification, the base case boils down to
 4611  1. [[Q s; ∀ s. Q s −→ (∃ t. (s, t) ∈ M ∧ Q t)]]
 4612  =⇒ (s, SOME t. (s, t) ∈ M ∧ Q t) ∈ M
 4613  A total of 2 subgoals...
 4614  The conclusion looks exceedingly trivial: after all, t is chosen such that (s,
 4615  t) ∈ M holds. However, we first have to show that such a t actually exists!
 4616  This reasoning is embodied in the theorem someI2_ex :
 4617  [[∃ a. ?P a; Vx. ?P x =⇒ ?Q x ]] =⇒ ?Q (SOME x. ?P x)
 4618  When we apply this theorem as an introduction rule, ?P x becomes (s, x) ∈
 4619  M ∧ Q x and ?Q x becomes (s, x) ∈ M and we have to prove two subgoals:
 4620  ∃ a. (s, a) ∈ M ∧ Q a, which follows from the assumptions, and (s, x) ∈ M
 4621  ∧ Q x =⇒ (s, x) ∈ M, which is trivial. Thus it is not surprising that fast

 4622  can prove the base case quickly:
 4623  apply(fast intro: someI2_ex)
 4624  What is worth noting here is that we have used fast rather than blast. The
 4625  reason is that blast would fail because it cannot cope with someI2_ex : unify-

 4626  ing its conclusion with the current subgoal is non-trivial because of the nested
 4627  schematic variables. For efficiency reasons blast does not even attempt such
 4628  unifications. Although fast can in principle cope with complicated unifica-

 4629  tion problems, in practice the number of unifiers arising is often prohibitive

 4630  and the offending rule may need to be applied explicitly rather than auto-

 4631  matically. This is what happens in the step case.

 4632  The induction step is similar, but more involved, because now we face
 4633  nested occurrences of SOME. As a result, fast is no longer able to solve the
 4634  subgoal and we apply someI2_ex by hand. We merely show the proof com-

 4635  mands but do not describe the details:
 4636  apply(simp)
 4637  apply(rule someI2_ex)
 4638  apply(blast)
 4639  apply(rule someI2_ex)
 4640  apply(blast)
 4641  apply(blast)
 4642  done
 4643  Function path has fulfilled its purpose now and can be forgotten. It was
 4644  merely defined to provide the witness in the proof of the infinity_lemma.

 4645  Aficionados of minimal proofs might like to know that we could have given

 4646  the witness without having to define a new function: the term
 4647  rec_nat s (λn t. SOME u. (t, u) ∈ M ∧ Q u)
 4648  is extensionally equal to path s Q, where rec_nat is the predefined primitive
 4649  recursor on nat.
=== PAGE 135 ===

 4650  6.5 Fixed Point Operators 125

 4651  At last we can prove the opposite direction of AF_lemma1:
 4652  theorem AF_lemma2: "{s. ∀ p ∈ Paths s. ∃ i. p i ∈ A} ⊆ lfp(af A)"

 4653  The proof is again pointwise and then by contraposition:
 4654  apply(rule subsetI)
 4655  apply(erule contrapos_pp)
 4656  apply simp

 4657  1. Vx. x ∈/ lfp (af A) =⇒ ∃ p∈Paths x. ∀ i. p i ∈/ A
 4658  Applying the infinity_lemma as a destruction rule leaves two subgoals, the
 4659  second premise of infinity_lemma and the original subgoal:
 4660  apply(drule infinity_lemma)

 4661  1. Vx. ∀ s. s ∈/ lfp (af A) −→ (∃ t. (s, t) ∈ M ∧ t ∈/ lfp (af A))
 4662  2. Vx. ∃ p∈Paths x. ∀ i. p i ∈/ lfp (af A) =⇒
 4663  ∃ p∈Paths x. ∀ i. p i ∈/ A

 4664  Both are solved automatically:
 4665  apply(auto dest: not_in_lfp_afD)
 4666  done

 4667  If you find these proofs too complicated, we recommend that you read

 4668  Sect. 9.2.4, where we show how inductive definitions lead to simpler argu-

 4669  ments.

 4670  The main theorem is proved as for PDL, except that we also derive the
 4671  necessary equality lfp(af A) = ... by combining AF_lemma1 and AF_lemma2

 4672  on the spot:
 4673  theorem "mc f = {s. s |= f}"
 4674  apply(induct_tac f)
 4675  apply(auto simp add: EF_lemma equalityI[OF AF_lemma1 AF_lemma2])
 4676  done

 4677  The language defined above is not quite CTL. The latter also includes an
 4678  until-operator EU f g with semantics "there Exists a path where f is true
 4679  Until g becomes true". We need an auxiliary function:
 4680  primrec
 4681  until:: "state set ⇒ state set ⇒ state ⇒ state list ⇒ bool" where
 4682  "until A B s [] = (s ∈ B)" |
 4683  "until A B s (t#p) = (s ∈ A ∧ (s,t) ∈ M ∧ until A B t p)"
 4684  Expressing the semantics of EU is now straightforward:
 4685  s |= EU f g = (∃ p. until {t. t |= f} {t. t |= g} s p)
 4686  Note that EU is not definable in terms of the other operators!
 4687  Model checking EU is again a least fixed point construction:
 4688  mc(EU f g) = lfp(λT. mc g ∪ mc f ∩ (M−1 '' T))
=== PAGE 136 ===

 4689  126 6. Sets, Functions and Relations

 4690  Exercise 6.5.2 Extend the datatype of formulae by the above until operator

 4691  and prove the equivalence between semantics and model checking, i.e. that
 4692  mc (EU f g) = {s. s |= EU f g}

 4693  For more CTL exercises see, for example, Huth and Ryan [15].

 4694  Let us close this section with a few words about the executability of our

 4695  model checkers. It is clear that if all sets are finite, they can be represented as
 4696  lists and the usual set operations are easily implemented. Only lfp requires
 4697  a little thought. Fortunately, theory While_Combinator in the Library [4] pro-

 4698  vides a theorem stating that in the case of finite sets and a monotone function F, the value of lfp F can be computed by iterated application of F to {}

 4699  until a fixed point is reached. It is actually possible to generate executable

 4700  functional programs from HOL definitions, but that is beyond the scope of

 4701  the tutorial.
=== PAGE 137 ===

 4702  7. Inductively Defined Sets

 4703  This chapter is dedicated to the most important definition principle after

 4704  recursive functions and datatypes: inductively defined sets.

 4705  We start with a simple example: the set of even numbers. A slightly

 4706  more complicated example, the reflexive transitive closure, is the subject

 4707  of Sect. 7.2. In particular, some standard induction heuristics are discussed.

 4708  Advanced forms of inductive definitions are discussed in Sect. 7.3. To demon-

 4709  strate the versatility of inductive definitions, the chapter closes with a case

 4710  study from the realm of context-free grammars. The first two sections are

 4711  required reading for anybody interested in mathematical modelling.
 4712  !!Predicates can also be defined inductively. See Sect. 7.1.7.

 4713  7.1 The Set of Even Numbers

 4714  The set of even numbers can be inductively defined as the least set containing

 4715  0 and closed under the operation +2. Obviously, even can also be expressed
 4716  using the divides relation (dvd ). We shall prove below that the two formula-

 4717  tions coincide. On the way we shall examine the primary means of reasoning

 4718  about inductively defined sets: rule induction.

 4719  7.1.1 Making an Inductive Definition
 4720  Using inductive set, we declare the constant even to be a set of natural

 4721  numbers with the desired properties.
 4722  inductive set even :: "nat set" where
 4723  zero[intro!]: "0 ∈ even" |
 4724  step[intro!]: "n ∈ even =⇒ (Suc (Suc n)) ∈ even"

 4725  An inductive definition consists of introduction rules. The first one above

 4726  states that 0 is even; the second states that if n is even, then so is n + 2.
 4727  Given this declaration, Isabelle generates a fixed point definition for Even.even

 4728  and proves theorems about it, thus following the definitional approach (see

 4729  Sect. 2.8). These theorems include the introduction rules specified in the dec-

 4730  laration, an elimination rule for case analysis and an induction rule. We can
=== PAGE 138 ===

 4731  128 7. Inductively Defined Sets

 4732  refer to these theorems by automatically-generated names. Here are two ex-

 4733  amples:
 4734  0 ∈ Even.even (even.zero)
 4735  n ∈ Even.even =⇒ Suc (Suc n) ∈ Even.even (even.step)

 4736  The introduction rules can be given attributes. Here both rules are specified as intro!, directing the classical reasoner to apply them aggressively.
 4737  Obviously, regarding 0 as even is safe. The step rule is also safe because

 4738  n + 2 is even if and only if n is even. We prove this equivalence later.

 4739  7.1.2 Using Introduction Rules

 4740  Our first lemma states that numbers of the form 2×k are even. Introduction

 4741  rules are used to show that specific values belong to the inductive set. Such

 4742  proofs typically involve induction, perhaps over some other inductive set.
 4743  lemma two_times_even[intro!]: "2*k ∈ even"
 4744  apply (induct_tac k)
 4745  apply auto
 4746  done
 4747  The first step is induction on the natural number k, which leaves two subgoals:
 4748  1. 2 * 0 ∈ Even.even
 4749  2. Vn. 2 * n ∈ Even.even =⇒ 2 * Suc n ∈ Even.even
 4750  Here auto simplifies both subgoals so that they match the introduction rules,

 4751  which are then applied automatically.

 4752  Our ultimate goal is to prove the equivalence between the traditional
 4753  definition of even (using the divides relation) and our inductive definition.

 4754  One direction of this equivalence is immediate by the lemma just proved,
 4755  whose intro! attribute ensures it is applied automatically.
 4756  lemma dvd_imp_even: "2 dvd n =⇒ n ∈ even"
 4757  by (auto simp add: dvd_def)

 4758  7.1.3 Rule Induction

 4759  From the definition of the set Even.even, Isabelle has generated an induction

 4760  rule:
 4761  [[x ∈ Even.even; P 0;
 4762  Vn. [[n ∈ Even.even; P n]]
 4763  =⇒ P (Suc (Suc n))]]
 4764  =⇒ P x (even.induct)
 4765  A property P holds for every even number provided it holds for 0 and is closed
 4766  under the operation Suc(Suc ·). Then P is closed under the introduction rules
 4767  for Even.even, which is the least set closed under those rules. This type of

 4768  inductive argument is called rule induction.
=== PAGE 139 ===

 4769  7.1 The Set of Even Numbers 129
 4770  Apart from the double application of Suc, the induction rule above resem-

 4771  bles the familiar mathematical induction, which indeed is an instance of rule

 4772  induction; the natural numbers can be defined inductively to be the least set
 4773  containing 0 and closed under Suc.

 4774  Induction is the usual way of proving a property of the elements of an
 4775  inductively defined set. Let us prove that all members of the set Even.even

 4776  are multiples of two.
 4777  lemma even_imp_dvd: "n ∈ even =⇒ 2 dvd n"
 4778  We begin by applying induction. Note that even.induct has the form of
 4779  an elimination rule, so we use the method erule. We get two subgoals:
 4780  apply (erule even.induct)
 4781  1. semiring_parity_class.even 0
 4782  2. Vn. [[n ∈ Even.even; semiring_parity_class.even n ]]
 4783  =⇒ semiring_parity_class.even (Suc (Suc n))
 4784  We unfold the definition of dvd in both subgoals, proving the first one and

 4785  simplifying the second:
 4786  apply (simp_all add: dvd_def)
 4787  1. Vn. [[n ∈ Even.even; ∃ k. n = 2 * k]] =⇒ ∃ k. Suc (Suc n) = 2 * k

 4788  The next command eliminates the existential quantifier from the assumption
 4789  and replaces n by 2 * k.
 4790  apply clarify
 4791  1. Vn k. 2 * k ∈ Even.even =⇒ ∃ ka. Suc (Suc (2 * k)) = 2 * ka
 4792  To conclude, we tell Isabelle that the desired value is Suc k. With this hint,
 4793  the subgoal falls to simp.
 4794  apply (rule_tac x = "Suc k" in exI, simp)

 4795  Combining the previous two results yields our objective, the equivalence
 4796  relating Even.even and dvd.
 4797  theorem even_iff_dvd: "(n ∈ even) = (2 dvd n)"
 4798  by (blast intro: dvd_imp_even even_imp_dvd)

 4799  7.1.4 Generalization and Rule Induction

 4800  Before applying induction, we typically must generalize the induction for-

 4801  mula. With rule induction, the required generalization can be hard to find

 4802  and sometimes requires a complete reformulation of the problem. In this ex-

 4803  ample, our first attempt uses the obvious statement of the result. It fails:
 4804  lemma "Suc (Suc n) ∈ even =⇒ n ∈ even"
=== PAGE 140 ===

 4805  130 7. Inductively Defined Sets

 4806  apply (erule even.induct)
 4807  oops
 4808  Rule induction finds no occurrences of Suc (Suc n) in the conclusion,
 4809  which it therefore leaves unchanged. (Look at even.induct to see why this

 4810  happens.) We have these subgoals:
 4811  1. n ∈ Even.even
 4812  2. Vna. [[na ∈ Even.even; n ∈ Even.even]] =⇒ n ∈ Even.even

 4813  The first one is hopeless. Rule induction on a non-variable term discards

 4814  information, and usually fails. How to deal with such situations in general is

 4815  described in Sect. 9.2.1 below. In the current case the solution is easy because

 4816  we have the necessary inverse, subtraction:
 4817  lemma even_imp_even_minus_2: "n ∈ even =⇒ n - 2 ∈ even"
 4818  apply (erule even.induct)
 4819  apply auto
 4820  done

 4821  This lemma is trivially inductive. Here are the subgoals:
 4822  1. 0 - 2 ∈ Even.even
 4823  2. Vn. [[n ∈ Even.even; n - 2 ∈ Even.even]] =⇒ Suc (Suc n) - 2 ∈ Even.even
 4824  The first is trivial because 0 - 2 simplifies to 0, which is even. The second is
 4825  trivial too: Suc (Suc n) - 2 simplifies to n, matching the assumption.

 4826  Using our lemma, we can easily prove the result we originally wanted:
 4827  lemma Suc_Suc_even_imp_even: "Suc (Suc n) ∈ even =⇒ n ∈ even"
 4828  by (drule even_imp_even_minus_2, simp)
 4829  We have just proved the converse of the introduction rule even.step. This
 4830  suggests proving the following equivalence. We give it the iff attribute be-

 4831  cause of its obvious value for simplification.
 4832  lemma [iff]: "((Suc (Suc n)) ∈ even) = (n ∈ even)"
 4833  by (blast dest: Suc_Suc_even_imp_even)

 4834  7.1.5 Rule Inversion

 4835  Case analysis on an inductive definition is called rule inversion. It is fre-

 4836  quently used in proofs about operational semantics. It can be highly effective

 4837  when it is applied automatically. Let us look at how rule inversion is done in

 4838  Isabelle/HOL.
 4839  Recall that Even.even is the minimal set closed under these two rules:
 4840  0 ∈ Even.even
 4841  n ∈ Even.even =⇒ Suc (Suc n) ∈ Even.even
 4842  Minimality means that Even.even contains only the elements that these rules
 4843  force it to contain. If we are told that a belongs to Even.even then there are
 4844  only two possibilities. Either a is 0 or else a has the form Suc (Suc n), for
 4845  some suitable n that belongs to Even.even. That is the gist of the cases rule,

 4846  which Isabelle proves for us when it accepts an inductive definition:
=== PAGE 141 ===

 4847  7.1 The Set of Even Numbers 131

 4848  [[a ∈ Even.even; a = 0 =⇒ P;
 4849  Vn. [[a = Suc (Suc n); n ∈ Even.even]]
 4850  =⇒ P]]
 4851  =⇒ P (even.cases)

 4852  This general rule is less useful than instances of it for specific patterns. For
 4853  example, if a has the form Suc (Suc n) then the first case becomes irrelevant, while the second case tells us that n belongs to Even.even. Isabelle will

 4854  generate this instance for us:
 4855  inductive cases Suc_Suc_cases [elim!]: "Suc(Suc n) ∈ even"
 4856  The inductive cases command generates an instance of the cases rule

 4857  for the supplied pattern and gives it the supplied name:
 4858  [[Suc (Suc n) ∈ Even.even; n ∈ Even.even =⇒ P ]] =⇒ P (Suc_Suc_cases)
 4859  Applying this as an elimination rule yields one case where even.cases would

 4860  yield two. Rule inversion works well when the conclusions of the introduction rules involve datatype constructors like Suc and # (list "cons"); freeness

 4861  reasoning discards all but one or two cases.
 4862  In the inductive cases command we supplied an attribute, elim!, in-

 4863  dicating that this elimination rule can be applied aggressively. The original
 4864  cases rule would loop if used in that manner because the pattern a matches

 4865  everything.
 4866  The rule Suc_Suc_cases is equivalent to the following implication:
 4867  Suc (Suc n) ∈ Even.even =⇒ n ∈ Even.even

 4868  Just above we devoted some effort to reaching precisely this result. Yet we

 4869  could have obtained it by a one-line declaration, dispensing with the lemma
 4870  even_imp_even_minus_2. This example also justifies the terminology rule inversion: the new rule inverts the introduction rule even.step. In general, a

 4871  rule can be inverted when the set of elements it introduces is disjoint from

 4872  those of the other introduction rules.
 4873  For one-off applications of rule inversion, use the ind_cases method. Here

 4874  is an example:
 4875  apply (ind_cases "Suc(Suc n) ∈ even")
 4876  The specified instance of the cases rule is generated, then applied as an

 4877  elimination rule.
 4878  To summarize, every inductive definition produces a cases rule. The inductive cases command stores an instance of the cases rule for a given
 4879  pattern. Within a proof, the ind_cases method applies an instance of the
 4880  cases rule.

 4881  The even numbers example has shown how inductive definitions can be

 4882  used. Later examples will show that they are actually worth using.
=== PAGE 142 ===

 4883  132 7. Inductively Defined Sets

 4884  7.1.6 Mutually Inductive Definitions

 4885  Just as there are datatypes defined by mutual recursion, there are sets defined

 4886  by mutual induction. As a trivial example we consider the even and odd

 4887  natural numbers:
 4888  inductive set
 4889  Even :: "nat set" and
 4890  Odd :: "nat set"
 4891  where
 4892  zero: "0 ∈ Even"
 4893  | EvenI: "n ∈ Odd =⇒ Suc n ∈ Even"
 4894  | OddI: "n ∈ Even =⇒ Suc n ∈ Odd"

 4895  The mutually inductive definition of multiple sets is no different from that

 4896  of a single set, except for induction: just as for mutually recursive datatypes,

 4897  induction needs to involve all the simultaneously defined sets. In the above
 4898  case, the induction rule is called Even_Odd.induct (simply concatenate the

 4899  names of the sets involved) and has the conclusion
 4900  (?x ∈ Even −→ ?P ?x) ∧ (?y ∈ Odd −→ ?Q ?y)

 4901  If we want to prove that all even numbers are divisible by two, we have

 4902  to generalize the statement as follows:
 4903  lemma "(m ∈ Even −→ 2 dvd m) ∧ (n ∈ Odd −→ 2 dvd (Suc n))"

 4904  The proof is by rule induction. Because of the form of the induction theorem,
 4905  it is applied by rule rather than erule as for ordinary inductive definitions:
 4906  apply(rule Even_Odd.induct)

 4907  1. even 0
 4908  2. Vn. [[n ∈ Odd; even (Suc n)]] =⇒ even (Suc n)
 4909  3. Vn. [[n ∈ Even; even n]] =⇒ even (Suc (Suc n))

 4910  The first two subgoals are proved by simplification and the final one can

 4911  be proved in the same manner as in Sect. 7.1.3 where the same subgoal was

 4912  encountered before. We do not show the proof script.

 4913  7.1.7 Inductively Defined Predicates

 4914  Instead of a set of even numbers one can also define a predicate on nat:
 4915  inductive evn :: "nat ⇒ bool" where
 4916  zero: "evn 0" |
 4917  step: "evn n =⇒ evn(Suc(Suc n))"

 4918  Everything works as before, except that you write inductive instead of inductive set and evn n instead of n ∈ Even. When defining an n-ary rela-

 4919  tion as a predicate, it is recommended to curry the predicate: its type should
=== PAGE 143 ===

 4920  7.2 The Reflexive Transitive Closure 133

 4921  be τ 1 ⇒ . . . ⇒ τ n ⇒ bool rather than τ 1 × . . . × τ n ⇒ bool. The curried

 4922  version facilitates inductions.

 4923  When should you choose sets and when predicates? If you intend to com-

 4924  bine your notion with set theoretic notation, define it as an inductive set. If
 4925  not, define it as an inductive predicate, thus avoiding the ∈ notation. But

 4926  note that predicates of more than one argument cannot be combined with
 4927  the usual set theoretic operators: P ∪ Q is not well-typed if P, Q :: τ1 ⇒
 4928  τ2 ⇒ bool, you have to write λx y. P x y ∧ Q x y instead.

 4929  7.2 The Reflexive Transitive Closure

 4930  An inductive definition may accept parameters, so it can express functions

 4931  that yield sets. Relations too can be defined inductively, since they are just

 4932  sets of pairs. A perfect example is the function that maps a relation to its

 4933  reflexive transitive closure. This concept was already introduced in Sect. 6.3,
 4934  where the operator ∗ was defined as a least fixed point because inductive

 4935  definitions were not yet available. But now they are:
 4936  inductive set
 4937  rtc :: "('a × 'a)set ⇒ ('a × 'a)set" ("_*" [1000] 999)
 4938  for r :: "('a × 'a)set"
 4939  where
 4940  rtc_refl[iff]: "(x,x) ∈ r*"
 4941  | rtc_step: "[[ (x,y) ∈ r; (y,z) ∈ r* ]] =⇒ (x,z) ∈ r*"
 4942  The function rtc is annotated with concrete syntax: instead of rtc r we can
 4943  write r*. The actual definition consists of two rules. Reflexivity is obvious and
 4944  is immediately given the iff attribute to increase automation. The second
 4945  rule, rtc_step, says that we can always add one more r -step to the left.
 4946  Although we could make rtc_step an introduction rule, this is dangerous: the

 4947  recursion in the second premise slows down and may even kill the automatic

 4948  tactics.

 4949  The above definition of the concept of reflexive transitive closure may be

 4950  sufficiently intuitive but it is certainly not the only possible one: for a start,

 4951  it does not even mention transitivity. The rest of this section is devoted to

 4952  proving that it is equivalent to the standard definition. We start with a simple

 4953  lemma:
 4954  lemma [intro]: "(x,y) ∈ r =⇒ (x,y) ∈ r*"
 4955  by(blast intro: rtc_step)

 4956  Although the lemma itself is an unremarkable consequence of the basic rules,

 4957  it has the advantage that it can be declared an introduction rule without
 4958  the danger of killing the automatic tactics because r* occurs only in the

 4959  conclusion and not in the premise. Thus some proofs that would otherwise
 4960  need rtc_step can now be found automatically. The proof also shows that
 4961  blast is able to handle rtc_step. But some of the other automatic tactics are
=== PAGE 144 ===

 4962  134 7. Inductively Defined Sets

 4963  more sensitive, and even blast can be lead astray in the presence of large

 4964  numbers of rules.
 4965  To prove transitivity, we need rule induction, i.e. theorem rtc.induct:
 4966  [[(?x1.0, ?x2.0) ∈ ?r*; Vx. ?P x x;
 4967  Vx y z. [[(x, y) ∈ ?r; (y, z) ∈ ?r*; ?P y z]] =⇒ ?P x z ]]
 4968  =⇒ ?P ?x1.0 ?x2.0
 4969  It says that ?P holds for an arbitrary pair (?x1.0, ?x2.0) ∈ ?r* if ?P is
 4970  preserved by all rules of the inductive definition, i.e. if ?P holds for the con-

 4971  clusion provided it holds for the premises. In general, rule induction for an
 4972  n-ary inductive relation R expects a premise of the form (x1, . . . ,xn) ∈ R.

 4973  Now we turn to the inductive proof of transitivity:
 4974  lemma rtc_trans: "[[ (x,y) ∈ r*; (y,z) ∈ r* ]] =⇒ (x,z) ∈ r*"
 4975  apply(erule rtc.induct)

 4976  Unfortunately, even the base case is a problem:
 4977  1. Vx. (y, z) ∈ r* =⇒ (x, z) ∈ r*
 4978  A total of 2 subgoals...

 4979  We have to abandon this proof attempt. To understand what is going on,
 4980  let us look again at rtc.induct. In the above application of erule, the first
 4981  premise of rtc.induct is unified with the first suitable assumption, which is
 4982  (x, y) ∈ r* rather than (y, z) ∈ r*. Although that is what we want, it is

 4983  merely due to the order in which the assumptions occur in the subgoal, which
 4984  it is not good practice to rely on. As a result, ?xb becomes x, ?xa becomes
 4985  y and ?P becomes λu v. (u, z) ∈ r*, thus yielding the above subgoal. So

 4986  what went wrong?
 4987  When looking at the instantiation of ?P we see that it does not depend

 4988  on its second parameter at all. The reason is that in our original goal, of
 4989  the pair (x, y) only x appears also in the conclusion, but not y. Thus our

 4990  induction statement is too general. Fortunately, it can easily be specialized:
 4991  transfer the additional premise (y, z) ∈ r* into the conclusion:
 4992  lemma rtc_trans[rule_format]:
 4993  "(x,y) ∈ r* =⇒ (y,z) ∈ r* −→ (x,z) ∈ r*"

 4994  This is not an obscure trick but a generally applicable heuristic:
 4995  When proving a statement by rule induction on (x1, . . . ,xn) ∈ R, pull
 4996  all other premises containing any of the xi into the conclusion using

 4997  −→.

 4998  A similar heuristic for other kinds of inductions is formulated in Sect. 9.2.1.
 4999  The rule_format directive turns −→ back into =⇒: in the end we obtain the

 5000  original statement of our lemma.
 5001  apply(erule rtc.induct)

 5002  Now induction produces two subgoals which are both proved automatically:
=== PAGE 145 ===

 5003  7.2 The Reflexive Transitive Closure 135

 5004  1. Vx. (x, z) ∈ r* −→ (x, z) ∈ r*
 5005  2. Vx y za.
 5006  [[(x, y) ∈ r; (y, za) ∈ r*; (za, z) ∈ r* −→ (y, z) ∈ r*]]
 5007  =⇒ (za, z) ∈ r* −→ (x, z) ∈ r*

 5008  apply(blast)
 5009  apply(blast intro: rtc_step)
 5010  done
 5011  Let us now prove that r* is really the reflexive transitive closure of r, i.e.
 5012  the least reflexive and transitive relation containing r. The latter is easily

 5013  formalized
 5014  inductive set
 5015  rtc2 :: "('a × 'a)set ⇒ ('a × 'a)set"
 5016  for r :: "('a × 'a)set"
 5017  where
 5018  "(x,y) ∈ r =⇒ (x,y) ∈ rtc2 r"
 5019  | "(x,x) ∈ rtc2 r"
 5020  | " [[ (x,y) ∈ rtc2 r; (y,z) ∈ rtc2 r ]] =⇒ (x,z) ∈ rtc2 r"

 5021  and the equivalence of the two definitions is easily shown by the obvious rule

 5022  inductions:
 5023  lemma "(x,y) ∈ rtc2 r =⇒ (x,y) ∈ r*"
 5024  apply(erule rtc2.induct)
 5025  apply(blast)
 5026  apply(blast)
 5027  apply(blast intro: rtc_trans)
 5028  done

 5029  lemma "(x,y) ∈ r* =⇒ (x,y) ∈ rtc2 r"
 5030  apply(erule rtc.induct)
 5031  apply(blast intro: rtc2.intros)
 5032  apply(blast intro: rtc2.intros)
 5033  done

 5034  So why did we start with the first definition? Because it is simpler. It

 5035  contains only two rules, and the single step rule is simpler than transitivity.
 5036  As a consequence, rtc.induct is simpler than rtc2.induct. Since inductive

 5037  proofs are hard enough anyway, we should always pick the simplest induction
 5038  schema available. Hence rtc is the definition of choice.

 5039  Exercise 7.2.1 Show that the converse of rtc_step also holds:
 5040  [[(x, y) ∈ r*; (y, z) ∈ r ]] =⇒ (x, z) ∈ r*

 5041  Exercise 7.2.2 Repeat the development of this section, but starting with
 5042  a definition of rtc where rtc_step is replaced by its converse as shown in

 5043  exercise 7.2.1.
=== PAGE 146 ===

 5044  136 7. Inductively Defined Sets

 5045  7.3 Advanced Inductive Definitions

 5046  The premises of introduction rules may contain universal quantifiers and

 5047  monotone functions. A universal quantifier lets the rule refer to any number

 5048  of instances of the inductively defined set. A monotone function lets the rule

 5049  refer to existing constructions (such as "list of") over the inductively defined

 5050  set. The examples below show how to use the additional expressiveness and

 5051  how to reason from the resulting definitions.

 5052  7.3.1 Universal Quantifiers in Introduction Rules

 5053  As a running example, this section develops the theory of ground terms:

 5054  terms constructed from constant and function symbols but not variables. To

 5055  simplify matters further, we regard a constant as a function applied to the
 5056  null argument list. Let us declare a datatype gterm for the type of ground

 5057  terms. It is a type constructor whose argument is a type of function symbols.
 5058  datatype 'f gterm = Apply 'f "'f gterm list"

 5059  To try it out, we declare a datatype of some integer operations: integer

 5060  constants, the unary minus operator and the addition operator.
 5061  datatype integer_op = Number int | UnaryMinus | Plus
 5062  Now the type integer_op gterm denotes the ground terms built over those

 5063  symbols.
 5064  The type constructor gterm can be generalized to a function over sets. It
 5065  returns the set of ground terms that can be formed over a set F of function

 5066  symbols. For example, we could consider the set of ground terms formed from
 5067  the finite set {Number 2, UnaryMinus, Plus}.
 5068  This concept is inductive. If we have a list args of ground terms over F
 5069  and a function symbol f in F, then we can apply f to args to obtain another

 5070  ground term. The only difficulty is that the argument list may be of any

 5071  length. Hitherto, each rule in an inductive definition referred to the induc-

 5072  tively defined set a fixed number of times, typically once or twice. A universal

 5073  quantifier in the premise of the introduction rule expresses that every element
 5074  of args belongs to our inductively defined set: is a ground term over F. The
 5075  function set denotes the set of elements in a given list.
 5076  inductive set
 5077  gterms :: "'f set ⇒ 'f gterm set"
 5078  for F :: "'f set"
 5079  where
 5080  step[intro!]: "[[∀ t ∈ set args. t ∈ gterms F; f ∈ F]]
 5081  =⇒ (Apply f args) ∈ gterms F"

 5082  To demonstrate a proof from this definition, let us show that the function
 5083  gterms is monotone. We shall need this concept shortly.
=== PAGE 147 ===

 5084  7.3 Advanced Inductive Definitions 137

 5085  lemma gterms_mono: "F⊆G =⇒ gterms F ⊆ gterms G"
 5086  apply clarify
 5087  apply (erule gterms.induct)
 5088  apply blast
 5089  done

 5090  Intuitively, this theorem says that enlarging the set of function symbols

 5091  enlarges the set of ground terms. The proof is a trivial rule induction. First
 5092  we use the clarify method to assume the existence of an element of gterms
 5093  F. (We could have used intro subsetI.) We then apply rule induction. Here

 5094  is the resulting subgoal:
 5095  1. Vx args f.
 5096  [[F ⊆ G; ∀ t ∈set args. t ∈ gterms F ∧ t ∈ gterms G; f ∈ F]]
 5097  =⇒ Apply f args ∈ gterms G
 5098  The assumptions state that f belongs to F, which is included in G, and that
 5099  every element of the list args is a ground term over G. The blast method

 5100  finds this chain of reasoning easily.

 5101  !!Why do we call this function gterms instead of gterm ? A constant may have
 5102  the same name as a type. However, name clashes could arise in the theorems
 5103  that Isabelle generates. Our choice of names keeps gterms.induct separate from
 5104  gterm.induct.

 5105  Call a term well-formed if each symbol occurring in it is applied to the

 5106  correct number of arguments. (This number is called the symbol's arity.)

 5107  We can express well-formedness by generalizing the inductive definition of
 5108  gterms. Suppose we are given a function called arity, specifying the arities
 5109  of all symbols. In the inductive step, we have a list args of such terms and a
 5110  function symbol f. If the length of the list matches the function's arity then
 5111  applying f to args yields a well-formed term.
 5112  inductive set
 5113  well_formed_gterm :: "('f ⇒ nat) ⇒ 'f gterm set"
 5114  for arity :: "'f ⇒ nat"
 5115  where
 5116  step[intro!]: "[[∀ t ∈ set args. t ∈ well_formed_gterm arity;
 5117  length args = arity f ]]
 5118  =⇒ (Apply f args) ∈ well_formed_gterm arity"

 5119  The inductive definition neatly captures the reasoning above. The universal quantification over the set of arguments expresses that all of them are

 5120  well-formed.

 5121  7.3.2 Alternative Definition Using a Monotone Function

 5122  An inductive definition may refer to the inductively defined set through an

 5123  arbitrary monotone function. To demonstrate this powerful feature, let us

 5124  change the inductive definition above, replacing the quantifier by a use of the
=== PAGE 148 ===

 5125  138 7. Inductively Defined Sets

 5126  function lists. This function, from the Isabelle theory of lists, is analogous
 5127  to the function gterms declared above: if A is a set then lists A is the set of
 5128  lists whose elements belong to A.

 5129  In the inductive definition of well-formed terms, examine the one introduction rule. The first premise states that args belongs to the lists of well-

 5130  formed terms. This formulation is more direct, if more obscure, than using a

 5131  universal quantifier.
 5132  inductive set
 5133  well_formed_gterm' :: "('f ⇒ nat) ⇒ 'f gterm set"
 5134  for arity :: "'f ⇒ nat"
 5135  where
 5136  step[intro!]: "[[args ∈ lists (well_formed_gterm' arity);
 5137  length args = arity f ]]
 5138  =⇒ (Apply f args) ∈ well_formed_gterm' arity"
 5139  monos lists_mono
 5140  We cite the theorem lists_mono to justify using the function lists.1
 5141  A ⊆ B =⇒ lists A ⊆ lists B (lists_mono)

 5142  Why must the function be monotone? An inductive definition describes an

 5143  iterative construction: each element of the set is constructed by a finite number of introduction rule applications. For example, the elements of even are

 5144  constructed by finitely many applications of the rules
 5145  0 ∈ Even.even
 5146  n ∈ Even.even =⇒ Suc (Suc n) ∈ Even.even

 5147  All references to a set in its inductive definition must be positive. Applications

 5148  of an introduction rule cannot invalidate previous applications, allowing the

 5149  construction process to converge. The following pair of rules do not constitute

 5150  an inductive definition:
 5151  0 ∈ Even.even
 5152  n ∈/ Even.even =⇒ Suc n ∈ Even.even

 5153  Showing that 4 is even using these rules requires showing that 3 is not even.

 5154  It is far from trivial to show that this set of rules characterizes the even

 5155  numbers.
 5156  Even with its use of the function lists, the premise of our introduction

 5157  rule is positive:
 5158  args ∈ lists (well_formed_gterm' arity)
 5159  To apply the rule we construct a list args of previously constructed wellformed terms. We obtain a new term, Apply f args. Because lists is mono-

 5160  tone, applications of the rule remain valid as new terms are constructed.

 5161  Further lists of well-formed terms become available and none are taken away.

 5162  1 This particular theorem is installed by default already, but we include the monos
 5163  declaration in order to illustrate its syntax.
=== PAGE 149 ===

 5164  7.3 Advanced Inductive Definitions 139

 5165  7.3.3 A Proof of Equivalence

 5166  We naturally hope that these two inductive definitions of "well-formed" co-

 5167  incide. The equality can be proved by separate inclusions in each direction.

 5168  Each is a trivial rule induction.
 5169  lemma "well_formed_gterm arity ⊆ well_formed_gterm' arity"
 5170  apply clarify
 5171  apply (erule well_formed_gterm.induct)
 5172  apply auto
 5173  done
 5174  The clarify method gives us an element of well_formed_gterm arity on

 5175  which to perform induction. The resulting subgoal can be proved automati-

 5176  cally:
 5177  1. Vx args f.
 5178  [[∀ t∈set args.
 5179  t ∈ well_formed_gterm arity ∧ t ∈ well_formed_gterm' arity;
 5180  length args = arity f ]]
 5181  =⇒ Apply f args ∈ well_formed_gterm' arity

 5182  This proof resembles the one given in Sect. 7.3.1 above, especially in the form

 5183  of the induction hypothesis. Next, we consider the opposite inclusion:
 5184  lemma "well_formed_gterm' arity ⊆ well_formed_gterm arity"
 5185  apply clarify
 5186  apply (erule well_formed_gterm'.induct)
 5187  apply auto
 5188  done

 5189  The proof script is virtually identical, but the subgoal after applying in-

 5190  duction may be surprising:
 5191  1. Vx args f.
 5192  [[args
 5193  ∈ lists
 5194  (well_formed_gterm' arity ∩
 5195  {a. a ∈ well_formed_gterm arity});
 5196  length args = arity f ]]
 5197  =⇒ Apply f args ∈ well_formed_gterm arity
 5198  The induction hypothesis contains an application of lists. Using a monotone

 5199  function in the inductive definition always has this effect. The subgoal may
 5200  look uninviting, but fortunately lists distributes over intersection:
 5201  lists (A ∩ B) = lists A ∩ lists B (lists_Int_eq)

 5202  Thanks to this default simplification rule, the induction hypothesis is quickly

 5203  replaced by its two parts:
 5204  args ∈ lists (well_formed_gterm' arity)
 5205  args ∈ lists (well_formed_gterm arity)
=== PAGE 150 ===

 5206  140 7. Inductively Defined Sets

 5207  Invoking the rule well_formed_gterm.step completes the proof. The call to
 5208  auto does all this work.

 5209  This example is typical of how monotone functions can be used. In par-

 5210  ticular, many of them distribute over intersection. Monotonicity implies one

 5211  direction of this set equality; we have this theorem:
 5212  mono f =⇒ f (A ∩ B) ⊆ f A ∩ f B (mono_Int)

 5213  7.3.4 Another Example of Rule Inversion

 5214  Does gterms distribute over intersection? We have proved that this function
 5215  is monotone, so mono_Int gives one of the inclusions. The opposite inclusion
 5216  asserts that if t is a ground term over both of the sets F and G then it is also
 5217  a ground term over their intersection, F ∩ G.
 5218  lemma gterms_IntI:
 5219  "t ∈ gterms F =⇒ t ∈ gterms G −→ t ∈ gterms (F∩G)"
 5220  Attempting this proof, we get the assumption Apply f args ∈ gterms G,

 5221  which cannot be broken down. It looks like a job for rule inversion:
 5222  inductive cases gterm_Apply_elim [elim!]: "Apply f args ∈ gterms F"

 5223  Here is the result.
 5224  [[Apply f args ∈ gterms F;
 5225  [[∀ t ∈set args. t ∈ gterms F; f ∈ F ]] =⇒ P]]
 5226  =⇒ P (gterm_Apply_elim)
 5227  This rule replaces an assumption about Apply f args by assumptions about
 5228  f and args. No cases are discarded (there was only one to begin with) but
 5229  the rule applies specifically to the pattern Apply f args. It can be applied
 5230  repeatedly as an elimination rule without looping, so we have given the elim!

 5231  attribute.

 5232  Now we can prove the other half of that distributive law.
 5233  lemma gterms_IntI [rule_format, intro!]:
 5234  "t ∈ gterms F =⇒ t ∈ gterms G −→ t ∈ gterms (F∩G)"
 5235  apply (erule gterms.induct)
 5236  apply blast
 5237  done
 5238  The proof begins with rule induction over the definition of gterms, which

 5239  leaves a single subgoal:
 5240  1. Vargs f.
 5241  [[∀ t∈set args.
 5242  t ∈ gterms F ∧ (t ∈ gterms G −→ t ∈ gterms (F ∩ G));
 5243  f ∈ F ]]
 5244  =⇒ Apply f args ∈ gterms G −→
 5245  Apply f args ∈ gterms (F ∩ G)
=== PAGE 151 ===

 5246  7.4 Case Study: A Context Free Grammar 141

 5247  To prove this, we assume Apply f args ∈ gterms G. Rule inversion, in the
 5248  form of gterm_Apply_elim, infers that every element of args belongs to gterms
 5249  G; hence (by the induction hypothesis) it belongs to gterms (F ∩ G). Rule
 5250  inversion also yields f ∈ G and hence f ∈ F ∩ G. All of this reasoning is
 5251  done by blast.

 5252  Our distributive law is a trivial consequence of previously-proved results:
 5253  lemma gterms_Int_eq [simp]:
 5254  "gterms (F ∩ G) = gterms F ∩ gterms G"
 5255  by (blast intro!: mono_Int monoI gterms_mono)

 5256  Exercise 7.3.1 A function mapping function symbols to their types is called

 5257  a signature. Given a type ranging over type symbols, we can represent

 5258  a function's type by a list of argument types paired with the result type.

 5259  Complete this inductive definition:
 5260  inductive set
 5261  well_typed_gterm :: "('f ⇒ 't list * 't) ⇒ ('f gterm * 't)set"
 5262  for sig :: "'f ⇒ 't list * 't"

 5263  7.4 Case Study: A Context Free Grammar

 5264  Grammars are nothing but shorthands for inductive definitions of nontermi-

 5265  nals which represent sets of strings. For example, the production A → Bc is

 5266  short for

 5267  w ∈ B =⇒ wc ∈ A

 5268  This section demonstrates this idea with an example due to Hopcroft and

 5269  Ullman, a grammar for generating all words with an equal number of a's

 5270  and b's:

 5271  S → | bA | aB

 5272  A → aS | bAA

 5273  B → bS | aBB

 5274  At the end we say a few words about the relationship between the original

 5275  proof [13, p. 81] and our formal version.
 5276  We start by fixing the alphabet, which consists only of a 's and b's:
 5277  datatype alfa = a | b

 5278  For convenience we include the following easy lemmas as simplification rules:
 5279  lemma [simp]: "(x 6= a) = (x = b) ∧ (x 6= b) = (x = a)"
 5280  by (case_tac x, auto)
=== PAGE 152 ===

 5281  142 7. Inductively Defined Sets

 5282  Words over this alphabet are of type alfa list, and the three nonterminals

 5283  are declared as sets of such words. The productions above are recast as a
 5284  mutual inductive definition of S, A and B:
 5285  inductive set
 5286  S :: "alfa list set" and
 5287  A :: "alfa list set" and
 5288  B :: "alfa list set"
 5289  where
 5290  "[] ∈ S"
 5291  | "w ∈ A =⇒ b#w ∈ S"
 5292  | "w ∈ B =⇒ a#w ∈ S"
 5293  | "w ∈ S =⇒ a#w ∈ A"
 5294  | " [[ v ∈A; w ∈A ]] =⇒ b#v@w ∈ A"
 5295  | "w ∈ S =⇒ b#w ∈ B"
 5296  | " [[ v ∈ B; w ∈ B ]] =⇒ a#v@w ∈ B"
 5297  First we show that all words in S contain the same number of a 's and b 's.
 5298  Since the definition of S is by mutual induction, so is the proof: we show at
 5299  the same time that all words in A contain one more a than b and all words
 5300  in B contain one more b than a.
 5301  lemma correctness:
 5302  "(w ∈ S −→ size[x ←w. x=a] = size[x←w. x=b]) ∧
 5303  (w ∈ A −→ size[x ←w. x=a] = size[x←w. x=b] + 1) ∧
 5304  (w ∈ B −→ size[x ←w. x=b] = size[x←w. x=a] + 1)"
 5305  These propositions are expressed with the help of the predefined filter function on lists, which has the convenient syntax [x ←xs. P x], the list of all elements x in xs such that P x holds. Remember that on lists size and length

 5306  are synonymous.

 5307  The proof itself is by rule induction and afterwards automatic:
 5308  by (rule S_A_B.induct, auto)

 5309  This may seem surprising at first, and is indeed an indication of the power

 5310  of inductive definitions. But it is also quite straightforward. For example,

 5311  consider the production A → bAA: if v, w ∈ A and the elements of A contain

 5312  one more a than b's, then bvw must again contain one more a than b's.

 5313  As usual, the correctness of syntactic descriptions is easy, but completeness is hard: does S contain all words with an equal number of a 's and b 's? It

 5314  turns out that this proof requires the following lemma: every string with two
 5315  more a 's than b 's can be cut somewhere such that each half has one more a
 5316  than b. This is best seen by imagining counting the difference between the
 5317  number of a 's and b's starting at the left end of the word. We start with

 5318  0 and end (at the right end) with 2. Since each move to the right increases

 5319  or decreases the difference by 1, we must have passed through 1 on our way

 5320  from 0 to 2. Formally, we appeal to the following discrete intermediate value
 5321  theorem nat0_intermed_int_val
=== PAGE 153 ===

 5322  7.4 Case Study: A Context Free Grammar 143

 5323  [[∀ i<n. |f (i + 1) - f i| ≤ 1; f 0 ≤ k; k ≤ f n]]
 5324  =⇒ ∃i ≤n. f i = k
 5325  where f is of type nat ⇒ int, int are the integers, |.| is the absolute value
 5326  function2, and 1 is the integer 1 (see Sect. 8.4).

 5327  First we show that our specific function, the difference between the numbers of a 's and b's, does indeed only change by 1 in every move to the right.
 5328  At this point we also start generalizing from a 's and b 's to an arbitrary property P. Otherwise we would have to prove the desired lemma twice, once as
 5329  stated above and once with the roles of a 's and b's interchanged.
 5330  lemma step1: " ∀ i < size w.
 5331  |(int(size[x←take (i+1) w. P x])-int(size[x←take (i+1) w. ¬P x]))
 5332  - (int(size[x←take i w. P x])-int(size[x←take i w. ¬P x]))| ≤ 1"
 5333  The lemma is a bit hard to read because of the coercion function int :: nat
 5334  ⇒ int. It is required because size returns a natural number, but subtraction
 5335  on type nat will do the wrong thing. Function take is predefined and take
 5336  i xs is the prefix of length i of xs ; below we also need drop i xs, which is
