 5337  what remains after that prefix has been dropped from xs.
 5338  The proof is by induction on w, with a trivial base case, and a not so trivial

 5339  induction step. Since it is essentially just arithmetic, we do not discuss it.
 5340  apply(induct_tac w)
 5341  apply(auto simp add: abs_if take_Cons split: nat.split)
 5342  done

 5343  Finally we come to the above-mentioned lemma about cutting in half a

 5344  word with two more elements of one sort than of the other sort:
 5345  lemma part1:
 5346  "size[x ←w. P x] = size[x←w. ¬P x]+2 =⇒
 5347  ∃ i≤size w. size[x ←take i w. P x] = size[x ←take i w. ¬P x]+1"
 5348  This is proved by force with the help of the intermediate value theorem,

 5349  instantiated appropriately and with its first premise disposed of by lemma
 5350  step1:
 5351  apply(insert nat0_intermed_int_val[OF step1, of "P" "w" "1"])
 5352  by force
 5353  Lemma part1 tells us only about the prefix take i w. An easy lemma
 5354  deals with the suffix drop i w :
 5355  lemma part2:
 5356  " [[size[x ←take i w @ drop i w. P x] =
 5357  size[x ←take i w @ drop i w. ¬P x]+2;
 5358  size[x ←take i w. P x] = size[x ←take i w. ¬P x]+1 ]]
 5359  =⇒ size[x ←drop i w. P x] = size[x ←drop i w. ¬P x]+1"
 5360  by(simp del: append_take_drop_id)

 5361  In the proof we have disabled the normally useful lemma
 5362  2 See Table A.1 in the Appendix for the correct ascii syntax.
=== PAGE 154 ===

 5363  144 7. Inductively Defined Sets

 5364  take n xs @ drop n xs = xs (append_take_drop_id)

 5365  to allow the simplifier to apply the following lemma instead:
 5366  [x ∈xs@ys. P x] = [x∈xs. P x] @ [x∈ys. P x]

 5367  To dispose of trivial cases automatically, the rules of the inductive defini-

 5368  tion are declared simplification rules:
 5369  declare S_A_B.intros[simp]

 5370  This could have been done earlier but was not necessary so far.

 5371  The completeness theorem tells us that if a word has the same number of
 5372  a's and b 's, then it is in S, and similarly for A and B:
 5373  theorem completeness:
 5374  "(size[x ←w. x=a] = size[x←w. x=b] −→ w ∈ S) ∧
 5375  (size[x ←w. x=a] = size[x←w. x=b] + 1 −→ w ∈ A) ∧
 5376  (size[x ←w. x=b] = size[x←w. x=a] + 1 −→ w ∈ B)"
 5377  The proof is by induction on w. Structural induction would fail here because,

 5378  as we can see from the grammar, we need to make bigger steps than merely
 5379  appending a single letter at the front. Hence we induct on the length of w,
 5380  using the induction rule length_induct:
 5381  apply(induct_tac w rule: length_induct)
 5382  apply(rename_tac w)
 5383  The rule parameter tells induct_tac explicitly which induction rule to use.

 5384  For details see Sect. 9.2.2 below. In this case the result is that we may assume
 5385  the lemma already holds for all words shorter than w. Because the induction
 5386  step renames the induction variable we rename it back to w.
 5387  The proof continues with a case distinction on w, on whether w is empty

 5388  or not.
 5389  apply(case_tac w)
 5390  apply(simp_all)

 5391  Simplification disposes of the base case and leaves only a conjunction of two
 5392  step cases to be proved: if w = a # v and
 5393  length (if x = a then [x ∈ v] else []) =
 5394  length (if x = b then [x ∈ v] else []) + 2
 5395  then b # v ∈ A, and similarly for w = b # v. We only consider the first case

 5396  in detail.
 5397  After breaking the conjunction up into two cases, we can apply part1 to
 5398  the assumption that w contains two more a's than b 's.
 5399  apply(rule conjI)
 5400  apply(clarify)
 5401  apply(frule part1[of " λx. x=a", simplified])
 5402  apply(clarify)
 5403  This yields an index i ≤ length v such that
=== PAGE 155 ===

 5404  7.4 Case Study: A Context Free Grammar 145

 5405  length (filter (λx. x = a) (take i v)) =
 5406  length (filter (λx. x = b) (take i v)) + 1
 5407  With the help of part2 it follows that
 5408  length (filter (λx. x = a) (drop i v)) =
 5409  length (filter (λx. x = b) (drop i v)) + 1
 5410  apply(drule part2[of " λx. x=a", simplified])
 5411  apply(assumption)
 5412  Now it is time to decompose v in the conclusion b # v ∈ A into take i v @
 5413  drop i v,
 5414  apply(rule_tac n1=i and t=v in subst[OF append_take_drop_id])
 5415  (the variables n1 and t are the result of composing the theorems subst and
 5416  append_take_drop_id) after which the appropriate rule of the grammar reduces the goal to the two subgoals take i v ∈ A and drop i v ∈ A :
 5417  apply(rule S_A_B.intros)
 5418  Both subgoals follow from the induction hypothesis because both take i
 5419  v and drop i v are shorter than w :
 5420  apply(force simp add: min_less_iff_disj)
 5421  apply(force split: nat_diff_split)
 5422  The case w = b # v is proved analogously:
 5423  apply(clarify)
 5424  apply(frule part1[of " λx. x=b", simplified])
 5425  apply(clarify)
 5426  apply(drule part2[of " λx. x=b", simplified])
 5427  apply(assumption)
 5428  apply(rule_tac n1=i and t=v in subst[OF append_take_drop_id])
 5429  apply(rule S_A_B.intros)
 5430  apply(force simp add: min_less_iff_disj)
 5431  by(force simp add: min_less_iff_disj split: nat_diff_split)

 5432  We conclude this section with a comparison of our proof with Hopcroft

 5433  and Ullman's [13, p. 81]. For a start, the textbook grammar, for no good

 5434  reason, excludes the empty word, thus complicating matters just a little bit:

 5435  they have 8 instead of our 7 productions.

 5436  More importantly, the proof itself is different: rather than separating the

 5437  two directions, they perform one induction on the length of a word. This

 5438  deprives them of the beauty of rule induction, and in the easy direction
 5439  (correctness) their reasoning is more detailed than our auto. For the hard
 5440  part (completeness), they consider just one of the cases that our simp_all

 5441  disposes of automatically. Then they conclude the proof by saying about the

 5442  remaining cases: "We do this in a manner similar to our method of proof

 5443  for part (1); this part is left to the reader". But this is precisely the part

 5444  that requires the intermediate value theorem and thus is not at all similar to

 5445  the other cases (which are automatic in Isabelle). The authors are at least
=== PAGE 156 ===

 5446  146 7. Inductively Defined Sets

 5447  cavalier about this point and may even have overlooked the slight difficulty

 5448  lurking in the omitted cases. Such errors are found in many pen-and-paper

 5449  proofs when they are scrutinized formally.
=== PAGE 157 ===

 5450  Part III

 5451  Advanced Material
=== PAGE 159 ===

 5452  8. More about Types

 5453  So far we have learned about a few basic types (for example bool and nat),

 5454  type abbreviations (types) and recursive datatypes (datatype). This chap-

 5455  ter will introduce more advanced material:

 5456  – Pairs (Sect. 8.1) and records (Sect. 8.2), and how to reason about them.

 5457  – Type classes: how to specify and reason about axiomatic collections of

 5458  types (Sect. 8.3). This section leads on to a discussion of Isabelle's numeric

 5459  types (Sect. 8.4).

 5460  – Introducing your own types: how to define types that cannot be constructed

 5461  with any of the basic methods (Sect. 8.5).

 5462  The material in this section goes beyond the needs of most novices. Serious

 5463  users should at least skim the sections as far as type classes. That material

 5464  is fairly advanced; read the beginning to understand what it is about, but

 5465  consult the rest only when necessary.

 5466  8.1 Pairs and Tuples

 5467  Ordered pairs were already introduced in Sect. 2.6.2, but only with a minimal
 5468  repertoire of operations: pairing and the two projections fst and snd. In

 5469  any non-trivial application of pairs you will find that this quickly leads to

 5470  unreadable nests of projections. This section introduces syntactic sugar to

 5471  overcome this problem: pattern matching with tuples.

 5472  8.1.1 Pattern Matching with Tuples

 5473  Tuples may be used as patterns in λ-abstractions, for example λ(x,y,z).x+y+z
 5474  and λ((x,y),z).x+y+z. In fact, tuple patterns can be used in most variable

 5475  binding constructs, and they can be nested. Here are some typical examples:
 5476  let (x, y) = f z in (y, x)
 5477  case xs of [] ⇒ 0 | (x, y) # zs ⇒ x + y
 5478  ∀ (x,y)∈A. x=y
 5479  {(x,y,z). x=z}
 5480  S ∈
 5481  (x, y) A {x + y}
=== PAGE 160 ===

 5482  150 8. More about Types

 5483  The intuitive meanings of these expressions should be obvious. Unfortunately,

 5484  we need to know in more detail what the notation really stands for once

 5485  we have to reason about it. Abstraction over pairs and tuples is merely a

 5486  convenient shorthand for a more complex internal representation. Thus the

 5487  internal and external form of a term may differ, which can affect proofs. If
 5488  you want to avoid this complication, stick to fst and snd and write λp. fst
 5489  p + snd p instead of λ(x,y). x+y. These terms are distinct even though they

 5490  denote the same function.
 5491  Internally, λ(x, y). t becomes case_prod (λx y. t), where split is the
 5492  uncurrying function of type ('a ⇒ 'b ⇒ 'c) ⇒ 'a × 'b ⇒ 'c defined as
 5493  case_prod = (λc p. c (fst p) (snd p)) (split_def)

 5494  Pattern matching in other variable binding constructs is translated similarly.

 5495  Thus we need to understand how to reason about such constructs.

 5496  8.1.2 Theorem Proving

 5497  The most obvious approach is the brute force expansion of split :
 5498  lemma "(λ(x,y).x) p = fst p"
 5499  by(simp add: split_def)
 5500  This works well if rewriting with split_def finishes the proof, as it does

 5501  above. But if it does not, you end up with exactly what we are trying to
 5502  avoid: nests of fst and snd. Thus this approach is neither elegant nor very

 5503  practical in large examples, although it can be effective in small ones.

 5504  If we consider why this lemma presents a problem, we realize that we need
 5505  to replace variable p by some pair (a, b). Then both sides of the equation
 5506  would simplify to a by the simplification rules (case (a, b) of (c, d) ⇒ f
 5507  c d) = f a b and fst (x1, x2) = x1. To reason about tuple patterns requires

 5508  some way of converting a variable of product type into a pair. In case of a
 5509  subterm of the form case p of (x, xa) ⇒ f x xa this is easy: the split rule
 5510  prod.split replaces p by a pair:
 5511  lemma "(λ(x,y).y) p = snd p"
 5512  apply(split prod.split)

 5513  1. ∀ x1 x2. p = (x1, x2) −→ x2 = snd p

 5514  This subgoal is easily proved by simplification. Thus we could have combined

 5515  simplification and splitting in one command that proves the goal outright:
 5516  by(simp split: prod.split)

 5517  Let us look at a second example:
 5518  lemma "let (x,y) = p in fst p = x"
 5519  apply(simp only: Let_def)
=== PAGE 161 ===

 5520  8.1 Pairs and Tuples 151
 5521  1. case p of (x, y) ⇒ fst p = x
 5522  A paired let reduces to a paired λ-abstraction, which can be split as above.

 5523  The same is true for paired set comprehension:
 5524  lemma "p ∈ {(x,y). x=y} −→ fst p = snd p"
 5525  apply simp
 5526  1. (case p of (x, xa) ⇒ x = xa) −→ fst p = snd p
 5527  Again, simplification produces a term suitable for prod.split as above. If you
 5528  are worried about the strange form of the premise: case_prod (=) is short for
 5529  λ(x, y). x = y. The same proof procedure works for
 5530  lemma "p ∈ {(x,y). x=y} =⇒ fst p = snd p"
 5531  except that we now have to use prod.split_asm, because split occurs in the

 5532  assumptions.
 5533  However, splitting split is not always a solution, as no split may be

 5534  present in the goal. Consider the following function:
 5535  primrec swap :: "'a × 'b ⇒ 'b × 'a" where "swap (x,y) = (y,x)"
 5536  Note that the above primrec definition is admissible because × is a datatype.

 5537  When we now try to prove
 5538  lemma "swap(swap p) = p"
 5539  simplification will do nothing, because the defining equation for swap expects
 5540  a pair. Again, we need to turn p into a pair first, but this time there is no
 5541  split in sight. The only thing we can do is to split the term by hand:
 5542  apply(case_tac p)
 5543  1. Va b. p = (a, b) =⇒ swap (swap p) = p
 5544  Again, case_tac is applicable because × is a datatype. The subgoal is easily
 5545  proved by simp.
 5546  Splitting by case_tac also solves the previous examples and may thus

 5547  appear preferable to the more arcane methods introduced first. However, see
 5548  the warning about case_tac in Sect. 2.5.5.
 5549  Alternatively, you can split all V-quantified variables in a goal with the
 5550  rewrite rule split_paired_all :
 5551  lemma "Vp q. swap(swap p) = q −→ p = q"
 5552  apply(simp only: split_paired_all)
 5553  1. Va b aa ba. swap (swap (a, b)) = (aa, ba) −→ (a, b) = (aa, ba)
 5554  apply simp
 5555  done
 5556  Note that we have intentionally included only split_paired_all in the first

 5557  simplification step, and then we simplify again. This time the reason was not
=== PAGE 162 ===

 5558  152 8. More about Types

 5559  merely pedagogical: split_paired_all may interfere with other functions of

 5560  the simplifier. The following command could fail (here it does not) where two
 5561  separate simp applications succeed.
 5562  apply(simp add: split_paired_all)
 5563  Finally, the simplifier automatically splits all ∀ and ∃ -quantified variables:
 5564  lemma "∀ p. ∃ q. swap p = swap q"
 5565  by simp

 5566  To turn off this automatic splitting, disable the responsible simplification

 5567  rules:
 5568  (∀ x. P x) = (∀ a b. P (a, b)) (split_paired_All)
 5569  (∃ x. P x) = (∃ a b. P (a, b)) (split_paired_Ex)

 5570  8.2 Records

 5571  Records are familiar from programming languages. A record of n fields is

 5572  essentially an n-tuple, but the record's components have names, which can

 5573  make expressions easier to read and reduces the risk of confusing one field

 5574  for another.

 5575  A record of Isabelle/HOL covers a collection of fields, with select and

 5576  update operations. Each field has a specified type, which may be polymorphic.

 5577  The field names are part of the record type, and the order of the fields is

 5578  significant — as it is in Pascal but not in Standard ML. If two different

 5579  record types have field names in common, then the ambiguity is resolved in

 5580  the usual way, by qualified names.

 5581  Record types can also be defined by extending other record types. Extensible records make use of the reserved pseudo-field more , which is present in

 5582  every record type. Generic record operations work on all possible extensions

 5583  of a given type scheme; polymorphism takes care of structural sub-typing

 5584  behind the scenes. There are also explicit coercion functions between fixed

 5585  record types.

 5586  8.2.1 Record Basics

 5587  Record types are not primitive in Isabelle and have a delicate internal rep-

 5588  resentation [21], based on nested copies of the primitive product type. A

 5589  record declaration introduces a new record type scheme by specifying its

 5590  fields, which are packaged internally to hold up the perception of the record

 5591  as a distinguished entity. Here is a simple example:
 5592  record point =
 5593  Xcoord :: int
=== PAGE 163 ===

 5594  8.2 Records 153

 5595  Ycoord :: int
 5596  Records of type point have two fields named Xcoord and Ycoord, both of
 5597  type int. We now define a constant of type point :
 5598  definition pt1 :: point where
 5599  "pt1 ≡ (| Xcoord = 999, Ycoord = 23 |)"

 5600  We see above the ASCII notation for record brackets. You can also use the
 5601  symbolic brackets (| and |). Record type expressions can be also written di-

 5602  rectly with individual fields. The type name above is merely an abbreviation.
 5603  definition pt2 :: " (|Xcoord :: int, Ycoord :: int|)" where
 5604  "pt2 ≡ (|Xcoord = -45, Ycoord = 97|)"

 5605  For each field, there is a selector function of the same name. For example, if p has type point then Xcoord p denotes the value of the Xcoord field
 5606  of p. Expressions involving field selection of explicit records are simplified

 5607  automatically:
 5608  lemma "Xcoord (|Xcoord = a, Ycoord = b|) = a"
 5609  by simp
 5610  The update operation is functional. For example, p (|Xcoord := 0|) is a
 5611  record whose Xcoord value is zero and whose Ycoord value is copied from p.

 5612  Updates of explicit records are also simplified automatically:
 5613  lemma "(|Xcoord = a, Ycoord = b |)(|Xcoord := 0|) =
 5614  (|Xcoord = 0, Ycoord = b |)"
 5615  by simp

 5616  !!Field names are declared as constants and can no longer be used as variables. It
 5617  would be unwise, for example, to call the fields of type point simply x and y.

 5618  8.2.2 Extensible Records and Generic Operations

 5619  Now, let us define coloured points (type cpoint ) to be points extended with
 5620  a field col of type colour :
 5621  datatype colour = Red | Green | Blue
 5622  record cpoint = point +
 5623  col :: colour
 5624  The fields of this new type are Xcoord, Ycoord and col, in that order.
 5625  definition cpt1 :: cpoint where
 5626  "cpt1 ≡ (|Xcoord = 999, Ycoord = 23, col = Green|)"

 5627  We can define generic operations that work on arbitrary instances of a
 5628  record scheme, e.g. covering point, cpoint, and any further extensions. Every
 5629  record structure has an implicit pseudo-field, more , that keeps the extension
=== PAGE 164 ===

 5630  154 8. More about Types

 5631  as an explicit value. Its type is declared as completely polymorphic: 'a. When

 5632  a fixed record value is expressed using just its standard fields, the value of
 5633  more is implicitly set to (), the empty tuple, which has type unit. Within the
 5634  record brackets, you can refer to the more field by writing ". . . " (three dots):
 5635  lemma "Xcoord (|Xcoord = a, Ycoord = b, . . . = p |) = a"
 5636  by simp
 5637  This lemma applies to any record whose first two fields are Xcoord
 5638  and Ycoord. Note that (|Xcoord = a, Ycoord = b, . . . = ()|) is exactly the
 5639  same as (|Xcoord = a, Ycoord = b |). Selectors and updates are always polymorphic wrt. the more part of a record scheme, its value is just ignored (for

 5640  select) or copied (for update).
 5641  The more pseudo-field may be manipulated directly as well, but the iden-

 5642  tifier needs to be qualified:
 5643  lemma "point.more cpt1 = (|col = Green|)"
 5644  by (simp add: cpt1_def)
 5645  We see that the colour part attached to this point is a rudimentary record
 5646  in its own right, namely (|col = Green|). In order to select or update col, this

 5647  fragment needs to be put back into the context of the parent type scheme,
 5648  say as more part of another point.

 5649  To define generic operations, we need to know a bit more about records.
 5650  Our definition of point above has generated two type abbreviations:
 5651  point = (|Xcoord :: int, Ycoord :: int|)
 5652  'a point_scheme = (|Xcoord :: int, Ycoord :: int, . . . :: 'a |)
 5653  Type point is for fixed records having exactly the two fields Xcoord and Ycoord,
 5654  while the polymorphic type 'a point_scheme comprises all possible extensions
 5655  to those two fields. Note that unit point_scheme coincides with point, and
 5656  (|col :: colour |) point_scheme with cpoint.

 5657  In the following example we define two operations — methods, if we regard
 5658  records as objects — to get and set any point's Xcoord field.
 5659  definition getX :: "'a point_scheme ⇒ int" where
 5660  "getX r ≡ Xcoord r"
 5661  definition setX :: "'a point_scheme ⇒ int ⇒ 'a point_scheme" where
 5662  "setX r a ≡ r(|Xcoord := a|)"
 5663  Here is a generic method that modifies a point, incrementing its Xcoord
 5664  field. The Ycoord and more fields are copied across. It works for any record
 5665  type scheme derived from point (including cpoint etc.):
 5666  definition incX :: "'a point_scheme ⇒ 'a point_scheme" where
 5667  "incX r ≡
 5668  (|Xcoord = Xcoord r + 1, Ycoord = Ycoord r, . . . = point.more r|)"

 5669  Generic theorems can be proved about generic methods. This trivial
 5670  lemma relates incX to getX and setX :
 5671  lemma "incX r = setX r (getX r + 1)"
=== PAGE 165 ===

 5672  8.2 Records 155

 5673  by (simp add: getX_def setX_def incX_def)

 5674  !!If you use the symbolic record brackets (| and |), then you must also use the
 5675  symbolic ellipsis, ". . . ", rather than three consecutive periods, "...". Mixing
 5676  the ASCII and symbolic versions causes a syntax error. (The two versions are more
 5677  distinct on screen than they are on paper.)

 5678  8.2.3 Record Equality

 5679  Two records are equal if all pairs of corresponding fields are equal. Concrete

 5680  record equalities are simplified automatically:
 5681  lemma "((|Xcoord = a, Ycoord = b |) = (|Xcoord = a', Ycoord = b'|)) =
 5682  (a = a' ∧ b = b')"
 5683  by simp
 5684  The following equality is similar, but generic, in that r can be any instance
 5685  of 'a point_scheme:
 5686  lemma "r(|Xcoord := a, Ycoord := b |) = r (|Ycoord := b, Xcoord := a |)"
 5687  by simp

 5688  We see above the syntax for iterated updates. We could equivalently have
 5689  written the left-hand side as r(|Xcoord := a|)(|Ycoord := b|).

 5690  Record equality is extensional: a record is determined entirely by the

 5691  values of its fields.
 5692  lemma "r = (|Xcoord = Xcoord r, Ycoord = Ycoord r |)"
 5693  by simp
 5694  The generic version of this equality includes the pseudo-field more :
 5695  lemma "r = (|Xcoord = Xcoord r, Ycoord = Ycoord r, . . . = point.more r|)"
 5696  by simp

 5697  The simplifier can prove many record equalities automatically, but general

 5698  equality reasoning can be tricky. Consider proving this obvious fact:
 5699  lemma "r(|Xcoord := a|) = r (|Xcoord := a'|) =⇒ a = a'"
 5700  apply simp?
 5701  oops

 5702  Here the simplifier can do nothing, since general record equality is not elimi-

 5703  nated automatically. One way to proceed is by an explicit forward step that
 5704  applies the selector Xcoord to both sides of the assumed record equality:
 5705  lemma "r(|Xcoord := a|) = r (|Xcoord := a'|) =⇒ a = a'"
 5706  apply (drule_tac f = Xcoord in arg_cong)

 5707  1. Xcoord (r(|Xcoord := a|)) = Xcoord (r(|Xcoord := a'|)) =⇒ a = a'
=== PAGE 166 ===

 5708  156 8. More about Types

 5709  Now, simp will reduce the assumption to the desired conclusion.
 5710  apply simp
 5711  done
 5712  The cases method is preferable to such a forward proof. We state the

 5713  desired lemma again:
 5714  lemma "r(|Xcoord := a|) = r (|Xcoord := a'|) =⇒ a = a'"
 5715  The cases method adds an equality to replace the named record term by

 5716  an explicit record expression, listing all fields. It even includes the pseudo-field
 5717  more, since the record equality stated here is generic for all extensions.
 5718  apply (cases r)

 5719  1. VXcoord Ycoord more.
 5720  [[r(|Xcoord := a|) = r (|Xcoord := a'|);
 5721  r = (|Xcoord = Xcoord, Ycoord = Ycoord, . . . = more |)]]
 5722  =⇒ a = a'
 5723  Again, simp finishes the proof. Because r is now represented as an explicit

 5724  record construction, the updates can be applied and the record equality can

 5725  be replaced by equality of the corresponding fields (due to injectivity).
 5726  apply simp
 5727  done

 5728  The generic cases method does not admit references to locally bound

 5729  parameters of a goal. In longer proof scripts one might have to fall back on
 5730  the primitive rule_tac used together with the internal field representation
 5731  rules of records. The above use of (cases r) would become (rule_tac r = r
 5732  in point.cases_scheme).

 5733  8.2.4 Extending and Truncating Records

 5734  Each record declaration introduces a number of derived operations to refer

 5735  collectively to a record's fields and to convert between fixed record types.
 5736  They can, for instance, convert between types point and cpoint. We can add
 5737  a colour to a point or convert a cpoint to a point by forgetting its colour.
 5738  – Function make takes as arguments all of the record's fields (including those

 5739  inherited from ancestors). It returns the corresponding record.
 5740  – Function fields takes the record's very own fields and returns a record
 5741  fragment consisting of just those fields. This may be filled into the more

 5742  part of the parent record scheme.
 5743  – Function extend takes two arguments: a record to be extended and a record

 5744  containing the new fields.
 5745  – Function truncate takes a record (possibly an extension of the original

 5746  record type) and returns a fixed record, removing any additional fields.
=== PAGE 167 ===

 5747  8.2 Records 157

 5748  These functions provide useful abbreviations for standard record expressions

 5749  involving constructors and selectors. The definitions, which are not unfolded
 5750  by default, are made available by the collective name of defs (point.defs,
 5751  cpoint.defs, etc.). For example, here are the versions of those functions generated for record point. We omit point.fields, which happens to be the same
 5752  as point.make.
 5753  point.make Xcoord Ycoord ≡
 5754  (|point.Xcoord = Xcoord, Ycoord = Ycoord |)
 5755  point.extend r more ≡
 5756  (|Xcoord = Xcoord r, Ycoord = Ycoord r, . . . = more |)
 5757  point.truncate r ≡ (|Xcoord = Xcoord r, Ycoord = Ycoord r |)
 5758  Contrast those with the corresponding functions for record cpoint. Observe
 5759  cpoint.fields in particular.
 5760  cpoint.make Xcoord Ycoord col ≡
 5761  (|point.Xcoord = Xcoord, Ycoord = Ycoord, cpoint.col = col |)
 5762  cpoint.fields col ≡ (|cpoint.col = col|)
 5763  cpoint.extend r more ≡
 5764  (|Xcoord = Xcoord r, Ycoord = Ycoord r, col = col r, . . . = more |)
 5765  cpoint.truncate r ≡
 5766  (|Xcoord = Xcoord r, Ycoord = Ycoord r, col = col r |)

 5767  To demonstrate these functions, we declare a new coloured point by
 5768  extending an ordinary point. Function point.extend augments pt1 with a

 5769  colour value, which is converted into an appropriate record fragment by
 5770  cpoint.fields.
 5771  definition cpt2 :: cpoint where
 5772  "cpt2 ≡ point.extend pt1 (cpoint.fields Green)"
 5773  The coloured points cpt1 and cpt2 are equal. The proof is trivial, by
 5774  unfolding all the definitions. We deliberately omit the definition of pt1 in
 5775  order to reveal the underlying comparison on type point.
 5776  lemma "cpt1 = cpt2"
 5777  apply (simp add: cpt1_def cpt2_def point.defs cpoint.defs)

 5778  1. Xcoord pt1 = 999 ∧ Ycoord pt1 = 23
 5779  apply (simp add: pt1_def)
 5780  done

 5781  In the example below, a coloured point is truncated to leave a point. We
 5782  use the truncate function of the target record.
 5783  lemma "point.truncate cpt2 = pt1"
 5784  by (simp add: pt1_def cpt2_def point.defs)

 5785  Exercise 8.2.1 Extend record cpoint to have a further field, intensity, of
 5786  type nat. Experiment with generic operations (using polymorphic selectors
 5787  and updates) and explicit coercions (using extend, truncate etc.) among the

 5788  three record types.
=== PAGE 168 ===

 5789  158 8. More about Types

 5790  Exercise 8.2.2 (For Java programmers.) Model a small class hierarchy using

 5791  records.

 5792  8.3 Type Classes

 5793  The programming language Haskell has popularized the notion of type

 5794  classes: a type class is a set of types with a common interface: all types

 5795  in that class must provide the functions in the interface. Isabelle offers a sim-

 5796  ilar type class concept: in addition, properties (class axioms) can be specified

 5797  which any instance of this type class must obey. Thus we can talk about a

 5798  type τ being in a class C, which is written τ :: C. This is the case if τ satisfies

 5799  the axioms of C. Furthermore, type classes can be organized in a hierarchy.

 5800  Thus there is the notion of a class D being a subclass of a class C, written

 5801  D < C. This is the case if all axioms of C are also provable in D.

 5802  In this section we introduce the most important concepts behind type

 5803  classes by means of a running example from algebra. This should give you an

 5804  intuition how to use type classes and to understand specifications involving

 5805  type classes. Type classes are covered more deeply in a separate tutorial [11].

 5806  8.3.1 Overloading

 5807  Type classes allow overloading; thus a constant may have multiple definitions

 5808  at non-overlapping types.

 5809  Overloading. We can introduce a binary infix addition operator ⊕ for ar-

 5810  bitrary types by means of a type class:
 5811  class plus =
 5812  fixes plus :: "'a ⇒ 'a ⇒ 'a" (infixl "⊕" 70)
 5813  This introduces a new class plus, along with a constant plus with nice infix
 5814  syntax. plus is also named class operation. The type of plus carries a class
 5815  constraint "'a :: plus" on its type variable, meaning that only types of class
 5816  plus can be instantiated for "'a". To breathe life into plus we need to declare
 5817  a type to be an instance of plus :
 5818  instantiation nat :: plus
 5819  begin

 5820  Command instantiation opens a local theory context. Here we can now
 5821  instantiate plus on nat:
 5822  primrec plus_nat :: "nat ⇒ nat ⇒ nat" where
 5823  "(0::nat) ⊕ n = n"
 5824  | "Suc m ⊕ n = Suc (m ⊕ n)"
=== PAGE 169 ===

 5825  8.3 Type Classes 159
 5826  Note that the name plus carries a suffix _nat; by default, the local name of a
 5827  class operation f to be instantiated on type constructor κ is mangled as f_κ.
 5828  In case of uncertainty, these names may be inspected using the print context

 5829  command.
 5830  Although class plus has no axioms, the instantiation must be formally

 5831  concluded by a (trivial) instantiation proof "..":
 5832  instance ..

 5833  More interesting instance proofs will arise below.

 5834  The instantiation is finished by an explicit
 5835  end
 5836  From now on, terms like Suc (m ⊕ 2) are legal.
 5837  instantiation prod :: (plus, plus) plus
 5838  begin
 5839  Here we instantiate the product type prod to class plus, given that its type
 5840  arguments are of class plus:
 5841  fun plus_prod :: "'a × 'b ⇒ 'a × 'b ⇒ 'a × 'b" where
 5842  "(x, y) ⊕ (w, z) = (x ⊕ w, y ⊕ z)"

 5843  Obviously, overloaded specifications may include recursion over the syntactic

 5844  structure of types.
 5845  instance ..
 5846  end

 5847  This way we have encoded the canonical lifting of binary operations to prod-

 5848  ucts by means of type classes.

 5849  8.3.2 Axioms

 5850  Attaching axioms to our classes lets us reason on the level of classes. The

 5851  results will be applicable to all types in a class, just as in axiomatic mathe-

 5852  matics.
 5853  !!Proofs in this section use structured Isar proofs, which are not covered in this
 5854  tutorial; but see [24].

 5855  Semigroups. We specify semigroups as subclass of plus :
 5856  class semigroup = plus +
 5857  assumes assoc: "(x ⊕ y) ⊕ z = x ⊕ (y ⊕ z)"
 5858  This class specification requires that all instances of semigroup obey assoc: "Vx
 5859  y z :: 'a::semigroup. (x ⊕ y) ⊕ z = x ⊕ (y ⊕ z)".
=== PAGE 170 ===

 5860  160 8. More about Types

 5861  We can use this class axiom to derive further abstract theorems relative
 5862  to class semigroup:
 5863  lemma assoc_left:
 5864  fixes x y z :: "'a::semigroup"
 5865  shows "x ⊕ (y ⊕ z) = (x ⊕ y) ⊕ z"
 5866  using assoc by (rule sym)
 5867  The semigroup constraint on type 'a restricts instantiations of 'a to types of
 5868  class semigroup and during the proof enables us to use the fact assoc whose
 5869  type parameter is itself constrained to class semigroup. The main advantage

 5870  of classes is that theorems can be proved in the abstract and freely reused

 5871  for each instance.

 5872  On instantiation, we have to give a proof that the given operations obey

 5873  the class axioms:
 5874  instantiation nat :: semigroup
 5875  begin
 5876  instance proof

 5877  The proof opens with a default proof step, which for instance judgements
 5878  invokes method intro_classes.
 5879  fix m n q :: nat
 5880  show "(m ⊕ n) ⊕ q = m ⊕ (n ⊕ q)"
 5881  by (induct m) simp_all
 5882  qed
 5883  end

 5884  Again, the interesting things enter the stage with parametric types:
 5885  instantiation prod :: (semigroup, semigroup) semigroup
 5886  begin
 5887  instance proof
 5888  fix p 1 p 2 p 3 :: "'a::semigroup × 'b::semigroup"
 5889  show "p 1 ⊕ p2 ⊕ p3 = p 1 ⊕ (p 2 ⊕ p 3)"
 5890  by (cases p 1, cases p2, cases p 3) (simp add: assoc)

 5891  Associativity of product semigroups is established using the hypothetical associativity assoc of the type components, which holds due to the semigroup
 5892  constraints imposed on the type components by the instance proposition.

 5893  Indeed, this pattern often occurs with parametric types and type classes.
 5894  qed
 5895  end
 5896  Monoids. We define a subclass monoidl (a semigroup with a left-hand neutral) by extending semigroup with one additional parameter neutral together

 5897  with its property:
 5898  class monoidl = semigroup +
=== PAGE 171 ===

 5899  8.3 Type Classes 161

 5900  fixes neutral :: "'a" ("0")
 5901  assumes neutl: " 0 ⊕ x = x"

 5902  Again, we prove some instances, by providing suitable parameter definitions

 5903  and proofs for the additional specifications.
 5904  instantiation nat :: monoidl
 5905  begin
 5906  definition
 5907  neutral_nat_def: " 0 = (0::nat)"
 5908  instance proof
 5909  fix n :: nat
 5910  show " 0 ⊕ n = n"
 5911  unfolding neutral_nat_def by simp
 5912  qed
 5913  end

 5914  In contrast to the examples above, we here have both specification of class

 5915  operations and a non-trivial instance proof.

 5916  This covers products as well:
 5917  instantiation prod :: (monoidl, monoidl) monoidl
 5918  begin
 5919  definition
 5920  neutral_prod_def: " 0 = (0, 0)"
 5921  instance proof
 5922  fix p :: "'a::monoidl × 'b::monoidl"
 5923  show " 0 ⊕ p = p"
 5924  by (cases p) (simp add: neutral_prod_def neutl)
 5925  qed
 5926  end

 5927  Fully-fledged monoids are modelled by another subclass which does not add

 5928  new parameters but tightens the specification:
 5929  class monoid = monoidl +
 5930  assumes neutr: "x ⊕ 0 = x"
 5931  Corresponding instances for nat and products are left as an exercise to the

 5932  reader.

 5933  Groups. To finish our small algebra example, we add a group class:
 5934  class group = monoidl +
 5935  fixes inv :: "'a ⇒ 'a" (" ÷ _" [81] 80)
 5936  assumes invl: " ÷ x ⊕ x = 0"

 5937  We continue with a further example for abstract proofs relative to type

 5938  classes:
=== PAGE 172 ===

 5939  162 8. More about Types

 5940  lemma left_cancel:
 5941  fixes x y z :: "'a::group"
 5942  shows "x ⊕ y = x ⊕ z ←→ y = z"
 5943  proof
 5944  assume "x ⊕ y = x ⊕ z"
 5945  then have "÷ x ⊕ (x ⊕ y) = ÷ x ⊕ (x ⊕ z)" by simp
 5946  then have "(÷ x ⊕ x) ⊕ y = (÷ x ⊕ x) ⊕ z" by (simp add: assoc)
 5947  then show "y = z" by (simp add: invl neutl)
 5948  next
 5949  assume "y = z"
 5950  then show "x ⊕ y = x ⊕ z" by simp
 5951  qed

 5952  Any group is also a monoid ; this can be made explicit by claiming an additional

 5953  subclass relation, together with a proof of the logical difference:
 5954  instance group ⊆ monoid
 5955  proof
 5956  fix x
 5957  from invl have " ÷ x ⊕ x = 0" .
 5958  then have "÷ x ⊕ (x ⊕ 0) = ÷ x ⊕ x"
 5959  by (simp add: neutl invl assoc [symmetric])
 5960  then show "x ⊕ 0 = x" by (simp add: left_cancel)
 5961  qed

 5962  The proof result is propagated to the type system, making group an instance
 5963  of monoid by adding an additional edge to the graph of subclass relation; see

 5964  also Figure 8.1.

 5965  semigroup semigroup

 5966  ? ?
 5967  monoidl monoidl

 5968  B

 5969  B
 5970  monoid monoid

 5971  B

 5972  B PP

 5973  PP

 5974  BBN PPq
 5975  group group
 5976  Fig. 8.1. Subclass relationship of monoids and groups: before and after establishing
 5977  the relationship group ⊆ monoid ; transitive edges are left out.

 5978  Inconsistencies. The reader may be wondering what happens if we attach

 5979  an inconsistent set of axioms to a class. So far we have always avoided to add

 5980  new axioms to HOL for fear of inconsistencies and suddenly it seems that we

 5981  are throwing all caution to the wind. So why is there no problem?

 5982  The point is that by construction, all type variables in the axioms of a

 5983  class are automatically constrained with the class being defined (as shown for
=== PAGE 173 ===

 5984  8.4 Numbers 163

 5985  axiom refl above). These constraints are always carried around and Isabelle

 5986  takes care that they are never lost, unless the type variable is instantiated

 5987  with a type that has been shown to belong to that class. Thus you may be
 5988  able to prove False from your axioms, but Isabelle will remind you that this

 5989  theorem has the hidden hypothesis that the class is non-empty.

 5990  Even if each individual class is consistent, intersections of (unrelated)

 5991  classes readily become inconsistent in practice. Now we know this need not

 5992  worry us.

 5993  Syntactic Classes and Predefined Overloading. In our algebra example, we have started with a syntactic class plus which only specifies opera-

 5994  tions but no axioms; it would have been also possible to start immediately
 5995  with class semigroup, specifying the ⊕ operation and associativity at the same

 5996  time.

 5997  Which approach is more appropriate depends. Usually it is more conve-

 5998  nient to introduce operations and axioms in the same class: then the type

 5999  checker will automatically insert the corresponding class constraints when-

 6000  ever the operations occur, reducing the need of manual annotations. However,

 6001  when operations are decorated with popular syntax, syntactic classes can be

 6002  an option to re-use the syntax in different contexts; this is indeed the way

 6003  most overloaded constants in HOL are introduced, of which the most impor-

 6004  tant are listed in Table A.2 in the appendix. Section 8.4.5 covers a range of

 6005  corresponding classes with axioms.

 6006  Further note that classes may contain axioms but no operations. An example is class finite from theory HOL.Finite_Set which specifies a type to
 6007  be finite: "finite (UNIV :: 'a::finite set)".

 6008  8.4 Numbers

 6009  Until now, our numerical examples have used the type of natural numbers, nat. This is a recursive datatype generated by the constructors zero

 6010  and successor, so it works well with inductive proofs and primitive recursive
 6011  function definitions. HOL also provides the type int of integers, which lack

 6012  induction but support true subtraction. With subtraction, arithmetic reason-

 6013  ing is easier, which makes the integers preferable to the natural numbers for

 6014  complicated arithmetic expressions, even if they are non-negative. There are
 6015  also the types rat, real and complex : the rational, real and complex num-

 6016  bers. Isabelle has no subtyping, so the numeric types are distinct and there

 6017  are functions to convert between them. Most numeric operations are over-

 6018  loaded: the same symbol can be used at all numeric types. Table A.2 in the

 6019  appendix shows the most important operations, together with the priorities

 6020  of the infix symbols. Algebraic properties are organized using type classes

 6021  around algebraic concepts such as rings and fields; a property such as the
=== PAGE 174 ===

 6022  164 8. More about Types

 6023  commutativity of addition is a single theorem (add.commute) that applies to

 6024  all numeric types.

 6025  Many theorems involving numeric types can be proved automatically by
 6026  Isabelle's arithmetic decision procedure, the method arith . Linear arithmetic

 6027  comprises addition, subtraction and multiplication by constant factors; sub-

 6028  terms involving other operators are regarded as variables. The procedure can

 6029  be slow, especially if the subgoal to be proved involves subtraction over type
 6030  nat, which causes case splits. On types nat and int, arith can deal with
 6031  quantifiers—this is known as Presburger arithmetic—whereas on type real

 6032  it cannot.

 6033  The simplifier reduces arithmetic expressions in other ways, such as di-

 6034  viding through by common factors. For problems that lie outside the scope

 6035  of automation, HOL provides hundreds of theorems about multiplication, di-

 6036  vision, etc., that can be brought to bear. You can locate them using Proof

 6037  General's Find button. A few lemmas are given below to show what is avail-

 6038  able.

 6039  8.4.1 Numeric Literals

 6040  The constants 0 and 1 are overloaded. They denote zero and one, respectively,

 6041  for all numeric types. Other values are expressed by numeric literals, which

 6042  consist of one or more decimal digits optionally preceeded by a minus sign
 6043  (-). Examples are 2, -3 and 441223334678. Literals are available for the types

 6044  of natural numbers, integers, rationals, reals, etc.; they denote integer values

 6045  of arbitrary size.

 6046  Literals look like constants, but they abbreviate terms representing the

 6047  number in a two's complement binary notation. Isabelle performs arithmetic

 6048  on literals by rewriting rather than using the hardware arithmetic. In most

 6049  cases arithmetic is fast enough, even for numbers in the millions. The arith-

 6050  metic operations provided for literals include addition, subtraction, multipli-

 6051  cation, integer division and remainder. Fractions of literals (expressed using

 6052  division) are reduced to lowest terms.
 6053  !! The arithmetic operators are overloaded, so you must be careful to ensure that
 6054  each numeric expression refers to a specific type, if necessary by inserting type
 6055  constraints. Here is an example of what can go wrong:
 6056  lemma "2 * m = m + m"
 6057  Carefully observe how Isabelle displays the subgoal:
 6058  1. (2::'a) * m = m + m
 6059  The type 'a given for the literal 2 warns us that no numeric type has been specified.
 6060  The problem is underspecified. Given a type constraint such as nat, int or real,
 6061  it becomes trivial.
=== PAGE 175 ===

 6062  8.4 Numbers 165
 6063  !! Numeric literals are not constructors and therefore must not be used in patterns. For example, this declaration is rejected:
 6064  function h where
 6065  "h 3 = 2"
 6066  |"h i = i"
 6067  You should use a conditional expression instead:
 6068  "h i = (if i = 3 then 2 else i)"

 6069  8.4.2 The Type of Natural Numbers, nat

 6070  This type requires no introduction: we have been using it from the beginning.

 6071  Hundreds of theorems about the natural numbers are proved in the theories
 6072  Nat and Divides. Basic properties of addition and multiplication are available

 6073  through the axiomatic type class for semirings (Sect. 8.4.5).

 6074  Literals. The notational options for the natural numbers are confusing. Re-

 6075  call that an overloaded constant can be defined independently for each type;
 6076  the definition of 1 for type nat is
 6077  1 ≡ Suc 0 (One_nat_def)

 6078  This is installed as a simplification rule, so the simplifier will replace every
 6079  occurrence of 1::nat by Suc 0. Literals are obviously better than nested Sucs

 6080  at expressing large values. But many theorems, including the rewrite rules

 6081  for primitive recursive functions, can only be applied to terms of the form
 6082  Suc n.

 6083  The following default simplification rules replace small literals by zero and

 6084  successor:
 6085  2 + n = Suc (Suc n) (add_2_eq_Suc)
 6086  n + 2 = Suc (Suc n) (add_2_eq_Suc')
 6087  It is less easy to transform 100 into Suc 99 (for example), and the simpli-

 6088  fier will normally reverse this transformation. Novices should express natural
 6089  numbers using 0 and Suc only.
 6090  Division. The infix operators div and mod are overloaded. Isabelle/HOL

 6091  provides the basic facts about quotient and remainder on the natural num-

 6092  bers:
 6093  m mod n = (if m < n then m else (m - n) mod n) (mod_if)
 6094  m div n * n + m mod n = m (div_mult_mod_eq)

 6095  Many less obvious facts about quotient and remainder are also provided.

 6096  Here is a selection:
=== PAGE 176 ===

 6097  166 8. More about Types

 6098  a * b div c = a * (b div c) + a * (b mod c) div c (div_mult1_eq)
 6099  a * b mod c = a * (b mod c) mod c (mod_mult_right_eq)
 6100  a div (b*c) = a div b div c (div_mult2_eq)
 6101  a mod (b*c) = b * (a div b mod c) + a mod b (mod_mult2_eq)
 6102  0 < c =⇒ (c * a) div (c * b) = a div b (div_mult_mult1)
 6103  (m mod n) * k = (m * k) mod (n * k) (mod_mult_distrib)
 6104  m ≤ n =⇒ m div k ≤ n div k (div_le_mono)

 6105  Surprisingly few of these results depend upon the divisors' being nonzero.

 6106  That is because division by zero yields zero:
 6107  a div 0 = 0 (DIVISION_BY_ZERO_DIV)
 6108  a mod 0 = a (DIVISION_BY_ZERO_MOD)
 6109  In div_mult_mult1 above, one of the two divisors (namely c ) must still be

 6110  nonzero.

 6111  The divides relation has the standard definition, which is overloaded over

 6112  all numeric types:
 6113  m dvd n ≡ ∃ k. n = m * k (dvd_def)

 6114  Section 5.18 discusses proofs involving this relation. Here are some of the

 6115  facts proved about it:
 6116  [[m dvd n; n dvd m ]] =⇒ m = n (dvd_antisym)
 6117  [[k dvd m; k dvd n ]] =⇒ k dvd (m + n) (dvd_add)
 6118  Subtraction. There are no negative natural numbers, so m - n equals zero
 6119  unless m exceeds n. The following is one of the few facts about m - n that is
 6120  not subject to the condition n ≤ m.
 6121  (m - n) * k = m * k - n * k (diff_mult_distrib)

 6122  Natural number subtraction has few nice properties; often you should remove

 6123  it by simplifying with this split rule.
 6124  P(a-b) = ((a<b −→ P 0) ∧ (∀ d. a = b+d −→ P d)) (nat_diff_split)

 6125  For example, splitting helps to prove the following fact.
 6126  lemma "(n - 2) * (n + 2) = n * n - (4::nat)"
 6127  apply (simp split: nat_diff_split, clarify)
 6128  1. Vd. [[n < 2; n * n = 4 + d ]] =⇒ d = 0

 6129  The result lies outside the scope of linear arithmetic, but it is easily found if
 6130  we explicitly split n<2 as n=0 or n=1:
 6131  apply (subgoal_tac "n=0 | n=1", force, arith)
 6132  done

 6133  8.4.3 The Type of Integers, int

 6134  Reasoning methods for the integers resemble those for the natural numbers,
 6135  but induction and the constant Suc are not available. HOL provides many
=== PAGE 177 ===

 6136  8.4 Numbers 167

 6137  lemmas for proving inequalities involving integer multiplication and division,
 6138  similar to those shown above for type nat. The laws of addition, subtraction

 6139  and multiplication are available through the axiomatic type class for rings

 6140  (Sect. 8.4.5).
 6141  The absolute value function abs is overloaded, and is defined for all types
 6142  that involve negative numbers, including the integers. The arith method can
 6143  prove facts about abs automatically, though as it does so by case analysis,

 6144  the cost can be exponential.
 6145  lemma "abs (x+y) ≤ abs x + abs (y :: int)"
 6146  by arith

 6147  For division and remainder, the treatment of negative divisors follows

 6148  mathematical practice: the sign of the remainder follows that of the divisor:
 6149  0 < b =⇒ 0 ≤ a mod b (pos_mod_sign)
 6150  0 < b =⇒ a mod b < b (pos_mod_bound)
 6151  b < 0 =⇒ a mod b ≤ 0 (neg_mod_sign)
 6152  b < 0 =⇒ b < a mod b (neg_mod_bound)

 6153  ML treats negative divisors in the same way, but most computer hardware

 6154  treats signed operands using the same rules as for multiplication. Many facts

 6155  about quotients and remainders are provided:
 6156  (a + b) div c =
 6157  a div c + b div c + (a mod c + b mod c) div c (div_add1_eq)
 6158  (a + b) mod c = (a mod c + b mod c) mod c (mod_add_eq)
 6159  (a * b) div c = a * (b div c) + a * (b mod c) div c (zdiv_zmult1_eq)
 6160  (a * b) mod c = a * (b mod c) mod c (zmod_zmult1_eq)
 6161  0 < c =⇒ a div (b*c) = a div b div c (zdiv_zmult2_eq)
 6162  0 < c =⇒ a mod (b*c) = b*(a div b mod c) + a mod b (zmod_zmult2_eq)
 6163  The last two differ from their natural number analogues by requiring c to
 6164  be positive. Since division by zero yields zero, we could allow c to be zero.
 6165  However, c cannot be negative: a counterexample is a = 7, b = 2 and c = −3,
 6166  when the left-hand side of zdiv_zmult2_eq is −2 while the right-hand side
 6167  is −1. The prefix z in many theorem names recalls the use of Z to denote the

 6168  set of integers.

 6169  Induction is less important for integers than it is for the natural numbers,

 6170  but it can be valuable if the range of integers has a lower or upper bound.

 6171  There are four rules for integer induction, corresponding to the possible re-

 6172  lations of the bound (≥, >, ≤ and <):
 6173  [[k ≤ i; P k; Vi. [[k ≤ i; P i ]] =⇒ P(i+1) ]] =⇒ P i (int_ge_induct)
 6174  [[k < i; P(k+1); Vi. [[k < i; P i]] =⇒ P(i+1) ]] =⇒ P i (int_gr_induct)
 6175  [[i ≤ k; P k; Vi. [[i ≤ k; P i ]] =⇒ P(i-1) ]] =⇒ P i (int_le_induct)
 6176  [[i < k; P(k-1); Vi. [[i < k; P i]] =⇒ P(i-1) ]] =⇒ P i (int_less_induct)
=== PAGE 178 ===

 6177  168 8. More about Types

 6178  8.4.4 The Types of Rational, Real and Complex Numbers

 6179  These types provide true division, the overloaded operator /, which differs
 6180  from the operator div of the natural numbers and integers. The rationals

 6181  and reals are dense: between every two distinct numbers lies another. This

 6182  property follows from the division laws, since if x 6= y then (x + y)/2 lies

 6183  between them:
 6184  a < b =⇒ ∃ r. a < r ∧ r < b (dense)

 6185  The real numbers are, moreover, complete: every set of reals that is

 6186  bounded above has a least upper bound. Completeness distinguishes the reals
 6187  from the rationals, for which the set {x | x2 < 2} has no least upper bound.

 6188  √

 6189  (It could only be 2, which is irrational.) The formalization of completeness,

 6190  which is complicated, can be found in theory RComplete.
 6191  Numeric literals for type real have the same syntax as those for type
 6192  int and only express integral values. Fractions expressed using the division

 6193  operator are automatically simplified to lowest terms:
 6194  1. P ((3 / 4) * (8 / 15))
 6195  apply simp
 6196  1. P (2 / 5)
 6197  Exponentiation can express floating-point values such as 2 * 10ˆ6, which will

 6198  be simplified to integers.
 6199  !!Types rat, real and complex are provided by theory HOL-Complex, which is
 6200  Main extended with a definitional development of the rational, real and complex
 6201  numbers. Base your theory upon theory Complex_Main, not the usual Main.
 6202  Available in the logic HOL-NSA is the theory Hyperreal, which define the
 6203  type hypreal of non-standard reals. These hyperreals include infinitesimals,

 6204  which represent infinitely small and infinitely large quantities; they facilitate

 6205  proofs about limits, differentiation and integration [8]. The development defines an infinitely large number, omega and an infinitely small positive number, epsilon. The relation x ≈ y means "x is infinitely close to y." Theory
 6206  Hyperreal also defines transcendental functions such as sine, cosine, exponential and logarithm — even the versions for type real, because they are

 6207  defined using nonstandard limits.

 6208  8.4.5 The Numeric Type Classes

 6209  Isabelle/HOL organises its numeric theories using axiomatic type classes.
 6210  Hundreds of basic properties are proved in the theory Ring_and_Field. These

 6211  lemmas are available (as simprules if they were declared as such) for all

 6212  numeric types satisfying the necessary axioms. The theory defines dozens of

 6213  type classes, such as the following:
=== PAGE 179 ===

 6214  8.4 Numbers 169

 6215  – semiring and ordered_semiring: a semiring provides the associative operators + and *, with 0 and 1 as their respective identities. The operators
 6216  enjoy the usual distributive law, and addition (+ ) is also commutative. An

 6217  ordered semiring is also linearly ordered, with addition and multiplication
 6218  respecting the ordering. Type nat is an ordered semiring.
 6219  – ring and ordered_ring : a ring extends a semiring with unary minus (the
 6220  additive inverse) and subtraction (both denoted -). An ordered ring includes the absolute value function, abs. Type int is an ordered ring.
 6221  – field and ordered_field: a field extends a ring with the multiplicative
 6222  inverse (called simply inverse and division (/)). An ordered field is based
 6223  on an ordered ring. Type complex is a field, while type real is an ordered

 6224  field.
 6225  – division_by_zero includes all types where inverse 0 = 0 and a / 0 = 0.

 6226  These include all of Isabelle's standard numeric types. However, the basic

 6227  properties of fields are derived without assuming division by zero.

 6228  Hundreds of basic lemmas are proved, each of which holds for all types in

 6229  the corresponding type class. In most cases, it is obvious whether a property

 6230  is valid for a particular type. No abstract properties involving subtraction
 6231  hold for type nat ; instead, theorems such as diff_mult_distrib are proved
 6232  specifically about subtraction on type nat. All abstract properties involving

 6233  division require a field. Obviously, all properties involving orderings required

 6234  an ordered structure.
 6235  The class ring_no_zero_divisors of rings without zero divisors satisfies

 6236  a number of natural cancellation laws, the first of which characterizes this

 6237  class:
 6238  (a * b = (0::'a)) = (a = (0::'a) ∨ b = (0::'a)) (mult_eq_0_iff)
 6239  (a * c = b * c) = (c = (0::'a) ∨ a = b) (mult_cancel_right)

 6240  Setting the flag Isabelle > Settings > Show Sorts will display the type classes of
 6241  all type variables.
 6242  Here is how the theorem mult_cancel_left appears with the flag set.
 6243  ((c::'a::ring_no_zero_divisors) * (a::'a::ring_no_zero_divisors) =
 6244  c * (b::'a::ring_no_zero_divisors)) =
 6245  (c = (0::'a::ring_no_zero_divisors) ∨ a = b)

 6246  Simplifying with the AC-Laws. Suppose that two expressions are equal,

 6247  differing only in associativity and commutativity of addition. Simplifying with

 6248  the following equations sorts the terms and groups them to the right, making

 6249  the two expressions identical.
 6250  a + b + c = a + (b + c) (add.assoc)
 6251  a + b = b + a (add.commute)
 6252  a + (b + c) = b + (a + c) (add.left_commute)
=== PAGE 180 ===

 6253  170 8. More about Types

 6254  The name ac_simps refers to the list of all three theorems; similarly there is
 6255  ac_simps. They are all proved for semirings and therefore hold for all numeric

 6256  types.

 6257  Here is an example of the sorting effect. Start with this goal, which involves type nat.
 6258  1. Suc (i + j * l * k + m * n) = f (n * m + i + k * j * l)
 6259  Simplify using ac_simps and ac_simps.
 6260  apply (simp add: ac_simps ac_simps)

 6261  Here is the resulting subgoal.
 6262  1. Suc (i + (m * n + j * (k * l))) = f (i + (m * n + j * (k * l)))

 6263  Division Laws for Fields. Here is a selection of rules about the division

 6264  operator. The following are installed as default simplification rules in order

 6265  to express combinations of products and quotients as rational expressions:
 6266  a * (b / c) = a * b / c (times_divide_eq_right)
 6267  b / c * a = b * a / c (times_divide_eq_left)
 6268  a / (b / c) = a * c / b (divide_divide_eq_right)
 6269  a / b / c = a / (b * c) (divide_divide_eq_left)

 6270  Signs are extracted from quotients in the hope that complementary terms

 6271  can then be cancelled:
 6272  - (a / b) = - a / b (minus_divide_left)
 6273  - (a / b) = a / - b (minus_divide_right)

 6274  The following distributive law is available, but it is not installed as a

 6275  simplification rule.
 6276  (a + b) / c = a / c + b / c (add_divide_distrib)
 6277  Absolute Value. The absolute value function abs is available for all ordered
 6278  rings, including types int, rat and real. It satisfies many properties, such as

 6279  the following:
 6280  |x * y| = |x | * |y | (abs_mult)
 6281  (|a | ≤ b) = (a ≤ b ∧ - a ≤ b) (abs_le_iff)
 6282  |a + b| ≤ |a | + |b | (abs_triangle_ineq)

 6283  !!The absolute value bars shown above cannot be typed on a keyboard. They can
 6284  be entered using the X-symbol package. In ascii, type abs x to get |x |.

 6285  Raising to a Power. Another type class, ordered idom , specifies rings that

 6286  also have exponentation to a natural number power, defined using the obvious primitive recursion. Theory Power proves various theorems, such as the

 6287  following.
 6288  a ˆ (m + n) = a ˆ m * a ˆ n (power_add)
 6289  a ˆ (m * n) = (a ˆ m) ˆ n (power_mult)
 6290  |a ˆ n| = |a | ˆ n (power_abs)
=== PAGE 181 ===

 6291  8.5 Introducing New Types 171

 6292  8.5 Introducing New Types

 6293  For most applications, a combination of predefined types like bool and ⇒

 6294  with recursive datatypes and records is quite sufficient. Very occasionally

 6295  you may feel the need for a more advanced type. If you are certain that your

 6296  type is not definable by any of the standard means, then read on.
 6297  !!Types in HOL must be non-empty; otherwise the quantifier rules would be
 6298  unsound, because ∃x . x = x is a theorem.

 6299  8.5.1 Declaring New Types

 6300  The most trivial way of introducing a new type is by a type declaration:
 6301  typedecl my_new_type
 6302  This does not define my_new_type at all but merely introduces its name. Thus

 6303  we know nothing about this type, except that it is non-empty. Such declara-

 6304  tions without definitions are useful if that type can be viewed as a parameter

 6305  of the theory. A typical example is given in Sect. ??, where we define a tran-

 6306  sition relation over an arbitrary type of states.

 6307  In principle we can always get rid of such type declarations by making

 6308  those types parameters of every other type, thus keeping the theory generic.

 6309  In practice, however, the resulting clutter can make types hard to read.

 6310  If you are looking for a quick and dirty way of introducing a new type to-

 6311  gether with its properties: declare the type and state its properties as axioms.

 6312  Example:
 6313  axiomatization where
 6314  just_one: "∃ x::my_new_type. ∀ y. x = y"

 6315  However, we strongly discourage this approach, except at explorative stages

 6316  of your development. It is extremely easy to write down contradictory sets

 6317  of axioms, in which case you will be able to prove everything but it will

 6318  mean nothing. In the example above, the axiomatic approach is unnecessary:
 6319  a one-element type called unit is already defined in HOL.

 6320  8.5.2 Defining New Types

 6321  Now we come to the most general means of safely introducing a new type,

 6322  the type definition. All other means, for example datatype, are based on

 6323  it. The principle is extremely simple: any non-empty subset of an existing

 6324  type can be turned into a new type. More precisely, the new type is specified

 6325  to be isomorphic to some non-empty subset of an existing type.

 6326  Let us work a simple example, the definition of a three-element type. It

 6327  is easily represented by the first three natural numbers:
=== PAGE 182 ===

 6328  172 8. More about Types

 6329  typedef three = "{0::nat, 1, 2}"

 6330  In order to enforce that the representing set on the right-hand side is non-

 6331  empty, this definition actually starts a proof to that effect:
 6332  1. ∃ x. x ∈ {0, 1, 2}
 6333  Fortunately, this is easy enough to show, even auto could do it. In general,

 6334  one has to provide a witness, in our case 0:
 6335  apply(rule_tac x = 0 in exI)
 6336  by simp
 6337  This type definition introduces the new type three and asserts that it is a
 6338  copy of the set {0, 1, 2}. This assertion is expressed via a bijection between
 6339  the type three and the set {0, 1, 2}. To this end, the command declares the

 6340  following constants behind the scenes:
 6341  Rep_three :: three ⇒ nat
 6342  Abs_three :: nat ⇒ three

 6343  The situation is best summarized with the help of the following diagram,

 6344  where squares denote types and the irregular region denotes a set:

 6345  nat

 6346  three {0,1,2}

 6347  Finally, typedef asserts that Rep_three is surjective on the subset and
 6348  Abs_three and Rep_three are inverses of each other:

 6349  Rep_three x ∈ {0, 1, 2} (Rep_three )
 6350  Abs_three (Rep_three x) = x (Rep_three_inverse )
 6351  y ∈ {0, 1, 2} =⇒ Rep_three (Abs_three y) = y (Abs_three_inverse )

 6352  From this example it should be clear what typedef does in general given a
 6353  name (here three ) and a set (here {0, 1, 2}).

 6354  Our next step is to define the basic functions expected on the new type.

 6355  Although this depends on the type at hand, the following strategy works well:

 6356  – define a small kernel of basic functions that can express all other functions

 6357  you anticipate.

 6358  – define the kernel in terms of corresponding functions on the representing
 6359  type using Abs and Rep to convert between the two levels.
=== PAGE 183 ===

 6360  8.5 Introducing New Types 173

 6361  In our example it suffices to give the three elements of type three names:
 6362  definition A :: three where "A ≡ Abs_three 0"
 6363  definition B :: three where "B ≡ Abs_three 1"
 6364  definition C :: three where "C ≡ Abs_three 2"
 6365  So far, everything was easy. But it is clear that reasoning about three
 6366  will be hell if we have to go back to nat every time. Thus our aim must be to
 6367  raise our level of abstraction by deriving enough theorems about type three

 6368  to characterize it completely. And those theorems should be phrased in terms
 6369  of A, B and C, not Abs_three and Rep_three. Because of the simplicity of the
 6370  example, we merely need to prove that A, B and C are distinct and that they

 6371  exhaust the type.

 6372  In processing our typedef declaration, Isabelle proves several helpful lemmas. The first two express injectivity of Rep_three and Abs_three:
 6373  (Rep_three x = Rep_three y) = (x = y) (Rep_three_inject )
 6374  [[x ∈ {0, 1, 2}; y ∈ {0, 1, 2} ]]
 6375  (Abs_three_inject )
 6376  =⇒ (Abs_three x = Abs_three y) = (x = y)
 6377  The following ones allow to replace some x::three by Abs_three(y::nat), and
 6378  conversely y by Rep_three x:

 6379  [[y ∈ {0, 1, 2}; Vx. y = Rep_three x =⇒ P ]] =⇒ P (Rep_three_cases )
 6380  (Vy. [[x = Abs_three y; y ∈ {0, 1, 2}]] =⇒ P) =⇒ P (Abs_three_cases )
 6381  [[y ∈ {0, 1, 2}; Vx. P (Rep_three x)]] =⇒ P y (Rep_three_induct )
 6382  (Vy. y ∈ {0, 1, 2} =⇒ P (Abs_three y)) =⇒ P x (Abs_three_induct )
 6383  These theorems are proved for any type definition, with three replaced by

 6384  the name of the type in question.
 6385  Distinctness of A, B and C follows immediately if we expand their definitions and rewrite with the injectivity of Abs_three:
 6386  lemma "A 6= B ∧ B 6= A ∧ A 6= C ∧ C 6= A ∧ B 6= C ∧ C 6= B"
 6387  by(simp add: Abs_three_inject A_def B_def C_def)
 6388  Of course we rely on the simplifier to solve goals like 0 6= 1.
 6389  The fact that A, B and C exhaust type three is best phrased as a case
 6390  distinction theorem: if you want to prove P x (where x is of type three ) it
 6391  suffices to prove P A, P B and P C :
 6392  lemma three_cases: "[[ P A; P B; P C ]] =⇒ P x"

 6393  Again this follows easily using the induction principle stemming from the

 6394  type definition:
 6395  apply(induct_tac x)

 6396  1. Vy. [[P A; P B; P C; y ∈ {0, 1, 2}]] =⇒ P (Abs_three y)
 6397  Simplification leads to the disjunction y = 0 ∨ y = 1 ∨ y = 2 which auto

 6398  separates into three subgoals, each of which is easily solved by simplification:
=== PAGE 184 ===

 6399  174 8. More about Types

 6400  apply(auto simp add: A_def B_def C_def)
 6401  done
 6402  This concludes the derivation of the characteristic theorems for type three.

 6403  The attentive reader has realized long ago that the above lengthy defini-

 6404  tion can be collapsed into one line:
 6405  datatype better_three = A | B | C

 6406  In fact, the datatype command performs internally more or less the same

 6407  derivations as we did, which gives you some idea what life would be like

 6408  without datatype.
 6409  Although three could be defined in one line, we have chosen this exam-

 6410  ple to demonstrate typedef because its simplicity makes the key concepts

 6411  particularly easy to grasp. If you would like to see a non-trivial example

 6412  that cannot be defined more directly, we recommend the definition of finite

 6413  multisets in the Library [4].

 6414  Let us conclude by summarizing the above procedure for defining a new

 6415  type. Given some abstract axiomatic description P of a type ty in terms of a

 6416  set of functions F, this involves three steps:

 6417  1. Find an appropriate type τ and subset A which has the desired properties

 6418  P, and make a type definition based on this representation.

 6419  2. Define the required functions F on ty by lifting analogous functions on

 6420  the representation via Abs ty and Rep ty.

 6421  3. Prove that P holds for ty by lifting P from the representation.

 6422  You can now forget about the representation and work solely in terms of the

 6423  abstract functions F and properties P.
=== PAGE 185 ===

 6424  9. Advanced Simplification and Induction

 6425  Although we have already learned a lot about simplification and induction,

 6426  there are some advanced proof techniques that we have not covered yet and

 6427  which are worth learning. The sections of this chapter are independent of

 6428  each other and can be read in any order.

 6429  9.1 Simplification

 6430  This section describes features not covered until now. It also outlines the

 6431  simplification process itself, which can be helpful when the simplifier does

 6432  not do what you expect of it.

 6433  9.1.1 Advanced Features

 6434  Congruence Rules. While simplifying the conclusion Q of P =⇒ Q, it is

 6435  legal to use the assumption P. For =⇒ this policy is hardwired, but contextual
 6436  information can also be made available for other operators. For example, xs
 6437  = [] −→ xs @ xs = xs simplifies to True because we may use xs = [] when
 6438  simplifying xs @ xs = xs. The generation of contextual information during

 6439  simplification is controlled by so-called congruence rules. This is the one
 6440  for −→:
 6441  [[P = P'; P' =⇒ Q = Q']] =⇒ (P −→ Q) = (P' −→ Q')
 6442  It should be read as follows: In order to simplify P −→ Q to P' −→ Q', simplify P to P' and assume P' when simplifying Q to Q'.

 6443  Here are some more examples. The congruence rules for bounded quanti-

 6444  fiers supply contextual information about the bound variable:
 6445  [[A = B; Vx. x ∈ B =⇒ P x = Q x]]
 6446  =⇒ (∀ x ∈A. P x) = (∀ x ∈B. Q x)

 6447  One congruence rule for conditional expressions supplies contextual information for simplifying the then and else cases:
 6448  [[b = c; c =⇒ x = u; ¬ c =⇒ y = v]]
 6449  =⇒ (if b then x else y) = (if c then u else v)
=== PAGE 186 ===

 6450  176 9. Advanced Simplification and Induction

 6451  An alternative congruence rule for conditional expressions actually prevents

 6452  simplification of some arguments:
 6453  b = c =⇒ (if b then x else y) = (if c then x else y)

 6454  Only the first argument is simplified; the others remain unchanged. This

 6455  makes simplification much faster and is faithful to the evaluation strategy in

 6456  programming languages, which is why this is the default congruence rule for
 6457  if. Analogous rules control the evaluation of case expressions.
 6458  You can declare your own congruence rules with the attribute cong , either

 6459  globally, in the usual manner,
 6460  declare theorem-name [cong]
 6461  or locally in a simp call by adding the modifier
 6462  cong: list of theorem names
 6463  The effect is reversed by cong del instead of cong.
 6464  !!The congruence rule conj_cong
 6465  [[P = P'; P' =⇒ Q = Q']] =⇒ (P ∧ Q) = (P' ∧ Q')
 6466  is occasionally useful but is not a default rule; you have to declare it explicitly.

 6467  Permutative Rewrite Rules. An equation is a permutative rewrite

 6468  rule if the left-hand side and right-hand side are the same up to renaming of
 6469  variables. The most common permutative rule is commutativity: x + y = y +
 6470  x. Other examples include x - y - z = x - z - y in arithmetic and insert
 6471  x (insert y A) = insert y (insert x A) for sets. Such rules are problematic

 6472  because once they apply, they can be used forever. The simplifier is aware of

 6473  this danger and treats permutative rules by means of a special strategy, called

 6474  ordered rewriting: a permutative rewrite rule is only applied if the term

 6475  becomes smaller with respect to a fixed lexicographic ordering on terms. For
 6476  example, commutativity rewrites b + a to a + b, but then stops because a +
 6477  b is strictly smaller than b + a. Permutative rewrite rules can be turned into
 6478  simplification rules in the usual manner via the simp attribute; the simplifier

 6479  recognizes their special status automatically.

 6480  Permutative rewrite rules are most effective in the case of associative-

 6481  commutative functions. (Associativity by itself is not permutative.) When

 6482  dealing with an AC-function f, keep the following points in mind:

 6483  – The associative law must always be oriented from left to right, namely

 6484  f(f(x,y), z) = f(x, f(y, z)). The opposite orientation, if used with commu-

 6485  tativity, can lead to nontermination.

 6486  – To complete your set of rewrite rules, you must add not just associativ-

 6487  ity (A) and commutativity (C) but also a derived rule, left-commutativ-

 6488  ity (LC): f(x,f (y,z)) = f(y, f(x, z)).
=== PAGE 187 ===

 6489  9.1 Simplification 177

 6490  Ordered rewriting with the combination of A, C, and LC sorts a term lexi-

 6491  cographically:

 6492  f(f(b, c), a) ;A f(b, f(c, a)) ;C f(b, f(a, c)) LC; f (a,f(b,c))

 6493  Note that ordered rewriting for + and * on numbers is rarely necessary

 6494  because the built-in arithmetic prover often succeeds without such tricks.

 6495  9.1.2 How the Simplifier Works

 6496  Roughly speaking, the simplifier proceeds bottom-up: subterms are simplified

 6497  first. A conditional equation is only applied if its condition can be proved,

 6498  again by simplification. Below we explain some special features of the rewrit-

 6499  ing process.

 6500  Higher-Order Patterns. So far we have pretended the simplifier can deal

 6501  with arbitrary rewrite rules. This is not quite true. For reasons of feasibility,

 6502  the simplifier expects the left-hand side of each rule to be a so-called higher-

 6503  order pattern [23]. This restricts where unknowns may occur. Higher-order

 6504  patterns are terms in β-normal form. (This means there are no subterms

 6505  of the form (λx.M )(N).) Each occurrence of an unknown is of the form
 6506  ?f x1 . . . xn, where the xi are distinct bound variables. Thus all ordinary
 6507  rewrite rules, where all unknowns are of base type, for example ?a + ?b + ?c
 6508  = ?a + (?b + ?c), are acceptable: if an unknown is of base type, it cannot
 6509  have any arguments. Additionally, the rule (∀ x. ?P x ∧ ?Q x) = ((∀ x. ?P
 6510  x) ∧ (∀ x. ?Q x)) is also acceptable, in both directions: all arguments of the
 6511  unknowns ?P and ?Q are distinct bound variables.

 6512  If the left-hand side is not a higher-order pattern, all is not lost. The

 6513  simplifier will still try to apply the rule provided it matches directly: without much λ-calculus hocus pocus. For example, (?f ?x ∈ range ?f) = True
 6514  rewrites g a ∈ range g to True, but will fail to match g(h b) ∈ range(λx.
 6515  g(h x)). However, you can eliminate the offending subterms — those that are

 6516  not patterns — by adding new variables and conditions. In our example, we
 6517  eliminate ?f ?x and obtain ?y = ?f ?x =⇒ (?y ∈ range ?f) = True, which

 6518  is fine as a conditional rewrite rule since conditions can be arbitrary terms.

 6519  However, this trick is not a panacea because the newly introduced conditions

 6520  may be hard to solve.

 6521  There is no restriction on the form of the right-hand sides. They may not

 6522  contain extraneous term or type variables, though.

 6523  The Preprocessor. When a theorem is declared a simplification rule, it

 6524  need not be a conditional equation already. The simplifier will turn it into a
 6525  set of conditional equations automatically. For example, f x = g x ∧ h x =
 6526  k x becomes the two separate simplification rules f x = g x and h x = k x.

 6527  In general, the input theorem is converted as follows:
=== PAGE 188 ===

 6528  178 9. Advanced Simplification and Induction
 6529  ¬P 7→ P = False

 6530  P −→ Q 7→ P =⇒ Q

 6531  P ∧ Q 7→ P, Q

 6532  ∀x. P x 7→ P ?x

 6533  ∀x ∈ A. P x 7→ ?x ∈ A =⇒ P ?x
 6534  if P then Q else R 7→ P =⇒ Q, ¬P =⇒ R

 6535  Once this conversion process is finished, all remaining non-equations P are
 6536  turned into trivial equations P = True. For example, the formula
 6537  (p −→ t = u ∧ ¬ r) ∧ s

 6538  is converted into the three rules
 6539  p =⇒ t = u, p =⇒ r = False, s = True.

 6540  9.2 Advanced Induction Techniques

 6541  Now that we have learned about rules and logic, we take another look at

 6542  the finer points of induction. We consider two questions: what to do if the

 6543  proposition to be proved is not directly amenable to induction (Sect. 9.2.1),

 6544  and how to utilize (Sect. 9.2.2) and even derive (Sect. 9.2.3) new induction

 6545  schemas. We conclude with an extended example of induction (Sect. 9.2.4).

 6546  9.2.1 Massaging the Proposition

 6547  Often we have assumed that the theorem to be proved is already in a form

 6548  that is amenable to induction, but sometimes it isn't. Here is an example.
 6549  Since hd and last return the first and last element of a non-empty list, this

 6550  lemma looks easy to prove:
 6551  lemma "xs 6= [] =⇒ hd(rev xs) = last xs"
 6552  apply(induct_tac xs)

 6553  But induction produces the warning

 6554  Induction variable occurs also among premises!

 6555  and leads to the base case
 6556  1. xs 6= [] =⇒ hd (rev []) = last []
 6557  A total of 2 subgoals...

 6558  Simplification reduces the base case to this:
 6559  1. xs 6= [] =⇒ hd [] = last []
=== PAGE 189 ===

 6560  9.2 Advanced Induction Techniques 179

 6561  We cannot prove this equality because we do not know what hd and last
 6562  return when applied to [].

 6563  We should not have ignored the warning. Because the induction formula
 6564  is only the conclusion, induction does not affect the occurrence of xs in the

 6565  premises. Thus the case that should have been trivial becomes unprovable.
 6566  Fortunately, the solution is easy:1

 6567  Pull all occurrences of the induction variable into the conclusion using −→.
 6568  Thus we should state the lemma as an ordinary implication (−→), letting
 6569  rule_format (Sect. 5.15) convert the result to the usual =⇒ form:
 6570  lemma hd_rev [rule_format]: "xs 6= [] −→ hd(rev xs) = last xs"

 6571  This time, induction leaves us with a trivial base case:
 6572  1. [] 6= [] −→ hd (rev []) = last []
 6573  A total of 2 subgoals...
 6574  And auto completes the proof.
 6575  If there are multiple premises A1, . . . , An containing the induction vari-

 6576  able, you should turn the conclusion C into

 6577  A1 −→ · · ·An −→ C.

 6578  Additionally, you may also have to universally quantify some other variables,
 6579  which can yield a fairly complex conclusion. However, rule_format can remove any number of occurrences of ∀ and −→.

 6580  A second reason why your proposition may not be amenable to induction

 6581  is that you want to induct on a complex term, rather than a variable. In

 6582  general, induction on a term t requires rephrasing the conclusion C as

 6583  ∀y1 . . . yn. x = t −→ C. (9.1)

 6584  where y1 . . . yn are the free variables in t and x is a new variable. Now you

 6585  can perform induction on x. An example appears in Sect. 9.2.2 below.

 6586  The very same problem may occur in connection with rule induction.

 6587  Remember that it requires a premise of the form (x , . . . ,x ) ∈ R, where R
 6588  1 k
 6589  is some inductively defined set and the xi are variables. If instead we have a

 6590  premise t ∈ R, where t is not just an n-tuple of variables, we replace it with
 6591  (x1, . . . ,xk) ∈ R, and rephrase the conclusion C as

 6592  ∀y1 . . . yn. (x1, . . . ,xk) = t −→ C.

 6593  For an example see Sect. 9.2.4 below.

 6594  Of course, all premises that share free variables with t need to be pulled
 6595  into the conclusion as well, under the ∀ , again using −→ as shown above.
 6596  1 A similar heuristic applies to rule inductions; see Sect. 7.2.
=== PAGE 190 ===

 6597  180 9. Advanced Simplification and Induction

 6598  Readers who are puzzled by the form of statement (9.1) above should

 6599  remember that the transformation is only performed to permit induction.

 6600  Once induction has been applied, the statement can be transformed back

 6601  into something quite intuitive. For example, applying wellfounded induction

 6602  on x (w.r.t. ≺) to (9.1) and transforming the result a little leads to the goal

 6603  ^y. ∀z. t z ≺ t y −→ C z =⇒ C y

 6604  where y stands for y1 . . . yn and the dependence of t and C on the free vari-

 6605  ables of t has been made explicit. Unfortunately, this induction schema cannot

 6606  be expressed as a single theorem because it depends on the number of free

 6607  variables in t — the notation y is merely an informal device.

 6608  9.2.2 Beyond Structural and Recursion Induction

 6609  So far, inductive proofs were by structural induction for primitive recursive

 6610  functions and recursion induction for total recursive functions. But some-

 6611  times structural induction is awkward and there is no recursive function that

 6612  could furnish a more appropriate induction schema. In such cases a general-

 6613  purpose induction schema can be helpful. We show how to apply such induc-

 6614  tion schemas by an example.
 6615  Structural induction on nat is usually known as mathematical induc-

 6616  tion. There is also complete induction, where you prove P(n) under the

 6617  assumption that P(m) holds for all m < n. In Isabelle, this is the theorem
 6618  nat_less_induct:
 6619  (Vn. ∀ m<n. P m =⇒ P n) =⇒ P n

 6620  As an application, we prove a property of the following function:
 6621  axiomatization f :: "nat ⇒ nat"
 6622  where f_ax: "f(f(n)) < f(Suc(n))" for n :: nat
