 6623  !!We discourage the use of axioms because of the danger of inconsistencies. Axiom f_ax does not introduce an inconsistency because, for example, the identity
 6624  function satisfies it. Axioms can be useful in exploratory developments, say when
 6625  you assume some well-known theorems so that you can quickly demonstrate some
 6626  point about methodology. If your example turns into a substantial proof development, you should replace axioms by theorems.
 6627  The axiom for f implies n ≤ f n, which can be proved by induction on f n .

 6628  Following the recipe outlined above, we have to phrase the proposition as

 6629  follows to allow induction:
 6630  lemma f_incr_lem: "∀ i. k = f i −→ i ≤ f i"
 6631  To perform induction on k using nat_less_induct, we use the same general

 6632  induction method as for recursion induction (see Sect. 3.5.4):
 6633  apply(induct_tac k rule: nat_less_induct)

 6634  We get the following proof state:
=== PAGE 191 ===

 6635  9.2 Advanced Induction Techniques 181

 6636  1. Vn. ∀ m<n. ∀ i. m = f i −→ i ≤ f i =⇒ ∀ i. n = f i −→ i ≤ f i
 6637  After stripping the ∀ i, the proof continues with a case distinction on i. The
 6638  case i = 0 is trivial and we focus on the other case:
 6639  apply(rule allI)
 6640  apply(case_tac i)
 6641  apply(simp)

 6642  1. Vn i nat.
 6643  [[∀ m<n. ∀ i. m = f i −→ i ≤ f i; i = Suc nat]] =⇒ n = f i −→ i
 6644  ≤ f i

 6645  by(blast intro!: f_ax Suc_leI intro: le_less_trans)

 6646  If you find the last step puzzling, here are the two lemmas it employs:
 6647  m < n =⇒ Suc m ≤ n (Suc_leI)
 6648  [[x ≤ y; y < z ]] =⇒ x < z (le_less_trans)
 6649  The proof goes like this (writing j instead of nat). Since i = Suc j it suffices to show j < f (Suc j), by Suc_leI . This is proved as follows. From f_ax
 6650  we have f (f j) < f (Suc j) (1) which implies f j ≤ f (f j) by the induction hypothesis. Using (1) once more we obtain f j < f (Suc j) (2) by the
 6651  transitivity rule le_less_trans. Using the induction hypothesis once more
 6652  we obtain j ≤ f j which, together with (2) yields j < f (Suc j) (again by
 6653  le_less_trans).

 6654  This last step shows both the power and the danger of automatic proofs.

 6655  They will usually not tell you how the proof goes, because it can be hard to

 6656  translate the internal proof into a human-readable format. Automatic proofs

 6657  are easy to write but hard to read and understand.
 6658  The desired result, i ≤ f i, follows from f_incr_lem:
 6659  lemmas f_incr = f_incr_lem[rule_format, OF refl]
 6660  The final refl gets rid of the premise ?k = f ?i. We could have included this

 6661  derivation in the original statement of the lemma:
 6662  lemma f_incr[rule_format, OF refl]: "∀ i. k = f i −→ i ≤ f i"

 6663  Exercise 9.2.1 From the axiom and lemma for f, show that f is the identity

 6664  function.
 6665  Method induct_tac can be applied with any rule r whose conclusion is of
 6666  the form ?P ?x1 . . .?xn, in which case the format is
 6667  apply(induct_tac y1 . . . yn rule: r)
 6668  where y1, . . . ,yn are variables in the conclusion of the first subgoal.
 6669  A further useful induction rule is length_induct, induction on the length

 6670  of a list
 6671  (Vxs. ∀ ys. length ys < length xs −→ P ys =⇒ P xs) =⇒ P xs
=== PAGE 192 ===

 6672  182 9. Advanced Simplification and Induction
 6673  which is a special case of measure_induct
 6674  (Vx. ∀ y. f y < f x −→ P y =⇒ P x) =⇒ P a
 6675  where f may be any function into type nat.

 6676  9.2.3 Derivation of New Induction Schemas

 6677  Induction schemas are ordinary theorems and you can derive new ones

 6678  whenever you wish. This section shows you how, using the example of
 6679  nat_less_induct. Assume we only have structural induction available for nat

 6680  and want to derive complete induction. We must generalize the statement as

 6681  shown:
 6682  lemma induct_lem: "(Vn::nat. ∀ m<n. P m =⇒ P n) =⇒ ∀ m<n. P m"
 6683  apply(induct_tac n)
 6684  The base case is vacuously true. For the induction step (m < Suc n) we distinguish two cases: case m < n is true by induction hypothesis and case m =
 6685  n follows from the assumption, again using the induction hypothesis:
 6686  apply(blast)
 6687  by(blast elim: less_SucE)
 6688  The elimination rule less_SucE expresses the case distinction:
 6689  [[m < Suc n; m < n =⇒ P; m = n =⇒ P]] =⇒ P
 6690  Now it is straightforward to derive the original version of nat_less_induct
 6691  by manipulating the conclusion of the above lemma: instantiate n by Suc n
 6692  and m by n and remove the trivial condition n < Suc n. Fortunately, this

 6693  happens automatically when we add the lemma as a new premise to the

 6694  desired goal:
 6695  theorem nat_less_induct: "(Vn::nat. ∀ m<n. P m =⇒ P n) =⇒ P n"
 6696  by(insert induct_lem, blast)

 6697  HOL already provides the mother of all inductions, well-founded induction (see Sect. 6.4). For example theorem nat_less_induct is a special
 6698  case of wf_induct where r is < on nat. The details can be found in theory
 6699  Wellfounded_Recursion.

 6700  9.2.4 CTL Revisited

 6701  The purpose of this section is twofold: to demonstrate some of the induction

 6702  principles and heuristics discussed above and to show how inductive defi-

 6703  nitions can simplify proofs. In Sect. 6.5.2 we gave a fairly involved proof of

 6704  the correctness of a model checker for CTL. In particular the proof of the
 6705  infinity_lemma on the way to AF_lemma2 is not as simple as one might expect,
=== PAGE 193 ===

 6706  9.2 Advanced Induction Techniques 183

 6707  due to the SOME operator involved. Below we give a simpler proof of AF_lemma2

 6708  based on an auxiliary inductive definition.
 6709  Let us call a (finite or infinite) path A-avoiding if it does not touch any
 6710  node in the set A. Then AF_lemma2 says that if no infinite path from some state
 6711  s is A-avoiding, then s ∈ lfp (af A). We prove this by inductively defining
 6712  the set Avoid s A of states reachable from s by a finite A-avoiding path:
 6713  inductive set
 6714  Avoid :: "state ⇒ state set ⇒ state set"
 6715  for s :: state and A :: "state set"
 6716  where
 6717  "s ∈ Avoid s A"
 6718  | " [[ t ∈ Avoid s A; t ∈/ A; (t,u) ∈ M ]] =⇒ u ∈ Avoid s A"
 6719  It is easy to see that for any infinite A-avoiding path f with f 0 ∈ Avoid
 6720  s A there is an infinite A -avoiding path starting with s because (by definition
 6721  of Avoid ) there is a finite A-avoiding path from s to f 0. The proof is by
 6722  induction on f 0 ∈ Avoid s A. However, this requires the following reformulation, as explained in Sect. 9.2.1 above; the rule_format directive undoes the

 6723  reformulation after the proof.
 6724  lemma ex_infinite_path[rule_format]:
 6725  "t ∈ Avoid s A =⇒
 6726  ∀ f ∈Paths t. (∀ i. f i ∈/ A) −→ (∃ p ∈Paths s. ∀ i. p i ∈/ A)"
 6727  apply(erule Avoid.induct)
 6728  apply(blast)
 6729  apply(clarify)
 6730  apply(drule_tac x = " λi. case i of 0 ⇒ t | Suc i ⇒ f i" in bspec)
 6731  apply(simp_all add: Paths_def split: nat.split)
 6732  done
 6733  The base case (t = s ) is trivial and proved by blast. In the induction step,
 6734  we have an infinite A -avoiding path f starting from u, a successor of t. Now
 6735  we simply instantiate the ∀ f ∈Paths t in the induction hypothesis by the
 6736  path starting with t and continuing with f. That is what the above λ-term
 6737  expresses. Simplification shows that this is a path starting with t and that

 6738  the instantiated induction hypothesis implies the conclusion.
 6739  Now we come to the key lemma. Assuming that no infinite A -avoiding
 6740  path starts from s, we want to show s ∈ lfp (af A). For the inductive proof
 6741  this must be generalized to the statement that every point t "between" s
 6742  and A, in other words all of Avoid s A, is contained in lfp (af A):
 6743  lemma Avoid_in_lfp[rule_format(no_asm)]:
 6744  " ∀p ∈Paths s. ∃ i. p i ∈ A =⇒ t ∈ Avoid s A −→ t ∈ lfp(af A)"
 6745  The proof is by induction on the "distance" between t and A. Remember that
 6746  lfp (af A) = A ∪ M−1 '' lfp (af A). If t is already in A, then t ∈ lfp (af
 6747  A) is trivial. If t is not in A but all successors are in lfp (af A) (induction
 6748  hypothesis), then t ∈ lfp (af A) is again trivial.

 6749  The formal counterpart of this proof sketch is a well-founded induction
 6750  on M restricted to Avoid s A - A, roughly speaking:
=== PAGE 194 ===

 6751  184 9. Advanced Simplification and Induction

 6752  {(y, x). (x, y) ∈ M ∧ x ∈ Avoid s A ∧ x ∈/ A}
 6753  As we shall see presently, the absence of infinite A -avoiding paths starting
 6754  from s implies well-foundedness of this relation. For the moment we assume

 6755  this and proceed with the induction:
 6756  apply(subgoal_tac "wf{(y,x). (x,y) ∈ M ∧ x ∈ Avoid s A ∧ x ∈/ A}")
 6757  apply(erule_tac a = t in wf_induct)
 6758  apply(clarsimp)

 6759  1. Vt. [[∀ p ∈Paths s. ∃ i. p i ∈ A;
 6760  ∀ y. (t, y) ∈ M ∧ t ∈/ A −→
 6761  y ∈ Avoid s A −→ y ∈ lfp (af A);
 6762  t ∈ Avoid s A]]
 6763  =⇒ t ∈ lfp (af A)
 6764  2. ∀ p ∈Paths s. ∃ i. p i ∈ A =⇒
 6765  wf {(y, x). (x, y) ∈ M ∧ x ∈ Avoid s A ∧ x ∈/ A}
 6766  Now the induction hypothesis states that if t ∈/ A then all successors of t
 6767  that are in Avoid s A are in lfp (af A). Unfolding lfp in the conclusion of
 6768  the first subgoal once, we have to prove that t is in A or all successors of
 6769  t are in lfp (af A). But if t is not in A, the second Avoid-rule implies that
 6770  all successors of t are in Avoid s A, because we also assume t ∈ Avoid s A.
 6771  Hence, by the induction hypothesis, all successors of t are indeed in lfp (af
 6772  A). Mechanically:
 6773  apply(subst lfp_unfold[OF mono_af])
 6774  apply(simp (no_asm) add: af_def)
 6775  apply(blast intro: Avoid.intros)

 6776  Having proved the main goal, we return to the proof obligation that the

 6777  relation used above is indeed well-founded. This is proved by contradiction: if
 6778  the relation is not well-founded then there exists an infinite A -avoiding path
 6779  all in Avoid s A, by theorem wf_iff_no_infinite_down_chain:
 6780  wf r = (@ f. ∀i. (f (Suc i), f i) ∈ r)
 6781  From lemma ex_infinite_path the existence of an infinite A -avoiding path
 6782  starting in s follows, contradiction.
 6783  apply(erule contrapos_pp)
 6784  apply(simp add: wf_iff_no_infinite_down_chain)
 6785  apply(erule exE)
 6786  apply(rule ex_infinite_path)
 6787  apply(auto simp add: Paths_def)
 6788  done
 6789  The (no_asm) modifier of the rule_format directive in the statement of
 6790  the lemma means that the assumption is left unchanged; otherwise the ∀ p
 6791  would be turned into a Vp, which would complicate matters below. As it is,
 6792  Avoid_in_lfp is now
 6793  [[∀ p ∈Paths s. ∃ i. p i ∈ A; t ∈ Avoid s A]] =⇒ t ∈ lfp (af A)
=== PAGE 195 ===

 6794  9.2 Advanced Induction Techniques 185
 6795  The main theorem is simply the corollary where t = s, when the assumption
 6796  t ∈ Avoid s A is trivially true by the first Avoid -rule. Isabelle confirms this:
 6797  theorem AF_lemma2: "{s. ∀ p ∈ Paths s. ∃ i. p i ∈ A} ⊆ lfp(af A)"
 6798  by(auto elim: Avoid_in_lfp intro: Avoid.intros)
=== PAGE 197 ===

 6799  10. Case Study: Verifying a Security Protocol

 6800  Communications security is an ancient art. Julius Caesar is said to have

 6801  encrypted his messages, shifting each letter three places along the alphabet.

 6802  Mary Queen of Scots was convicted of treason after a cipher used in her

 6803  letters was broken. Today's postal system incorporates security features. The

 6804  envelope provides a degree of secrecy. The signature provides authenticity

 6805  (proof of origin), as do departmental stamps and letterheads.

 6806  Networks are vulnerable: messages pass through many computers, any of

 6807  which might be controlled by an adversary, who thus can capture or redirect

 6808  messages. People who wish to communicate securely over such a network can

 6809  use cryptography, but if they are to understand each other, they need to

 6810  follow a protocol: a pre-arranged sequence of message formats.

 6811  Protocols can be attacked in many ways, even if encryption is unbreak-

 6812  able. A splicing attack involves an adversary's sending a message composed

 6813  of parts of several old messages. This fake message may have the correct for-

 6814  mat, fooling an honest party. The adversary might be able to masquerade as

 6815  somebody else, or he might obtain a secret key.

 6816  Nonces help prevent splicing attacks. A typical nonce is a 20-byte random

 6817  number. Each message that requires a reply incorporates a nonce. The reply

 6818  must include a copy of that nonce, to prove that it is not a replay of a past

 6819  message. The nonce in the reply must be cryptographically protected, since

 6820  otherwise an adversary could easily replace it by a different one. You should

 6821  be starting to see that protocol design is tricky!

 6822  Researchers are developing methods for proving the correctness of security

 6823  protocols. The Needham-Schroeder public-key protocol [22] has become a

 6824  standard test case. Proposed in 1978, it was found to be defective nearly two

 6825  decades later [18]. This toy protocol will be useful in demonstrating how to

 6826  verify protocols using Isabelle.

 6827  10.1 The Needham-Schroeder Public-Key Protocol

 6828  This protocol uses public-key cryptography. Each person has a private key,

 6829  known only to himself, and a public key, known to everybody. If Alice wants

 6830  to send Bob a secret message, she encrypts it using Bob's public key (which
=== PAGE 198 ===

 6831  188 10. Case Study: Verifying a Security Protocol

 6832  everybody knows), and sends it to Bob. Only Bob has the matching private

 6833  key, which is needed in order to decrypt Alice's message.

 6834  The core of the Needham-Schroeder protocol consists of three messages:

 6835  1. A → B : {|Na, A|}
 6836  Kb

 6837  2. B → A : {|Na, Nb|}
 6838  Ka

 6839  3. A → B : {|Nb|}
 6840  Kb

 6841  First, let's understand the notation. In the first message, Alice sends Bob a

 6842  message consisting of a nonce generated by Alice (Na) paired with Alice's

 6843  name (A) and encrypted using Bob's public key (Kb). In the second message,

 6844  Bob sends Alice a message consisting of Na paired with a nonce generated

 6845  by Bob (Nb), encrypted using Alice's public key (Ka). In the last message,

 6846  Alice returns Nb to Bob, encrypted using his public key.

 6847  When Alice receives Message 2, she knows that Bob has acted on her mes-

 6848  sage, since only he could have decrypted {|Na,A|} and extracted Na. That
 6849  Kb

 6850  is precisely what nonces are for. Similarly, message 3 assures Bob that Alice

 6851  is active. But the protocol was widely believed [7] to satisfy a further prop-

 6852  erty: that Na and Nb were secrets shared by Alice and Bob. (Many protocols

 6853  generate such shared secrets, which can be used to lessen the reliance on

 6854  slow public-key operations.) Lowe found this claim to be false: if Alice runs

 6855  the protocol with someone untrustworthy (Charlie say), then he can start

 6856  a new run with another agent (Bob say). Charlie uses Alice as an oracle,

 6857  masquerading as Alice to Bob [18].

 6858  1. A → C : {|Na, A|} 10 . C → B : {|Na, A|}
 6859  Kc Kb

 6860  2. B → A : {|Na, Nb|}
 6861  Ka
 6862  3. A → C : {|Nb|} 30 . C → B : {|Nb|}
 6863  Kc Kb

 6864  In messages 1 and 3, Charlie removes the encryption using his private key

 6865  and re-encrypts Alice's messages using Bob's public key. Bob is left thinking

 6866  he has run the protocol with Alice, which was not Alice's intention, and Bob

 6867  is unaware that the "secret" nonces are known to Charlie. This is a typical

 6868  man-in-the-middle attack launched by an insider.

 6869  Whether this counts as an attack has been disputed. In protocols of

 6870  this type, we normally assume that the other party is honest. To be hon-

 6871  est means to obey the protocol rules, so Alice's running the protocol with

 6872  Charlie does not make her dishonest, just careless. After Lowe's attack, Alice

 6873  has no grounds for complaint: this protocol does not have to guarantee any-

 6874  thing if you run it with a bad person. Bob does have grounds for complaint,

 6875  however: the protocol tells him that he is communicating with Alice (who is

 6876  honest) but it does not guarantee secrecy of the nonces.

 6877  Lowe also suggested a correction, namely to include Bob's name in mes-

 6878  sage 2:
=== PAGE 199 ===

 6879  10.2 Agents and Messages 189

 6880  1. A → B : {|Na, A|}
 6881  Kb

 6882  2. B → A : {|Na, Nb, B|}
 6883  Ka

 6884  3. A → B : {|Nb|}
 6885  Kb

 6886  If Charlie tries the same attack, Alice will receive the message {|Na, Nb, B|}
 6887  Ka

 6888  when she was expecting to receive {|Na,Nb,C|} . She will abandon the run,
 6889  Ka

 6890  and eventually so will Bob. Below, we shall look at parts of this protocol's

 6891  correctness proof.

 6892  In ground-breaking work, Lowe [18] showed how such attacks could be

 6893  found automatically using a model checker. An alternative, which we shall

 6894  examine below, is to prove protocols correct. Proofs can be done under more

 6895  realistic assumptions because our model does not have to be finite. The strat-

 6896  egy is to formalize the operational semantics of the system and to prove

 6897  security properties using rule induction.

 6898  10.2 Agents and Messages

 6899  All protocol specifications refer to a syntactic theory of messages. Datatype
 6900  agent introduces the constant Server (a trusted central machine, needed for
 6901  some protocols), an infinite population of friendly agents, and the Spy :
 6902  datatype agent = Server | Friend nat | Spy
 6903  Keys are just natural numbers. Function invKey maps a public key to the

 6904  matching private key, and vice versa:
 6905  type synonym key = nat
 6906  consts invKey :: "key ⇒ key"
 6907  Datatype msg introduces the message forms, which include agent names,

 6908  nonces, keys, compound messages, and encryptions.
 6909  datatype
 6910  msg = Agent agent
 6911  | Nonce nat
 6912  | Key key
 6913  | MPair msg msg
 6914  | Crypt key msg
 6915  The notation {|X1, . . . Xn−1, Xn|} abbreviates MPair X1 . . . (MPair Xn−1 Xn).

 6916  Since datatype constructors are injective, we have the theorem
 6917  Crypt K X = Crypt K' X' =⇒ K = K' ∧ X = X'

 6918  A ciphertext can be decrypted using only one key and can yield only one

 6919  plaintext. In the real world, decryption with the wrong key succeeds but

 6920  yields garbage. Our model of encryption is realistic if encryption adds some

 6921  redundancy to the plaintext, such as a checksum, so that garbage can be

 6922  detected.
=== PAGE 200 ===

 6923  190 10. Case Study: Verifying a Security Protocol

 6924  10.3 Modelling the Adversary

 6925  The spy is part of the system and must be built into the model. He is a mali-

 6926  cious user who does not have to follow the protocol. He watches the network

 6927  and uses any keys he knows to decrypt messages. Thus he accumulates ad-

 6928  ditional keys and nonces. These he can use to compose new messages, which

 6929  he may send to anybody.
 6930  Two functions enable us to formalize this behaviour: analz and synth.

 6931  Each function maps a sets of messages to another set of messages. The set
 6932  analz H formalizes what the adversary can learn from the set of messages H.

 6933  The closure properties of this set are defined inductively.
 6934  inductive set
 6935  analz :: "msg set ⇒ msg set"
 6936  for H :: "msg set"
 6937  where
 6938  Inj [intro,simp] : "X ∈ H =⇒ X ∈ analz H"
 6939  | Fst: "{|X,Y|} ∈ analz H =⇒ X ∈ analz H"
 6940  | Snd: "{|X,Y|} ∈ analz H =⇒ Y ∈ analz H"
 6941  | Decrypt [dest]:
 6942  "[[Crypt K X ∈ analz H; Key(invKey K) ∈ analz H ]]
 6943  =⇒ X ∈ analz H"
 6944  Note the Decrypt rule: the spy can decrypt a message encrypted with
 6945  key K if he has the matching key, K−1. Properties proved by rule induction

 6946  include the following:
 6947  G ⊆ H =⇒ analz G ⊆ analz H (analz_mono)
 6948  analz (analz H) = analz H (analz_idem)
 6949  The set of fake messages that an intruder could invent starting from H is
 6950  synth(analz H), where synth H formalizes what the adversary can build from

 6951  the set of messages H.
 6952  inductive set
 6953  synth :: "msg set ⇒ msg set"
 6954  for H :: "msg set"
 6955  where
 6956  Inj [intro]: "X ∈ H =⇒ X ∈ synth H"
 6957  | Agent [intro]: "Agent agt ∈ synth H"
 6958  | MPair [intro]:
 6959  "[[X ∈ synth H; Y ∈ synth H ]] =⇒ {|X,Y |} ∈ synth H"
 6960  | Crypt [intro]:
 6961  "[[X ∈ synth H; Key K ∈ H ]] =⇒ Crypt K X ∈ synth H"

 6962  The set includes all agent names. Nonces and keys are assumed to be

 6963  unguessable, so none are included beyond those already in H . Two elements
 6964  of synth H can be combined, and an element can be encrypted using a key

 6965  present in H.
 6966  Like analz, this set operator is monotone and idempotent. It also satisfies
 6967  an interesting equation involving analz :
=== PAGE 201 ===

 6968  10.4 Event Traces 191

 6969  analz (synth H) = analz H ∪ synth H (analz_synth)
 6970  Rule inversion plays a major role in reasoning about synth, through declara-

 6971  tions such as this one:
 6972  inductive cases Nonce_synth [elim!]: "Nonce n ∈ synth H"
 6973  The resulting elimination rule replaces every assumption of the form Nonce n
 6974  ∈ synth H by Nonce n ∈ H, expressing that a nonce cannot be guessed.
 6975  A third operator, parts, is useful for stating correctness properties. The set
 6976  parts H consists of the components of elements of H. This set includes H and

 6977  is closed under the projections from a compound message to its immediate
 6978  parts. Its definition resembles that of analz except in the rule corresponding
 6979  to the constructor Crypt :
 6980  Crypt K X ∈ parts H =⇒ X ∈ parts H

 6981  The body of an encrypted message is always regarded as part of it. We can
 6982  use parts to express general well-formedness properties of a protocol, for

 6983  example, that an uncompromised agent's private key will never be included

 6984  as a component of any message.

 6985  10.4 Event Traces

 6986  The system's behaviour is formalized as a set of traces of events. The most
 6987  important event, Says A B X, expresses A → B : X, which is the attempt

 6988  by A to send B the message X. A trace is simply a list, constructed in
 6989  reverse using #. Other event types include reception of messages (when we

 6990  want to make it explicit) and an agent's storing a fact.

 6991  Sometimes the protocol requires an agent to generate a new nonce. The

 6992  probability that a 20-byte random number has appeared before is effectively
 6993  zero. To formalize this important property, the set used evs denotes the set of
 6994  all items mentioned in the trace evs. The function used has a straightforward
 6995  recursive definition. Here is the case for Says event:
 6996  used (Says A B X # evs) = parts {X} ∪ used evs
 6997  The function knows formalizes an agent's knowledge. Mostly we only care
 6998  about the spy's knowledge, and knows Spy evs is the set of items available
 6999  to the spy in the trace evs. Already in the empty trace, the spy starts with

 7000  some secrets at his disposal, such as the private keys of compromised users.
 7001  After each Says event, the spy learns the message that was sent:
 7002  knows Spy (Says A B X # evs) = insert X (knows Spy evs)

 7003  Combinations of functions express other important sets of messages derived
 7004  from evs :
 7005  – analz (knows Spy evs) is everything that the spy could learn by decryption
=== PAGE 202 ===

 7006  192 10. Case Study: Verifying a Security Protocol

 7007  – synth (analz (knows Spy evs)) is everything that the spy could generate

 7008  The function pubK maps agents to their public keys. The function priK

 7009  maps agents to their private keys. It is merely an abbreviation (cf. Sect. 4.1.4)
 7010  defined in terms of invKey and pubK.
 7011  consts pubK :: "agent ⇒ key"
 7012  abbreviation priK :: "agent ⇒ key"
 7013  where "priK x ≡ invKey(pubK x)"
 7014  The set bad consists of those agents whose private keys are known to the spy.

 7015  Two axioms are asserted about the public-key cryptosystem. No two

 7016  agents have the same public key, and no private key equals any public key.
 7017  axiomatization where
 7018  inj_pubK: "inj pubK" and
 7019  priK_neq_pubK: "priK A 6= pubK B"

 7020  10.5 Modelling the Protocol

 7021  inductive set ns_public :: "event list set"
 7022  where

 7023  Nil: "[] ∈ ns_public"

 7024  | Fake: "[[evsf ∈ ns_public; X ∈ synth (analz (knows Spy evsf))]]
 7025  =⇒ Says Spy B X # evsf ∈ ns_public"

 7026  | NS1: " [[evs1 ∈ ns_public; Nonce NA ∈/ used evs1]]
 7027  =⇒ Says A B (Crypt (pubK B) {|Nonce NA, Agent A|})
 7028  # evs1 ∈ ns_public"

 7029  | NS2: " [[evs2 ∈ ns_public; Nonce NB ∈/ used evs2;
 7030  Says A' B (Crypt (pubK B) {|Nonce NA, Agent A |}) ∈ set evs2]]
 7031  =⇒ Says B A (Crypt (pubK A) {|Nonce NA, Nonce NB, Agent B|})
 7032  # evs2 ∈ ns_public"

 7033  | NS3: " [[evs3 ∈ ns_public;
 7034  Says A B (Crypt (pubK B) {|Nonce NA, Agent A |}) ∈ set evs3;
 7035  Says B' A (Crypt (pubK A) {|Nonce NA, Nonce NB, Agent B |})
 7036  ∈ set evs3]]
 7037  =⇒ Says A B (Crypt (pubK B) (Nonce NB)) # evs3 ∈ ns_public"

 7038  Fig. 10.1. An Inductive Protocol Definition
=== PAGE 203 ===

 7039  10.6 Proving Elementary Properties 193

 7040  Let us formalize the Needham-Schroeder public-key protocol, as corrected

 7041  by Lowe:

 7042  1. A → B : {|Na, A|}
 7043  Kb

 7044  2. B → A : {|Na, Nb, B|}
 7045  Ka

 7046  3. A → B : {|Nb|}
 7047  Kb

 7048  Each protocol step is specified by a rule of an inductive definition. An
 7049  event trace has type event list, so we declare the constant ns_public to be

 7050  a set of such traces.
 7051  Figure 10.1 presents the inductive definition. The Nil rule introduces the
 7052  empty trace. The Fake rule models the adversary's sending a message built
 7053  from components taken from past traffic, expressed using the functions synth
 7054  and analz. The next three rules model how honest agents would perform the

 7055  three protocol steps.
 7056  Here is a detailed explanation of rule NS2. A trace containing an event of

 7057  the form
 7058  Says A' B (Crypt (pubK B) {|Nonce NA, Agent A |})

 7059  may be extended by an event of the form
 7060  Says B A (Crypt (pubK A) {|Nonce NA, Nonce NB, Agent B|})
 7061  where NB is a fresh nonce: Nonce NB ∈/ used evs2. Writing the sender as A'
 7062  indicates that B does not know who sent the message. Calling the trace variable evs2 rather than simply evs helps us know where we are in a proof after
 7063  many case-splits: every subgoal mentioning evs2 involves message 2 of the

 7064  protocol.

 7065  Benefits of this approach are simplicity and clarity. The semantic model

 7066  is set theory, proofs are by induction and the translation from the informal

 7067  notation to the inductive rules is straightforward.

 7068  10.6 Proving Elementary Properties

 7069  Secrecy properties can be hard to prove. The conclusion of a typical secrecy
 7070  theorem is X ∈/ analz (knows Spy evs). The difficulty arises from having to
 7071  reason about analz, or less formally, showing that the spy can never learn X.
 7072  Much easier is to prove that X can never occur at all. Such regularity properties are typically expressed using parts rather than analz.
 7073  The following lemma states that A 's private key is potentially known to the
 7074  spy if and only if A belongs to the set bad of compromised agents. The statement uses parts: the very presence of A's private key in a message, whether
 7075  protected by encryption or not, is enough to confirm that A is compromised.

 7076  The proof, like nearly all protocol proofs, is by induction over traces.
=== PAGE 204 ===

 7077  194 10. Case Study: Verifying a Security Protocol

 7078  lemma Spy_see_priK [simp]:
 7079  "evs ∈ ns_public
 7080  =⇒ (Key (priK A) ∈ parts (knows Spy evs)) = (A ∈ bad)"
 7081  apply (erule ns_public.induct, simp_all)

 7082  The induction yields five subgoals, one for each rule in the definition of
 7083  ns_public. The idea is to prove that the protocol property holds initially (rule
 7084  Nil ), is preserved by each of the legitimate protocol steps (rules NS1–3), and
 7085  even is preserved in the face of anything the spy can do (rule Fake ).

 7086  The proof is trivial. No legitimate protocol rule sends any keys at all,
 7087  so only Fake is relevant. Indeed, simplification leaves only the Fake case, as
 7088  indicated by the variable name evsf :
 7089  1. Vevsf X.
 7090  [[evsf ∈ ns_public;
 7091  (Key (priK A) ∈ parts (knows Spy evsf)) = (A ∈ bad);
 7092  X ∈ synth (analz (knows Spy evsf))]]
 7093  =⇒ (Key (priK A) ∈ parts (insert X (knows Spy evsf))) =
 7094  (A ∈ bad)
 7095  by blast
 7096  The Fake case is proved automatically. If priK A is in the extended trace

 7097  then either (1) it was already in the original trace or (2) it was generated by

 7098  the spy, who must have known this key already. Either way, the induction

 7099  hypothesis applies.

 7100  Unicity lemmas are regularity lemmas stating that specified items can

 7101  occur only once in a trace. The following lemma states that a nonce cannot

 7102  be used both as Na and as Nb unless it is known to the spy. Intuitively,

 7103  it holds because honest agents always choose fresh values as nonces; only

 7104  the spy might reuse a value, and he doesn't know this particular value. The
 7105  proof script is short: induction, simplification, blast. The first line uses the
 7106  rule rev_mp to prepare the induction by moving two assumptions into the

 7107  induction formula.
 7108  lemma no_nonce_NS1_NS2:
 7109  " [[Crypt (pubK C) {|NA', Nonce NA, Agent D |} ∈ parts (knows Spy evs);
 7110  Crypt (pubK B) {|Nonce NA, Agent A |} ∈ parts (knows Spy evs);
 7111  evs ∈ ns_public]]
 7112  =⇒ Nonce NA ∈ analz (knows Spy evs)"
 7113  apply (erule rev_mp, erule rev_mp)
 7114  apply (erule ns_public.induct, simp_all)
 7115  apply (blast intro: analz_insertI)+
 7116  done
 7117  The following unicity lemma states that, if NA is secret, then its appear-

 7118  ance in any instance of message 1 determines the other components. The

 7119  proof is similar to the previous one.
 7120  lemma unique_NA:
 7121  " [[Crypt(pubK B) {|Nonce NA, Agent A |} ∈ parts(knows Spy evs);
 7122  Crypt(pubK B') {|Nonce NA, Agent A'|} ∈ parts(knows Spy evs);
=== PAGE 205 ===

 7123  10.7 Proving Secrecy Theorems 195

 7124  Nonce NA ∈/ analz (knows Spy evs); evs ∈ ns_public]]
 7125  =⇒ A=A' ∧ B=B'"

 7126  10.7 Proving Secrecy Theorems

 7127  The secrecy theorems for Bob (the second participant) are especially impor-

 7128  tant because they fail for the original protocol. The following theorem states

 7129  that if Bob sends message 2 to Alice, and both agents are uncompromised,

 7130  then Bob's nonce will never reach the spy.
 7131  theorem Spy_not_see_NB [dest]:
 7132  "[[Says B A (Crypt (pubK A) {|Nonce NA, Nonce NB, Agent B|}) ∈ set evs;
 7133  A ∈/ bad; B ∈/ bad; evs ∈ ns_public]]
 7134  =⇒ Nonce NB ∈/ analz (knows Spy evs)"

 7135  To prove it, we must formulate the induction properly (one of the assumptions mentions evs ), apply induction, and simplify:
 7136  apply (erule rev_mp, erule ns_public.induct, simp_all)

 7137  The proof states are too complicated to present in full. Let's examine the

 7138  simplest subgoal, that for message 1. The following event has just occurred:

 7139  1. A0 → B0 : {|Na0, A0|}
 7140  Kb0

 7141  The variables above have been primed because this step belongs to a different

 7142  run from that referred to in the theorem statement — the theorem refers to a

 7143  past instance of message 2, while this subgoal concerns message 1 being sent
 7144  just now. In the Isabelle subgoal, instead of primed variables like B0 and Na0
 7145  we have Ba and NAa :
 7146  1. Vevs1 NAa Ba.
 7147  [[A ∈/ bad; B ∈/ bad; evs1 ∈ ns_public;
 7148  Says B A (Crypt (pubK A) {|Nonce NA, Nonce NB, Agent B|})
 7149  ∈ set evs1 −→
 7150  Nonce NB ∈/ analz (knows Spy evs1);
 7151  Nonce NAa ∈/ used evs1]]
 7152  =⇒ Ba ∈ bad −→
 7153  Says B A (Crypt (pubK A) {|Nonce NA, Nonce NB, Agent B |})
 7154  ∈ set evs1 −→
 7155  NB 6= NAa

 7156  The simplifier has used a default simplification rule that does a case analysis

 7157  for each encrypted message on whether or not the decryption key is compro-

 7158  mised.
 7159  analz (insert (Crypt K X) H) =
 7160  (if Key (invKey K) ∈ analz H
 7161  then insert (Crypt K X) (analz (insert X H))
 7162  else insert (Crypt K X) (analz H)) (analz_Crypt_if)
=== PAGE 206 ===

 7163  196 10. Case Study: Verifying a Security Protocol

 7164  The simplifier has also used Spy_see_priK, proved in Sect. 10.6 above, to yield
 7165  Ba ∈ bad.

 7166  Recall that this subgoal concerns the case where the last message to be

 7167  sent was
 7168  1. A0 → B0 : {|Na0, A0|} .
 7169  Kb0
 7170  This message can compromise Nb only if Nb = Na0 and B0 is compromised,

 7171  allowing the spy to decrypt the message. The Isabelle subgoal says precisely
 7172  this, if we allow for its choice of variable names. Proving NB 6= NAa is easy: NB
 7173  was sent earlier, while NAa is fresh; formally, we have the assumption Nonce
 7174  NAa ∈/ used evs1.
 7175  Note that our reasoning concerned B's participation in another run.

 7176  Agents may engage in several runs concurrently, and some attacks work by

 7177  interleaving the messages of two runs. With model checking, this possibility

 7178  can cause a state-space explosion, and for us it certainly complicates proofs.

 7179  The biggest subgoal concerns message 2. It splits into several cases, such as

 7180  whether or not the message just sent is the very message mentioned in the

 7181  theorem statement. Some of the cases are proved by unicity, others by the

 7182  induction hypothesis. For all those complications, the proofs are automatic
 7183  by blast with the theorem no_nonce_NS1_NS2.

 7184  The remaining theorems about the protocol are not hard to prove. The
 7185  following one asserts a form of authenticity: if B has sent an instance of
 7186  message 2 to A and has received the expected reply, then that reply really
 7187  originated with A. The proof is a simple induction.
 7188  theorem B_trusts_NS3:
 7189  "[[Says B A (Crypt (pubK A) {|Nonce NA, Nonce NB, Agent B|}) ∈ set evs;
 7190  Says A' B (Crypt (pubK B) (Nonce NB)) ∈ set evs;
 7191  A ∈/ bad; B ∈/ bad; evs ∈ ns_public]]
 7192  =⇒ Says A B (Crypt (pubK B) (Nonce NB)) ∈ set evs"
 7193  From similar assumptions, we can prove that A started the protocol run
 7194  by sending an instance of message 1 involving the nonce NA . For this theorem,

 7195  the conclusion is
 7196  Says A B (Crypt (pubK B) {|Nonce NA, Agent A|}) ∈ set evs
 7197  Analogous theorems can be proved for A, stating that nonce NA remains secret and that message 2 really originates with B. Even the flawed protocol
 7198  establishes these properties for A; the flaw only harms the second participant.

 7199  Detailed information on this protocol verification technique can be found

 7200  elsewhere [30], including proofs of an Internet protocol [31]. We must stress

 7201  that the protocol discussed in this chapter is trivial. There are only three

 7202  messages; no keys are exchanged; we merely have to prove that encrypted

 7203  data remains secret. Real world protocols are much longer and distribute

 7204  many secrets to their participants. To be realistic, the model has to include

 7205  the possibility of keys being lost dynamically due to carelessness. If those

 7206  keys have been used to encrypt other sensitive information, there may be
=== PAGE 207 ===

 7207  197

 7208  cascading losses. We may still be able to establish a bound on the losses and

 7209  to prove that other protocol runs function correctly [32]. Proofs of real-world

 7210  protocols follow the strategy illustrated above, but the subgoals can be much

 7211  bigger and there are more of them.
